Prologue. 

Above the barren, cratered terrain, the steep jagged, blackened peaks of an unnamed mountain range rise to pierce the heavens. The sky seems to bleed, with its tendrils of light leaving glimmering scars across the starry night. The reaches of this night are painted with vibrant auras, their glowing green and blue waves adding otherworldly vibrancy to the dark expanse. These celestial lights twist and weave in a hypnotic display, their ethereal glow providing a stark contrast to the shadowy, brooding landscape below. The atmosphere of the ground radiates a faint luminescence, casting a soft, eerie blue hue across the horizon below. 
Shadows strobe across the arid and rocky ground, some the size of pebbles, some that are so giant, they cover this world for days. These shadows are a wreck of asteroid debris that ebb and flow like tides of an ocean across the sky, bearing the scars of their recent birth, poked with crevasses and gouges. Some are a deep, rusted red, while others are icy bodies glinting with a frosty blue, reflecting the faint starlight like not so distant, glittering diamonds.
As the dull, colorless terrain contrasts with the splendors of the heavens, one can not help but find the tranquility in the chaos of it all. The sky and all of its infinite expanse tends to be filled with contradictions. Gravity's invisible hands can both gather the lost and wandering bodies of the cosmos, birthing planets, or ruthlessly annihilate them, pulling all into the inescapable abyss of a black hole, where creation is undone, where all is devoured by the void. Everything in the universe is governed by a delicate scale, ruling the balance of light and dark, beauty and horror, life and death, existence and absence. 

The sky is a place where the laws of nature seem both familiar and profoundly alien.

Chapter 1.

The serene silence of the long evening was calmly broken by the curious voice of a child. "What's that star mommy?" a young boy asked, his eyes wide with wonder, his finger pointed at the sky. He cuddled on his mother's lap, sharing a wooden rocking chair with her on their small porch. He let out a small earnest yawn, as his eyes grew more tired as she held him close and peacefully ran her fingers through his hair. His hair was always messy, but his manners were kept. 
The pair shared this nightly tradition together, staring at the enchanted canvas above them, imagining just how different their lives would be on the numerous planets that twinkled overhead. Lives that always seemed just out of reach. Despite many efforts, the boy never took up a passion for bedtime stories, instead he yearned for something more tangible, learning about the many worlds, hoping to one day visit them all. But he knew those days were a lifetime away.
His mother rocked gently on their chair, her voice carrying a sense of allure, "Thats Modanick" she paused briefly answering his question, taking one long look at the yellow glimmering dot and gently kissing his head. "It's where you were born Jimzy". 
"I've been there before?" Jimzy asked his mother, his voice squeaking with enthusiasm and a hint of confusion. "I don't remember it," he said passively.
His mother's eyes softened their gaze, carrying a sense of sweet nostalgia for a moment before answering. "You were far too young honey, someday soon I'll bring you. Your daddy and I studied there for a few years before we had you", she hugged him a bit tighter followed by another kiss on his cheek. Jimzy giggled, he sometimes wondered if she likes their nightly 'star-time' even more than he does. Jimzy continued to wonder.
"What's home like?" 
His mom paused for another second before answering. "Well honey, home is here. Home is wherever mommy and daddy are. I know this rock we're on now isn't much, but someday our home is going to be even bigger, so big, you'll even have your own room." 
She kept looking at Modanick in the sky, trying to find the words to describe a place so beautiful, so intricate, and astoundingly different from the small asteroid they currently are settled on.
"Well honey, Modanick is a planet where everything is bigger. Even the trees there are taller than houses. It's a place where it's always daytime, and always warm" her smile reflecting the star's sunny glow. 
Jimzy let out a soft "wow" that trailed off, as he imagined a place shining with more shades of green had ever seen in his life. Jimzy wondered if he would like Modanick, he would surely miss his star time here. 
"And that one?" Jimzy's finger shifted, pointing to another celestial body.
"That's Creta, it's going to be our home in just a month or two" she said excitedly, gently shaking him with enthusiasm. "I know living here has been tough, but your Daddy and I have been working very hard here, and it's all going to pay off, I promise" she reassured him. 
He thought about having a whole wide room for himself. His mind was buzzing with ideas on decor, the bunk beds for his stuffed animals, hologram posters, entire troves for his new toys, but he couldn't quite figure it out. Yet, it all would seem a bit empty without his parents sharing the opposite wall. At least he wouldn't have to worry about daddy's snoring. 
His mother glanced inside of their small home, starting to smell the welcoming allure of dinner. "Jimzy sweetie, it's almost time to fetch your father from work, he can tell you all about Creta during supper, go get your shoes ready".
The sleepiness that filled Jimzy's body vanished in an instant, replaced by an all to common burst of youthful energy. He leapt out of the rocking chair, hugged his mother for the last time, and raced for his pair of shoes. He slipped them on, attempted to tie them, before promptly giving up and shoving the laces in the sole. 
Jimzy's strut was a brisk and exciting pace, but his short legs made the pace moderate. He set off on the gravel trail, the pebbles crunching underneath him. He walked past thin saplings that were planted but hadn't yet blossomed. He left the dim orange glow of his home, to begin his journey. His father's station was on the other side of their asteroid colony, a whole mile away. 
His mind continued to daydream of his new life as he ventured forth, he looked around the rocky landscape, and tried to imagine grass covering everything he saw, but struggled to. His imagination usually knew no bounds, but he knew better than to get too excited, there was no way grass could be as abundant as his mother said it could be. In fact, he somewhat liked his home here. His parents always said "it's not much" but they also said "home is wherever we are", and they were all together, here. He never knew why they talked down about the Wakeful Islands. Sure these asteroids weren't as humongous as planets, but it was cozy. He could run around the diameter of the asteroid in under a day, all beneath a changing sky. His neighbors always gave him sweets when he saw them, speaking of which, would they be moving too? Who is going to take care of the greenhouse garden after his family leaves? 
Jimzy's mind continued to wander about his future, and he took a look at all of the things he would miss. He looked at a sculpture in the town square, which consisted of a series of scrap metals lumped together from an old aircraft that was painted teal and magenta. He was never able to make out what exactly the sculpture was supposed to be. It usually bothered him, but not this time.
He began to pass the houses that lined the road. Twelve houses dotted the street, all identical upon first glance, but the personality of the inhabitants began to shine through. Each home was a modest wooden building, with simple designs and muted colors, primarily gray and faded cyan. The slanted metal roofs are equipped with a series of short corroded antennas, and a stout chimney emitting a wisp of smoke hinting at the warmth inside. Some of the houses are surrounded by makeshift fences, made of roughly hewn wooden planks, marking off small, personal areas—perhaps gardens, yards, or storage spaces for firewood or supplies.
The first house he passed was Mr. Armstin's, who could often be seen tinkering on various contraptions in his front yard when he wasn’t at the station with his father. His job was to repair the gravity spires that were spread sparsely across their asteroid. He always waved at Jimzy, and even led the decoration on the small holidays the town shared. Sometimes during the festivals, he’d get sad and slur his words whenever he talked about the gravity spires. He’d talk about how he couldn’t find the parts he needed, but Jimzy didn’t mind, the less gravity made it so he could jump even higher. 
As Jimzy’s youthful energy propelled him forward on his journey, he passed the docking pad. The docking pad was made of flat smooth concrete, painted a nice azure, and had some lights dotting the perimeter. Once every week, a ship would drop by with three men from The Guard. They’d deliver supplies, and pick up the fuel Daddy makes. Sometimes they’d even bring Jimzy trinkets from off-world. Tiny medallions from Maldo, small shiny faceless iron figurines from Nancast, or even bright fruits from Kinton. 
Slowly, The Guard would hover down, landing their shining dual winged cruiser, and after just a moment of stability, the silence would be broken with an expulsion of air, signaling the back hatch was open. Out would walk out the soldiers, in glimmering white armor, trimmed with a lapis blue. They'd walk down the small ramp, removing their headgear, revealing friendly human faces behind the noble yet intimidating helmets. Sometimes, they'd be followed by a hovering cart filled with goods, or even a rusted brown robot, about two feet tall, shaped like a rusted filing cabinet that emitted some cheerful muffled music. 
Those days were always Jimzy's favorite day of the week, he always hoped to one day see the inside of the ship, but was too mesmerized or too shy to ask. Usually, that day would have been today, but due to the orbit of the Wakeful Islands he lived on, one week every six months they have no radio contact. The good news was that next week, they'd always bring twice as many things. Jimzy almost forgot he was moving soon, maybe next time he'll work up the courage to ask to see the inside. 
Jimzy kept admiring the small details in the places he passed. The uncomfortable stone benches underneath the street lamps, the dirt pit he'd frequently scrape his knees in when playing with his toys, he'd miss it all. As he walked, he started to keep his head down, staring at the gravel he crunched along. His lip began to quiver, his jaw tense, he almost wanted to cry, but he knew he'd have his own room soon. He swallowed his sadness, and kicked a small stone that was stagnant in his path. He glanced at the gray stone, and realized he'd miss it too. He knelt over and picked it up. It looked and felt just like every other stone he'd come across, but he decided to keep it safe in his pocket. 
After a few more paces, he soon heard the soft hum from his father's work station. Everyone in town worked on this station, but a lot of what they did went over Jimzy's head. He knew they drilled down into the asteroids, digging for something, using machines to melt it perhaps, then selling it to The Guard. His father was the one who invented the machine to do this, and makes a lot of money doing it, although it doesn't feel like it yet. He made some kind of promise to stay on the Wakeful islands until he gets enough, which he must be close to since his mother told him they're moving soon.
The station was a worn three story gray outpost, illuminated with orange hazy lights that would flash on occasion, the building was a mixture between fortified and functional, large radar dishes and antennas stuck out from the roof, in between mounted solar panels. It had small round, thick windows, and a thick gray stripe at the base of each floor. On the outside, The Guard had their ensign painted on the walls, a simple skinny blue trident, accompanied by a few posters. 
Attached to the building was a series of pipes and generators, connected to thick cables, that powered both the station, and the space rovers that always seemed to be under maintenance parked just outside. Jimzy had hardly seen those things move, but he would sometimes sit on the hood and snack while he waited to see his father.
He jogged up the stairs and stood at the steel doors, before they wooshed open and he hurriedly walked inside. He walked to the front desk, to a woman relaxingly sipping chai, while typing on her tablet. He peeped his head over the counter.
"Hi miss Jaley!" he squeaked
"Hi Jimzy" she said with a radiant smile. "You're here early, but boy do I have something for you"
Jimzy's eyes perked up, he hoped it was sweets or a cool printed poster of The Guard. She reached out on her desk, picked up something small, and gently hovered her fist over his palms. She let go, dropping a wrapped candy in his hands. She let out a small gentle giggle and playfully gestured her finger to shush him.
"Don't tell your daddy I gave you sugar before dinner time" she said softly. "Those candies were my favorite growing up, I had them ordered special months ago, they're from my home in Shayron, I doubt you've had anything sweeter"
Jimzy stared at the candy, it looked like a small squishy piece of charcoal, but possessed tiny sprinkles of reflective dots of every color he could imagine. His mouth began to water. 
"Thank you missy Jaley!' He gave her a small hug and squeezed tight. "Miss Jaley, when is Daddy done working?"
"Shouldn't be too long kiddo, he should be out…" she glanced at her tablet. "in five minutes?"
It was gonna be a close one, but he knew what he had to do at that time. He looked at the door to the hallway, then glanced at the time on Miss Jaley's tablet, and plopped the piece of candy in his mouth.
As his teeth gently bit down, the hard shell of the candy gave way with a satisfying crack, releasing a flood of sweetness that spread across his tongue like a burst of sunlight. The flavor was unlike anything Jimzy had ever tasted before. It was as if every sweet thing he'd ever known—ripe strawberries, honeyed dew, the essence of the sun itself—had been distilled into a single, perfect drop. The taste was pure, intense, and utterly mesmerizing.
But as the intense sweetness hit him, it was so overwhelming that his mouth puckered up involuntarily, almost as if he had bitten into something sour. The sheer force of the sugary explosion was enough to make his eyes squeeze shut, and a small shiver ran down his spine. It was a sweetness so pure, so concentrated, that it wrapped around his taste buds like a vice, pulling every sensation tight until his whole face scrunched up in response.
Mid euphoria, the door to the hallway wooshed open. Out walked a man in his early thirties, wearing a formal cobalt suede uniform, sporting a well trimmed mustache and stubble, thinly framed glasses whose lenses faintly flickered with an icy blue blur, reflecting back the tiny screen within them. He took off his white yet dusty hard hat, and placed it on the wall.
“Daddy!” Jimzy exclaimed, running over to his father leaping into his arms. 
His dad let out a loving grunt and picked him up and swept him up into the warmest hug the boy will ever know, and always miss.
"Hey buddy I missed you" he said adoringly as he lifted the boy onto his shoulders. He tapped the boy's knee, beckoning him. "Hey champ, if you could have anything in the world what would it be?"
Jimzy scrunched his nose and tried to think of an answer. "anything?" he asked.
"Anything." His father said reassuringly.
"Even a spaceship?" 
"Even a spaceship. We're gonna get you one".
Jimzy's mind raced with ideas. He had seen loads of spaceships on the waiting room screens in his father's work station, some were painted a sleek crimson and gold, others heavy duty, some were no more than hoverbikes that could zoom faster than his eyes could catch up with. This might be the toughest decision he had ever faced.
Miss Jaley softly spoke up to Jimzy's father, "Korjin, does this mean we have work off the next week?"
"You know what? Even better, let's just call it wraps today. We finally hit that quota, all those nights and days that we all spent here, we earned this." he said determinedly. "But… there is one last thing I want from everyone". 
Miss Jaley and Jimzon paused looking at Korjin in anticipation. 
	"Gather the town, in two days let's throw the biggest community party this dusty rock will ever see". 
	Miss Jaley and Jimzon's eyes lit up, instantly brainstorming about their favorite activities. 

Unfortunately, this festival would never happen. 

	"Oh, and tell Spara I have two bottles of white Astrovin I could bring!" Miss Jaley chirped excitedly. 
Korjin chuckled, "You know my wife and I do love Astrovin". 
	Miss Jaley "I think you'll love the ones I've been saving, they're Pre-War aged."
	"Pre-War? How long have you been holding onto those? Well, I do suppose it is a big milestone for everyone, plus any hangovers won't be a problem, work's well and done with"
	Jimzy never knew why adults liked to drink astrovin so much, it smelled like rotten fruit, despite how he was never allowed to try the grown up drink, his excitement brewed nonetheless. 
Jimzy was eager to see everyone. Many days he didn't have anyone to play with, as the only other child on the Wakeful Islands was his little brother, but it had been so long, he was used to playing alone. It's been a while since he'd stop by his brother's headstone to show him his new trinkets from The Guard, sometimes he was too scared to, but maybe he would after supper, it felt like the right thing to do.
"Hey Jimzy, did you decide what type of spaceship you want?" Korjin asked his son, noticing he got a little quiet.
"I want a purple one that can shoot yellow lasers" 
His father chuckled, "I'll see what we can do about the lasers, but I think we can do purple"
Together the two trekked home triumphantly, dreaming of their future, a dream within grasp, but that is about to shatter within the night
Not long after, the small family found themselves gathered around their old, well-worn wooden table. The faint glow of the evening sky, now streaked with the last hints of the glowing auras above, filtered through the windows, cast a peaceful light across the room. Jimzy, perched eagerly in his chair, began to absentmindedly dance his fork across the plate in front of him. He poked and prodded at the seared tofu and the mashed rockroot, his thoughts far away, likely among the stars he and his mother had been admiring earlier.
His mother, Spara, noticed his lack of focus. "Jimzy, honey," she said, her voice carrying the same gentle warmth that made the evening air feel calm, "you’ve hardly touched your spireleafs. You didn’t spoil your appetite with candy before supper, did you?"
Jimzy froze, his fork hovering just above the plate. He quickly shook his head no, his messy hair bouncing with the motion, but his guilty expression and wide eyes were apparent. With swift and exaggerated effort, he speared a large forkful of mashed rockroot and stuffed it into his mouth, as if to prove his innocence. 
Korjin and Spara shared a quiet chuckle, exchanging a glance that only parents could—bemused but affectionate, as if they were watching a familiar performance unfold. Their smiles, however, held the joy of a family who, despite everything, still found peace in moments like this.
"So," Spara said, a playful lilt to her voice as she looked at her son, "what’s this I’ve been hearing about a purple spaceship?"
Jimzy’s eyes lit up, and he sat up a little straighter, his earlier guilt forgotten. "Daddy says I can get a super star carrier with a million lasers that can go the fastest in the world!" he exclaimed, his voice brimming with the unrestrained enthusiasm only a child can muster.
Spara smiled indulgently, clearly entertained by the wild hyperbole of her son’s dreams. "Well, sweetie, maybe we should start with some flight lessons first. I can teach you when we get to Creta," she said, her words carrying both a promise and a challenge.
Jimzy’s mouth was still full, but that didn’t stop him from responding, his words muffled by the food. "Mommy, you know how to fly?" His eyes widened, as if discovering a new superpower his mother had kept hidden.
"Who do you think flew us here, Jimzy?" Korjin chimed in, his voice filled with an endearing pride for his wife. "Your mommy was a decorated pilot in the Royal Space Force."
Spara blushed slightly, brushing off the compliment with a small wave of her hand. "It was a long time ago," she said softly, though there was a flicker of wistfulness in her eyes.
Jimzy’s gaze never left her. He looked at her with a new kind of admiration, as if she were no longer just the one who tucked him in at night or kissed him on the cheek but someone who had once soared through the stars, navigating the vastness of space.
"Did you meet Daddy flying?" Jimzy asked, curiosity brimming in his voice.
Spara chuckled softly, casting a glance at Korjin. "Not exactly," she said, her voice laced with fondness. "But we did meet through our work. Your father, well, he was always more comfortable with his feet on the ground, inventing machines to make our lives easier." She looked at Korjin, a playful twinkle in her eyes. "Your father’s more of a… ground specialist."
Jimzy tilted his head, confused. "Wait, Daddy, you can’t fly?"
Korjin gave a sheepish grin, rubbing the back of his neck. "I can fly, I just… Well, let's say I prefer to let Mommy handle the piloting." He chuckled lightly, though there was a hint of truth in his admission. "Plus I suffer from uh, space hiccups, so I'm happy being a passenger".
The boy giggled at his father’s explanation but quickly moved on, his mind returning to the spaceship of his dreams. "Have you thought of a name for it yet?" Korjin asked, trying to shift the focus.
Jimzy’s expression softened, and for a brief moment, the light in his eyes dimmed. He had already thought of a proper name to christen his ship – The Powitt, after his brother. As much of a struggle it could be sharing toys at times, there wasn’t anyone else Jimzy would rather share his adventures with. Naturally, it wouldn't be the same, but it was something
"Not yet," Jimzy lied, managing a small smile. 
He knew better than to mention his brother’s name when everyone was happy. He hadn’t seen his parents this lighthearted in a long time, and he didn’t want to ruin it. In truth, this meal would be his last happy memory with them.
Jimzy stood at the sink, rinsing his plate, watching the spireleafs and rockroot swirl down into the drain, the warm water coating his hands. After a few scrubs of his sponge, the water turned ice cold, shocking his fingertips. He pulled his hands away, leaving the unfinished plate under the faucet. 
Jimzy trotted into the living room. The familiar scent of home filled the air, blending with the soft crackle of an old record player spinning a tune his parents loved—something slow and happy, from before the war, back when things were simpler. The gentle melody filled the room, wrapping them in warmth.
His parents lounged on the couch, Korjin’s arm draped around Spara as they shared a rare bottle of red Astrovin. The mismatched furniture and the worn woven rug beneath their feet added to the cozy feel, the dim lights casting a soft, golden glow. Spara laughed quietly at something Korjin murmured in her ear, and they clinked their glasses in a quiet toast, savoring the peace of the evening.
"Mommy, Daddy, can I play outside in the yard before bedtime?" Jimzy asked, poking his head through the doorway.
Spara glanced up with a smile still lingering on her lips. "Of course, sweetie. Just be sure to come back in before it gets too dark."
Korjin, with his voice gentle and content, added, "Go on, kiddo." He poured another glass of Astrovin, the rich crimson liquid swirling in the bottle, as the soft music played on.
Jimzy nodded and stepped outside, the crunch of his worn shoes against the worn floorboards fading into the cool night air. The yard was quiet, save for the distant echoes of the record player drifting through the open door behind him.
He paced outside, crunching the gravel beneath his feet, the cool night air settling on his skin. His steps were slow, deliberate, as he headed toward the side of the house he always tended to avoid. The shadows seemed deeper here, away from the soft glow spilling from the living room window. The faint strains of the happy music still reached his ears, but it felt distant now, as if it belonged to another world entirely.

POWITT AVARIS

5 Years Old
The imposing capital letters carried a weight Jimzy wasn’t prepared for. They seemed too bold, too final, and carried a sense of imposing dread that wrapped around him like a cold wind. The headstone was made of stern sandstone imported from some distant beach off the shores of Modanick, its surface weathered but strong. The headstone was trimmed with dark bronze, the letters stood out in an obsidian black, sharp and unforgiving.
Jimzy couldn’t bring himself to look directly at it, his gaze stuck on the small rocks beneath his feet. He shuffled them with his toes, trying to find comfort in the small, meaningless movements. Finally, he managed to glance up slightly, his eyes landing on the withering flowers scattered at the base of the stone, petals cracking, colors fading. The sight made his chest tighten. He squeezed his eyes shut and negan to think.
He had so much to say, yet nothing came. His mind raced, but his expression remained blank, frozen. Was Powitt even listening? Could he hear him from wherever he was now? Jimzy didn’t know whether to pray— to him, to the universe, to gods, everything or to nothing at all. The uncertainty gnawed at him. He just stood there, silent.
Memories flooded back, the hardest ones first. The nightmares that chased him night after night, vivid dreams of his brother trying to speak to him, but no words ever audibly coming out. The haunting image of his father, usually so strong, breaking down in tears. The family had started sleeping in the same room after a while, hoping it would help chase away the bad dreams.
Guilt pressed down on him, heavy and unrelenting. His parents always told him it wasn’t his fault, but his mind betrayed him, replaying every moment he had ever hurt Powitt, every selfish word or teasing jab. The memories came in waves, like a broken radio playing the same awful tune. He knew he couldn’t have stopped what happened, but he could’ve been kinder. He just hoped Powitt remembered the good times more than the bad.
Whenever he played with Powitt’s old action figures, he made sure they were always the heroes. In their world, nothing bad ever happened. They always won.
Jimzy’s eyes twitched, his vision blurred as tears welled up. He blinked rapidly, trying to hold them back, but a single tear escaped, trailing down his cheek. As he wiped it away, something strange caught his eye. The grave—it seemed to glow, faintly, with a soft red hue. He blinked again, unsure if it was his imagination, but no. The glow was real, and it wasn’t just the headstone. Everything around him, the trees, the ground, the sky—everything was bathed in a faint, eerie crimson light.
His heart raced as he looked up, and there, high above, a red star hung in the sky. It wasn’t like anything he’d ever seen before, and it seemed to grow brighter, larger with each passing second. Luckily he knew the perfect person to ask. Jimzy took one last look at the headstone, bowed his head, and trotted inside. 
The fireplace crackled softly, sending the faint smell of smoke into the air, mingling with the lingering scents of the dinner they’d just shared. Jimzy felt the heat wrap around him like a comforting blanket as he stepped inside. The soft, upbeat music from his parents' record player played in the background, the kind they always listened to on quiet simple nights like these.
His parents were nestled together on the couch, snuggled up close, sharing a rare moment of peace. The soft glow of the fireplace lit their faces, and the occasional clink of glasses being placed on the table marked the sound of them enjoying another sip of the astrovin. Jimzy hesitated for a moment, not wanting to break the peace, but the sight of that red star lingered in his mind.
"Hey, Mommy," he said quietly, "what does a red star mean?"
Spara turned her head slowly, a gentle smile on her face as she met his eyes. "What was that, honey?"
"There’s a red star outside," Jimzy continued, "it’s growing, and it’s making everything red."
The change in her was immediate. The smile faded, replaced with a tightness in her jaw as she sat up straight, her body suddenly tense. She stood, adjusting her pajamas, trying to keep her voice calm. "I’m sure it’s nothing, sweetheart," she said, though her eyes betrayed the worry beginning to creep in. "But let’s go take a look."
Together, they strolled outside, the chilly night air a stark contrast to the warmth inside. They both looked up at the sky. And there it was—glowing red, massive, and unnatural. Spara froze, her face drained of color as the reality of it hit her. She opened her mouth to speak, but the words came out as a choked whisper.
"Korjin…" she muttered, barely audible, her voice trembling. Then, louder, with the kind of urgency Jimzy had never heard from her before: "KORJIN!"
In an instant, Korjin burst outside, his face painted red by the light of the star. His first expression was one of awe, but it quickly shifted into something far more serious, his eyes narrowing as his jaw tightened.
"Inside. Now." His tone left no room for argument. He grabbed Jimzy's wrist, the grip firm but protective, and pulled him through the front door. His eyes darted to Spara, the tension between them palpable. "Take Jimzy to the board. Do not come out until I tell you."
Jimzy's heart pounded in his chest, each thud growing louder as the peaceful evening shattered in an instant. The urgency in his parents' voices cut through the air like a blade, leaving him stiff and frozen. His knees felt weak, trembling beneath him, while his stomach churned with fear. He didn't know what was happening—was it just a meteor shower, or something worse? The uncertainty gnawed at him, leaving him helpless as the world around him descended into chaos.
Korjin moved swiftly, his usual calm replaced by a grim urgency. He slammed a button on the mounted intercom, his voice a sharp bark as he paged the neighboring house. "Armstin, get those defenses up and running now! Those ships should’ve been tracked—how the hell did they get through the skies without detection?" His fingers flew over the keypad, activating the alarms. The sudden, shrill cries of the alarms pierced the quiet night, their relentless wails amplifying the tension in the air.
Without wasting a moment, Korjin rushed to the fireplace. His hands reached up to the mantel, grabbing the old rifle that hung there—an heirloom from a time long past. He ripped open a nearby drawer, frantically rummaging through its contents, his fingers clumsily searching for a magazine. The intercom crackled to life again, this time filled with the panicked, muffled screams of Mr. Armstin next door. "The spires are down! I’ve been trying to get the fucking parts shipped for weeks! Just stay put—"
But there was no time for waiting. Korjin’s eyes darkened as the reality of the situation set in. He flipped the dinner table with a grunt, sending cutlery clattering to the floor and food spilling across the room. The chaos echoed the storm brewing outside, as he kicked the table several times until it braced against the door. His face set in grim determination, Korjin racked the chamber of his rifle and moved to the window. He took position, his eyes scanning the sky for the threat looming above, knowing this was only the beginning.
His mother’s urgency was palpable as she hurriedly pulled Jimzy up the narrow staircase, each step vibrating with the tremors of the chaos unfolding around them. Jimzy’s small hands, cold and clammy with fear, gripped her leg with a desperate tightness. His heart pounded violently in his chest, each beat a frantic drum echoing his rising dread. His head was buried against her side, his breath coming in short, shallow gasps as he sought solace in the warm, familiar presence of his mother amidst the encroaching terror.
They reached the top floor, and his mother’s movements were a blur of focused efficiency. She swiftly crossed the room to a heavy, ornately carved wooden box concealed beneath the bed. With nimble fingers, she unlocked the box, the faint click of the latch barely audible over the cacophony of alarmed shouts and distant explosions. Inside, a royal service pistol lay nestled against a bed of velvet lining, its sleek, polished surface gleaming with an ominous elegance. She grasped the weapon with a mixture of resolve and trembling anxiety, her knuckles white against the dark metal.
Turning her attention to a discreet panel in the wooden floorboards, she pushed against it with determined force. The panel was cleverly disguised, blending seamlessly with the room’s decor, but she knew exactly where to press. As she exerted pressure, the hidden trapdoor creaked open, revealing a secret compartment beneath. The space was tiny, cramped and shadowed, but it offered a temporary refuge from the impending danger for their son.
Outside, the night sky was shattered by a blinding streak of white light. The streak cut through the darkness with a relentless, searing intensity. It moved with terrifying speed, a harbinger of the destruction that followed in its wake. As the light collided with Mr. Armstin's home, it unleashed a cataclysmic explosion. The impact was accompanied by a thunderous roar that reverberated through the asteroid, causing the very ground to convulse violently.
Dust and debris erupted from the point of impact, billowing into the air in a swirling, choking cloud. Windows in nearby structures shattered with a sharp, crystalline explosion, sending shards of glass flying through the night, including the upstairs bedroom. The asteroid’s surface trembled and quaked, the once-stable ground now a chaotic mosaic of crumbling rock and smoldering fragments. 
Jimzy’s heart raced even faster, his fear manifesting in silent tears that streaked down his cheeks. The violent upheaval outside, combined with the terrifying roar of destruction, made his entire world feel like it was collapsing around him. He clung desperately to his mother, his small frame trembling uncontrollably as he tried to shut out the nightmarish spectacle unfolding just beyond the walls of their home.
Jimzy squinted, his wide eyes straining against the blinding chaos outside. He brushed off some shards of glass and dust off his clothes. As the dust began to settle and the clamor of explosions ebbed into a distant, eerie rumble, he forced himself to focus on the night sky. What he saw next froze him in place and tightened the knot of terror in his stomach.
High above, a massive alien ship loomed ominously, it's dark silhouette dominating the star-speckled void. The vessel was an immense, sinister structure, its hull adorned with jagged, menacing contours that glinted malevolently in the fleeting flashes of the dying fires below. The ship’s underbelly was illuminated by a harsh, cold light, casting long, eerie shadows across the asteroid’s surface.
Without warning, another blinding white missile was launched from the ship, streaking through the void with a terrifying speed. The missile cut through the darkness like a shooting star, trailing a brilliant, blinding contrail of searing light that seemed to warp the very fabric of space around it. It raced toward its target with an almost deliberate precision, its trajectory a grim reminder of the destruction it was poised to deliver.
As Jimzy’s gaze followed the missile’s path, his attention was drawn downward to a more immediate threat. Four speeder bikes descended from the alien ship, cutting through the atmosphere with an unsettling hum. Each bike was a sleek, menacing craft, its design sharp and angular, reflecting the harsh, artificial light of the ship. They moved in a coordinated formation, their approach deliberate and calculated. The riders, their forms shrouded in dark, foreboding silhouettes, were barely discernible against the backdrop of the ship's infernal glow.
The speeder bikes sliced through the air with an eerie, mechanical whine, their engines emitting a low, throbbing drone that resonated with an unsettling menace. Each rider seemed to be a phantom in the night, their features obscured by the shadows of their helmets and the dark visors that shielded their faces. The bikes’ headlights cast eerie beams of light that danced across the asteroid’s surface, highlighting the smoldering wreckage and the shattered remnants of the town.
Jimzy’s heart skipped a beat as he observed the slow, deliberate descent of these agents of chaos. The riders maneuvered their bikes with an almost predatory grace, their movements precise, methodical and routine.
As the speeder bikes drew closer, the full scope of their mission became chillingly apparent. The asteroid was now a battleground engulfed in flames and fear. Jimmy continued to tremble uncontrollably as he watched, his mind struggling to process the enormity of the invasion unfolding before him. The stark contrast between the overwhelming grandeur of the alien ship and the tiny, vulnerable world it sought to annihilate left him feeling small and powerless, a mere observer in the midst of a cosmic horror. 
This is just a nightmare right? Surely Jimzy would wake up at any moment.
Jimzy’s mother, her face etched with urgency, motioned for him to climb into the hidden trunk she had uncovered earlier. With swift, practiced movements, she helped him into the dark, confined space. Jimzy’s small frame barely fit, and the air inside felt stifling. As he settled into the trunk, his eyes met hers, reflecting his mounting fear.
She handed him her royal service pistol, its weight surprising against his small hands. Her expression was a mixture of stern resolve and tender affection. “Jimzy,” she said, her voice steady despite the chaos raging outside, “stay very quiet. Don’t make a sound, no matter what happens. Do not use this unless you have to. I love you more than anything, and I’ll come back for you as soon as I can.”
With a final, gentle kiss on his forehead, she closed the lid of the trunk over him. The darkness of the trunk enveloped him, and the muffled sounds of the outside world became more distant. The last thing Jimzy heard was the faint echo of her stride as she moved away, leaving him in the safety of the hidden compartment.
Jimzy huddled in the cramped, musty trunk, the confined space pressing in on him from all sides. The air inside was thick and stale, mingling with the faint, acrid scent of old wood. As he adjusted to the darkness, a narrow sliver of light seeped through the small gap between two floorboards, casting a thin beam into the trunk.
Through this fragile window, Jimzy could see the chaos unfolding below. The light revealed glimpses of the living room: overturned furniture, shattered glass, and the frantic movements of his parents. The distant, echoing sounds of the violence outside filtered through the gaps, mingling with the harsh cries and the relentless blare of alarms.
The sliver of light became a window to a world that was rapidly spiraling out of control. Jimzy held his breath as he watched multitudes of shadows darting across the room and heard the muffled sounds of gunfire, screams and explosions. The sight through the gap was distorted and fragmented, but it was enough to convey the desperation of the situation. Jimzy could do nothing but wait in fearful silence.
Downstairs, Korjin's hands were steady as he took aim, his fingers working with practiced precision. Each shot he fired was a burst of raw defiance against the encroaching terror. The sharp, staccato rhythm of gunfire punctuated the air, cutting through the tumultuous noise of the attack. In the eerie glow of the red star, Korjin's silhouette was a fierce beacon of resistance.
Despite the haze of smoke and debris, Korjin’s aim found it's mark. One of the invaders, a dark figure on a zooming speeder bike, was struck by a well-placed shot. The impact sent the figure sprawling, limbs twisting grotesquely as they fell off the bike, which then collided with a nearby wall. The bike erupted in a fiery explosion, flames casting erratic shadows that danced across the walls. The invader’s body lay crumpled and lifeless on the ground. The bike continued to burn a hot pink flame, its fire adding to the hellish mess of the night.
Korjin’s determined stance remained unbroken as he continued to fire, his every movement reflecting his resolve and the desperate need to protect his family. The night outside sounded like a schizophrenic orchestral piece, each moment a clash of fiery fury or silent fear in an unpredictable erratic rhythm.
The speeder bikes continued to weave through the colony with a menacing precision. The riders, nearly humanlike in their shape but starkly alien in their mercilessness, cut through the mayhem with expertise. Their bikes, a deep rusted magenta, left behind trails of fiery pink that illuminated the night with a hellish glow. The luminous streaks of their passing were like marks of destruction etched into the very fabric of the colony.
The aliens themselves were clad in thin, gray light armor that barely concealed their sinewy forms. The armor’s dull color contrasted sharply with the vibrant trails of their bikes. Each rider's face was a peculiar blend of light green or earthy orange, with small, writhing tentacles protruding from their upper lips and jowls that waved in the wind—features that added an unsettling, otherworldly quality to their already disturbing appearance.
Their orange visors glowed ominously, casting a harsh light that flickered as they sped past. The metal backpacks strapped to their bodies clinked with every movement, and their satchels, slung over one shoulder, were stuffed with ominous tools of war. The combination of their alien features and their equipment suggests they have been planning this for a time as they zipped through the colony, leaving trails of destruction in their wake.
Some of the riders displayed a chilling versatility in their methods of destruction. With a practiced ease, they used one hand to fire hand cannons into buildings, their shots tearing through walls and sending debris cascading into the streets. Each shot was a brutal testament to their intent, creating violent explosions that echoed through the night.
Others reached into their rugged satchels, pulling out crudely made grenades fashioned from rocks and other scavenged materials. These makeshift explosives were hurled with deadly accuracy, arcing through the air before crashing into any structure unfortunate enough to be within their sight. The grenades erupted on impact, spewing jagged shrapnel and fiery bursts that ignited whatever they touched. Buildings crumbled and ignited, sending plumes of smoke and fire into the sky.
Jimzy's father maintained his relentless marksmanship, watching as half of the shots he hit seemed to merely ricochet off the invaders' armor, leaving him increasingly frustrated and alarmed.
He turned his attention to the factory where he worked, now enveloped in a menacing yellow cloud of smoke. Sulfuric yellow toxic gas billowed outwards, swirling and suffocating everything it touched. Through the haze, Jimzy’s father saw Miss Jaley stumble out of the factory doors, her movements disoriented and desperate. She dropped to her knees on the gravel-strewn ground, her body convulsing as she struggled to breathe. The thick, poisonous cloud had taken its toll, rendering her a pitiable figure in the midst of the chaos.
Before she could find think to escape, a stream of laser fire cut through the air with deadly precision. The lasers struck her with ruthless accuracy, her body jerking violently as the energy beams pierced through her, leaving no chance for recovery. Her final moments were marked by the cold, indifferent glare of the invaders.
Their home shuddered violently as an explosion erupted outside, sending a shockwave of destruction through the air. The force of the blast hurled Korjin backward, his body flung through the air before crashing onto the ground. He landed hard on his back, disoriented and winded, the world spinning around him.
The explosion had obliterated the front wall of their home, leaving a gaping void where the door and porch once stood. Debris rained down, the once-sturdy wooden structure now reduced to splinters and shattered fragments scattered across the yard. The blast's force had torn through the house’s façade, leaving a jagged, open wound where the entrance used to be. Smoke and dust billowed from the ruined porch, mixing with the acrid stench of burning material.
Korjin lay there momentarily, stunned and struggling to regain his bearings, the ground beneath him vibrating with the aftershocks of the explosion.
Once the dust settled, a lone figure emerged with a menacing presence. Towering at an imposing seven feet, the invader moved with a deliberate and almost methodical grace as it marched through the rubble. Its hulking form was cloaked in a dark, battered exoskeleton that seemed to absorb the ambient light, the only reflection being the flames of his eye.
The beast's face was a mask of grim apathy, its yellow eyes glowing with a cold, calculating intensity beneath a jagged, heavy brow. It wielded a sleek, intimidating sidearm with a practiced ease, the weapon's polished chromatic surface reflecting the destruction around it.
With a slow, deliberate stride, the invader approached the fallen Korjin. The sheer weight of its footsteps sent tremors through the debris-strewn ground. The creature's gaze was fixed on Korjin, its expression a complex mix of grim satisfaction and cold purpose.
As it neared, the invader lifted its sidearm, the weapon aimed steadily at Korjin’s prone form. Its hand, large and clawed, gripped the gun with unwavering resolve. The beast's breathing was a deep, resonant growl, echoing through the wreckage as it prepared to deliver its final, calculated blow.
Korjin, struggling to regain his senses, could barely lift his head as the invader’s shadow loomed over him. Blood pouring out his nose and temple, he managed to take one last look at the ceiling, the same spot Jimzy was kept hidden. The harsh, focused light from the invader's weapon illuminated the determined set of its jaw and the steely resolve in its eyes, sealing Korjin's fate with a chilling finality.
The invader’s sidearm fired with a deafening crack, the sound cutting sharply through the tumultuous night. A searing beam of energy erupted from the weapon’s barrel, striking Korjin with unerring accuracy. The shot connected with a sickening thud, and the force of the impact sent Korjin’s head snapping backward.
The explosion of the energy beam tore through his skull with brutality, sending a gruesome spray of bone and tissue across the room. Fragments of Korjin’s skull erupted in a grotesque arc, scattering like shattered porcelain against the walls and floor. The sickly, metallic scent of blood mingled with the acrid smoke, created a nauseating stench that filled the room.
“Jimzy, I love you more than anything ever,” his mother’s voice trembled as she spoke, her words heavy with a profound urgency and a depth of emotion that seemed to fill the entire room.
“Momma, I—” Jimzy began to respond, but his attempt was abruptly cut short.
The abruptness was caused by a thunderous pounding of heavy boots ascending the stairs. His mother’s gunfire erupted in sharp, deafening bursts, the cracks echoing through the confined space. Each shot was a desperate plea for survival against the encroaching nightmare.
The nightmarish alien figure, colossal and grotesque, surged through the doorway, its form monstrous and alien in every sense. It was immediately met with a relentless hail of bullets from his mother’s rifle. The harsh reality of combat painted the walls with vivid splashes of purple blood, a sickening canvas that bore witness to the violence. The first invader crumpled grotesquely to the floor, its body collapsing in an unholy contortion.
But the terror did not end with the fall of the first home invading assailant. A second alien, driven by a relentless resolve, burst into the room. Its weapon blazed in a furious retaliation. His mother flinched under the onslaught, the sheer force of the alien’s attack driving her back. His mother crumpled lifeless to the floor, right on top of the hidden floorboard.
The alien, now severely injured, clutched at its bleeding neck with a desperate, anguished grip. It cast a fleeting, assessing glance around the room before it began to retreat. The once-sacred floorboards, which had served as a feeble shield for Jimzy, were now obscured by the spreading crimson pool.
Jimzy’s young eyes, wide with horror, were unable to bear the ghastly sight. He shut them tightly, hoping against hope to block out the nightmare that had engulfed him. With trembling hands, he clenched his t-shirt, biting down hard to suppress his cries. The relentless, distant sounds of gunfire outside pierced through the night, each burst of violence a sharp reminder of the world that had crumbled around him, a friendly face that was no more.
As time dragged on, the sounds of destruction outside became a constant, haunting presence. Jimzy could feel the tremors of each explosion deep within his tiny chest, his heart pounding in sync with the rhythm of the chaos. The alien ship’s malevolent hum, a reminder of the danger lurking just beyond the walls, seemed to reverberate through every corner of the house.
Finally, after what felt like an eternity, the distant roar of the alien ship began to fade. The once-overpowering sound of its engines dwindled to a distant, eerie silence. The oppressive weight of fear and dread seemed to lift, leaving a void that was almost as suffocating as the chaos had been.
Jimzy’s fingers shook uncontrollably as he held the sleek, cold pistol his mother had given him. Its weight felt unnervingly real, a solid mass that seemed to press down on him with every passing second. He raised the gun, its barrel pointing directly at himself. The metal was chilling against his skin, and the reality of what he was holding was stark and unforgiving.
He stared down the barrel, his breath coming in ragged gasps. For a brief, horrifying moment, he contemplated the unthinkable. The gun seemed to grow heavier, its coldness seeping into his bones. The thought of pulling the trigger was there, sharp and clear, slicing through the static in his mind. His heart pounded in his chest, and the weight of the gun felt as though it was dragging him into the abyss.
The magnitude of what he was considering hit him like a punch to the gut. Jimzy’s hands were trembling violently, barely able to hold the gun steady. He could almost hear the loud, echoing sound of the trigger being pulled, but he quickly turned the pistol away from himself. The weapon fell with a dull thud against the floor, a sound that seemed to cut through the oppressive silence of his hiding place. Jimzy slumped against the wall, his body shaking as the realization of what he had almost done washed over him.
Jimzy’s terror had reached its peak, his small body overwhelmed by the emotional and physical strain of the ordeal. His mind, battered by the trauma and the relentless noise, could no longer hold on. As the last echoes of violence faded into a haunting stillness, Jimzy succumbed to the overwhelming fear that had gripped him for so long. Exhausted and emotionally drained, he lost consciousness in his cramped hiding place, the darkness closing in around him like a final, merciful embrace.
An eternity appeared to stretch before the orphaned boy, his sense of time distorted by the violence that surrounded him. He lay in the confined space, every muscle aching from hours of tension and cramped position. The box felt like a prison, its claustrophobic walls pressing in on him, amplifying his discomfort. He was afraid to breathe too loudly, each inhalation and exhalation measured and cautious, as if any sound might shatter the fragile barrier between him and the chaos outside. His body was a rigid mass of soreness and fatigue, while his mind was a turbulent void, a storm of raw fear and anguish swirling amidst a relentless, suffocating static.
Hours, or maybe even days later, a familiar sound would break the silence, a small expulsion of air, signaling the back hatch was open to a ship. It was followed by muffled chatter, nothing more than a few words and long pauses. Eventually the soft murmur would get closer, and closer. Jimzy could hear footsteps crunching on the gravel.
Amidst the muffled chatter, Jimzy could barely make out snippets of conversation. “This one’s… Korjin Avaris, KIA,” one voice said, its tone heavy with resignation.
“Fuck, man,” another voice responded, tinged with pessimistic frustration.
One of the soldiers sighed deeply, his voice laden with fatigue. “You two check upstairs, see if that boy and his mom are alive. I’ll be at the ship, I just need a minute.”
Each creak of the steps grew louder, closer, and more ominous, inching closer to Jimzy.
"Gods... those raiders even killed this woman in her kids room. I don’t even want to see this; it’s just fucking terrible," one soldier murmured, his voice heavy with despair.
“I know, man,” came the somber reply. After a brief pause, the second soldier's voice carried a note of disbelief. “Wait, you said kid’s room?”
“Seems to be, with all the stuffed toys and books,” the first soldier confirmed.
“Any positive ID on the child?”
“It’s the last house, Sarge. There’s probably not even a body left after those ion bombs,” came the weary response.
The second soldier sighed deeply, his voice tinged with resignation. “That’s true. Let’s just ID the woman and try to find out who’s behind this.”
As the soldiers approached the body of Jimzy’s mother, one of them reluctantly checked her pockets, searching for any clues. His eyes caught the displaced floorboard beneath her, and with a sense of grim curiosity, he lifted it up. To his astonishment, he found Jimzy huddled beneath, the boy’s face streaked with dirt and blood, his wide, terrified eyes reflecting a world of trauma and loss, inhaling deep panicked breaths.
“Poor kid,” the soldier whispered softly to himself, his heart aching for the boy. He reached out a hand in a gesture of fragile comfort to a child whose world had been irrevocably shattered. He looked at the boy, whose bright blue eyes seemed to hold the weight of unimaginable horror, and were so deeply scarred.
Chapter Two.
Nearly twenty years later, those same eyes, once wide with fear, are now confident, determined and capable. The nickname of Jimzy, a reminder of a different time, had faded away, replaced by the more authoritative moniker engraved on his dog tags: Staff Sergeant Jimzon. Clad in a Guard uniform, meticulously preserved yet marred by battle scars — dents and markings etched into the metal, testaments to his experiences and painful victories.
In the dimly lit flickering room, six soldiers stood behind him, forming a precise 2x3 formation. Each of them carried imposing long black rifles, a pistol holstered on their thigh, and a compact shotgun slung across their back. The room quivered gently as the countdown echoed in their ears: "2 minutes until assault," a voice announced through their earpieces.
"Any last-minute questions, lads?" Jimzon inquired, his voice deep and steady. Silence met his query. "Good," he affirmed.
He pulled his night vision goggles over his face, his soldiers following suit in synchronicity. With a decisive motion, Jimzon tapped his gauntlet, and plunged the room into utter darkness, a darkness broken only by small green lights flickering to life, signifying activated night vision goggles. The ground beneath them rumbled, the vibrations growing more pronounced. Abruptly, the tremors ceased, and a latch creaked open, revealing a rocky field shrouded in the cloak of night — their transport had arrived.
Jimzon signaled with a swift hand gesture to move forward to leave their transit, his focus unwavering. Moving with precision, he advanced through a field towards a small village in the near distance, his keen eyes scanning the surroundings. He stacked up at a doorway, waiting patiently as his fellow soldiers mirrored his movements across the street. A nod passed between him and the soldier beside him, who smoothly unholstered his suppressed pistol, anticipation hanging heavy in the air.
The door thuds open under Jimzons's forceful kick, and he swiftly clears the compact kitchen before methodically sweeping through the adjacent living room. Two of his men move in silence, shadowing his every step, checking each corner. Jimzon drops to a knee, his keen eyes fixed out the window, fingers tense around the grip of his suppressed weapon. With a subtle gesture, he signals his men to hold, his fist suspended in the air.
Outside, beneath the veil of night, two armed figures patrol the desolate street. Their demeanor is one of weary boredom, small puffs of smoke briefly illuminating their silhouettes under the dim streetlights. The suppressed shot rings out, breaking the silence, and one silhouette crumples to the ground, dead. The second figure follows, its descent almost instant. Jimzon rises, his movements fluid, and directs his team forward with another precise hand signal.
Jimzon's team advances across the street, each soldier positioning themselves in front of their designated neighboring houses. Jimzon, undeterred by the tension in the air, unclips a grenade from his belt and hurls it through an open window. He waits, muscles coiled with anticipation, until a muted flash illuminates the interior. In a swift motion, he kicks open a door and fires one shot at an alien figure with his sidearm, dropping him. Almost in harmony, the neighboring houses echo with the muffled detonations of silenced flashbangs, followed by the ominous thud of a body hitting the floor.
However, the deceptive tranquility is shattered by a deafening explosion that shakes the very foundation of the village. Jimzon's head whips around as he instinctively darts to the window. He hastily removes his goggles, his eyes adjusting to the sudden brightness. The night sky is marred by the crimson streaks of red flares being fired, casting an eerie glow across the encampment. Something has gone wrong, and he was going to fix it.
Aware of the urgency, Jimzy charges out of the house, his radio crackling to life as he commands his soldiers to regroup at their entry point. He urges them to establish a perimeter, each soldier tasked with guarding a vital entry point. But, there was no copy from B Team. The village thunders with high pitched foreign gunfire, an all too familiar melody.
Jimzon's mind began to race, formulating a plan on the fly as he navigates this unforeseen chaos. Every decision weighs heavily on him as he strives to regain control and turn the tide in their favor. He returned to the first house he cleared, awaiting his men.
Jimzon's squad was tasked with clearing any and all houses in order to plant breaching devices at the compound they would be advancing to upon the hill to capture their HVT. Trusting his intuition, he instructed two of his capable squadmates to guard the house, given its limited entryways. Determined and driven, he unholstered his sidearm, sprinted across the street, propelled by adrenaline and urgency. The piercing sound of gunfire pursued him, bullets tearing into the damp soil at his feet, splattering his legs with mud.
Jimzon forcefully breached a door with his shoulder, dropping an unarmed hostile threat without a moment's hesitation with his pistol. His victory was short-lived as another adversary struck a blow to his face with a metal pipe, causing him to fall to his knees. 
Blood obscured his vision as he tried to grasp for his pistol. In between his blinks, he managed to make out the alien assailant, a formidable opponent with an intimidating appearance—seven feet tall, grey slimy skin, eight white eyes encircling its head, carrying atrophied vestigial insect-like wings upon its back, and an atmospheric breathing mask—engaged him in a chokehold.
Drawing upon his training and combat experience, Jimzon was relieved he had practiced this very maneuver. Swiftly, he dropped his arms, activated his forearm gauntlet, materializing a dense blue plasma knife—a lethal weapon that could only be sustained for a few moments. With precision, he stabbed the Vitula in the thigh, the blade gliding through its thigh's flesh effortlessly. 
This beast of a creature belonged to a species renowned for their programmable hive-like mind and remarkable pain tolerance. However, their Achilles' heel was singular and common: the heart.
Jimzon deftly maneuvered the plasma blade, dragging it up the Vitula's thigh until it met bone, nearly severing it. The alien stumbled, desperately trying and failing to maintain balance. In that fleeting moment, Jimzon seized the opportunity. He slipped out of the hold, delivering a powerful kick to the alien's knee, causing it to collapse. With a swift left hook to the head, he disoriented the creature before delivering the final blow—a decisive plunge of the dagger into its chest, ending the confrontation.
He rose to his feet, pausing briefly to catch his breath, and removed his helmet. Despite the brevity of the encounter, he understood that fifteen seconds could alter the course of their mission. He attempted to radio once more, but the transmission received no response. Observing a tattoo on the fallen enemy's neck, indicating a high rank, Jimzon realized they were nearing their target.
Drawing a curtain aside, he surveyed the scene outside through a window towards the compound. He wiped away the mix of his own blood and the creatures, his adrenaline allowing him to ignore the pain that shot through his broken nose. A dozen enemy soldiers were in full retreat, doors left swung open in their haste to escape. Jimzon fired a blue flare into the night sky from the underbarrel of his rifle—a signal that the area was secured. He radioed Team A to regroup on him, and they swiftly joined him. Another blue flare burst a few buildings away, signaling Team B’s area was clear as well. Despite the relief that both teams had survived, an uneasy feeling gnawed at Jimzon. Team B hadn’t regrouped yet, and something felt off.
Leading his men, he ordered them to follow him into the worn tavern from where Team B's blue flare had originated. He called out, “Blue, Blue,” and heard the response, “White, White.” Upon entering, he found the four soldiers huddled standing in a room, one of them sitting upright against a wall.
“Fucks going on? Are you alright?” Jimzon inquired, his tone grave.
The soldier on the ground, his mask off, grimaced in pain and fury. 
He glared at a soldier before angrily tirading "Private Dumbass over here threw a fucking frag instead of a flash into the houses we were clearing, blowing every fuckin' gas tank in their house and not a shitsecond later bullets rip through the door I was breaching shredding my leg." He applied more pressure to the side of his thigh before grunting in pain. "Look I'll be fine, but I just cannot keep up with you. I frosted the fucking Crag that shot me, so its clear here, but still theres like six or seven hostiles holed up in the compound.”
Jimzon looked at his comrade's wounds, the dents and holes in his armor and let out a frustrated sigh. "Look. I don't wanna leave you behind. Jax, stick with the corporal, provide long-range fire if necessary. Berji, Yakib, Pavlock, you're advancing with me. Check your gear and let's move,” Jimzon commanded, determination etched on his face. "Jax, if you see anything, and I mean anything. You radio me. Understand? Corporal, I'm glad you're okay. We won't be away long. Let's move"
Jimzon and his team moved with calculated precision through the darkness, their shadows barely discernible against the blackened landscape. They ascended the rugged hill parallel to the main road, the gravel crunching softly beneath their boots. The only sound was the crackle of dwindling fire behind them and the rhythmic thudding inside their hearts.
As they drew closer to the compound, the landscape shifted dramatically from the rustic chaos of worn wooden shacks and rugged terrain to a stark, metallic expanse. Steel fences and shining containers lined the perimeter, their surfaces gleaming under the harsh glare of blinding white lights. The hangar loomed ahead, an imposing monolith that exuded an air of intimidation. Its walls, constructed from thick, corrugated metal, absorbed and refracted the ambient light, casting erratic patterns across the compound. Standing four stories high and stretching fifty yards wide, the hangar dominated the scene.
Attached to the hangar were two narrower buildings, concealing staircases and internal structures within. Each window was a grimace of iron and shadow, demonstrating the fortress-like nature of the compound while offering no view of the interiors.
The exterior was a study in cold, implacable steel. Entry points were nonexistent or heavily fortified, with reinforced doors barred and walls bristling with barbed wire s and motion sensors. Their lenses glowed faintly, casting an unsettling light that suggested a constant, unyielding vigilance. The entire compound resembled a labyrinth of metal and darkness, designed to repel any intruders with its forbidding and impregnable visage.
Draped across the silver walls were long, yellow flag-like banners emblazoned with a black ensign. Jimzon recognized the emblem as representing the cartel, and all of the filth they preached. 
The four men huddled behind a rusted shipping container, its pitted metal offering scant cover as they debated their next move. Yakib, his voice tinged with frustration, broke the silence. "This building was supposed to be light work—an old, half-knocked down repurposed temple right? When the fuck did these crags turn it into a fortress? How the hell are we getting in?" His pragmatism was evident, mirroring the grim reality of their situation.
Jimzon, ever the calm center, responded with a steady tone, "look our intel might've been outdated, but so what? We're here now, they're on the retreat, there's gotta be a way in, I just gotta think for a second."
Berji, his gaze locked on the fortress’s impregnable façade, eyes darting around the building, added thoughtfully, "Guys, If they’ve set up this new base right, where are the defenses? I haven’t seen any turrets, traps, or even shields. Honestly, we should've been torn up by now. It’s gotta all be a front right?". 
	Jimzon raised an eyebrow "You think so?" he asked his squadmates. "Think we should just push in?"
	Berji nodded. "Look you can never be too safe, but it's not like we're any safer out here. I've coded for plasma turrets plenty of times. They would've found us by now. In fact, anything would've,"
	Jimzon gave a nod to Berji. "Alright man, I trust you. I'm in. Pav, Yake, you guys in?" 
"I mean… I’ve still got two grenades left," Pavlock chimed in, a glint of pyromania sparking in his eyes, grinning under his helmet.
"Or or…what if we wait it out? It's not like they can escape that place" Yakib suggested, trying to find a more logical and patient approach.
Jimzon: "Look I hear you man, but our corporal is wounded, we can't just wait, and I am certainly not gonna be the one to tell him that," he teased, reflecting their corporal's fiery personality. "Are you in?"
Yakib exhaled deeply, stretching his arms, preparing himself mentally and physically for what was coming. After a moment, he nodded, determination building. "I'm in."	
"Alright fellas, on me," Jimzon said, his voice steady and commanding as he signaled his squad to move out. 
Jimzon thrust his rifle up, swiftly checking his corners before jogging toward the base of the building, his squadmates moving in perfect unison behind him. With a quick motion, he let the rifle drop to his side, his sling catching it securely as he pulled out a sleek thin pistol. A bright orange beam shot from its muzzle, the glowing lavalike wire slicing through the air toward the roof. The grappling hook’s claws latched onto the ledge, melting slightly to weld into place for a secure connection. Without hesitation, Jimzon tugged the wire, testing its hold, then signaled to his team with a sharp nod to follow suit.
Standing on the roof, Jimzon scanned his surroundings, his boots clinking softly against the metal surface. As expected, all entry points were sealed shut—reinforced hatches bolted from the inside, no visible weaknesses. The flat expanse of the roof offered little cover, save for a few ventilation shafts and service panels that, like everything else, were securely locked down. He let out a quiet sigh through his nose, knowing this wouldn't be as simple as they hoped. His squad spread out, scanning for any overlooked access points, but the building's defenses were as meticulous as they were daunting.
"Our breach charges can’t penetrate those walls. We only have low tier equipment for a stealth op before they trapped themselves inside," Berji pointed out, voicing their collective dilemma.
"I have an idea. Like Pavlock said, we still have our frags" Jimzon answered.
Before they could act, Yakib gestured towards a darkened metal cluster on the ground. "Hey, wait a second, check these out. They did have defenses. It's a plasma turret just sitting here. It's connected to a battery, the power supply’s good, but… it’s just deactivated."
The squad stared down at the turret, as if it were a wounded animal, that in between ragged breaths, could lunge and pounce if they got too close.
Pavlock, ever observant, scanned the roof’s perimeter and pointed to the opposite corner. "There’s even another one over here," he said, his voice quiet but tense. "Why’d they turn them off?"
Jimzon felt the hairs on the back of his neck stand on end as a wave of unease spread through him, his instincts screaming that something was wrong. The silence of the base felt deliberate, like the calm before a storm. Bottling up the prickling feeling that urged him to stay cautious, Jimzon nudged the turret with his boot, giving it a small, almost hesitant shove. The heavy metal unit teetered on the edge for a second before toppling over, crashing onto the ground far below with a resounding clatter that echoed through the night. Bits of metal splintered off the wreckage, several stories down.
But Jimzon’s gaze lingered. Something wasn’t right. His instincts sharpened, and his eyes darted around the rooftop. Then he saw it: a small, almost inconspicuous camera mounted near the roof’s edge, its lens pointed right at them. The faint red light on its side blinked steadily, casting a dull infrared glow in the shadows.
"We’ve been watched this whole time," Jimzon muttered, his voice low but clear, the tension now thick in his throat. His mind raced, struggling to piece together the unsettling quiet, the disabled defenses, and the camera’s presence. 
Jimzon trotted over to the camera, seeing his blurry reflection on the black lens. He figured if they were all watching from inside anyway, he might as well say something.
Jimzon sighed, then stepped forward directly in front of the camera. "Uhh..hello!" He paused, trying to find the words. "This is The Guard" his tone faltering as he struggled to maintain authority. "You're wanted for… countless crimes, and now you're trapped so… surrender now, let the justice system do its job instead of us. We do accept surrenders. You have… twenty minutes to open every door and surrender peacefully," he announced a bit passively, his voice lacking the confidence of a seasoned speaker. He stood there, feeling the weight of his team's eyes on him, knowing it wasn't his strongest moment.	
Without hesitation, Pavlock fired a shot, sending a burst of sparks from the camera and rendering it useless.	
"Thanks man," Jimzon said with a sigh of relief. His shoulders relaxed slightly, and he shot Pavlock a grateful nod. He hated public speaking, and the awkward moment had only added to his stress. Whatever, at least he gave them a chance to give up peacefully. "Alright brothers, crank your frags to 10, copy as I do".	
Jimzon equipped a grenade, adjusting a dial to the red setting. He carefully positioned it in the center of the rooftop and retrieved a small, rolled-up wool-like fabric from his pack. Unraveling the fabric that was their breaching charge, he revealed a sticky, clay-like substance underneath by peeling off the velcro edges. He pressed the gluey clay over the grenade, forming a makeshift breaching charge.	
Next, he pulled out a small, six-inch antenna embedded in the center of the fabric. He watched intently as the light on the antenna flickered and then turned green, signaling that the charge was armed and ready.
The Castle Team found themselves waiting with anticipation.

A monotone, repetitive beep echoed from Yakib’s comm, barely drawing his attention. He glanced at it with a bored expression, then shrugged. “Well, that’s that then. Twenty minutes. They’ve made their choice.”
Without a word, the team moved to the edges of the rooftop with ease, not a hint of tension in their movements. This wasn’t their first standoff, and it wouldn’t be their last.
Jimzon, sounding almost casual, spoke up. “Switch your sights to thermals," he said, the command rolling off his tongue without emotion. "Time to clean house.”
The roof erupted with a deafening roar as four perfectly synchronized explosions tore through the air, sending shockwaves rippling through the squad's bones. The ground beneath their boots trembled, as though the fortress itself recoiled from the breach. Shards of gravel and jagged debris ricocheted off their armored suits, rattling against their helmets like hail in a storm. Thick, acrid smoke surged upwards in a violent plume, swallowing the sky and shrouding the battlefield in chaos.
From below, wild bursts of loud gunfire and streaks of energy lashed out through the breach, blazing like angry beams of light slicing through the smoke. The cartel inside fired frantically, disoriented and panicked, their shots blind and erratic as the thick veil obscured any chance of hitting a target. Each shot was a desperate attempt to push back an enemy they couldn’t see, but the more they fired into the choking haze, the more hopeless they became.
Within moments, the frantic hail of gunfire from inside began to sputter and fade, the cartel fighters hesitating as they waited for a clear line of sight through the swirling smoke. But in their pause, they unknowingly signed their own death warrants—without their suppressive fire to hold back the inevitable, their fate was sealed in that brief, fatal lull.
Jimzon’s men picked off targets from above with surgical efficiency, clearing the compound as if they were shooting fish in a barrel. Through the haze of smoke, the thermal sights revealed glowing white figures scrambling beneath them, exposing their every movement. Jimzon tracked one—a panicked crag ducking behind soft cover, its head darting frantically back and forth. Two quick shots pierced the air, and the figure crumpled, splattering a hot, bright canvas of blood across the wall. It was almost routine, an instinct honed through years of combat. He felt nothing, nothing but the recoil.
Jimzon's sights then locked onto a large, silhouetted white blob, arms raised in surrender, each of its four hands empty and trembling. With a flick, he switched off his thermals to get a clearer view. There stood their HVT, the crime lord they had been hunting. The figure was grotesque in the dim light—fat and gray-skinned, his body bulging awkwardly on multiple leg-like appendages that gave him an eerie, spiderlike stance. His lower jaw jutted forward with two massive golden tusks, a gaudy display of wealth and power. Above his squinting, fearful eyes, tufts of coarse black hair formed an uneven unibrow, an odd contrast to the dated, brown formal suit stretched over his bloated frame. The suit itself—centuries out of fashion—was a relic of his materialism, another of his trophies paid for with blood.	
Jimzon’s lips twisted into a cocky, almost predatory grin as he called out, his voice echoing down into the chaos below.
“Mister Nardow! Your men are all dead. Would you prefer to join them, or shall we take you into custody?" His voice dripped with smug satisfaction. "Surrender now, and this’ll all be over in seconds. It's our call." He cocked his rifle for theatrics, enjoying the expression of added horror to the pathetic creature's petrified face. 
He watched with cold amusement as terror spread across Nardow’s face—his wide, panicked eyes locking onto the weapon, golden tusks quivering beneath his trembling lip. The crime boss—this bloated, vile creature responsible for countless deaths—was now shaking uncontrollably, as if caught in the throes of hypothermia. His multiple legs buckled slightly beneath his weight, the whole grotesque mass of him trembling like jelly.
Jimzon took the moment to reflect, about the irony of a man capable of such incredible violence, could be so feeble and pathetic. It made Jimzon feel powerful. 	
Nardow's voice, thick with a blend of desperation and slimy regret, pierced through the charged silence. His deep, gravelly tone trembled with raw fear. “Take me, but spare my son’s life,” he pleaded, his words heavy with an anguished tremor. “I beg you, show mercy. He’s all I have left. Please, don’t let him suffer for my sins.” His eyes, wide with terror and glistening with unshed tears, darted nervously between Jimzon and the chaos around him.
Pavlock swiftly shot his grappling hook downward, the line unspooling and securing firmly against the floor. As he descended, he landed lightly, keeping his rifle trained on Nardow. The rest of the team followed his lead, each taking a knee and maintaining their focus on the target while Pavlock held his position, ready for any sudden moves.
Jimzon’s hand moved with practiced precision as he withdrew the high-tech cuffs from his gear. “Give me your wrists,” he commanded, his tone unyielding.
Nardow hesitated, his eyes flickering with a mix of fear and defiance, but he complied, extending his arms. Jimzon clamped the magnetic bands around the crime boss’s wrists. The moment the cuffs engaged, a soft hum filled the air as they powered up, and the weight of the cuffs surged, pulling down with the force of hundreds of pounds. Nardow’s arms were instantly yanked downward, the sheer pressure forcing him to his knees with a grunt.
The cuffs were designed to immobilize even the most unruly of prisoners. Nardow’s muscles strained under the burden, the strain visibly shifting his posture as the cuffs exerted their immense force, pinning him with an iron grip that was both physical and psychological. Jimzon watched with an unperturbed gaze, the weight of the cuffs a clear statement of his authority.
