As Nardow stumbled forward, the weight of the cuffs dragging his shoulders down, it revealed his son from the shadows behind him, who was a stark contrast to the corpulent crime boss. The boy stood at barely five feet tall, a creature of unsettling alien features. His body resembled that of a crustacean, with a hard, exoskeletal shell that gleamed under the dim lights. The deep, wine red of his skin was a canvas of intricate tattoos, each one a blend of gang symbols and cultural emblems, etched in a vibrant, almost luminous ink.
His face was an unnerving blend of human and crustacean traits. A gaping maw, reminiscent of a crab’s, replaced what would have been a regular mouth, its jagged edges twitching slightly as he breathed. Above it, his large, iris-less green eyes were striking and unsettling, their glossy surface reflecting the faint ambient light. Strands of purple hair, thin and wiry like seaweed, cascaded down from his scalp, contrasting sharply with the harsh reds of his skin.
He wore baggy cargo pants that hung loosely from his hips, the fabric rustling with each hesitant step he took. A large gold chain hung heavily around his neck, the metal catching and refracting the light, making it seem even more out of place against his rough, alien appearance. The sons posture was a mix of defiance and confusion, the gold chain swinging slightly with each movement, accentuating his awkward, yet determined stride as he followed in his father’s shadow.
“You too show me your hands,” Yakib commanded the son with a chilling growl, his voice devoid of any trace of sympathy.
Nardow’s anguished shout cut through the tense silence. “You told me you wouldn’t take him!” His voice was raw, desperate, as he struggled against his restraints, each plea wringing from him with palpable distress.
Berji’s gaze was cold and unyielding. “We didn’t make any deal with you. It’s protocol,” he said flatly, advancing toward Nardow’s son with purposeful steps.
Nardow’s pleas grew more frantic, his voice breaking with each word. “We did have a deal you fools! You can’t do this!” His eyes, wide with fear and anger, darted between The Guard soldier and his son.
	Pavlock, unfazed by Nardow’s escalating desperation, shot back with a steely edge. “Yeah, yeah, keep talking, and I’ll gag you. Understood?” His voice was sharp, the promise of further punishment hanging in the air like a sword.
	Nardow, clinging to a thread of hope, turned back to the team. “Just wait! Please, please! He doesn’t understand the common tongue!” His voice trembled, a mix of frustration and panic.
	Berji, unfazed by the drama unfolding before him, continued to bark orders at the boy. He waved his rifle impatiently, signaling for the boy to get on the ground. “Get down!” he shouted, his voice echoing harshly in the confined space, the son hissed at him.
“Stop! Stop! Tell Director Liles to unhand me now! You’ve already taken everything!” Nardow’s voice was a tortured roar, his eyes pleading as he tried to appeal to some sense of mercy.
Jimzon, momentarily distracted by Nardow’s mention of his boss’s name, looked at the scene with a mix of confusion and irritation. Rather than pursuing the lead, he tapped a button on his gauntlet. A jolt of electricity surged through Nardow, causing him to convulse violently. His scream of agony was a visceral, ear-splitting sound, filling the room with a raw, desperate energy.
As Berji moved closer, the alien’s stance grew more defiant. He stomped his foot and widened his stance, his claws twitching in a futile gesture of resistance.
“There’s no choice. Stop being a pussy,” Berji taunted, his voice dripping with disdain.
The alien, infuriated and in a panic, lashed out with his claw-like fingers, slicing through the air toward Berji’s face. The strike cut deep, leaving a fresh trail of blood on Berji’s cheek. “You fucker,” Berji muttered through clenched teeth, wiping the blood away as he glared at the alien with icy resolve. Without hesitation, he slammed his gauntlet screen, activating a small teal energy shield, similar to Jimzons plasma knife. The shield’s hum was menacing, its light glowing with a dangerous intensity.
"You fool! Stop! Stop!" Nardow's voice was raw with desperation, his cries piercing through the chaos as he watched his son suffer. His frantic pleas were abruptly cut short when Jimzon, without hesitation, activated the gauntlet once more, sending a jolt of electricity surging through Nardow's body. The force of the shock was so intense that he convulsed violently before collapsing with a thud, unconscious and silent.
The son, left in the throes of fear, hissed with increasing intensity. His panic was palpable, and his attempts to respond to Berji's advances were fraught with erratic movements. His body shook with uncontrollable spasms, a frantic response to the blistering heat emanating from the shield, before lunging towards him.
Berji, unmoved by the boy's suffering, pressed the shield closer to the alien’s face with unyielding determination. The heat was excruciatingly intense, causing the boy’s burgundy skin to sizzle and bubble grotesquely. The shield's edge dug into the alien’s flesh, frying it into a dark, crisped texture. The boy’s attempts to kick and claw at Berji were weak and futile, his strength rapidly fading as the searing pain overwhelmed him.
Finally, the boy could no longer maintain his struggle. He slumped to the ground, his body steaming and the air thick with the stench of scorched flesh. Berji remained resolute, the shield still glowing with a menacing light as he stood over the boy’s smoldering remains. His shield flickered and deactivated.
The men stared at Berji, indifferent to the alien's death
Jimzon tapped his earpiece, his voice calm but authoritative. "HVT is secure, compound cleared. Requesting EVAC."
The corporal's voice crackled through the static. "Copy that. EVAC is about an hour out. Regroup at entry."
"Copy," Jimzon responded, a touch of relief in his tone. "You hanging in there?"
"I’ll be fine," came the reply, albeit strained. "Just grab some good shit for me."
"You got it. See you then."
Jimzon raised his flare gun and fired a brilliant blue flare that streaked through the roof, its light cutting through the lingering smoke and darkness. He turned back to his team, who had gathered around, their faces tired but satisfied.
"Well, lads, great fucking job. It wasn’t perfect, but I’m proud of all you guys. It’s a job well done. EVAC will be here to pick us up in about an hour, so… you know what that means," Jimzon declared.
"Loot time," they all said, perfectly not in unison, but with a shared enthusiasm.
Jimzon grinned, his eyes twinkling with approval. "Given the stakes of this mission, the Director said we can take just about anything we can carry. Any weapons or documents just gotta be cleared at check-in back to the mothership. And guys, please don’t take enough drugs to fail any drug test. Next one isn't for six months, but still. Just meet back at the landing craft in 30 minutes."
	Excitedly, the troops began searching the compound, ripping jewelry from the sons neck, and rifling through cabinets and drawers. Jimzon sat at Nardow's desk, sifting through a stack of papers that were blown over in the chaos. He saw plans of other crime syndicate locations, records of plasma rifles and explosives being sold, names of buyers—typical stuff. He picked up a funny-looking mug, thinking it could make a nice pot for the plant growing in his barracks, but he placed it down.
	Jimzon's eyes landed on a locked drawer in the desk. He sighed, reaching for his plasma knife. With a hiss and a burst of intense heat, the blade seared through the metal, cutting a precise, glowing hole in the drawer’s side. The acrid scent of molten metal filled the air as he pried the drawer open with a metallic screech.
Inside, nestled among the detritus of more mundane office supplies, was a small metal box, its surface scratched and worn but sturdy. He flipped up the latches with a practiced flick, and the lid swung open with a faint click.
The contents were documents—more of the same kind he'd been sorting through, but these were more detailed and organized. Jimzon skimmed through the papers, hoping to find any helpful intel,  flipping them with a sense of urgency. There were locations of minor stash houses, meticulously noted down, their addresses and security details laid out in a way that suggested a careful, if not obsessive, organization.
In between the papers were lists of names—small-time police officers, their names and ranks scrawled across the pages. Each officer was noted with a scribble indicating their connection to the cartel, as well as bribes and favors exchanged. The details were damning, painting a picture of corruption and influence that spanned far beyond the walls of this compound.
As Jimzon sifted through the documents, a particular folder caught his eye. The label read “Nhaths,” and the moment he flipped it open, a chill ran down his spine. Inside were details about the Nhaths, a mercenary gang notorious for their merciless efficiency and brutal tactics. His eyes scanned the pages, each word dragging him deeper into a dark, personal abyss.
The Nhaths were infamous for their lightning-fast raids and the staggering collateral damage they left in their wake. Their operations were characterized by a ruthless disregard for life, a trait that had left a trail of devastation across multiple sectors, including the asteroid where Jimzon’s family had lived.
Time seemed to warp as Jimzon's gaze locked onto the grainy, black-and-white photographs within the dossier. He tried to keep his composure, but his body grew hot, and his vision pulsed. Time seemed to freeze or speed up; Jimzon wasn’t sure. He saw the faces of the same men who killed his family. He shot up, staring at the photos of these men, the men he hadn't seen in decades yet had thought about every day. 
He was going to kill them. Report this file to the Director, and plan an invasion tomorrow, wherever they were. Death wouldn't be enough justice for these crags, it was too quick. No. He was going to make them suffer. His hands welled up into fists. He imagined beating these aliens so hard they could barely move. Breaking their jaws, their arms, legs, their spirits, if they even had one—he’d make them remember who he was. In his nightmares, they always won, but tomorrow they wouldn’t.
"Jimzon, you okay, buddy?" Pavlock's voice cut through the haze of Jimzon's turbulent thoughts.
Jimzon snapped back to reality, nodding his head, his throat tight. "I'm fine," he replied hoarsely, trying to mask the tremor in his voice. "Just keep looting."
He slumped back into the chair, the adrenaline that had fueled him now ebbing away, leaving him feeling weak and unsteady. His muscles, once rigid with purpose, now ached with the fatigue of the intense confrontation. He felt the pain of his broken nose scour through his head. 
Pavlock glanced at his wrist display and frowned. "Didn't you say we needed to return to the ship in 30 minutes?"
Jimzon's eyes, still glazed with a mix of anger and exhaustion, met Pavlock’s. "Yeah… Why?"
Pavlock's expression shifted from concern to urgency. "It's almost been an hour, boss."
Jimzon shook his head, his eyes blinking rapidly as if trying to dispel the haze clouding his thoughts. He cast a glance at his squad, each soldier loaded down with a variety of spoils: ornate weapons, gleaming trinkets, piles of valuable jewelry. Berji stood out, his arm practically encased in an armory of dozens of flashy watches, their polished surfaces catching what little light there was.
The mission had been executed successfully, but the victory felt hollow. For now.

Chapter 3.

The elevator doors slide open with a soft chime, revealing the grand expanse of the Mothership’s Dining Hall, a place that seemed almost like a world unto itself. Ornate chandeliers hung from the high ceilings, casting a warm, inviting glow over the space. While the rest of the Mothership adhered to its usual white and blue aesthetic, the Dining Hall was a welcome contrast, decorated in warm tones of tan, amber, and gold. The sleek white beams that supported the ceiling still glowed faintly with the ship’s internal lighting, but the walls and furnishings embraced a homier feel. The vast room stretched out before the Castle Team, filled with rows of long tables and comfortable booths, packed with Guardsmen of all ranks. Conversations rose and fell in a symphony of laughter, clinking glasses, and shared war stories. It was a place where duty temporarily faded into the background and camaraderie took center stage, where soldiers who had risked their lives together found comfort in shared meals and moments of peace.
The proudest and most elite members of the Guard were gathered here, their uniforms crisp, adorned with badges of honor, though many still bore the scuffs and wear of recent missions. Each face told a story—some etched with the hardened lines of veterans, others still carrying the wide-eyed enthusiasm of new recruits. But here, in the Dining Hall, ranks seemed to blur. Friendships transcended the rigid structures of command as they relaxed in the company of those who had faced the same dangers, braved the same hostile planets, and survived countless battles
Jimzon stepped into the room, feeling an immediate sense of relief wash over him. This was more than just a dining hall; it was one of the few places in the galaxy that felt almost like a home to him. No matter where his missions had taken him—through the cold emptiness of space, to the blistering heat of desert worlds, or the hostile urban jungles of enemy territory—he could always count on the familiarity of this room. It was a sanctuary from the chaos of war, a place where the weight of his rank and the burdens of battle eased just a little. The high ceilings and the open space made him feel free, while the buzz of activity grounded him, reminding him of the bond that connected all those who served.
As he walked further in, the aroma of freshly baked bread, warm and inviting, hit him like a wave. It was intertwined with the faint but unmistakable scents of spices—subtle hints of exotic flavors that had been brought aboard from distant worlds. The smells were comforting, pulling him deeper into the moment, like open arms welcoming him back from the front lines. His stomach rumbled in response, a reminder of how long it had been since he had eaten anything more than rations. But it wasn’t just the food that drew him here. It was the sense of belonging, of being surrounded by people who understood the life he led.
Berji, Yakib, Pavlock, Jimzon, and Jax took their first steps out of the elevator, their minds already drifting toward the feast awaiting them at the food stations. The mere thought of warm, hearty meals, the kind that could send them all into a blissful food coma after the adrenaline-fueled mission, made the transition from battlefield to dining hall feel almost surreal. The absence of their armor and helmets, which usually weighed them down, made their movements feel weightless, like they were floating across the walkways as they took their steps.
Their near-identical Guard uniforms and standard-issue operation masks, which they’d worn just hours ago, often stripped them of their individuality during missions. But here, their unique features began to surface again. Berji, the tallest of the group, with his short, tightly curled blond hair and piercing emerald eyes, moved with an excited, almost lanky grace. His height always made him stand out, but now, free from his armor, his broad-shouldered frame seemed even more imposing, though his carefree demeanor balanced that out.
Beside him was Jax, who couldn’t have looked more different. With his wavy raven-black hair and a thick, well-groomed mustache that seemed almost out of place on his otherwise youthful face, Jax exuded a certain charm. His mustache was an anomaly among the usually clean-shaven faces of the Guard, a deliberate rebellion against the uniformity that came with military life, and yet, it suited him perfectly.
Pavlock, always the life of the group, followed with a dark brown beard, meticulously trimmed to perfection, outlining his jawline and accentuating his bright, pristine teeth whenever he burst into laughter—which was often. His laugh echoed through the hall, infectious and warm, always lifting the mood of those around him, even after the most grueling of missions. The cleanliness of his beard was a stark contrast to the grit and chaos they had just left behind, a sign of his dedication to appearance, even in the heat of battle.
Yakib's evenly tanned skin was a testament to his love for the outdoors during rec time. His sandy brown hair, which was usually tucked under his helmet, now fell freely across his forehead, the kind that looked like it could catch the breeze on an open field. His relaxed expression contrasted with the focused intensity and pragmatic attitude he often displayed during missions, and the fatigue in his eyes seemed to ease as he caught the aromas drifting from the food stations.
As for Jimzon, he always considered himself to have rather unremarkable features. His short brown hair was as ordinary as it came, and his jawline, though sharp, was marred by a modest amount of stubble that he never quite managed to tame. He couldn’t bring himself to care much about his appearance most days, but what really bothered him—what he truly hated—were the permanent reminders of the injuries he’d accumulated during his years of service.
His nose, slightly crooked from a fistfight that had gotten out of hand years ago, was a daily reminder of the unpredictability of his life. The break had been clean but had left it bent, just enough for him to notice every time he looked in the mirror. That crooked nose carried the memory of a hot-blooded brawl, the kind that left more than physical marks. Even worse, there was the faint burn scar etched across his left cheek, a thin line where no hair would ever grow again. It had been a careless accident—too much to drink, a handful of fireworks, and a reckless sense of invincibility after graduating from the Guard Academy. The scar, though faint, was like a brand on his face, a permanent consequence of his youthful stupidity.
Though others hardly noticed these marks, to Jimzon, they were inescapable. Every time he caught his reflection, they whispered of battles fought and mistakes made, of close calls and reckless moments that had shaped him into the man he was. Where his comrades bore their scars with pride or indifference, Jimzon found his to be a quiet constant reminder of the toll his duty had taken—not just on his body, but on the man he used to be. Yet, despite this, he wore them because they were his, a part of the life he had chosen.
Each of them, stripped of their combat gear, seemed to breathe easier, their personalities shining through now that the battle masks were off. The camaraderie they shared was evident, not just in their casual banter but in the way they moved together, a tight-knit unit both in and out of the field. The weight of their responsibilities, their mission, and their trauma momentarily lifted, leaving only the bond of brotherhood between them as they made their way to a much-needed feast.
The men continued their lively banter, voices rising and falling as they excitedly exchanged stories of the loot they had collected from their mission. Trinkets, weapons, and oddities were tossed around in playful offers for trade, each man trying to outdo the other with their findings. Berji displayed a small glowing stone, its faint light reflecting in his emerald eyes, while Jax twirled a strange metallic charm in his fingers, already scheming how he could use it in a future mission, while Pavlock and Yakib debated the usefulness of a discovered rusted antique pistol. Their camaraderie was effortless, their bond built not only on shared danger but on these light-hearted moments of normalcy amid the chaos.
Eventually, they parted ways to grab their meals, leaving the excitement of trading behind for the more immediate pleasure of food. Jimzon, however, found himself waiting longer than the others as his meal took an unusually long time to prepare. By the time his tray was ready, the rest of the team was already seated, laughing and exchanging more stories. His tray was filled with grilled white meat, its surface dusted with colorful teal and red flakes, a garnish of yellow spireleaf sprouts carefully arranged on the side. The circular tan grains, soft and steaming, completed the meal, alongside a tall glass of opaque pastel yellow chai that shimmered faintly under the lights.
As Jimzon walked toward the table where his comrades sat, he felt a brief wave of irritation at having been delayed. The frustration clung to him like an unwanted shadow, but with each step closer to his friends, it began to melt away. The warmth of their laughter, the clinking of their utensils against plates, and the familiar sound of their voices in mid-conversation made the stress feel trivial for now. The table was lively, filled with the smells of food and the animated gestures of his comrades as they recounted their exploits. Jimzon's tardiness no longer mattered as he slipped effortlessly back into the rhythm of the evening, grateful to be among friends.
“I still can’t believe they let us haul that much gear,” Berji said, his voice still carrying a sense of wonder. “When I was digging through some crates, I found these processors—like, the kind even our parents probably haven’t seen. This stuff is pre-war tech, man, advanced for even that era. I don’t know whether to upgrade my systems first or just sell them off-world and retire early.” His excitement was palpable, a grin splitting his face as he described his discovery.
Jimzon listened, nodding, even though most of Berji's tech jargon often went over his head. Berji lived and breathed that kind of stuff, and Jimzon had long since learned to rely on his teammate for anything remotely related to technology. Berji’s passion for tinkering was legendary within the team. He wasn’t just good—he was a wizard with machinery. Once, Jimzon had come to him with what he thought was a simple glitch in his helmet’s UI, only for Berji to take one look at it and decide to completely rebuild the entire interface from scratch. It had taken hours, but when Berji handed it back, it was smoother, faster, and packed with more features than Jimzon knew what to do with.
"You? Retire early?" Jimzon laughed at that, the idea of Berji settling down on some quiet beach seemed absurd. "Yeah, right. Like you could stay away from this life for more than a week."
Berji shrugged, grinning, still thinking about the possibilities.
"Okay, but hear me out," Yakib said, leaning forward with a glint in his eye. "What if we don’t sell the tech off to just anyone? We could auction it to one of those scummy black-market syndicates. Get a bidding war going. And heist it right back. Hell, we could fund our own missions forever with that kind of payout."
"And have every gun for hire and private army from here to Creta on our asses? No thanks. I like being alive, Yake. And I’d like to stay that way." Jax chirped.
Pavlock laughed, his booming voice filling the table. "Yake, you’ve always got a foot in the grave with these ideas. You’ve got to get that mercenary mindset outta your system. We’re Guardsmen, not criminals."
Yakib smirked, clearly not deterred. "Criminal, mercenary… depends on the paycheck, my friend. Besides, you know as well as I do that the Guard isn’t exactly flush with credits these days. Some of those missions barely cover our gear repairs."
Jax leaned back with a cocky grin. “Fuck the paycheck, man. As long as we keep pulling in loot like this and I get that fat pension in a couple years, I don’t care if the Guard sinks more into debt. Check this out.” He kicked his feet up onto the table, revealing a pair of sleek, rose-gold boots with pointed tips that hugged his feet like they were custom-made.
Pavlock did a double-take, nearly choking on his drink. “Are those magboots? No way! Those things still exist?”
The rest of the squad turned to look, eyes wide. Yakib, ever the opportunist, immediately jumped in. “I'll trade you a portaboard right now, no questions asked.”
Jax laughed, waving off the offer. “You’re gonna need to do better than that, Yake.”
Pavlock, not one to be outdone, cut in the conversation with excitement. “Yake, I’ll give you all my loot from today for that portaboard you just mentioned.”
"“I mean I will absolutely trade you, but Pav why the hell would you make an offer that good for one?” Yakib retorted, prepared to double his mission earnings.
Pavlock leaned in, almost reverently. “Do you have any idea how cool portaboards are?
“I mean they float so I guess so?”
	"They’re one of the last mass-produced Levitation Matrix techs. No one knows how to make them anymore. If I can get my hands on one, take it apart, reverse-engineer the thing, we could have access to all kinds of anti-grav tech.”
Yakib shook his head, amused. “It’s your call, man. Deal. But seriously, what kind of loot did you even grab?”
	Pavlock shrugged. “Bunch of jewelry, a broken pistol, and… four hundred grenades.”
Yakib burst out laughing. “Four hundred grenades? Pav, you’re insane.” He then turned back to Jax. “Alright, all his loot for your magboots. Fair trade?”
Jax chuckled but then grew more thoughtful. “Hold up—are those the same kind your dad used to have?”
Yakib nodded, a wistful look in his eyes. “Yeah. We used to share a pair. Back home, there’s this massive wrecked ship—crashed centuries ago, probably Maldo era. The thing’s the size of a city, but these trees and plants have taken over, so it’s kinda like climbing a mountain now. Those magboots were the only way to get up the steep parts.”
Berji, who’d been listening quietly, asked, “What happened to them?”
Yakib’s expression darkened slightly. “Fried in a lightning storm. Lost ‘em.”
“Damn, man,” Berji muttered.
Jax sighed, tapping the table in thought. Finally, he nodded. “Alright, deal. But you have to let me borrow them sometimes.”
Jimzon didn’t join the banter; he let the conversation swirl happily around him as his thoughts drifted back to their mission. It had been a success, sure, but there had been moments—flashes of danger and chaos—where things could’ve gone sideways in an instant. They were lucky to be sitting here now, laughing over a meal, instead of counting their dead.
He picked at the food on his tray, before placing it back down, his appetite fading as memories of his childhood played over in his mind. The screams, the sound of blaster fire, the acrid smell of burning metal. It never fully went away, not even in the quiet moments. Especially not then.
"Jimz, you okay?" Berji’s voice cut through his thoughts, pulling him back to the present.
"Yeah," Jimzon said quickly, sitting up straighter. "Yeah, Just thinking."
"About what?" Jax asked, ever the curious one. "Don’t tell me you’re brooding again, man. This is a victory dinner. You’re supposed to be celebrating, not moping."
Jimzon forced a smile. "I’m not moping. Just... remembering. We got lucky out there, that’s all."
The table fell quiet for a moment. They all knew what he meant. Every mission had its risks, but this one had been particularly close with the casualty of their corporal. 
“Speaking of which, how’s Corporal Barris doing anyway?” Jimzon asked, looking over at Jax. He hadn’t seen him since the chaotic evac.
Jax leaned back in his chair with a smirk. “Oh, he’s chillin’. Well, not exactly chillin’—his ankle’s in about a million pieces—but honestly, I think he’s kinda relieved. Guy was getting real tired of being deployed every other week. Probably enjoying his little vacation in the ICU more than he’s letting on.”
“You sure this is about the corporal, Jimzon? Not maybe… Kelsa?” Yakib teased, eyes narrowing in Jimzon’s direction with a knowing smirk.
Jimzon’s eyebrow raised at the mention of her name. He shot Yakib a look, caught off guard, but tried not to show it.
Pavlock jumped in before Jimzon could respond. “Look, man, you're gonna bounce back. I know it’s only been a week, but don’t be so hard on yourself. And until you do, you got us. The boys always got your back.”
“Hell yeah," Berji chimed in, nodding enthusiastically. "Maybe we need a guy’s trip. Let’s hit a club when we land, get trashed, or hell, let’s visit Zonar and go all out. Forget everything for a bit.”
Jimzon forced a smile, though it felt heavy on his face. For brief moments today, he had managed to forget about Kelsa and the ache her absence left behind. But now that her name was out there, that hollow feeling returned, gnawing at him. Still, he pushed it down, deeper, like he always did. No use letting it show.
"That sounds awesome, guys," he said, his voice a little too bright, too forced. "After today, maybe we all need it."
“Jimzon, you loot anything interesting?” Pavlock asked, leaning forward with curiosity. “I saw you checking out some papers earlier. You find any stashes we could hit?”
Jimzon shrugged, trying to dismiss the question with a casual air. “Just paper trails and loose ends. Nothing much yet. I’ll update you guys if anything useful comes out of it. Speaking of which who the fuck is gonna lead Castle Team now?” Jimzon inquired.
The team paused for a moment, contemplating the question while they continued to eat.
	“Dude like you basically took charge last night, you should go for that promotion Jimzy. You’ve been in this longer than anyone I know” Yakib said, breaking the silence.
	“Yeah Jimzon get that bag dude, we’d rather follow your calls than any of these desk jockey commanders we usually get anyways.” Berji agreed.
	Jimzon held a soft smile enjoying the flattery but was still reluctant, his mind was elsewhere. 
The dinner wound down as the men grew increasingly relaxed and sleepy. Their earlier excitement had faded, and their conversation became punctuated with yawns and tired chuckles. One by one, their eyelids grew heavy, mirroring each other’s fatigue. Eventually, the banter dwindled to a few scattered remarks, and the group started saying their goodnights. Jimzon, feeling the pull of exhaustion, bade his friends a tired but sincere farewell. “Catch you guys in the morning,” he said with a weary smile
He walked to his bedroom, each step feeling like a small victory against the heaviness of the day. The ship’s corridors seemed quieter now, the soft hum of the machinery a comforting backdrop as he made his way to his quarters.
His room was immaculate, meticulously organized to the point of starkness. The minimalist décor reflected his utilitarian lifestyle, shaped by years of fieldwork that left little room for sentimental clutter. The space featured only the essentials: a neatly made bed, a functional desk, and a pair of plants on the windowsill bathed in the soft glow of a lamp.
On the desk, was a small stone from the Wakeful Islands that usually laid in solitary, but for the past few weeks it rested near a small velvet box. A paper file was positioned next to the box, its contents obscured containing vital intel.
Jimzon approached the desk and let his gaze fall on the scattered papers. Jimzon stood there, paralyzed, staring at the file, his breath shallow and uneven. His hand twitched as it reached for the file, but it hovered just above the surface, trembling. Slowly, he picked up the stack, almost as if the papers themselves might burn him. His fingers traced the edges of the worn pages, flipping through reports, intelligence he’d seen a hundred times but could never fully absorb. It didn’t matter how many times he read them. The words were just noise — irrelevant, cold, detached from the searing agony that came with every glance.
And then his eyes landed on it again. The grainy photo of a Nhath. That same face he had seen that day through. His heart lurched, a sickening pulse of dread and fury crashing into him. He stared at the image, time stretching into a suffocating void. The alien’s dead, black eyes stared back at him, just as they had when Jimzy was a child, hiding and shaking beneath the floorboards. He couldn’t look away, no matter how much he wanted to.
It all rushed back, drowning him. The laughter. His mother’s laughter, bright and warm, echoing in his mind before it twisted into a scream — the scream that had never left him. He could still hear it, still feel the way it ripped through the air, the way it tore something out of him that he would never get back. And the face. That face. He blinked, but it was there, burned behind his eyelids. Every time he tried to shut it out, it was waiting for him, looming, bigger, darker, more monstrous than before.
The Nhath from that day stood in his memory like a grotesque statue, impossibly tall, even though Jimzon knew he wasn’t that much bigger than him now. But in his mind, it was always towering over him, casting a shadow so deep that Jimzy could never escape. The creature's orange skin, those slick, writhing tentacles on its lip, the sharp yellow teeth that glinted like the embodiment of cruelty. Its black, soulless eyes bore into him, empty of any flicker of remorse or recognition. Just an uncaring abyss in the form of a face, a walking nightmare draped in black, tight clothing, bulky shoulder pads, and a tool belt that held the instruments of his family’s death.
His teeth began to chatter, a coldness creeping through his veins even as his blood boiled with rage. He wanted to scream, to tear the world apart, to obliterate something — anything — just to feel like he had some control over the chaos that never left him. But he couldn’t. There was nothing left to fight, nothing to stop. The monster wasn’t here, wasn’t standing in front of him. It was in his mind, a phantom that refused to let go.
And so he collapsed. His legs gave out, his body folding onto the cold floor as the sobs wracked his chest, brutal and silent. He didn’t even recognize the sound of his own weeping. It had been so long since he’d let himself cry — years of swallowing the pain, of pushing it down so deep he had almost convinced himself it wasn’t there. But tonight, it was too much. The dam had broken, and now all the grief, all the rage, all the terror he had buried for so long poured out of him, choking him in its raw intensity.
He pressed his hands against his head, as if that could somehow stop the flood of tears. He tried to bite down on his shirt's collar, tried to squeeze his eyes shut so tightly that maybe — just maybe — the tears would stop. But they wouldn’t. They pushed their way through, relentless, unstoppable. His chest heaved as he gasped for air, but it felt like there was no oxygen left in the room. Everything was too much. The pain, the memories, the weight of it all crushed him into the floor. His shoulders shook as he buried his face into the pillow, trying to muffle the sobs that broke out in jagged breaths. No one could hear him like this. He wouldn’t let them.
The anxiety was clawing at him, tearing at the inside of his chest, his mind racing in a frantic blur of memories and despair. He hadn’t had an anxiety attack in years, not since those early days when the nightmares were still fresh, but now it all came back with a vengeance, like a wave of darkness crashing over him. His pulse raced, his body drenched in sweat as his heart pounded so loudly he thought it might burst. Every beat was a reminder of the helplessness, the horror, the deep, gnawing grief that had never really gone away.
His breath came in shallow gasps, and he felt like he was drowning. He tried to ground himself, to do anything to regain control. Count backward from ten, just like his old comrade had taught him. Force your mind to think of nothing else. Focus. Don’t let the thoughts consume you.
But it was impossible. Every number slipped away, and the faces of the dead, the sound of his mother’s last breath, the sight of his father’s lifeless body — it all rushed back. The alien faces. The carnage. The screams. The overwhelming loneliness.
After hours — or maybe minutes, he couldn’t tell anymore — he finally drifted into a restless, suffocating sleep, his body still shaking, his mind still lost in the torment of memories that refused to fade.
____________________________________________________________________________

Jimzon opened his eyes and stretched. He felt… calm. He knew what he had to do. There was no grogginess or emotional flurry anymore. He approached his neatly arranged closet and pulled out a crisp blue polo emblazoned with the Guard's trident emblem. He changed, grabbed his files, and marched out the door.
 He moved swiftly through the Mothership’s polished corridors, where the hum of the ship mingled with the rhythmic footsteps of soldiers, tech workers, and various personnel going about their duties. The air buzzed with quiet efficiency and the occasional burst of conversation.
Reaching a wooden door that read Director Liles's office, Jimzon took a deep breath and pushed open the door without the customary knock. The room was filled with the scent of polished wood and a faint hint of cigars, a stark contrast to the sterile environment outside. Commander Liles’s large mahogany desk was littered with maps, operational reports, and a half-empty coffee cup. The commander himself, a figure of authority and experience, looked up from his papers with a mix of surprise and curiosity.
“Jimzon! Just the man I wanted to see; I was about to page you,” Liles exclaimed, his cheerful demeanor always found a way to disarm Jimzon’s focus.
“You needed to talk to me, sir?” Jimzon asked, trying to keep his tone steady.
“First off, congratulations on the Nardow raid. Zero Guard fatalities, Nardow captured alive, the compound still intact, plenty of leads, all without a corporal. Jimzon, buddy, you’re a leader. That promotion is yours.”
“Thank you, Liles. I’d love to be a corporal, don’t get me wrong, but I’d be nowhere without my team.”
“Corporal? Oh, Jimzon, think bigger. I know your time here hasn’t been easy, but your growth and leadership over the past few years have been remarkable. I believe Captain would suit you better.”
“Captain?” Jimzon’s eyes widened, his surprise evident.
“If you fill out the paperwork today, I can have it approved by tonight. You’ll get upgraded quarters, a personal ship, medals, and everything that else comes with the rank," He paused and glanced at JImzon  "I see you’re a bit taken aback—was that not what you came here for?”
Jimzon hesitated. He’d been daydreaming about this promotion for so long, and now it was almost within reach. “I’ll get right on that paperwork, Liles and seriously, thank you. But actually, I might have a mission op that could be perfect for a Captain.”
“Really? Do tell. Any leads on artifacts, I hope?”
“Not quite” he handed Liles the Nhats file and looked him sternly yet solemnly in the eyes. “You know how much this means to me.”
Liles held the file, sipped his coffee and frowned. “Jimzon, I know you want to make things right, but taking these men out it's just a massive risk. It’s not even in our AO, hell I don’t know if I can even sanction this”. 
Jimzon’s expression hardened. “You don’t need to sanction it. My team and I don’t have any scheduled missions for months. Just let me use some of my leave time. Liles, I need this.”
“Jimzon,” Liles said, his voice firm but gentle, “you’re stronger than them now. Don’t risk throwing away your future for these scumbags. Letting go of revenge isn’t the same as letting go of your parents. You’ve already done so much good; your family would be proud of you.”
Jimzon fell silent, his resolve wavering but unshaken.
Director Liles continued, his tone softening. “That day my patrol found you on that asteroid, I felt like I had to protect you. I still worry about you every time you’re on a mission. You’re already a better man than I was at your age, and outpace me in experience. You have the potential to do so much good and help many people. That's why I want you to be Captain. Don’t throw it all away on a mission like this. Y'know, one day I’d like us both to be on the Director Board, together, away from the mess of combat.”
“Liles, as right as you may be, I can’t walk away from this,” Jimzon said, his voice resolute.
Liles took a deep sigh, a mix of respect and concern in his eyes. “I’ll grant you time off when the week starts. Don’t be gone too long. Don't tell anyone I'm turning a blind eye to this, and be back as soon as you can.”
Jimzon extended his hand, shaking it with the Director. "Thank you", he said in a low, yet appreciated tone. 
"One last thing. No looting. I don't want need proof that I gave this the OK."
"Yes, sir" he answered. He thought there was something else he wanted to ask him, but it couldn’t have been that important.

Chapter 4
	
	The fluorescent lights in The Grand Mothership's accounting division buzzed incessantly, their sterile glow casting an unrelenting pallor over the sea of desks. Each workstation was a small island of solitude, separated by towering stacks of paperwork that seemed to rise like grim fortresses around the employees. The lights flickered occasionally, casting eerie, shifting shadows that danced across the blank walls, mirroring the monotony of Quinn’s existence.
In the midst of this bleak environment, Quinn labored away, his face illuminated by the cold blue artificial light of his terminal. His once-promising career in accounting had dissolved into a soul-sucking routine. The rhythmic clanking of keyboards and the occasional shuffle of paper were the only sounds that punctuated the silence, each noise a stark reminder of the endless cycle of data entry and financial reconciliation.
Early in his career, Quinn had made a small error on a report—an innocuous mistake buried deep in a sea of numbers. The shame of drawing attention to his slip kept him silent, and to his surprise, no one ever noticed. The error remained hidden, and as time wore on, Quinn’s carelessness grew. In an act of quiet rebellion, he began to introduce minor mistakes deliberately, hoping to assert some semblance of agency in his otherwise monotonous job. Yet, his small acts of defiance proved futile; no one cared. The work was a blur of numbers and figures, likely never read or scrutinized beyond the most superficial glance, if that. What mattered was not the accuracy or integrity of his work, but simply the sheer volume of data he uploaded, a crushing reality that rendered his rebellion meaningless.
Quinn’s desk was a desolate landscape of spreadsheets and financial reports, each document a testament to the futility of his task. The terminal's harsh light accentuated the deep lines etched into his skin,and the dreary bags under his eyes, the weariness that had become a permanent fixture of his features. What had begun as a challenging puzzle had morphed into a monotonous slog, a never-ending procession of debits and credits that seemed to stretch into infinity.
His fingers moved with a robotic precision, each keystroke a mechanical response to an unrelenting tide of numbers that lost meaning the moment they appeared on the screen. The calculations that once ignited a spark of intellectual curiosity now felt like a relentless chore, each number a reminder of his own stagnation.
The office was a labyrinth of gray cubicles and lifeless decor, a visual echo of Quinn’s own internal desolation. The walls, painted in a drab gray, seemed to close in around him, amplifying the profound sense of isolation that had settled into his bones. The emptiness of the space mirrored the emptiness Quinn felt inside, a gaping void where once there had been dreams and aspirations.
Quinn's eyes, red-rimmed and dry from hours of unblinking focus, darted to the clock. Its hands moved with agonizing glacial slowness, each tick a cruel reminder of the time slipping away in this prison of paperwork. He longed for a break, a reprieve from the oppressive monotony that defined his days. But the escape he yearned for seemed as distant as the stars beyond the Mothership’s walls.
The task at hand was a particularly Sisyphean one: retyping every financial transaction from the past twenty years. The war had rendered the galaxy's computers obsolete, and the EMP attacks had left a trail of destruction in their wake. Now, with the Guardnet still in its infancy, Quinn was trapped in an endless cycle of manually transcribing paper records, for no other reason than some higher up demanded it to be done.
His face had become a mask of stoic resignation, a permanent expression of detachment that concealed the depth of his inner turmoil. The smile, once a natural gesture, had long since faded, replaced by a blank, emotionless stare. Quinn had forgotten how to express joy or anger; his existence had been reduced to a series of monotonous tasks, each one stripping away a piece of his humanity.
As the hours dragged on, Quinn felt more like a ghost than a man, a spectral figure lost in the sterile, soulless environment of the accounting division. The loneliness was palpable, a thick fog that enveloped his mind and obscured any trace of connection or meaning. In this bleak, artificial world, Quinn was a solitary figure, consumed by the unrelenting monotony of his existence.
The automatic door on the far side of the room hissed open with its usual, monotonous whine, revealing a fleeting glimpse of the bustling hallway beyond. Quinn watched, detached, as a young unpaid intern scurried in, his face a mask of naive hope that will surely be broken soon. The door’s brief parting revealed soldiers returning from battle, their raucous laughter and loud obnoxious voices piercing through the sterile hum of Quinn’s prison. They seemed alive with an energy that Quinn had long since forgotten, their camaraderie a stark contrast to the numbing isolation of his own existence.
He could hear their boisterous conversations, their voices rising and falling with exaggerated quality, as if they were celebrating something truly worth the noise. Quinn felt a pang of jealousy, sharp and bitter. He had never harbored dreams of dying for some grand cause, especially not dying for some large company, but the idea of existing in a world where disorder held meaning was strangely appealing. Hell, it seemed he was slowly dying here anyways. In his dark moments, he fantasized about the battlefield, not for the glory or honor, but for the freedom that seemed to come with the chaos. Even the visceral reality of conflict felt like an escape from the droning monotony of his office. Even if that idea ended with him dying face down in some warm puddle of mud.
But such thoughts were fleeting, like wisps of a smoky breath vanishing into the cold. His contract was nearing its end, and with it, the promise of freedom—freedom that felt increasingly elusive with each passing day. The idea of leaving this soul-crushing job behind was a small, flickering light at the end of a very long tunnel. Despite the years he had sacrificed to this soulless grind, Quinn clung to the hope of walking away from it all.
Yet, he had no concrete plans, no clear path beyond the exit of this cold environment. His mind, dulled by the relentless tedium, struggled to conjure any real ideas or direction. Maybe, if he could manage a few nights of uninterrupted sleep, he might find some semblance of clarity. But the crushing weight of his current existence made even that seem like a distant, improbable dream. For now, the thought of escaping this purgatory was his only solace, a desperate yearning to flee from a life that felt increasingly like a never-ending sentence.
He often indulged in darker fantasies, though they were less frequent than his daydreams of escape. His mind wandered to vivid scenarios where he would dramatically quit—where he would storm out of the office, or slam his fist through his computer screen, shattering it into a thousand fragments of splintered glass. He envisioned himself unleashing a torrent of venomous invective at his higher-ups, telling them exactly what he thought of their hollow promises and stifling bureaucracy. In these moments, the idea of rebellion felt intoxicating, a stark contrast to the crushing banality of his everyday life.
Sometimes, his mind veered towards more apocalyptic fantasies. He sometimes imagined one of the countless crag terrorist cells launching a catastrophic explosion in the office while he was out to lunch. The notion of a sudden, violent end to his daily grind—an escape from the soul-sucking routine—held a morbid allure. The fantasy offered a fleeting glimmer of excitement, he would take any chaotic break from the oppressive monotony that suffocated him.
Yet, the reality of quitting was far more daunting than he had anticipated when he first enlisted. The contract’s terms were a cruel trap, promising a lump sum payout of 95% of his four-year salary upon completion, with only a meager 1.3% annually in the interim. The justification for this seemingly exorbitant arrangement was the provision of safety, food, and sleeping quarters—essentially, everything necessary to keep him bound to the Mothership and its endless corridors. But in reality, Quinn knew that in truth it was because they were a monopoly. The thought of leaving was fraught with the anxiety of not having a viable alternative, of being cast adrift without a place to go.
Even if Quinn mustered the resolve to quit, the practical reality of his situation was almost insurmountable. He was trapped on a military ship in the vast, uncharted void of space, with no personal vessel or means of escape at his disposal. The enormity of the Mothership surrounded him like a steel cage, its corridors and compartments a labyrinthine prison from which there was no straightforward exit. Even if he somehow managed to sever his ties with the organization, the cold expanse of space outside offered no sanctuary—only an overwhelming sense of isolation and the stark realization that he had no place to go.
The Guard, with its constant rhetoric about stability and equality, was a cruel joke in Quinn’s eyes. Their preachings of a utopian order seemed hollow and mocking when weighed against the oppressive monotony of his daily existence. Despite their promises of safety and a fair society, Quinn felt less like a valued member of a cohesive unit and more like a prisoner shackled by the very system that claimed to protect him. In truth, he felt like a slave to the endless grind of paperwork and the unfeeling bureaucracy that dictated his every move. The idealistic assurances of the Guard only served to highlight the chasm between their lofty proclamations and the grim reality of his own life.
The sharp thud of a stack of papers crashing onto Quinn’s desk shattered the oppressive silence of the office, the sound echoing like a sudden, jarring gunshot in the cavernous void of the room. Quinn jolted in his chair, his heart hammering against his ribs as he looked up in a daze. Looming over his cubicle was an advisor, their imposing presence an unsettling blend of authority and disdain.
The advisor's gaze, cold and unfeeling, held a faint smirk that seemed to mock the very essence of Quinn's misery. "You're being transferred," the advisor announced, their voice slicing through the air with a chilling finality. The words fell heavily in the stagnant atmosphere, their implications lingering like a dark cloud.
Quinn’s mind reeled, struggling to grasp the gravity of the announcement. “Transferred? What do you mean transferred?” he repeated, his voice cracking under the weight of confusion and desperation. The very notion of his life being upended in this abrupt manner felt like a cruel joke. 
The advisor’s smirk widened, an almost palpable sneer at Quinn’s obvious distress. "Your skills are needed elsewhere," the advisor continued, their tone dripping with a casual indifference that cut deeper than any reprimand. "The Guard has decided that you are fit for a different kind of duty. Sometimes, the Guard needs more than just number-crunchers."
Quinn’s hands began to tremble slightly. He reached for the file that lay among the chaotic scatter of papers on his desk. Each movement was an agonizing reminder of his helplessness as his advisor stared at him, sipping his coffee. With a sense of resigned dread, he pried open the folder, the dry rustle of paper a harsh diversion to the stifling silence of the office. 
As he began to read, the words danced and swayed before his eyes, a blur of bureaucratic jargon and cold directives, before they seemed to shift and blur, merging into a single, overwhelming weight of hopelessness. The document informed him, with cold efficiency, that he was being dispatched to a security mission on a remote, icy moon—one that orbited a planet so obscure that it felt like an afterthought in the vast, indifferent expanse of the universe.
Questions spun in his mind, a maelstrom of panic and confusion. Why the fuck was he being sent to this desolate, frozen shithole wasteland? What had he done to deserve such a cruel twist of fate? The questions piled up, each one a knife twisting in his gut, amplifying his sense of betrayal and isolation. The very concept of his life being uprooted seemed surreal, as though he were trapped in a nightmarish alternate reality.
The full weight of his new assignment bore down on him, the gravity of the situation sinking like a boulder in his chest. The sterile, monotonous environment of his office, once a cage of routine and tedium, now seemed like a distant sanctuary compared to the bleak, frozen expanse awaiting him. The thought of being thrust into an unforgiving, cold hell where warmth was a forgotten luxury filled him with a deep, gnawing despair. Quinn’s sense of futility and abandonment was overwhelming, each beat of his heart echoing the profound emptiness and despair that now defined his existence.
The advisor slapped Quinn on the back, the impact jarring and uncomfortable. “Start packing, buddy,” he said, his tone overly cheerful. As the advisor walked away, the sting from the slap lingered, underscoring the impersonal nature of the news.

Chapter 5 (unfinished - needs rework, tone came off wrong, emphasize distance)

Honestly prob cut this whole chapter 👎👎 jimzon needs to stop whining 

Jimzon hunched over in the dim, claustrophobic space of his room, the only source of light a small desk lamp, casting an eerie glow on his battered helmet in front of him. He worked with a quiet intensity, meticulously painting it black, each stroke precise. His forehead glistened with sweat, his jaw tight with focus, and his hands—now smeared with black paint—moved with a mechanical rhythm. His body betrayed his exhaustion; the dark, heavy bags under his eyes were evidence of days spent without rest. Each blink was a slow, labored effort, but he didn’t stop, driven by some inner force that kept him tethered to this task.
The helmet, now almost entirely covered in fresh, glossy obsidian black paint, gleamed under the lamplight. His obsession was near compulsive, focused on the imperfections within the coating. Each brushstroke felt like another step toward a confrontation with the ghosts that haunted him, he was going to be ready.
A sudden knock broke through the silence, cutting through his focus like a blade. Jimzon’s hand stilled mid-stroke, the paintbrush trembling slightly in his grip. He paused, muscles tense, unsure if the sleep deprivation was catching up with him. The knock repeated, confirming he hadn't imagined it.
With a heavy sigh, he set the brush down, the soft clink of wood on metal echoing in the small room. Wiping his paint-smeared hands on his baggy pants, he slowly rose to his feet, the weight of exhaustion pressing down on him. 
He stood still for a moment, staring at the door. It was just the food he’d ordered, he reminded himself. But even that seemed like a cruel joke—an intrusion into the world he had sealed himself inside, a world where he could lose himself in the task of preparing for the fight ahead. The food had arrived too quickly, too soon for him to feel ready to step away from the helmet and the madness that came with it. The early arrival felt oddly intrusive, catching him off guard. Perhaps it was for the best. It was late—or maybe early; he wasn’t sure. Either way, he knew he needed to rest eventually.
Jimzon stumbled sluggishly to the door, his feet dragging across the floor like weights too heavy to lift. His vision blurred as he rubbed his tired eyes, trying to force himself into some semblance of alertness. His hand reached for the knob, hesitating for a moment before turning it. The door creaked open, and the sudden flood of harsh hallway light hit him like a slap. He squinted instinctively, raising his hand to shield his face from the glare. His eyes, already strained from the hours spent in the dim light of his room, struggled to adjust.
Through squinted lids, he made out the silhouette of someone standing before him. His breath caught in his throat as his heart gave an uneasy lurch. Kelsa. Her familiar figure came into focus, and with it, a wave of memories—some painful, some bittersweet. She stood there, perfect posture but distant, the formal blue of her Guard uniform crisp and sharply contrasted against the mess Jimzon knew he was.
"I have your stuff," she said flatly, holding up a bag. "Tried to give it to Pav, but he said you’ve been missing PT. No one’s seen you in days."
Her voice was steady, but there was a slight edge of annoyance underlining her words, a trace of something she couldn’t—or wouldn’t—express. She shifted her weight subtly, and Jimzon caught a fleeting glimpse of her face: the pale blonde hair, with small streaks of purple, tied into a hasty bun, the piercing green eyes that seemed to search for something in him, only to find disappointment. He couldn’t bear to meet her gaze for more than a second. His eyes dropped quickly to the floor, where he could only notice the chipped paint on his boots, and ink smears on his thigh.
As if on instinct, he nudged a half-empty astrovin bottle behind the door with his foot, the glass clinking softly against the wall. A few amber drops trickled onto the floor, and Jimzon wasn’t sure whether the stale scent of alcohol in the air was from the bottle or his own breath. 
Shame washed over him like a cold tide. Why did she have to come now, of all times? He looked like hell—clothes smeared with black paint, eyes hollow from lack of sleep, and a haze of alcohol clinging to him. Exhausted, drunk, and broken, he felt exposed. Did she want to see him like this? To confirm how far he’d fallen?
Jimzon did his best to mask the storm brewing inside him, his face settling into a carefully constructed neutral expression. He couldn’t let it show, not now. "Thanks," he muttered, his voice barely above a whisper. "Just… put it down there." He gestured vaguely, hoping to end the conversation before it began.
But Kelsa wasn’t so easily dismissed. She looked at him again, her eyes soft, not judgmental, just concerned. "Jimzon… what are you doing?" she asked quietly, her voice walking the line between frustration and worry. She took a step forward, her gaze sharp, as if trying to pierce through the haze that clung to him. "You look like you’ve been locked in here for days."
His chest tightened, and he struggled to find words. He wanted to explain, to give her something, but his throat felt like it had closed up. Instead, he forced a weak smile, the kind that barely touched his lips, let alone his eyes. "Just… working on something," he mumbled, the lie sliding off his tongue in a voice that was too low, too flat. "It’s nothing."
"Nothing?" Kelsa’s eyebrows knit together as she moved a step closer, and he could feel the weight of her gaze bearing down on him, making the air thicker, harder to breathe. "You’re drunk alone, disappearing for days… You look terrible, Jimzon. This isn’t ‘nothing.’"
Her words hit harder than he expected, slicing through the fragile wall he’d built to keep everything in. He finally glanced up, meeting her gaze, only to be struck by the depth of her concern. It clashed against the barricades he’d erected around himself, and for a moment, he faltered. "Look, it's fine, I'm fine," he shot back, the words coming out sharper than he intended, edged with defensiveness.
Kelsa blinked, surprised by the tone, but didn’t back down. "I hope you’ve got someone to talk to, because I know you’re not going to tell me," she said, her voice soft but pointed. There was an undercurrent of sadness in her words.
They had always been different in ways that both complemented and complicated their relationship. Kelsa was disciplined, methodical, the kind of person who always had a plan. She thrived on structure, on knowing what was coming next. Jimzon, on the other hand, lived for spontaneity, the thrill of the unknown. He had a habit of diving headfirst into situations, relying on instinct, while she preferred caution, analyzing every angle before making a move.
Sometimes, those differences balanced them perfectly. Kelsa’s steady, grounded nature kept him from flying off the handle, while his impulsiveness pulled her out of her shell, reminding her that life wasn’t always something you could plan for. When it worked, it felt like harmony—like they completed parts of each other they didn’t even realize were missing.
But other times, the very things that made them work drove them apart. His unpredictability could leave her feeling untethered, unsure of where they stood. Meanwhile, her need for certainty, for control, sometimes felt like pressure to him, a force that stifled the free-spirited way he moved through the world. It was in those moments of imbalance, when their differences clashed instead of meshed, that the cracks had begun to show. And now, those cracks had widened into a chasm neither of them knew how to bridge.
Had he been blind to it, thinking their differences made them invincible? Or had he just assumed that love alone would be enough to hold it all together?
Jimzon swallowed hard, looking away again. "I’m just trying to figure some things out, that's all," he lied, but even he could hear how thin it sounded. He hoped it would be enough to push her away, to keep her from digging deeper.
She shook her head slowly, her lips pressing into a thin line. "I know you, Jim. I’ve seen your bad days, remember? This isn’t you ‘figuring things out.’ This is you… hiding. Again." Her voice was steady, but the crackle of tension between them hung in the air, unspoken feelings lurking beneath the surface.
Maybe she was right. Maybe he was hiding. He didn’t have the energy to explain, didn’t have the words to make her understand the emptiness that gnawed at him every waking moment.
"Look I still care about you," Kelsa said, her tone softening, the sharp edges of frustration giving way to something more vulnerable. "But you can’t keep doing this to yourself. Drinking, shutting everyone out… What do you think is going to happen? "
Jimzon felt his frustration begin to bubble over, the alcohol dulling his restraint. The words tumbled out before he could stop them, sharp and bitter. “You care?” His voice wavered with disbelief, the edge cutting deep as he spoke. “You care? You never told me why you left. Not one reason.”
He winced the moment the words left his mouth, but it was too late to pull them back. The harshness in his voice hung between them like a weapon he hadn’t meant to draw, and for a second, the silence that followed felt unbearable. He couldn’t meet her eyes, didn’t want to see the hurt he’d just inflicted, but he could feel her presence tense, the air around them growing colder.
Kelsa's expression shifted from shock to something more complicated—first offense, then pity. The tension in her jaw relaxed, but her eyes brimmed with frustration and a sadness she’d kept bottled for too long.
"You think it was easy for me?" Her voice was measured, but the slight tremor betrayed her control. "You were gone, Jim. Always on some mission. And so was I. We stopped talking, and when we did… it felt like you weren’t even there anymore. I missed you, the real you, but you were always too far away. We both were. We were just fooling ourselves thinking those days were coming back." She paused, her breath hitching. "I still want you to be happy, to get your head right. But I need to be alone and figure my own life out."
Jimzon’s brow furrowed, the weight of her words sinking in too slowly. He frowned, lips tugging into a pout as the alcohol still dulled his senses. "What do you mean they weren't going to happen again?"
Kelsa’s eyes flicked up, and for the first time, she looked exhausted. Not the kind of exhaustion Jimzon knew from sleepless nights, but something deeper, something that couldn’t be fixed with a few hours of rest. "I’m leaving the Guard in a year, Jim. And you… you’re going to be a captain. This is the life you chose." She let the weight of it settle before continuing, her voice quieter now, more vulnerable. "I wanted to fight for us. I wanted to believe we could make it, but somewhere along the lines we both stopped fighting"
Jimzon opened his mouth to argue, to tell her she was wrong—that he had fought, that he was still fighting, but the denial wouldn’t come. His defiance faltered, his gaze softening as the truth began to seep in. Maybe it was true. He had been slipping away, one day at a time.
"I'm sorry," he muttered, the words thick on his tongue, bitter and insincere even as he forced them out. Apologies felt hollow after so much time, too late to heal the cracks between them, for the years they spent together.
Kelsa nodded, the smallest of sad smiles tugging at her lips, but it never reached her eyes. "I know you are," she whispered. The fight had drained from her voice, leaving behind only resignation. It was over, and they both knew it.
Jimzon stood there, silent and defeated. There was nothing more to say, no words that could change what had already been lost. He couldn't find the words.
"Goodbye, Jimzon." Her voice was soft, almost gentle.
Jimzon stood frozen, watching as the door closed with a soft click. The darkness of the room seemed to creep in from every corner, swallowing him whole. He was alone again.
Jimzon blinked twice, trying to process the emptiness that had swallowed him whole. Closure—what a useless word. It was supposed to help, wasn’t it? Tie things up neatly, make the pain easier to bear. But all it did was deepen the ache, sink its claws into him, refusing to let go. He clenched his jaw, scanning the room as if it could give him some kind of answer, some sense to the void settling in his chest.
Fuck, he thought, hating the way everything ended. He always despised how final things could be—like a door slamming shut with no way to open it again. It couldn’t be over. Not like this. Where was the resolution? The happy ending he had worked so hard for?
He tried to release the memories, but his heart refused to let them go. They clung to him, suffocating him with their weight. He thought of the first time he cried tears of joy—how raw and natural it had felt. They had been wrapped around each other on that old couch after a long day, and he’d said it. I love you. He’d known it was premature but she’d smiled. She’d said it back, almost instantly, and his heart had burst at that moment. How could he forget the way her voice had sounded, so soft, so full of promise?
And that night, on their six-month anniversary, when they’d been curled up in bed. Just before sleep took them, she’d whispered, "Kelsa Avaris… I like the sound of that." The way she said his last name made his chest tighten, made him believe that maybe this could last forever. He still felt that rush, even now. Fuck.
He swallowed the lump in his throat as he thought about how brutally the distance had torn them apart, how the time together had been so fleeting, so fragile. Yet, in those moments, it had felt like nothing could touch them. They’d always found ways to welcome each other home—small tokens, gifts from their travels. A reminder that even in the farthest corners of the galaxy, they were still thinking of each other. Now, all of it felt like a cruel joke.
Finality. He hated it. The silence, the way something so important could be reduced to a moment, a look, a closed door.
He couldn’t picture a future in the Guard without her. More than that, he couldn’t picture a future at all without her. Everything else felt wrong, twisted—like some other version of himself, one he didn’t recognize. He tried to pinpoint where he’d failed, why he’d lost her. He thought he’d done everything right. He tried so hard to be what she needed, to be the best version of himself. He never wanted to burden her, never wanted to drag her down with his own struggles. So he kept things in, kept his pain locked away in some corner of his heart where it festered.
But what did “being the best” even mean? He certainly wasn’t. She was gone. And no amount of trying, no amount of regret, could bring her back. He knew nobody was perfect, but he could have been better. He should have been better.
He thought back to the nights they’d spent together—laughing, joking, going on adventures. He had loved how easily she’d laughed at his jokes, how she always seemed to match his energy, the way she had looked at him. Recently, though, he couldn’t make her laugh. He couldn’t even make himself laugh. That spark, that easy chemistry, had vanished. He didn’t know when, or how, but it had slipped through his fingers.
He had felt so safe with her, so relaxed in a way that he couldn’t be with anyone else. Jimzon was always the extrovert—the loud, cocky guy in every room, always the one cracking jokes or leading conversations. But with her, he didn’t have to be that. He didn’t need the performance, didn’t need to fill the silence with noise. Just because he wasn’t his usual boisterous self around her didn’t mean he didn’t love her. It was the opposite. He loved her because he could be still. He could be quiet. He could just be.
But now, in the dark of his empty room, the realization hit him like a punch to the gut. He was just now understanding how his inconsistency must have felt to her—how the silences she might have found comforting in the beginning could have turned into something else, something uncertain. While he had felt safe, had felt at peace in their shared quiet moments, maybe she had felt neglected. His retreat into himself wasn’t a reflection of his feelings for her, but how was she supposed to know that?
Maybe she had taken his silence for disinterest, his quiet for withdrawal. He’d thought his love was clear in the way he held her, in the way he came home to her, in the way he always looked for her in a room. But had it been enough? Could she have seen it the way he meant for her to?
Jimzon’s gaze fell on the bag she’d left behind, its weight a cold reminder of her absence. He unzipped it slowly, and her scent hit him, flooding his senses. It was overwhelming, like she was there, standing right next to him, but at the same time, she was nowhere. Just a ghost, haunting the space they used to share. His hands trembled as he let the bag fall to the floor, its contents spilling out—a mess of clothes, fragments of her life, of their life.
He walked to his desk, picking up the small velvet box that sat there, untouched for weeks. His fingers lingered on the edge before he opened it, revealing the engagement ring. It glittered under the dim light, mocking him, a symbol of what could have been. A future they would never have. It looked wrong now, like a cruel reminder of everything he had lost.
With a sharp breath, he threw it. The box hit the wall with a thud, landing somewhere on the floor, forgotten. It didn’t matter anymore. Nothing mattered.
He collapsed onto the bed, but rest wouldn’t come. 

Chapter 6 

The scream of the wind across the frozen wasteland was that of a feral dying creature. A raw, agonized howl tearing through the seams of Quinn's gear, sinking into his flesh and bones with the precision of a thousand frozen needles. It was as though the storm itself was alive, insistent upon punishing him. He stood at the edge of the world, where the icy abyss stretched out endlessly before him, each gust of wind gnawing at his bones as his breath crystallized, fragile and fleeting, before vanishing into nothing. His thermal suit, soaked with cold sweat, clung to him like a second skin, useless against the merciless chill. The seams of the tattered cloak he bore over the suit were ragged, barely holding together, evidence of battles fought but not won, not that he would've cared to find out. The frost crept into his suit, freezing his appendages inch by inch. It coiled around him like a serpent, slowly suffocating him.
Beside him, five soldiers huddled together under the stars, a pitiful assembly of mismatched gear and haunted stares. Their thermal suits were patched and torn, just like his, their cloaks flapping in the tempest, helmets dented and scratched. Most of them, they looked young, but their eyes betrayed them—hollow gazes that spoke of fear too deep to voice, masked by the sternness their roles demanded. The storm made them statues, frozen in place by the weight of dread, waiting for something, anything, to break the monotony of survival.
The snow fell without end, a relentless curtain of white that swallowed the world whole. It muted everything—the growls of stomachs, the crunch of snow underfoot—until only silence remained, thick and oppressive. It pressed in on Quinn, squeezing his lungs with invisible hands, suffocating in its intensity. He strained to see anything through the swirling chaos, but the storm had engulfed everything. The snowflakes spun in disarray, mocking him collectively, thriving on his helplessness, building up on his shoulders. Even the near mountains had disappeared into the storm, their jagged peaks buried beneath the blizzard, leaving only a yawning emptiness in their wake. Quinn felt the weight of dread settle deep in his chest, heavy as lead, a sense of inevitability that gnawed at the edges of his thoughts.
They stood at the foot of an icy mountain, before a closed hangar that offered no solace. Their outpost was a wreck, little more than a scattering of crates, bolted-down chairs, and torn tarps that flapped uselessly in the storm. Red indicator lights dotted the area every few yards, marking their place in the void, but even those flickered under the assault of the storm, repeatedly falling over, getting buried in snow, only to be picked up again by shaking hands. 
The wind howled louder, driving the cold deeper into Quinn’s bones. His face stung with the bite of it, each exposed patch of skin numb and raw, despite the layers of gear. Breathing had become an ordeal, in between his chattering of teeth, each inhale like swallowing shards of ice. For a moment, Quinn wondered if they too would disappear, swallowed by the endless, uncaring white.
"This is bullshit," Quinn snarled, his voice nearly lost to the howling wind. Each word was forced through gritted teeth, barely audible against the storm's relentless fury. "What the fuck are we even guarding out here? Snow? Ice?"
"Lifes’ bullshit," Graves muttered, his tone flat as he kicked at the frozen ground beneath him, dislodging a small clump of ice only for it to be buried again. "Get over yourself."
Graves was the oldest among them, his weathered skin and the faint lines of crow’s feet beginning to appear above the scarf that concealed his mouth. He stared back at the storm approaching.
"It shouldn’t be. I'm not a soldier. I shouldn’t fucking be here." There was an edge of desperation laced in Quinn's words, the pride in his voice mingling with something darker, more fragile.
Graves barely bothered to glance at him, his movements slow and indifferent. "Well, you are one tonight," he said, his voice as cold as the storm surrounding them.
"Hey, wait, you're not? I’m not a soldier either," Oren chimed in, his voice shaky, trembling either from the weather or his own nerves, it was hard to tell. "I’m a cyberbiology scientist."
"Yeah, and I was a propagandist," Vick added with a bitter laugh. There was a dark weight behind his words, the kind that clung to a man who had seen too many things. He glanced back at Graves, "But not tonight I suppose", saying with some solidarity. 
"Royal journalist," came a dry, sarcastic voice from Cillus, slightly raising his hand. He had been standing silently, unmoving, as though the storm could no longer touch him. He hadn’t spoken until now, his words dripping with venomous irony. "Got deployed out here for a joint exercise between the King and the Guard. But, embedded journalism isn't my usual forte", he choked out stiffly, trying to sound lighthearted.
Quinn turned to face the group, his eyes wide with disbelief. "Wait, what do you mean you’re not soldiers? Why the fuck did they stick us here then?" His voice cracked with incredulity, the gnawing feeling of absurdity clawing at his mind.
Oren sighed, a weary, almost defeated sound. "Budget cuts, maybe?" He glanced at the swirling storm around them, a flicker of hope fading with each passing second. "It’s only for a few days, right?"
Cillus snorted, the sound rough and cynical. "What are we even guarding anyway?" He gestured toward the massive, empty hangar doors behind them, their shadows barely visible through the curtain of snow. "Honestly, I doubt there's anything in there."
Graves grunted, his expression unreadable behind his frost-covered helmet. "It doesn’t matter," he growled, his voice cutting through the air like the cold itself. "Just do your duty." His words carried the weight of a man who no longer questioned the senselessness of his orders—because asking ‘why’ had long since stopped bringing answers.
Vick’s smirk twisted his face into something almost feral, the gleam in his eyes sharp with bitter sarcasm. "Maybe it is nothing," he said, voice dripping with mockery. "Maybe we’re just here to shovel snow in case they ever decide to" His laugh was a short, hollow bark.
Quinn's jaw tightened, his frustration barely contained as he shook his head. "Can't be. It’d be a hell of a lot easier to shovel if they gave us food that didn’t freeze solid the second we opened it."
"Or hell, proper armor so we wouldn't freeze." Cillus muttered, rubbing his gloved hands together in a futile attempt to coax warmth back into his stiff fingers. 
Oren shifted nervously. "So you think we’re just here so some captain can check off a box on his paper? In his warm, cushy cabin?"
Quinn’s voice darkened. "Maybe it’s worse than that. It's punishment. That’s what this is." His fists clenched involuntarily, the heat of anger radiating off him yet vanishing before it could make a difference.
Oren’s voice dropped, almost a whisper, a tone of resignation. "Maybe we’re just unlucky. Wrong place, wrong time. Not everything’s a conspiracy, Quinn." He sounded defeated, yet trying to cling to the last remnants of his youthful optimism.
Quinn’s eyes blazed behind his visor."You really think this is just bad luck? Please. They could’ve sent anyone, but they sent us. You know why? They knew we wouldn't question it, they knew we wouldn't protest. Why? Because they don't give a fuck about us."
