"Maybe they do care." Vick chimed in. His broad shoulders lifted in a slow shrug beneath his battered thermal jacket. "Maybe this is their way of keeping us sharp. Throw us somewhere miserable, see if we break. Survival of the fittest, right? Let the weak ones rot while the strong ones crawl back."
"We’re all rotting," Graves said, his voice low and hollow, as if the truth of it had long since settled in his bones. "Doesn’t matter if it’s here or back on the Mothership, it’s the same thing. Go back to number crunching all you want, Quinn, but life ain’t much better in a cubicle."
"You miss the point," Quinn hissed, the words sharp and cutting as his anger seeped out. "I didn’t get sent here because I wasn’t good at my job. I got sent here because I was too good at it. I saw through their bullshit. I know they're laundering god knows how much money for god knows who".
Graves barked out a harsh, humorless laugh. "You think they sent you here because you’re too smart for them? Hell, Quinn, maybe you’re here because you pissed off the wrong guy in the dining hall. No one needs a reason to screw us over. It’s all one big lottery, and our numbers just came up." He paused, the words hanging heavy in the freezing air, before adding quietly, "There’s no rhyme or reason to any of this. Not here. Not anywhere."
Cillus, still fiddling with a strap on his gear that wouldn’t stay tight, finally broke the silence. His voice was quiet but had a firmness to it, like he’d been holding the thought in too long. "I dunno... Sometimes I wonder if any of this has a reason at all. I mean, think about it—life, the universe, all these battles we fight—what’s the point? We’re out here freezing to death, fighting wars we didn’t start, for people who don’t even know our names. It’s like we don’t even exist to them."
Vick nudged him with a rough elbow, the gesture more comradely than mocking. "That’s some deep shit for a guy who can’t aim straight," he teased, though his voice lacked the usual bite. There was a hollowness behind his words, a shared exhaustion that had eroded any real intent to insult. "But yeah, what’s the point of anything? We’re out here 'cause it’s what we do. Fighting, freezing, dying—it’s all just part of the gig. They don't pay us to ask questions."
Graves shifted beside them, his heavy boots crunching into the ice under his weight. He let out a low grunt, the sound heavy with disdain. "You all sound like a bunch of philosophers." He stared into the blizzard, his face an unreadable mask under layers of frost. "Truth is, none of it matters. Survival—that’s it. You either get the job done, or you don’t. It don’t matter why we’re here, don’t matter what comes after".
Quinn’s voice snapped back, "That’s easy for you to say. You’ve been doing this for how long now? You’re used to being treated like shit. The rest of us? Some of us had something better before this. Something more."
Graves turned to face him, his eyes hard beneath his visor, unflinching against the biting wind. "And you think that makes you special?" His tone was cold, cutting through the air like the storm itself. "We all had something before this. We all had lives. Some of us learned to shut up and survive. You think being smart makes you better? Out here, it’s all the same. The cold doesn't care how smart you think you are or how high you climbed. You'll freeze same as anyone else."
Vick chuckled, but it was a dark, bitter sound, devoid of warmth. "I used to think I had it all figured out too. Thought life was about getting ahead, proving your worth to somebody. Then you end up in a place like this, and none of that shit matters when your toes are turning black and you’re pissing icicles. What matters is who can hold the line when the real shit hits, not what you did after the cold got to you."
Quinn’s grip tightened on his rifle, his knuckles white beneath his gloves, fury simmering beneath the surface. "I’m not going to die out here," he hissed, his voice fierce and determined, cutting through the wind like a blade. "I didn’t crawl my way up from nothing just to freeze to death on this godforsaken moon. When this is over, I’m getting back to where I belong. Mark my words. I'm done"
Vick didn’t bother to look at him, but the smirk on his face was unmistakable, his eyes half-lidded behind the frosted visor. "Keep telling yourself that, Quinn. The cold’s got a funny way of showing you that what you think you deserve doesn't mean jack shit out here. Deservings got nothing to do with it."
Quinn’s voice dropped low, barely audible above the storm, as though the words themselves were too bitter to fully speak aloud. "Whatever. My time’s almost up anyway. Fuck the Guard. Enjoy swallowing their shit for the rest of your lives."
Graves, his gravely rugged voice spoke up once more, cutting through Quinn’s bravado like a knife. "And what exactly do you plan to do after this, Quinn? I certainly know you don’t have a wife and kids waiting for you."
That hit a nerve. Quinn bristled at Graves’ words "And what about you, Graves? You think you’ll make it out of here just because of your noble speeches and smug attitude? If you don't dream bigger, it's a matter of time before you're in a body bag on some bullshit front. Like you said, we’re all stuck in this hellhole, so don’t act like you're better than me. You don't have anything going for you after this shit ends."
Graves didn’t flinch, his face a cold mask beneath his visor, though the slightest flicker of weariness haunted his eyes. He’d heard this before—desperation disguised as anger.
Before Graves could reply, Vick shifted his stance, the usual bravado gone from his face, replaced by a distant, hollow look. His rifle, once held with purpose, now felt more like dead weight in his hands. "After this?" he muttered, almost to himself, staring out into the endless blizzard. "After this... what’s even left? We finish the mission, maybe get lucky and survive long enough to crawl back to the Mothership. But then what? More of the same? Another frozen wasteland, another meaningless assignment? We’re just trading one bleak station for another. Maybe it is time to cut my paycheck and quit too"
Graves huffed, his breath hanging in the air like smoke, dissipating into the icy wind. "Ain’t nothing waiting for us but more of this. That’s the game. You get one job done, they send you to the next, like clockwork. There’s no finish line, no retirement. You’re done when your body gives out, or when they decide you’re no longer useful. That’s it." His voice was devoid of emotion, like he’d accepted the weight of it long ago.
Oren, fidgeting as usual, spoke up, his voice quieter but strangely hopeful, a glimmer of something almost innocent that had yet to be snuffed out. "I don’t know, man. Sometimes I think... maybe there’s something better waiting for us after all this. Not just another mission, but something bigger. Something beyond all this war, all this survival. Maybe, when we’re done, we finally get to rest."
Quinn barked a laugh, sharp and bitter, shaking his head as if Oren’s words were too naïve to even consider. "Rest? You think there’s some grand reward waiting for us? Rest is for the ones who never stepped foot in this hell. We’re the expendable ones, the cogs in their machine. We fight their wars, clean up their messes, so they can sleep peacefully in their warm beds. No one’s going to remember us when we’re gone. We’ll be just like the rest—forgotten, swept aside, while they move on to the next batch of fools. We're wasting our lives here."
Oren frowned, his boyish features tightening. "You don’t believe in anything better? Not even after... I mean, if we die out here, you don’t think there’s anything waiting for us? Heaven? A second chance, maybe?"
Vick snorted, a dark, joyless chuckle escaping his lips as he stared down at the ground. "Second chance? No, kid. This is it. You’re born, you fight, you suffer, and then you die. I’ve seen too much to believe in fairy tales. After this? There’s nothing. Just blackness. Cold, empty nothing." His voice was a hollow echo, the words weighed down by a sense of inevitability.
"I wouldn’t be so sure," Graves interrupted, his voice gruff but thoughtful. "I’ve been out here a long time, and I’ve seen things. Shit that doesn’t add up. People dying and... coming back. Not the way you’d expect either. Maybe there’s something out there, beyond this life. Not heaven, not hell—just something in between."
Quinn’s eyes narrowed with skepticism, his lips curling into a sneer. "You’re talking about ghosts now? Come on, Graves. You don’t seriously believe in all that supernatural bullshit, do you?"
Graves shrugged, the movement slow and deliberate under his thick armor. "Don’t care what you call it. I’ve seen men who were dead—gone—stand back up and walk again. Call it whatever you want, but I know what I saw. There’s more to this world than what we understand, Quinn. I don't care if you believe it, but it’s out there."
Vick shook his head, a bitter smirk tugging at the corner of his mouth. "That’s just the cold getting to you, Graves. Freezing your brain, making you see things that aren’t there. Trust me, after a while out here, it all blends together—hallucinations, exhaustion, nightmares. It messes with your head. Ain’t no afterlife, no ghosts, no second chances. Just snow, ice, and eventually, the dark. Just enjoy it while you have it"
Oren shifted uncomfortably, his gloved hands tightening around the cold metal of his gear. His eyes, though hidden behind his visor, flickered with something fragile, a hope not yet broken by the harsh reality they were facing. "I don’t know... I’d like to believe there’s something more. That when we’re done here, there’s peace. Or maybe... maybe we just become something else. Energy, light. We go back to the stars or something." His voice was soft, almost pleading, as if the belief was the only thing keeping him from falling apart.
Graves grunted, his breath a cloud in the frozen air. "Stars? You think we’re that important, Oren?" He leaned forward, his voice a grim rasp. "Think the universe gives a damn where we go? You wanna believe we turn into something pure, fine. But me? I think we’re just meat. Meat that freezes, dies, and rots. You gotta be comfortable with that if you want to survive this job. There's something, but it's not that nice. Something indifferent."
Quinn’s lips twisted into a sneer, his tone sharp as the ice that surrounded them. "All of you... you’re looking for meaning in all the wrong places. You’re not going back to the stars, Oren. And Graves, your ghosts? They’re just reflections of how fucked up this job makes us. We need to get out of here. If there's anything, the answer is not here."
Cillus, who had been quietly listening, finally spoke again, his voice smooth, almost detached. "I think the real problem is that none of us want to admit we don’t know. You’re all so eager to cling to something—religion, ghosts, nihilism—because the unknown terrifies you. But that’s life, isn’t it? Not knowing. That’s what makes us human. We fight, we die, we rot—or maybe we go on. Maybe we don’t. But pretending you have the answers? That’s the real bullshit."
Quinn turned sharply toward Vance, his irritation flaring again. "Oh, you’ve got it all figured out, huh? You think you're so much smarter than the rest of us because you admit you don’t know? What kind of philosophy is that? It’s weak."
Vance shrugged, unbothered by Quinn’s rising anger. "It’s not about being smart, Quinn. It’s about being honest. I don’t need answers to keep moving. The only thing that matters is the present—the here and now. You do what you can with the time you have, and then... whatever comes next, comes next. Back home, in the Royal states there's… these stories we tell kids about gods, about heroes, but it's just to make them behave, and to not be afraid.
"That’s a cop-out," Quinn shot back, frustration evident in his voice. "You don’t want to face the reality that there’s no purpose. No meaning. No grand design. You’d rather sit back and pretend you’re okay with the uncertainty. But deep down, you’re just as scared as the rest of us."
Graves shook his head, his gaze hardening as he stared at Quinn. "You think fear is weakness, don’t you? It’s fear that keeps you alive. Fear of dying, fear of failing—it’s what pushes you forward, what makes you fight"
Quinn’s jaw tightened, his breath quickening as the wind howled around them, whipping the snow into a frenzy. "I’m not afraid of dying," he muttered, more to himself than to the others. "I’m afraid of being forgotten. Of everything I’ve done, everything I’ve fought for, meaning nothing."
Oren, his voice quiet and distant, broke the silence, his words laced with a quiet, desperate hope. "Maybe meaning isn't something we find. Maybe it’s something we make for ourselves. It’s not about what comes after, or what anyone else thinks. Maybe it’s about what we hold onto, what we choose to believe... in moments like this."
Cillus gave a small nod, his voice steady. "And maybe that’s enough."
Quinn ignored the biting wind, fixing his gaze on Graves, his voice sharp as the cold cutting through their gear. “You think this is all it comes down to? Just survival? That’s the best you’ve got?”
Graves remained unphased, jaw clenched, an expression worn by too many confrontations. “ “Survival’s all that matters when you’re staring down a barrel, Quinn. All that philosophical crap you spew means jack when you’re on your knees, begging for your last breath. I’ve seen it. Hell, I’ve been there. And trust me, no one’s thinking about what comes after when their blood spilling into the dirt,”
“Maybe not you,” Quinn shot back, frustration igniting in his chest. “But some of us need more than that. You can’t just boil everything down to ‘survive or die.’, and I'm sick of hearing it. 
What’s the point of accepting life's bullshit if there’s nothing after this? No reason we’re here, no purpose? I'm not going to spend my life doing what other people tell me to do if this the only chance I get”
Graves leaned forward, narrowing his eyes, the intensity of his stare cutting through the swirling snow. “Bold words coming from an accountant. What’s yours then, huh? Running numbers for some corporation that you think doesn’t give a shit about you? Filing reports that’ll end up in a database no one reads? That’s your grand ‘purpose’? That’s what keeps you warm at night?”
“At least I was doing something!” Quinn’s voice rose, matching the howl of the wind. “Something that made sense. I wasn’t out here freezing to death for no reason, or dying because I was 'unlucky',”
Graves snorted, the sound bitter as frost. “You weren’t doing shit, Quinn. You were sitting behind a desk, pretending you had control. Now you’re here, just like the rest of us, and all you want to do is run. At least I'm not wasting my breath to pretend to be anything else”
Vick, leaning against his rifle, let out a long sigh, the sound mingling with the wind. “God, enough of this. You two sound like an old married couple. We’re not solving life’s mysteries tonight. And we sure as hell aren’t doing it while our asses freeze off, I don't wanna wait to see anyone snap”
“And which one do you think snap first, Vick?” Quinn asked, half-sarcastic, half-serious, his eyes searching.
Vick gave a slow, mocking shrug, the hint of a smirk ghosting his lips. “Well, if we’re talking about who’s gonna snap, I’d put my money on the guy who keeps screaming about purpose. But hey, that’s just me.”
The silence stretched for hours, stretching into meaningless strands, thin and fragile, only to snap back with violent pulses as Quinn’s mind wandered, unraveling at the seams. Time had become elastic, losing all sense of rhythm, expanding and contracting until it was nothing but an empty expanse of cold. He could no longer tell if it had been minutes or hours since they last spoke, since the world around them had turned into a frozen tomb. His senses dulled, one by one, like candles snuffed out by an invisible hand. His hearing dimmed, the howl of the storm a faint whisper, barely more than a distant murmur. His skin, once alive with the sting of cold, had gone numb—dead to the touch.
His feeling of self, too, began to slip away. He felt like he was disappearing, becoming a ghost in the storm. His body, so stiff and unresponsive, no longer belonged to him. He couldn’t even shiver anymore; the cold had claimed even that small retaliation. The storm raged on, a relentless, roaring beast that drowned out everything. Snow whipped across his face, stinging his eyes until tears welled up—only to freeze solid against his skin, forming icy tracks down his cheeks.
Quinn felt like a skeleton. His joints creaked, stiff and aching with every tiny movement. His arms hung useless at his sides, heavy as lead, his fingers so numb he wasn’t sure they were even still there. His legs felt foreign, as though they belonged to someone else. Every part of him was shutting down, surrendering to the cold. The wind was merciless, biting into his flesh, turning him into something less than human, less than alive.
The men sat scattered around the encampment, huddling by whatever scrap of metal or container they could find, looking utterly defeated. Vick, Cillus, and Oren were huddled together, shoulder to shoulder, their bodies pressed tight like they were trying to merge into one, but it made no difference. They sat as still as statues, snow piling on their heads, on their shoulders, turning them into part of the landscape. They barely moved, save for the occasional stretch, as though even the act of breathing had become a burden.
Graves sat in a chair, facing the storm, his silhouette barely visible in the swirling white. For a moment, Quinn almost hoped he was frozen—gone, just another casualty of this endless nightmare. But the occasional tilt of Graves’ head, the slight shifting of his posture, proved otherwise. He was alive, if you could call it that. Alive, but as deadened as the rest of them.
And Quinn sat alone, apart from the others. He didn’t even have the strength to care anymore. The cold had hollowed him out, left him with nothing but the gnawing growl of his stomach—a hunger that didn’t matter anymore. He was starving, but food seemed like a distant concept, something that belonged to another world, another life. His eyelids grew heavier with each passing second, his mind teetering on the edge of oblivion.
Sleep beckoned him, seductive in its promise of escape. But in the back of his mind, a gnawing thought clawed at him: if he fell asleep now, he might never wake up. The cold would take him, would seep into his bones and steal the last remnants of warmth from his body.
Yet, as the minutes dragged on, the temptation to surrender became harder to resist. What was the point of waking up in this frozen hell? What was the point of any of this?
A muffled crash shattered the suffocating silence as one of the red marking lights toppled over in the wind, its bulb flickering before sinking into the snow. The men, listless and worn, barely stirred. They looked at the fallen light, then at each other—silent, exhausted, too broken to care whether it stayed down or not. The thought of getting up, of expending even the smallest bit of energy, hung in the air between them like a question no one dared ask aloud.
Vick was the first to move. He pushed himself up with a groan, the red glow of the remaining lights casting eerie reflections on his visor, the only sign of life in his otherwise mechanical movements. Kneeling beside the toppled lamp, he hesitated for a moment, his breath fogging up the glass. Then, slowly, desperately, his gloved hands reached out and gripped the bulb.
It was warm. Not hot, not enough to save them from the storm, but in this frozen wasteland, it was something. His hands pressed tighter against the fragile source of heat, his shoulders trembling not just from the cold but from a brief, fleeting moment of relief.
He caught the eyes of the others—Oren, Cillus, Graves, and Quinn—watching him from their makeshift shelters, their faces hidden behind visors, but he could sense their curiosity. “Oren, Cillus,” Vick muttered through chattering teeth, jerking his head toward the lamp. “Come here.”
They hesitated, then scrambled to their feet like men who had been starving and suddenly smelled food. They huddled around the small light, shuffling close together, shoulders touching, rubbing their hands together like beggars clinging to a single scrap of warmth. The light reflected on their visors, casting their faces in a soft, red glow.
Quinn and Graves stayed back for a moment, watching the others crowd around the pathetic source of heat, a flicker of hesitation passing between them. Finally, with a weary sigh, they, too, got up and began to trudge over. The cold still permeated at them, but the sight of the others—huddled together, soaking in the faint warmth—was too much to resist.
The trio of Cillus, Oren, and Vick shuffled closer, practically on top of the small lamp, as though it could offer salvation. The glow wasn’t a lot, but in that moment, it felt like a godsend.
“It ain't much,” Vick murmured, his voice soft but tinged with pleasure, “but holy shit, it’s better than nothing.”
Oren laughed, a hollow, shaky sound that was more out of desperation than humor. “I was almost tempted to fire my rifle just to grip the hot barrel. This... this is way better.”
“Tell me about it,” Cillus muttered, his gloved hands pressed so tight to the bulb he could feel the gentle pulse of warmth beneath the glass. 
“I think I can actually feel my fi-" Oren’s head snapped back as if pulled by an invisible string, his body collapsing with a sickening thud, lifeless in the snow. His slumped form lay still, the warmth of the red light already leaving his corpse as blood spread beneath him.
Cillus and Vick darted their heads toward Oren, eyes wide with disbelief, only for the realization to hit them too late. A deafening crack followed, splitting the icy air. The sharp echo rang out across the landscape as their visors were splattered with the blood of the young scientist.
“Sniper! Get down!” Graves’ voice cut through the chaos, ragged and frantic. “Get away from the light! Get down!”
The storm howled around them, swirling violently as if the cold itself had turned hostile. Cillus barely had time to react, pivoting to escape the glow of the marking light, but a bullet tore into his shoulder with a brutal, bone-crunching force. He was flung backward, his rifle slipping from his hands as he hit the ground with a painful thud, choking on the scream that rose in his throat.
Vick, breathless and panicked, lunged toward him, grabbing the back of Cillus’s collar. With a desperate grunt, he dragged him behind a nearby crate, barely out of the sniper’s line of fire. The snow beneath them was already stained red with the trail of blood leaking from Cillus’s shoulder, a deep, gaping wound that spilled crimson onto the white earth.
Cillus clutched at the wound, his gloved hands slick with blood as he pressed down, trying and failing to stanch the flow. “Fuck! Fuck!” he screamed, his voice breaking into ragged sobs of agony. The cold did nothing to numb the pain; it only seemed to heighten it, the freezing air gnawing at his exposed skin as he writhed in the snow.
Vick ducked low behind the crate, panting, his heart hammering so loud he could barely think straight. He glanced over at Cillus, helpless to stop the bleeding. “Stay still, man. Stay still,” Vick muttered through gritted teeth, though his own fear was palpable. His hands shook as he fumbled with his rifle, knowing it was useless against an enemy they couldn’t even see.
“Graves! What’s the plan, old man?” Vick shouted, his voice barely cutting through the storm.
“Plan?” Graves growled from behind his cover, his tone harsh and biting. “Stay in position, shoot anything you see that isn’t me.”
Quinn fumbled with his rifle, fingers trembling and nearly numb, struggling to chamber a round. His heart pounded so violently in his chest it felt like it was about to explode, each thud echoing in his eardrums. He stuttered, “Anyone… see anything?!”
Vick, adrenaline surging, risked a quick glance over the crate, just in time to yank his head back as a bullet ricocheted off the metal inches from his skull. “Nothing!” he barked, breathless, his nerves fraying.
“We gotta move!” Vick added, desperation rising in his voice. “We’re sitting ducks here!”
Graves didn’t even flinch. “If we move, we’re targets. We got nowhere to go!”
Cillus lay prone, a widening pool of blood staining the snow beneath him. His face contorted with pain, he forced himself to peek out, then immediately pulled back. “High ground,” he grunted through clenched teeth, pointing in the distance with a shaking hand. “The ledge, about a kilometer out, maybe less. The wind’s masking their fucking shots.”
Quinn felt an overwhelming urge to look, to do something, but his body wouldn’t respond. He was paralyzed, the fear clawing at him, suffocating him. His breaths came out shallow, frozen in the face of danger.
“Quinn! Vick!” Graves barked, his voice cutting through the haze in Quinn’s mind. “Lay down suppressive fire. Give me an angle on them!”
Vick didn’t hesitate. With a grunt, he mounted his rifle over the crate and let loose, firing blindly into the storm. The sharp crack of his bullets echoed into the void, but the enemy was still a ghost, unseen and untouchable. The sound of the shots tore through the howling wind, but it felt futile, a desperate scream into the abyss.
Quinn’s eyes locked onto the faint shapes in the distance—silhouettes moving in the blizzard’s haze. He blinked, unsure if his frozen mind was playing tricks. He squinted hard, wiping the frost from his visor, then saw it—no, them—three, four, maybe even a dozen more figures shifting in the snowstorm. His heart lurched.
“Stay down! They’re pushing in! It’s an ambush!” Quinn shouted, panic tightening his throat.
A bullet cracked past his head, whistling into the void. His rifle suddenly felt like lead in his hands, the weight of it pressing him into the ground. He barely had the strength to raise it.
Then they emerged. First as shadows, blending with the snowstorm, but then more defined, stepping into view like ghosts materializing from a nightmare. Raiders. Cloaked head-to-toe in white fabric, their figures were nearly indistinguishable from the blizzard. Their faces were hidden behind black, expressionless masks, the metallic eye holes reflecting the weak light like dull stars. Long rifles slung casually over their shoulders, they marched with a confidence that made Quinn’s blood run cold, their movements eerily calm, deliberate. They weren’t rushing. No, they moved like they already knew they had won. Like they were coming to claim what was already theirs.
"Raiders!" Graves bellowed, his voice cracking with fury, not fear. "Let's give ‘em a hell of a show!"
Vick and Cillus, still shaken, struggled to compose themselves. Cillus, his hand trembling, reached into his coat and pulled out a thin chain—a necklace. He pressed the small pendant between his fingers and muttered something under his breath, a desperate prayer to gods Quinn couldn’t even begin to name.
The ground trembled beneath their boots as the footsteps of the raiders grew louder, closer. The eerie silence of the frozen world was now punctuated by the slow, steady crunch of snow beneath advancing feet.
Vick’s breath fogged up his visor as he glanced at Cillus, his hand coming to rest on his shoulder, his touch heavy with the weight of what was to come. “Hopefully I’ll see you on the other side, man,” he muttered, his voice shaking with a grim acceptance.
Cillus gave a weak nod, unable to find words. His grip tightened around his rifle, though his eyes were fixed on the raiders drawing nearer, the prayer on his lips fading to silence. There was no time left for hope, no time for belief.
Vick charged forward, a blaze of defiance against the storm. He had always dreamed of going out like this, a hero in a battle that meant something—at least to him. “Cover me!” he shouted, his voice carrying over the wind as he sprinted without hesitation, gunfire erupting from his hands as he blasted at the advancing figures. They were forty yards away, but they were closing in, shadows that promised death.
Quinn watched in a daze, caught between admiration and horror as Vick threw himself into the fray. He dropped his rifle and pulled out his pistol, firing into the blizzard. But then—like a specter rising from the storm—a raider appeared, swift and silent. The flash of a bayonet gleamed as it lunged forward, piercing Vick’s torso. The raider lifted him effortlessly off the ground before discarding him like a piece of trash. Vick’s body crumpled into the snow, lifeless.
Quinn’s breath quickened, panic clawing at him. He fired at the shadows, bullets that might have struck someone—or perhaps just the wind. The sound of his gun clicking empty rang out like a death knell. He dropped it, desperate, scrambling for his sawed-off shotgun. The cold was seeping into his bones, but something else began to radiate from within him. He felt warm—no, hot—as if fire coursed through his veins.
He looked down. His fingers were slick, coated in blood that glistened off the muzzle flashes around him. His uniform was soaked, crimson pouring from a deep wound at his neck, warm and life-draining, like a faucet left running. His knee buckled, and he fell to a knee, gasping for breath.
Quinn turned his head, searching for cover, when suddenly he was face-to-face with a raider. The expressionless mask loomed inches from him, void of humanity. In that moment, time slowed, the world around him blurring into a hazy backdrop of dread.
With a surge of instinct, he raised his shotgun, but the raider was too quick. Its staff slammed against his barrel the moment he fired, the impact knocking him off balance and sending him sprawling back into the snow. 
The raider loomed above him, its silhouette blocking out the storm’s fury. It stabbed downward with its bayonet, plunging the blade into Quinn’s torso, a brutal and calculated movement, before turning its back, disappearing in the storm.
Quinn turned over, struggling to push himself up, but all he could muster the strength to lift his head. He watched in a detached haze as the thirsty ground eagerly swallowed the blood pooling beneath him, between ragged gasps releasing shudders of pain. His vision blurred, and he began to feel far away.  Blood pooled in a darkening circle around him, pulsing in sync with his weakening heartbeat. With each throb, a thin spray shot out, misting the snow in delicate arcs, as though the earth itself were drawing his life from him. The splatter stained the ground, leaving intricate red patterns that faded into the icy surface.
His blood, bright and red, looked almost beautiful contrasting against the pure white snow, shimmering under the cold light of the stars above. In this surreal moment, a strange feeling enveloped him, as if he were drifting away from the chaos, he felt calm.
He heard a muffled voice call out for him, maybe it was Graves, or his father, he wasn't sure, they sounded similar. Either way he'd be over in a second anyway, he was comfortable resting here for now. Being a few minutes late couldn't hurt before leaving for the academy anyways, and he didn't mind skipping breakfast now and then for some more time to sleep in, but he knew he didn't want to be late. Wasn't there something else he had to do? He was sure he could figure it out later. 
He rolled back over, slipping in and out of consciousness, in a daze. In between long blinks he recognized this planet, he'd been here before, although he wasn't sure why everyone was in such a rush running around, maybe it was just deja vu. 
Then he recognized it, more. He remembered he'd been shot. He wanted to muster something to say, he wasn't sure what, but the blood in his throat barely choked out a gurgle. The weight of his head gave out on him, and he sank back in the snow. 

As he faded away, the only thing he could imagine was the deafening hum of fluorescent lights. 
Then, there was nothing but dark.

Chapter 7 (unfinished) (needs a lot of rewrites)

Jimzon stood proudly on the table, his drunken grin widening beneath a crumpled brown napkin he had fashioned into a fake mustache. His friends were still catching their breath from laughter when he suddenly raised his hand, adopting a fake booming voice. “Attention!" he commanded, swaying a bit. “Captain Medarco speaking. As you know, there’s been way too much fun in our division. New rules, straight from the top are as follows: any enjoyment is strictly prohibited on this mothership! Under no circumstances are we to engage in any—and I mean any—shenanigan behavior!”
The table erupted into more laughter as Jimzon continued his performance, standing tall as their former drill sergeant. “Berji, Yake—the time you swapped out all the emergency exit signs with arrows pointing to the laundry chute? Some poor cadet actually slid down three decks before we found him, buried in a mountain of dirty socks! Drop and give me 40!”
Yake and Berji cackled as Jimzon shook his head dramatically. “No more... and I mean no more throwing microwaves.." Jimzon couldn't keep a straight face and tried to hold in a chuckle, "no more throwing microwaves out the window when you lose at cards! The last one was still plugged in! You nearly electrocuted two engineers down on level three. They’re still pissed! Say every time it rains, they see sparks!”
Pavlock burst out laughing, smacking the table. “Hey, not my fault! Jax was cheating!”
“Cheating?” Jimzon gasped dramatically, eyes widening as he turned to Jax. “You and your sneaky card hands Jax... I’ve got you on the Captain’s permanent watch list for unlawful shenanigans. I’m sure those five rounds of poker you won were just a coincidence, huh? Right?”
Jax, hands raised in mock innocence, grinned wide. “You can’t prove anything, Jim.”
Jimzon wagged a finger at him, then turned his attention back to Pavlock. “And Pavlock, no more swapping out the gravity controls in the gym. I still can’t look at a weight bench without flinching.”
Pavlock shrugged, holding back another laugh. “I was just increasing the challenge.”
The whole table roared, their laughter echoing across the restaurant balcony as they fell into nostalgic memories of all their crazy antics. Jimzon finally hopped off the table, still chuckling, and grabbed his drink.
Castle Team lounged on the open-air patio of the mothership, each of them dressed down in casual attire. Drinks were in hand as they reclined in their chairs, their plates scattered with only crumbs and smears of sauce, remnants of a meal enjoyed. 
“Now that I hear it all out loud, maybe we are a bit too crazy,” he said, wiping a tear of laughter from his eye.
“It’s not that we’re crazy,” Berji added with a grin, “it’s just that, looking around this mothership, we’ve got a memory in every corner. We’re just the only ones daring to live.”
Jimzon, still smiling, shook his head. “Yeah, well, it's weird cause I can see myself being a captain... but I’d never be like Captain Dickhead.”
“That’s ‘cause you’re not trying to sleep with a director’s wife and get demoted,” Yake shot back, and the table exploded into laughter again, their camaraderie filling the air.
Jimzon grinned mischievously. "Who's next?" he asked, gesturing to the group, still riding the high of his over-the-top performance.
Berji chuckled, raising his hands in mock surrender. "I don’t think any of us can top that one." The laughter finally settled, leaving behind a warmth that lingered in the air. They all took a moment to sip their drinks, still grinning from the fun.
Jimzon stretched exaggeratedly, loosening up his shoulders with a theatrical yawn before strolling over to the railing of the patio. He stared out at the massive green planet below, its landscapes of lush mountains and sparkling lakes stretching out endlessly beneath the fluffy white clouds.
“It’s beautiful, isn’t it?” Berji said, joining him at the railing. “Wish we could dock here more often. Even the air up here feels fresher.”
Pavlock leaned forward, squinting at the view. "What planet is this again?"
"Creta," Jimzon replied, nonchalantly popping the cap off his beer on the metal railing. He stared at the bottle for a moment before downing it in one long swig, then casually tossed it over the ledge, watching it fall until it became a tiny dot and finally disappeared.
“Hey, bro, maybe you should slow down,” Jax suggested, glancing over at him. “We gotta be in tip-top shape for the mission in two days. I wanna make sure you're ready."
Jimzon’s grin faded, replaced by a slow, almost too-serious look. “I’ve been ready,” he said, his voice steady but carrying a weight his friends couldn’t ignore.
Castle Team exchanged glances, trying to ease the tension with forced smiles, but the unspoken reminder of the mission loomed over them like a shadow.
Berji cleared his throat, breaking the silence. “Oh, Jimzon, I meant to tell you—I’ve been fooling around with those processors. We should be able to remotely hack just about anything on their base.”
Jimzon’s cocky smile returned, a flicker of his usual bravado. "Good," he muttered. He grabbed another bottle, popped it open, and took a slow sip, his eyes still fixed on the view below.
Yake leaned in a bit, his tone shifting. "You hanging in there, man?"
Jimzon gave a short nod. “Yeah.”
Yake hesitated, then pressed on. "Hey, uh, sorry about bringing up Kelsa the other night. I don’t know what I was thinking. I know breakups can be rough. I just... I just wanna say, if you ever need to talk, we’re here for you, man."
Jimzon gave another polite nod, his expression guarded. “Thanks, man. Not much to say.”
Pavlock chimed in, attempting to ease the tension. "Oh, actually, I ran into her the other day. She said she could give me your stuff back whenever’s good for you."
Jimzon’s jaw tightened. “Already got it,” he muttered, cutting him off. “Anyway, c’mon, it’s someone’s turn for charades. Let’s not waste the night.”
The tension hung in the air for a second before Berji glanced at him cautiously. "You saw her? Are you guys... good?"
Something snapped in Jimzon. His face darkened, and his voice cut.“Everyone just shut the fuck up. No, we’re not good. We’re never gonna be good. And it doesn’t fucking matter. Just... stop talking about it and enjoy the time we have.” he said somewhat frantically. 
His voice wavered, and the raw edge of it hung in the air like a wound exposed. The group fell silent.
“Sorry, man, I didn’t mean to br—”
Before Berji could finish, Jimzon hurled his bottle off the edge with a tight, almost strangled “Fuck!” The bottle shattered somewhere below, out of sight. He leaned heavily on the railing, resting his elbows, his hands sliding up to cover his face as he let out a deep, shuddering sigh.
“Look, guys, I love y’all,” Jimzon muttered, his voice thick, “but I fucking hate getting ganged up on like this. I don’t know why you gotta keep bringing it up.”
“We don’t mean to, man,” Berji said softly, resting a hand on Jimzon’s shoulder. “It’s just ‘cause we care. We’re family.”
Jimzon let out a shuddered exhale upon hearing the word "family", his face still buried in his palms. His eyes reddened, glistening with unshed tears. After a long, drawn-out inhale, he slowly calmed himself, though the weight of everything was still there, sitting heavy in his chest. He stood up facing them but kept his eyes on the floor.
“I just... I didn’t see it coming, y’know?” Jimzon’s voice cracked, quieter now. “I knew something was off or whatever, but not like that. Just... something else.”
“You got forever, man,” Yake added, his voice gentle, reassuring.
Jimzon’s face looked pale under the patio lights, drained and sad. He blinked, trying to push the emotions back but failing. “A little while ago... we thought we were gonna have a kid together,” he said, pausing to let the words settle. His friends exchanged glances, unsure how to respond, but they waited, letting him speak at his own pace. “And... I was scared at first. Terrified. But after I went to bed and woke up in the morning, I don’t know... I just had this feeling, that it all felt right, y’know?”
His gaze drifted to the distant planet below them, the green expanses of Creta almost too perfect to look at. “I knew I wasn’t ready, exactly. But I figured I could be when the time came. It’s why I tried so hard to make captain—it felt like the right path. Like everything was falling into place. I’d have her, she’d have me.”
The silence around the table was heavy as Jimzon turned his head back toward the others. “We talked about moving here, actually. Creta. My mom used to tell me stories about this place... and Kelsa had family here. It’s like it was all meant to happen. That everything I've done and been through had a fucking purpose”
Jimzon paused, his chest rising and falling more rapidly now as he fought to keep his voice steady. “But now... it’s like I’m alone again. No wife, no kid... It’s like I lost my family all over again.” Tears finally broke free, streaming down his face, but his voice remained unbroken, as if he refused to acknowledge the weight of them.
Jimzon’s voice cracked with exhaustion. “I’m just fucking tired of this bullshit, man. I hate how everything in my life is just rooted in this awful fucking thing I can’t stop thinking about. It’s like... every day, I wonder how different my life would be, how different I would be. I don’t even know who I am anymore. Am I just a soldier? Is that all I’ve got? I’ve got nobody waiting for me when this tenure ends—do I just keep doing this forever? I mean, I like being part of the Guard, and I’m good at it, but... is that all I am?”
The silence that followed was heavy, and Pavlock stepped in, his voice soft. “I’m sorry, man. We had no idea it was hitting you this deep. But you’re strong for going through all that and still showing up every day. I know it’s hard to see all the good things you’ve done when you’re stuck focusing on the bad, but you’ve saved my life more times than I can count. After our tenure? It’s not the end of Castle Team. Like Berji said, we’re family. We mean that. It’s okay not to know who you are yet. I don't think any of us know. But I do know spending time together feels right, and so it must be. We can all figure it out. Together.”
Pavlock gave Jimzon a quick hug, and for a moment, Jimzon allowed himself to be pulled into it. He swallowed back the tears, his voice deepening as he steadied himself. “Thanks, man.”
But the calm didn’t last. "On that raid last week, when I saw that Nhaths face, its just been burned in my mind and ever since I've been on edge. Every second since, it's just like this intrusive, always repeating thought I can't shut off. Just the thought of 'my familys dead', 'my familys dead' cycling again and again.” His hands balled into fists, trembling as his anger resurfaced. “I just can't stop thinking about seeing them again. I just fucking hate them, man. I wanna to kill him. I want to seriously make this crag suffer. Nothing—nothing—is going to be enough.”
His voice began to waver, but he pressed on, the rage pouring out, he continued. "I want him to know it’s me, I want him to look me in the eyes. I want him to see it’s me." He exhaled sharply, his breath catching, as though the air itself had turned heavy with his fury."I want him to be afraid. Just like I was. I’ve thought about how I'm gonna do it too. Shoot him, stab em, burn em, nah. I’m gonna use my bare fucking hands. Break his fucking jaw, tackle him to the ground, have my fucking knees on his shoulders so he’s pinned down and just fucking deck him again and again and again. I wanna hear him cough up the blood he’s drowning in while begging for his fucking life until his cries are just these shitty little gurgles, I wanna feel my knuckles get cut on his teeth while i knock them out, and just-" Jimzon’s voice faltered as his breath hitched, the rage crumbling into something rawer. "I just fucking hate him. Fuck.”
Jimzon's fist slammed into the wall beside him with a dull thud, the impact reverberating through the room. He sank into the nearest chair, his body folding inward as he covered his face with trembling hands. “I miss my mom. My dad. My brother.” His voice cracked, barely audible beneath the weight of his anguish. “I don’t even know if they can hear me up there.”
His shoulders shook as he continued, his words choking out between shallow breaths. “I don’t know if they’re proud of me. I mean... all I do is kill things and get shitfaced.” His hands slid down his face, revealing eyes red with exhaustion and grief. “I’m not proud of me.”
Jax shifted in his seat, glancing at the others before speaking softly. "Jimzon, I can't pretend to know how hard this is on you, but we're gonna be with you every step of the way. Not because we feel pity, but because you're our brother. We've got nothing but love for you dude." Jimzon didn’t lift his head, but Jax knew he was listening. "Since day one, you've been there, even when you didn't have to. You've seen me at my lowest, and you never hesitated to help. In fact, sometimes you insisted. And things got better, even when I didn't think they could. I look up to you man, not just because you stick your neck out, but because you've shown me, you've shown us, that no matter the shit you've been through, no matter the past, you can always make so much good. You have a good heart man" 
Jimzon’s shoulders slumped slightly, as if some of the weight had lifted, though he still didn’t respond. The room remained quiet, but now it felt like the silence was full of understanding, not the cold isolation that had been pressing in moments ago.
Pavlock stood next, clearing his throat. "You know, Jax is right." He said, voice a little shaky but gaining confidence. "My grandfather gave me some advice that still sticks with me. When I was just a kid, he asked me to think of what I wanted to be when I grew up. I said I wanted to mech pilot, but he stopped me and said, ‘No, not what job, what kind of person?’. So I thought about it. I decided I wanted to be brave, resilient, disciplined and kind, all those things. He then asked me what I thought it would take to get those traits, to be that person I wanted to be. He told me the only answer, is to overcome tremendous hardship. Trying everyday, never stopping is the only way to be that best version of yourself. That's what you're doing now, in fact you've done it everyday. I know finding yourself is hard, but I know your parents would be proud. I'm proud of you, and of everyone in this room."
"You know Pav's got a point." Yake said, sitting up. "Years ago, when I first joined the infantry, boot camp scared the hell out of me. I felt like a fish out of water, every single day, especially when it was time for airborne drills. The thought of jumping out of a ship? Terrified me." He chuckled, though there was no humor behind it. "The day came, they handed me a chute and the hatch opened, and I could feel the air dragging me toward it. I started second-guessing every choice I ever made in my life. I almost walked away from it all. I almost quit" Yake looked at Jimzon, his eyes steady. "But then something hit me—‘just do it scared.’ Fear didn’t have to stop me. I could be scared and still jump."
Jimzon’s eyes flicked toward Yake now, the words reaching him on some level. Yake met his gaze and continued, "So I did. I was shaking like a leaf, but the second the wind caught my parachute, I knew I was going to be okay. Fear didn’t win. It's okay to not be okay. Life can be scary, but that doesn't mean it's over."
Berji stood now, his expression soft but determined. "You know, Jimzon," he said, walking toward his friend, "you’ve been a great friend. A great friend to everyone, except to yourself dude." Berji placed a hand on Jimzon’s shoulder. "I don’t think I’ve ever heard you give yourself the credit you deserve. I get it—you’re humble, but man, start treating yourself like you treat us. You can’t hate yourself into being better. Only love can do that. You gotta love what you have, and love what you do. Right now, you’re buried under so much stress, I don’t think you’re seeing it clearly. You’re a good guy, Jimzon. I know you said you can’t imagine your life without the Guard, but maybe you should try. Theres an entire galaxy out there."
Berji squeezed his shoulder. "If you’re about to be a captain but still don’t feel like you're in the right spot, maybe it’s time to step back and take a look around. You’ve lived and breathed being a soldier pretty much your whole life, but there’s more out there. On my home planet Monver, we have this saying, ‘A spaceship docked in the harbor is safe, but its place is among the stars.’ That ship is you, man."
The words hit Jimzon harder than he expected. For the first time in years, he felt truly seen. Not just as a soldier or a drinking buddy, but as himself. That advice was like a punch to the gut, but not the kind that left you breathless with pain—this was something else entirely. Something that loosened the knots he'd kept buried so deep.
His face was still streaked with tears, the tracks glistening under the dim light, but there was something different in his eyes now. Not the usual torment, not the dark haze that had been consuming him, but something softer, warmer—a flicker of hope trying to claw its way through the shadows. He blinked, trying to understand what he was feeling, as though the concept of being cared for without condition was foreign to him. Maybe it was. Maybe he had forgotten what it was like to be loved, not for what he could do, but for who he was, broken pieces and all.
"When did you all turn into philosophers?" he muttered, voice fragile but laced with a weak smile. He wiped at his face, a shaky laugh escaping. "I fucking love you guys."
There was a pause, and then, without hesitation, his friends closed in, pulling him into a tight huddle. Their arms around him were warm, holding him together where he felt like he might shatter. It didn’t take away the pain, but it reminded him he wasn’t alone.
"We’ve got your back on this mission," Jax whispered, voice thick with emotion. "No question".
For the first time in a long while, he allowed himself to believe it might be okay.
			
Alas, it wouldn't.

Chapter 8 
A bloodcurdling scream ripped across the air of the medbay, sharp and haunting. Though it was striking, it wasn’t unfamiliar, but this one carried a particular intensity. A patient bolted upright in his bed, his head swathed in layers of gauze, covering his eyes like a blindfold. His scream continued, rising in panic as he clawed frantically at his face, fingers fumbling over the bandages, desperately searching for eyes he could no longer feel.
His movements were wild, erratic, sending the thin tubes running from his arms and nose into a tangle. The machines beside him beeped frantically, matching the chaos of his gasping breaths. His voice, strained and broken, cracked between bouts of hyperventilation as he cried out for help repeatedly, each word more desperate than the last.
"Nurses! Restrain this one!" a doctor barked from across the room, his tone clipped, businesslike, as though the scene unfolding before him was routine. Two nurses, practiced and swift, moved in to pin the man's arms down, pushing him back against the thin mattress.
“No! No! No!” the man screamed, his body thrashing in vain, muscles straining under the weight of their hold. His voice was raw, echoing with terror and the futility of his struggle.
"Some fight in this one," the doctor remarked dryly, barely sparing a glance at the writhing figure. "You, grab the gas." His voice was detached, almost bored, for he had seen this too many times to care.
Within moments, a respirator was pressed over the man's face, muffling his protests as his screams faded into a strained whimper. His body shuddered, then slowly fell limp, the sedative pulling him back into unconsciousness as the medbay settled into an eerie quiet hum once more.
The man lay motionless on the bed, his breath shallow and faint, barely detectable beneath the hum of machines. He could have easily been mistaken for a corpse, so still he was. The room held its breath with him, thick with the oppressive silence of waiting. Minutes passed, and then, a twitch—a small, involuntary movement in his fingers, as if something deep within him was stirring. His chest began to convulse, a sudden violent tremor, and before long, his body jolted upright again.
His bandaged head whipped from side to side, his breathing erratic as panic took hold. His hands went to his face once more, clawing in desperation, trying to peel away the suffocating darkness. The scene began to repeat itself, a horrifying loop of confusion and fear.
Suddenly, a warm hand gently grasped his forearm, halting his frantic movements.
The man froze, his entire body tensing as if bracing for a blow. His breath caught in his throat, his muscles coiled tight with fear.
"Quinn... Quinn, hey buddy, you're okay. You're safe. It's me."
The voice was calm, familiar. Quinn's panic paused, confusion clouding his mind as he strained to recognize it. Then it clicked—Cillus. But something was different. Cillus, whose voice was once on the verge of breaking from screams of agony, now spoke with a steady, almost soothing tone.
Quinn managed only a weak, shaky “Huh?” His voice barely above a whisper, tinged with fear. His heart still pounded, but the sound of Cillus's voice was like an anchor in a storm.
"You survived, Quinn. You're safe now, it's okay, man." Cillus spoke softly, his words slow and deliberate, as though he was speaking to a wounded animal. "Here, let's get those bandages off your eyes, yeah? You cool with that?"
Quinn hesitated, his body going still once more. He slowly lowered his head, a silent signal of assent. His muscles loosened, surrendering to the presence of someone he trusted. Cillus’s hands moved with care, fingers gently working to untie the tight knots that had held the bandages in place. Layer by layer, the pressure began to lift from Quinn’s skull, each unraveling section letting in slivers of light. A faint glow penetrated the white gauze, growing stronger with every turn.
Finally, the last strip of cloth was removed. Quinn squinted against the blinding light that flooded his vision, his eyes unaccustomed to the harsh brightness. Instinctively, he raised a trembling hand to shield them, his breath still ragged as he tried to steady himself. The world was too bright, too overwhelming, but the warmth of Cillus’s touch remained steady, a lifeline in the dizzying haze.
Quinn spoke through gritted teeth, his voice tight with pain. "Cillus... Cillus, get those lights off… get those lights off..."
Cillus leaned in, his hand still steady on Quinn’s arm. "You’re safe, man, I promise. Your eyes will adjust. Just give it time."
Quinn shook his head, his voice trembling now, as if fighting something deeper than the light. "No... no, not the brightness. It’s the sound, Cillus. That sound... that hum.. that hum. Turn it off, please. Please."
His voice cracked on the last word, desperation leaking through. Cillus furrowed his brow, confused for a moment, glancing around the room at the medbay’s quiet machines, their gentle mechanical hum usually unnoticed. 
"I can’t, Quinn. I would if I could, man, just give it some time," Cillus replied, his voice soft but firm. He watched Quinn’s eyes dart around the room, wild and searching.
"Where are we?" Quinn blurted out, his words quick and desperate.
"Back on the mothership. Far away from that moon," Cillus said, his voice steady, but carrying a weight of its own. "Home, I suppose."
Quinn’s face twisted as if the word cut deep. "Fuck… FUCK FUCK FUCK FUCK!" His heart monitor spiked, the frantic beeping of the machine matching the rising panic in his chest.
Cillus took a step closer, concern etched on his face. "What’s going on, dude?"
Quinn’s breath came in short, sharp gasps. His voice dropped to a hoarse whisper. "I’m in fucking hell. I died. So did you. And we’re stuck here... forever." His words, once frantic, now dripped with hopelessness, as if the reality had just sunk in. He stared blankly ahead, caught between fear and disbelief.
"No, no, Quinn, we’re not dead," Cillus said quickly, his voice calm but urgent. "I know it’s scary, but you’ve been in a coma for almost a week. It’s a miracle, actually. You’re going to be okay—maybe a scar or two, but hey, at least we’ll match."
Quinn, still squinting against the light, managed to focus on Cillus for the first time. He looked to be around twenty-four, with dark ginger hair that fell messily over his forehead and tanned skin that hinted at someone who had spent a lot of time under harsher suns. His green eyes, sharp yet softened by concern, stood out against the sterile blue of his medical scrubs.
Cillus reached up and tugged down the collar of his shirt, revealing a jagged, darkened scar that slashed across his shoulder, the bruising spreading like an ugly shadow across his chest.
"Pretty gnarly one, right?" Cillus said with a small, forced smile, trying to break the tension. "But those days are over. We’re out of that hell."
Quinn glanced down at his chest, where sterile medical patches covered the places he had been stabbed. A sprawling black bruise stretched across his torso onto his neck, its dark, swollen shape resembling an infection creeping beneath his skin. The sight sent a shiver through him, his body feeling foreign and broken.
As his eyes shifted to the medbay around him, disappointment gnawed at his gut. He had always imagined the infirmary as a sleek, high-tech space—clean, orderly, efficient. But what he saw was far from that vision. Row after row of injured men and women filled the room, their pain manifest in every corner. Some coughed or groaned, others cried or screamed, their voices blending into a sickening symphony of misery.
Though the room was spotless, the cleanliness couldn’t mask the deeper filth that lingered—the overwhelming sense of indifference. The apathy in the air weighed heavily on Quinn, more suffocating than the wounds he carried, making the space feel as lifeless as the people within it.
Quinn stared at the lights, his vision still hazy, the world feeling distant and unreal. He blinked rapidly, trying to push away the brightness, but his thoughts were already racing far beyond the medbay.
“Cillus," Quinn’s voice cracked slightly, rough from the sedatives, "look at me. We can’t stay here long. We gotta get the others and find some way out of this place.”
Cillus stiffened beside him. His frown deepened as he glanced down at Quinn, then back across the medbay. His silence felt heavy, stretching into an uncomfortable pause before he finally spoke. “There are no others. Just us.”
The words hung in the air like a sudden chill, cutting through the sterile hum of machines. Everything went quiet. The faint murmur of patients in the distance faded to a dull hum as Quinn’s heart seemed to stop.
Quinn’s lips parted, but no sound came out at first. His mind scrambled to make sense of what Cillus had just said, but it didn’t register—it couldn’t. “But Vick... Vick took the same blade as I did, right? I don’t understand. Graves is gone too?”
Cillus nodded slowly, his eyes dull, weighed down by something Quinn hadn’t noticed before. “He fought until his last bullet... and his last breath.”
Quinn’s stomach twisted. He stared blankly ahead, his chest tightening, each breath feeling like a weight pressing down on him. “Graves...” he whispered, the name barely more than a breath. His head shook in disbelief, rejecting the reality Cillus was painting for him. “What even happened? I... I can’t remember anything after I got stabbed.” His hand unconsciously went to his chest, where the wound still ached beneath layers of gauze.
Cillus hesitated, his mouth opening slightly, then closing. His brow furrowed as though wrestling with whether to say something or keep it buried. “I’m not sure it’s the best time to relive everything, Quinn…”
“Just tell me.” His hands gripped the edge of the bed, knuckles white, desperation clear in his eyes. “I need to know.”
Cillus exhaled slowly, his shoulders sagging under the weight of the memories. “After Graves went down, it wasn’t long... maybe just a couple of moments after you fell. I remember you, Quinn—you were still fighting, even after you were hit. But me?” He paused, his eyes drifting, as though searching for the right words. “I wish I could say I fought like you all did, but without my rifle, I was just useless. Just lying there, bleeding, praying. All I had left.”
Quinn stayed silent, his gaze fixed on Cillus, his eyebrows furrowed. 
Cillus swallowed hard, the memory clearly cutting deep. “One of the raiders—he came over to me, bayonet drawn, ready to finish me off. I saw him coming, and I knew I had nothing. I couldn’t move, couldn’t fight back. I just... I just closed my eyes, held onto my necklace, and prayed harder than I’ve ever prayed in my life. Thought that was it.” His voice faltered for a second before he pushed on.
“He got real close—so close I could hear his breathing over the gunfire. Then, something strange happened. He... he noticed the necklace. The one I was praying to. I don’t know why, but that stopped him.”
Cillus’s eyes flickered with disbelief, even now. “We just... stared at each other. For a moment, it was like everything else froze. I looked up at him, he looked at me, and there it was—he was wearing the same necklace. I don’t know what it meant to him, but something changed. His whole posture just relaxed. He lowered the bayonet.”
Quinn’s breath hitched slightly, caught between disbelief and something else—something he couldn’t quite name.
“Then,” Cillus continued, “he did something even stranger. He unhooked a canteen from his belt—small, battered—and tossed it at me. I didn’t know what to think, but I was so thirsty, I drank from it. The water was warm, tasted like metal, but I didn’t care. I drank until I couldn’t anymore.” Cillus's gaze shifted, as if seeing the scene play out again. “I thought maybe I was safe for a second. But then he looked around, at the other raiders nearby... and next thing I know, he knocked me out with the butt of his rifle.”
Cillus rubbed his temple absently, wincing at the memory. “When I woke up, I was here, in this medbay. That was… two days ago.”
Quinn sat there for a moment, turning Cillus’s words over in his mind. The idea seemed foreign to him, almost absurd. “They spared you... because you prayed?” His voice dripped with disbelief, as though saying it out loud made it even more impossible to comprehend.
Cillus gave a small, resigned shrug. “I suppose so.”
He shook his head, incredulous. “Thought I heard you say religion was just ‘fairy tales to keep kids in line’.
“That was last week,” Cillus replied, his voice steady but tinged with weariness. “But you’ve seen how fast things can change. We were blessed with a second chance, Quinn.”
“Blessed?” Quinn repeated, the word feeling strange on his tongue. His gaze drifted down to his bandaged, broken body. He flexed his fingers, feeling the ache in his bones. “I don’t feel blessed.”
“Aster works in mysterious ways,” he said softly, the tone of his voice suggesting he believed it more now than he ever had before. Cillus looked at him with something like sympathy in his eyes, though it was hard to tell. 
“Aster...” Quinn echoed, looking up from his injuries, suspicion creeping into his voice. “Aster’s your god, I presume?”
Cillus gave a small nod. “Well, goddess, actually. But essentially, yes.”
Quinn’s gaze hardened, his bitterness simmering beneath the surface. He muttered under his breath, almost as if speaking to himself. “Ask her why she spared me.”
Cillus’s expression didn’t waver. He spoke with quiet conviction, but there was a softness to his voice, a kind of patience. “Ask her yourself, Quinn. Just pray.”
Quinn scoffed, the sound sharp and cold. A smug smile tugged at his lips, though it didn’t reach his eyes. “I think I’ll pass on that. Maybe I’d worship a god who could’ve saved all of us, not just some.”
Cillus didn’t flinch, but something in his expression darkened, a flash of hurt or perhaps frustration. He leaned forward slightly, his voice quieter now, more insistent. “There’s some plan to all this, Quinn. There has to be. You don’t survive something like that without a reason. Us making it through, it wasn’t just dumb luck.”
Quinn’s expression hardened as he stared up at the ceiling, the bitterness in his voice now laced with quiet determination. “Well, how about a plan we can see,” he muttered, lowering his voice as though afraid someone might overhear. “Once we’re healed, we get out of Guard Territory. For good. We never come back.”
Cillus blinked, caught off guard by the sudden shift in tone. “Leave?” he repeated, almost incredulously. “We’re both nearly done with our duty, Quinn. We can just ride it out for the last few months. Then it’s over. You could even come with me to Shayron if you wanted.” His voice carried a touch of optimism, as if trying to convince himself just as much as Quinn. 
“Your royalist 'virtues 'are showing through, Cillus. You’re still clinging to the idea that there’s an ‘end’ to this.” He paused, his eyes finally meeting Cillus’s. There was something fierce, almost desperate, in them. “But there isn’t. Not with the Guard. There’s no ‘almost done’—there’s just done when they say so. They’ll find a way to rope us back in. Some bullshit reason. Another contract, another raid, another war, another enemy. They always do.”
Cillus stared at Quinn, confusion flickering across his face. “That can’t possibly be true,” he said, his voice wavering slightly, as if hoping to dismiss the harsh reality.
Quinn met his gaze, his expression steady, calm but fierce. “You and I are living proof of it,” he replied, the weight of his words hanging heavily in the air.
Cillus shifted uncomfortably, his brow furrowing. “Quinn, I think we should ask the doctor to lower the painkillers… You’re starting to sound a bit—”
“Think about it.” Quinn interrupted, his voice firm, cutting through Cillus’s unease. He leaned forward slightly, his intensity sharpening as he laid out his thoughts with a precision that demanded attention. “The five of us were sent on a suicide mission. None of us were soldiers. We were just numbers—expendable. All of us were almost out, close to the end of our tenure. Call it budget cuts or whatever, but we gave more than our worth to the Guard. And instead of repaying us for our service, it would be cheaper for them to let us die than to pay our pensions.”
He paused, letting the weight of his words settle between them. Cillus opened his mouth to respond, but Quinn continued, undeterred. “Think about those contracts we signed. Ninety-five percent of the payment after your tenure? Oren, Vick, Graves—they all died for pennies. Pennies.”
Quinn’s gaze hardened, his voice low but intense as he spoke, the fire of his conviction burning bright. “There are always levels to this sort of thing. Who knows what we were really protecting? Certainly not us. But someone did. Someone knew we’d die out there. They banked on it.” He clenched his fists, the tension in his body radiating through the air. “Who knows how many others like us have died in those same patched uniforms?”
Cillus listened, his unease growing, but he couldn’t look away. Quinn’s passion was palpable, igniting a spark of awareness in the pit of his stomach.
“Whatever was in that hangar—if there even was anything worth protecting—whatever those raiders took, the Guard now has an excuse to go in with brute force.” Quinn’s voice dropped to a fierce whisper, each word laced with frustration. “They martyred us for cents on the dollar, Cillus. That’s the reality we’re facing. We’re not heroes; we’re just collateral".
Cillus was slowly fitting those puzzle pieces together, and Quinn could see the tension in his posture as it shifted. The confusion morphed into something heavier, something darker. He glanced around the room, his eyes scanning the sterile white walls as if they were now foreign territory. “No… no,” he muttered, his voice trembling slightly as he tried to convince himself of a reality that felt increasingly fragile. “Hang on, I’m not even directly part of the Guard. If the Guard and the Royal Territories are allies, why would they send me to die? That doesn’t make sense. They know I’m part of the royal family; that could ruin so much of their support.”
Quinn raised an eyebrow, caught off guard by the revelation. “Did you just say royal family? Cillus, what? I thought you were a journalist.”
Cillus shifted uncomfortably, rubbing the back of his neck as he took a deep breath. “Well, I am that too, yeah.” His voice faltered slightly, revealing the conflict within him. “Um… Growing up I always wanted to see the world beyond the walls of the castle. When I finally did, it was a lot worse than I could have imagined. People back home don’t really know what’s going on across the galaxy, and they seem fine with that. But that just didn’t sit right with me.”
Quinn observed as Cillus’s green eyes flickered with a mix of determination and regret. “I figured if anyone would show them, maybe they’d want to help, maybe they’d even care. Since I don’t have or want any claim to the throne, to boost relations, the Guard and my family agreed to let me do some journalism with a few squads.” He paused, the words tumbling out as if they had been trapped inside him for too long. “The stories are always out on the field, so I didn’t want my identity to get in the way of that.”
Quinn’s brow furrowed in surprise at Cillus’s revelation. He had always viewed him as just another nobody, a fellow soldier lost in the chaos, but now that perception shifted. For a fleeting moment, jealousy flickered within him—a feeling quickly replaced by admiration. There was something noble about Cillus's determination to seek the truth, even if it put him at risk.
“I know the truth matters to you,” Quinn said, his voice steady, cutting through the tension. “This is more than a story. It’s real. People are dying. People need to know.” Cillus looked up at him, and Quinn could see the conflict in his eyes, as if the weight of those words pierced him deeply. 
“Maybe you found out things you shouldn’t have,” Quinn pressed on, urgency creeping into his tone. “And that’s why they decided it. Did you film a warzone? Some massacre they wanted to cover up to save face?”
Cillus shook his head, the distress on his face deepening. “No, last week was the only time I’ve seen anything close to it. I’ve seen the aftermaths— maybe a body or two, wreckage, but nothing serious. Just snippets, pieces of a bigger story.”
“What were you covering anyway?” Quinn asked, curious despite the gravity of the situation.
Cillus hesitated, his gaze falling to the floor as he searched for the right words. “Well, just general camaraderie to boost morale. Soldiers being happy, showcasing the aftermaths of successful missions, capturing good memories,” he explained, but his voice trailed off as realization washed over him. “Like I was stationed with Squad 412, and I filmed them celebrating with the loot they…” His voice faltered, horror dawning on his face. “The looting… oh god…oh god. That’s what it was. The looting. They were killing me to avoid some fucking PR disaster…”
Quinn watched Cillus wrestle with the implications of his previous work, feeling the heaviness of the moment settle between them. The laughter, the camaraderie that had once seemed so innocent now took on a sinister edge.
Quinn gritted his teeth, determination blazing in his eyes. “We’ve been through hell and back. You said yourself we have a second chance. We cannot let them kill us again—or anyone else. We can come up with a plan to get out of this place.”
Cillus was still struggling with denial, his brow furrowed in uncertainty. “Are you sure? We hardly have enough evidence or proof. Maybe we need to think this through.” Doubt laced his voice, and Quinn could see the conflict written all over his friend’s face.
Quinn's stoic expression hardened, his resolve sharpening. He reached over and tapped the yellow button on the side of his hospital bed, summoning a doctor. The sound of the button echoed in the sterile room, a small yet firm declaration that he was done waiting.
Cillus looked at him, puzzled by his sudden shift in demeanor. “What are you doing?” he asked, a hint of concern creeping into his voice.
A few moments later, a doctor stepped into the room, his clipboard in hand, his expression neutral. “You needed something… Mr. Pastoral?” He glanced down at the paperwork attached to Quinn’s hospital bed, the mechanical hum of the medbay filling the silence.
Quinn looked up, purposefully adopting a demeanor that hinted at vulnerability. “Yes, doctor. I was just wondering—once I’m healed up, when would I return to my accounting position?”
The doctor furrowed his brow, his eyes scanning the file with disinterest. “Hard to say. Your file here indicates you haven’t completed enough hours in the field or in accounting to be discharged. But don’t worry, you’ll be back to killing Crags in no time.” His tone was almost dismissive, as if Quinn’s fate was nothing more than a footnote in a long list of medical reports.
“How many hours would I need?” Quinn pressed, a subtle intensity creeping into his voice.
The doctor shifted uncomfortably, the clinical mask slipping slightly. “Well, given that you’ve been medevaced and in a coma for quite some time, I’d say you owe the Guard your life. They seem to think so too. If you can’t pay the medical fees, another year or two of service should cover it.”
Quinn’s facade of weakness fell away, his expression morphing into one of cold clarity. “Thanks,” he replied dryly, watching the doctor turn and leave, the sound of his footsteps fading into the background.

Quinn's voice was steady, resonating with conviction. “This isn’t just about us anymore. We owe it to them—Oren, Vick, Graves, and whoever else came before us.” 
Cillus met his gaze, the wheels in his mind slowly turning. “You really think we can do this?” he asked, his voice barely above a whisper.
Quinn nodded firmly. “I know we can. Together.”

The duo knew what they had to do. 
Chapter 9:
Jimzon stood rigid, his face a mask of cold determination, jaw locked tight, his entire body unmoving like a carved statue in the dim, flickering light of the ship’s interior. His armor, a matte black that seemed to drink in the surrounding shadows, gave off no shine, making him appear like a specter carved from darkness itself. His eyes, though, were alive—darting, scanning the turbulent clouds streaking past the window, every flash of orange reflected in their depths like the embers of a distant, smoldering blaze. The ship bucked occasionally under the strain of minor turbulence, but not a muscle in Jimzon’s body shifted. He remained rooted to the spot, only his eyes betraying the storm that churned inside.
The pale glow from the passing clouds cast eerie light on his face, momentarily painting him in fiery hues. No one spoke. The silence was oppressive, broken only by the faint hum of the plasma engines and the soft, rhythmic patter of rain splattering on the hull. It was quiet before the storm. And Jimzon stood at its eye.
His gaze flickered away from the window, settling on the men around him. Berji, Pavlock, Yakib—his brothers in arms—gripped the cargo straps, bracing themselves against the ship’s shuddering. They held no words for him, just a mutual understanding reflected in their eyes. There was something unspoken in their gaze—loyalty, yes, but also pity, a kind of unnameable sorrow for the path their friend had chosen. 
Jimzon returned their gaze with dead, hollow eyes, the weight of his vendetta pulling any warmth from his stare. After a moment, he nodded to them—no words necessary. They understood. One by one, they activated their helmets, the visors flickering to life with the cold, sterile glow of combat mode. His flickered blue. Berji’s glowed green. Pav’s teal. Yake’s a faint yellow. Each nod returned to Jimzon was a silent vow of shared purpose. There would be no turning back.
He drew his pistol with a deliberate slowness, the quiet snick of the weapon leaving its holster sharp in the silence. Its sleek, polished surface gleamed with an ominous elegance. He grasped the weapon with a mixture of resolve and trembling hatred, his gauntlets black against the sleek metal. His mother’s royal service pistol. The pistol was heavy in his hand, not with weight but with the burden of history, of loss, of vengeance. The round he chambered clicked with finality. There would be no mercy. Not tonight.
His voice crackled over their comms, low, controlled, but tinged with the simmering fury that never left him. “No quarter given. Just like we planned. On me.”
With a swift, brutal motion, Jimzon slammed his fist into the red button on the wall, and the hangar door groaned open. The ship filled with the deafening roar of the storm outside, rain lashing into the bay in wild sheets. Wind howled through the open space, cold and biting, as if the very skies were raging against them. Jimzon strode forward to the edge of the ramp, the storm’s chaos swirling around him, a violent mirror of the tempest inside his heart.
He hit the chestplate of his armor, and with a hiss, his wingsuit deployed, sleek sheets of material stretching out between his limbs. The glow of his helmet visor cut through the gloom, casting him in sharp relief against the violent storm beyond. His pulse quickened, but his movements were calm—precise. Every motion was one of intent, every breath measured.
Jimzon turned one last time to face his squad, his family in arms, a grim salute in the form of a final, fleeting glance. And then, without hesitation, he let himself fall backward into the storm, arms spread wide, plummeting headfirst into the fury below.
The wind screamed past Jimzon’s helmet, the sharp whistle piercing his ears as he plummeted through the atmosphere. Rain streaked across his visor, droplets splattering and flying off in trails, the world around him a blur of dark clouds and flickering lightning. He could hear the distant rumble of thunder, muted by the howling wind, and for a brief moment, a flash of white lit the sky. But none of it mattered. His eyes closed, blocking out everything but the raw surge of adrenaline pumping through his veins. His breath quickened, each pulse sending waves of goosebumps crawling up his arms and neck. He was no longer just falling—he was hurtling towards his destiny.
His eyes snapped open, sharp and determined, and with a practiced motion, he spread his arms wide. The wind roared back at him, trying to shove him off course, but he cut through it like a knife, his wingsuit catching the current perfectly. He was in control.
As the copper clouds parted below him, the terrain unfolded—an unforgiving landscape of jagged black volcanic rock. Rivers of molten lava bubbled and churned, their fiery glow painting the land in an eerie red light. Plumes of ash floated like specters in the air, carried by the same winds that bore him. The ground seemed to pulse with life, alive with danger and heat, a place that mirrored the fury burning inside him.
