He angled his body downward, leaning into the dive as the volcanic surface rushed up to meet him. The air thickened with ash, and the heat from the magma below made the suit’s temperature regulators kick in, keeping the searing atmosphere at bay. He curved his descent, cutting through the ashen haze like a phantom, barely skimming the ground for a few kilometers. His boots hovered just above the scorched earth, lava bubbling mere feet beneath him as he flew.
At the last moment, he let himself descend fully, legs extending to make contact with the ground. The impact was harder than he’d planned—his feet slammed into the rocky surface, sending him into an unsteady slide. His balance faltered, and he dropped to one knee, skidding across the ash-laden ground until he came to a stop. His chest heaved as he paused, head down, catching his breath. The roar of the wind faded, and the sound of the crackling, molten world around him filled his ears.
Slowly, deliberately, he rose to his feet. The wingsuit hissed as he hit a panel on his chest, deactivating the material, which folded neatly back into his armor. His breath still ragged, Jimzon reached over his shoulder, fingers finding the familiar grip of his rifle. With a swift motion, he drew it, the weight in his hands grounding him, centering him. His gaze fixed on the desolate horizon, where the enemy awaited.
A sharp click sounded as he activated the rifle. In an instant, a plasma bayonet flared to life beneath the barrel, the blade crackling with bright blue energy, casting a cold glow across his armor. The hum of the weapon vibrated in his hands, a living thing ready to unleash hell.
Jimzon stood there for a moment longer, eyes narrowing as he surveyed the hellscape before him. His heart still pounded with adrenaline. The enemy was out there, entrenched, unaware of the storm that was about to descend upon them. He felt the weight of his purpose settle on his shoulders. His vengeance had arrived.
With a final breath, he took a step forward, his rifle raised, the glow of the bayonet cutting through the ash-choked air like a beacon of death.
The hunt had begun.
Jimzon tilted his head back, eyes tracing the sky as three silent silhouettes darted overhead, cutting through the haze like shadows. Camouflaged against the swirling ash, they were barely distinguishable—his men, gliding effortlessly in formation. The sight of them brought a fleeting sense of solidarity, a grim reminder that he wasn’t alone in this quest for revenge, even if his burden felt uniquely his own.
His gaze dropped to his gauntlet, fingers tapping swiftly against the interface. A faint hum accompanied the screen’s activation, lighting up with the topographic map of the area. The map blinked as Jimzon set the coordinates to regroup with his team. Jagged ridges and molten flows sprawled across the display, a deadly maze of volcanic rock and magma that would swallow anyone unprepared. 
Jimzon's footsteps crunched softly in the ash as he jogged, his pace swift but steady. The thick, acrid air clung to him like a second skin, every breath filling his lungs with the scent of sulfur and molten rock. Behind him, his footprints vanished almost immediately, the ever-moving ash swept over by the relentless winds as if nature itself sought to erase his presence. 
The sky above rumbled, faint tremors coursing through the ground beneath his boots. He reached the rally point, a jagged hill of black volcanic stone that blended seamlessly with his matte black suit. From a distance, he was nearly invisible, just another silhouette amidst the chaotic landscape of magma rivers and floating ash. He dropped to the ground, his body low against the earth, the cold, gritty texture of volcanic dirt pressing against his armor. The heat from the nearby lava flows radiated through the air, the ground beneath him warmer than it should have been, as if it were alive.
His fingers tapped his helmet, activating the binoculars. He scanned the horizon, zooming in past the haze of fog and smoke that hung over the landscape like a suffocating shroud. Slowly, the shapes of the Nhath base came into focus. At first, it was a blur, a mess of indistinct structures barely visible through the haze, but Jimzon’s eyes sharpened, recognizing the scene instantly, exactly like the file he had burned into his mind.
The base was exactly as it had been described in the file—a decrepit industrial mining outpost, its dilapidated walls reinforced with crude additions. Ramshackle storage areas for bikes and piles of stolen goods were thrown together with little care for order. It was a pit of chaos, a mockery of any military operation. Yet, the only feature he did not account for was the heaps of trash. Piles of discarded plastic, tin, and broken machinery littered the corners of the base, some of it drifting lazily in the breeze. The wind carried these scraps like ghosts, fluttering through the air before being consumed by the bubbling magma pools, vanishing with a hiss and a final puff of toxic smoke.
After a few tense minutes, his men began to rally at the designated point. One by one, they emerged from the ashen haze, their dark forms materializing like shadows summoned by the landscape itself. Each soldier dropped to a knee behind him, their rifles trained outward, covering the rear with a practiced precision that came from years of shared battles.
"Jax, you in position?" Jimzon’s voice cut through the crackling comms, low and controlled despite the anticipation simmering beneath the surface.
"Roger," Jax’s reply came, distorted slightly by static but clear enough to confirm readiness.
"Keep that ship warm," Jimzon muttered, his tone carrying a finality that brooked no argument. "We won't be here long. Out."
He turned to his team, his visor’s glow momentarily catching the faint light of the magma beyond. "Stay put," he commanded, his voice sharp and measured, each word a promise of calculated destruction. "I’ll tell you when to move."
With a flick of his wrist, he brought up the screen on his gauntlet, the faint blue glow illuminating his hand as he tapped through the interface. A moment later, his form began to shimmer, the contours of his armor dissolving into a rippling distortion. The holocloak activated with a faint hum, bending the light around him until he was nothing more than a ghost—a whisper of heat and movement, blending seamlessly into the volatile surroundings.
Only the faint imprint of his boots on the ashen ground betrayed his passage, vanishing quickly as the relentless wind swept them away. His figure melted into the landscape, leaving his team behind to guard the rally point in vigilant silence.
Jimzon clutched his rifle as if it were a bleeding artery, his knuckles turned white under his camouflage. Every bit of anger, fear or pain he'd had suppressed came surging forward, not to oppress him, but empower him. His footsteps were heavier, but faster. He craved the carnage.
Jimzon moved with calculated precision, following a crude, well-worn path etched into the charred earth. The trail wound its way toward the front gate, a jagged puncture in the reinforced walls of the Nhath base. The gate stood as the only barrier between him and a direct entry, but it wasn’t the gate itself that held his attention. It was the tollbooth perched alongside it, a decrepit outpost that housed his first target.
Inside, a Nhath sat slouched with a bored expression, its grotesque form bathed in the faint orange glow of a flickering overhead lamp. Jimzon smirked beneath his visor. Perfect. He knew just the cure for boredom.
He glanced at his gauntlet, the holosuit’s power gauge flashing faintly—just over half capacity. Just enough time. With deliberate movements, he stepped off the path, his camouflaged form blending into the haze as he circled toward the booth.
Reaching the tollbooth’s rear, he crouched low, pressing his back against the rough, pitted metal. The muffled wheeze of the creature inside reached his ears, each labored breath a grating sound that added fuel to his simmering rage. Slowly, Jimzon angled himself for a clearer view, peering over the creature’s shoulder.
The Nhath was a pitiful sight. Though broad-shouldered and visibly strong, it seemed smaller than Jimzon remembered—less intimidating, more decrepit. Its greasy green skin glistened faintly under the dim light, interrupted by sporadic patches of wiry hair that sprouted haphazardly from its eczema-riddled hide. The creature’s enormous nasal cavities should have provided efficient breathing, yet it struggled for each wheezing inhale, the exertion evident in the thick mucus that dripped steadily from its chin. That chin, if it could be called one, was a tangled mess of nappy, stubby tentacles that twitched intermittently.
The Nhath wore a teal tunic trimmed with black, the fabric worn and frayed at the edges. Its legs were propped carelessly on the desk, near a battered pistol that had clearly seen better days. Its vacant gaze was fixed on some distant point beyond the gate, the faint flicker of intelligence in its eyes dulled by complacency.
Jimzon slung his rifle over his shoulder, the movement slow and deliberate to avoid detection. His hand reached for his belt, pulling free a compact, high-voltage taser. He paused, his grip firm as he readied the device, the faint hum of its charge building in his palm. His other hand hovered near his target, ready to stifle any cries.
With a swift, decisive motion, he plunged the taser into the Nhath’s exposed neck, pressing his gloved palm over its gaping mouth to muffle the inevitable screams. The room erupted in a burst of yellow light as the taser discharged, sparks flying in chaotic arcs that momentarily illuminated the grimy interior.
The Nhath convulsed violently, its body jerking against the chair as the electrical current coursed through it. Jimzon held the taser steady, his grip unyielding, his expression hard as stone. He didn’t flinch, didn’t waver, until the creature’s thrashing subsided and its limbs stiffened, locking in place like a grotesque marionette.
The stench of burnt flesh hung in the air as the sparks faded, the room falling silent save for the faint hum of the taser powering down. Jimzon released his hold, letting the creature slump lifelessly against the desk, its grotesque features frozen in a twisted rictus.
He hauled the unconscious alien upright, its limp body slumping against the grimy wall with a dull thud. With practiced efficiency, he secured its wrists with magnetized cuffs. The cuffs clamped shut with a resonant click and bonded to the metallic floor, anchoring the creature in place.
Jimzon straightened, pulling his mother’s service pistol from its holster. The weapon gleamed with a cold elegance in the faint light, its barrel now leveled directly at the Nhath’s head. Without hesitation, he delivered a sharp kick to the alien’s leg.
The Nhath’s head jerked upward, its heavy eyelids fluttering as it clawed its way back to consciousness. It let out a ragged, wheezing gasp, the sound wet and uneven, a testament to its labored breathing.
Jimzon’s voice, low and calm, cut through the silence like a blade. He spoke in flawless Nhathian, each word sharp and precise: “Ar spi marlo von bäyn trei.” (Scream, and you die.)
The Nhath froze, its trembling form stiffening under Jimzon’s unyielding gaze. Its wide, scared orange eyes flitted to the pistol aimed at its skull. Slowly, almost imperceptibly, it gave a shaky nod, the subtle motion barely visible beneath its quivering frame.
“Vai. Oo rem haiso dei bäyn Kon-Grai.” (Good. I need to speak to your boss.)
The Nhath’s lips pressed together hesitantly, its slimy tentacles twitching as it seemed to weigh its options. Time crawled, the silence stretching unbearably long before it dared to speak as if he were waiting for approval. Its voice a faint, trembling whisper.
“Bäyn te?” (Speak what to him?)
Jimzon didn’t reply immediately. His hand moved to his belt, fingers curling around the hilt of his plasma knife. With a deliberate motion, he drew the weapon, its activation button pressed in a practiced gesture. A sharp snap-hiss filled the room as the blade ignited, its edge crackling with bright teal energy. The faint hum of the plasma knife was unnervingly quiet, a predator’s purr in the charged silence.
The tollbooth was instantly bathed in the knife’s ghostly glow, the dim light catching on every curve of Jimzon’s armor and painting his silhouette in stark relief. The Nhath’s breath hitched as its terrified eyes locked onto the blade, its orange pupils dilating at the sight of the deadly weapon.
“Te bäyn aga Pilsa,” Jimzon said, his tone a deadly calm, every syllable enunciated with measured precision. (Tell him I have a message.)

	Pav and Berji crouched at the rally point, waiting for orders. Berji lay prone, his rifle pressed snugly against his shoulder, the faint glint of starlight reflecting off its barrel. His sharp gaze was locked through the scope, trained on the compound ahead, every muscle coiled like a spring. Beside him, Pav hunched over a strange, improvised device sprawled on the dirt. His rifle rested nearby, temporarily abandoned as his gloved hands fiddled with an array of wires and mismatched components.
The device looked like a bundle of misfit parts scavenged from a scrapyard—dented gray metals fused together into a crude box. A primitive antenna jutted out at an awkward angle, vibrating slightly with each adjustment Pav made. Red lights blinked intermittently, casting fleeting shadows on his focused expression. He muttered under his breath, twisting a stubborn bolt.
The radio on Pav's chest crackled to life, disrupting the quiet. Jax’s voice buzzed through the static with a low hum of curiosity. “You guys hearing this shit?”
A beat later, Yake chimed in, his tone half-amused, half-concerned. “Yeah… hope he hasn’t gone off the deep end.”
Berji exhaled sharply, the sound barely audible under the muffled rustle of his gear. “Is he speaking fucking Nhath - Nhathanese or whatever?” he muttered, keeping his eye pressed to the scope. He shifted slightly, the cold metal of the rifle biting into his cheek. His crosshairs hovered over the tollbooth in the compound, where the muffled voice continued to echo faintly.
“What’s he even saying?” Berji asked, his voice tinged with irritation and curiosity as he adjusted the magnification for a clearer view.
Pavlock chuckled faintly. “Something about the weather, I think,” he muttered, his focus still on the tangle of wires in front of him.
Berji smirked, not breaking his sightline through the scope. “Why the fuck would he talk about the weather to a target?” His tone carried no real annoyance, just a teasing edge.
“I don’t know, man,” Pavlock replied, shrugging as he tightened a screw. “This place sucks—it literally rains fire. You’d probably wanna know too if magma was about to fall out of the sky.”
Berji resisted the urge to glance over, staying fixed on the tollbooth. “Lava,” he said evenly.
“What?”
“If it rained lava. Magma’s lava but underground,” Berji corrected, his voice flat but with a hint of amusement.
“It’s all the same thing anyway,” Pavlock quipped, a small smile forming as a dumb thought crossed his mind. “You know, my dad told me you can drink lava… but only once.”
Berji’s laugh came out as a soft chortle, the kind that sneaks up on you. “Your old man said what?”
“Think about it.” Pavlock leaned back slightly, punching the side of his contraption. 
Berji shifted slightly, his tone dry but with a hint of concern. “Be careful with that thing.”
Pavlock didn’t even look up, his hands still working deftly. “I’m careful. I know what I’m doing.”
Berji let out a soft snort, adjusting his position. “Yeah? Well, I don’t wanna be anywhere close to you if you set that thing off.”
“It won’t detonate near us,” Pavlock reassured, holding up a finger for emphasis. “I’ve got a proximity safety on the detonator.” He reached into his belt, pulling out a handheld remote with a stubby antenna. A faint red light blinked on its surface. Pav fiddled with a switch, then pressed a button that turned the light green. “See? Perfectly safe.”
Berji gave him a sidelong glance, one brow raised in silent doubt. After a beat, he returned his focus to the scope. “Uh-huh,” he muttered under his breath.
The radio crackled again, cutting through their banter. Jimzon’s voice came through steady, carrying his usual edge of command. “About done in here. Yake, you ready?”
“Roger,” Yake’s voice replied, calm and clipped over the radio.
Jimzon continued. “Berji, Pav, Jax—you ready?”
A round of confirmations echoed back through the comms, each one as routine as the last.
Berji tightened his grip on his rifle, scanning the compound through his scope. “Showtime,” he murmured, more to himself than anyone else.
Jimzon pressed the cold barrel of his pistol to the back of the Nhath captive’s spine, the alien’s mottled, scaly skin twitching under the pressure. With a sharp nudge, Jimzon forced the creature to stumble forward, his voice low and steady. “Dea Gor Kadin,” he muttered, his words thick with mockery as he spoke in the alien tongue. Go ask for help.

The Nhath’s mouth hung open, purple blood dribbling in thick rivulets down its chin, staining the once-teal tunic it wore, for Jimzon had cut out the beast's tongue. The creature glanced over its shoulder, its reptilian eyes wide with a mixture of fear and horror. The trembling in its legs betrayed its desperation, but a quick jab from the pistol urged it onward.
Reluctantly, the Nhath staggered toward the tollbooth door. Its pace was slow at first, as if clinging to the faint hope that hesitation might spare its life. But as the weight of Jimzon’s presence bore down on it, the creature broke into a frantic jog, the shuffle of its clawed feet growing louder as it pushed through the door and out into the open.
It sprinted to the tall gate and  tilted its head back and let out a guttural scream—a sound that was meant to be a warning, but emerged as a series of pained, agonized grunts. Each garbled cry was laced with alarm, but the absence of a tongue left it almost unintelligible. Still, it pointed wildly toward the tollbooth, its gestures frantic and pleading.
After a tense thirty seconds, the gates began to groan, metal grinding against metal as they slowly creaked open. Two Nhath guards emerged, their sleek rifles glinting faintly under the compound lights. Their steps were cautious, their elongated necks craning as they scanned their surroundings.
One of them gestured at the screaming Nhath, lowering his weapon slightly. The other followed suit, stepping closer with an air of curiosity and unease. The wounded toll booth worker continued his erratic grunting, pointing at the booth with trembling fingers.
Then came the crack of two shots.
The first Nhath dropped instantly, a spray of purple mist painting the air as its lifeless body crumpled to the ground. The second barely had time to register what had happened before the second shot followed, striking true and folding it over like a rag doll.
The captive Nhath froze, its screams faltering into a choked gasp as it turned to flee. Jimzon didn’t hesitate. The third shot rang out, and the back of the creature’s head burst outward in a gruesome bloom of blood and bone. Its body collapsed in a heap near the others, a macabre pile of stillness.
Jimzon lowered his pistol, his tone calm as he keyed his radio. “Berji, Jax—great shots. Send in the cavalry, Pav.”
From the rally point, a small, ramshackle hoverboard hummed to life, carrying a precarious-looking box of blinking lights and exposed gears. The device glided over the treacherous terrain with an almost casual grace, skimming across glowing streaks of magma and loose stones. Occasionally, it wobbled, the unstable hoverfield sputtering as it adjusted to the uneven ground, before veering straight through the compound's gate.
For a heartbeat, nothing happened. The hoverboard hovered in stillness just past the threshold, the box’s lights flickering faster as if drawing one last breath. Then came the explosion. A brilliant blue flash lit up the sky, a concussive wave shaking the earth as the device detonated with ferocious intensity. Shards of metal and stone shot skyward, trailing smoke, and a luminous mushroom cloud, tinged electric blue, rose defiantly against the fiery red and black skyline.
Jimzon’s voice crackled through the comms, calm and clipped. “Good effect on target. EMP should take out their hoverbikes.”
The massive gate groaned under the blast’s force, its hinges shrieking before giving way entirely. With a thunderous crash, it toppled, throwing up a choking cloud of dirt and rubble. The walls on either side quivered, sections crumbling in a cascade of stone.
From within the compound, chaos erupted. Alien shouts rang out in their guttural language, rising in a cacophony of panic and alarm. A piercing wail of sirens followed, their shrill cry slicing through the smoky air. Jimzon crouched behind a chunk of the fallen wall, his sharp gaze scanning the compound as the unmistakable thud of approaching Nhathian boots reached his ears.
He keyed his radio. “Jax, your scope clear?”
“All clear, Jimmy,” came the steady reply. “You’re good to move.”
With a nod, Jimzon darted from cover, his armor-clad form a shadow against the glowing backdrop of destruction. The compound stretched out before him, its layout stark and utilitarian. four squat buildings sat in a cluster, their iron-clad windows gleaming faintly in the fiery light. At the center stood the remains of a larger, three-story structure, still smouldering from the explosion.
“Pav, Berji, on me,” Jimzon ordered, his voice carrying the weight of command. “Yake, you know what to do.”
Copy,” Yake replied, his tone calm but focused.
On the far side of the compound’s wall, Yake adjusted the straps of his bulky tactical backpack, the weight sitting comfortably after years of experience. He unslung his rifle, holding it in one hand, and took his first step onto the vertical surface. His rose-gold magboots—loot from the raid on Nardow’s compound—hummed faintly with each step, their magnetic grips latching securely to the metallic structure.
The rhythmic clink of the boots echoed in his ears as he ascended, step by deliberate step. He glanced upward, calculating the distance to the top, and pressed on with mechanical precision. The weight of the rifle in his hand and the slight strain on his shoulders were ignored, his focus singular.
Reaching the top, he crouched low, scanning his surroundings. Below him stretched a two-story drop, the compound’s interior illuminated by fires and the distant, shifting glow of emergency lights. Without hesitation, he leapt from the wall to the nearest building’s roof. He landed in a smooth roll, absorbing the impact with practiced ease, his movements silent as a shadow.
Drawing a grenade from his vest, he studied its transparent casing, the green liquid within sloshing slightly as he twisted the primer. The device hissed softly, emitting a faint trail of foam as he secured it to the rooftop. The adhesive spread quickly, anchoring the grenade firmly in place.
Yake’s sharp gaze swept the compound. The central three-story building was a ruined husk, its skeletal frame standing amid burning rubble, pillars of twisted metal reaching skyward like jagged claws. The other four buildings clustered around it, each close enough for a well-aimed throw.
From his pack, Yake retrieved another grenade. With a fluid motion, he hurled it at the farthest building, watching as it arced through the smoky air and struck the base of the wall with a muted thunk. The adhesive foam expanded, securing it tightly in place.

He repeated the process with precision, throwing grenades at the remaining two structures. Each landed exactly where he intended, their translucent forms clinging to the walls like malevolent barnacles.
Satisfied, Yake slung his rifle back over his shoulder and keyed his comms. “Gas is in place. On your mark.”
Jimzon crouched at the front gate, stacked with Berji and Pav at his flanks. His usual calm demeanor had been replaced by an almost feral intensity. His muscles were loose, his body coiled and ready to strike. Adrenaline coursed through him like a firestorm, his sharp gaze darting between his men and the compound beyond.
The three exchanged a brief nod, a silent acknowledgment of their readiness. Jimzon shifted slightly, peering over the wall that served as their cover. The compound was eerily quiet. The Nhaths had retreated into their shelters, no doubt hiding from the chaos outside. The only sounds were the low crackle of the still-burning central building and the distant wail of alarms.
“Blow it,” Jimzon growled, his voice carrying an edge of cold resolve.
The gas was a brutal weapon, designed for wiping out entire ecosystems of invasive pests in dense woodland biomes. It wasn’t just lethal—it was catastrophic. The invasive poison bonded to exposed skin, penetrating deeply before clogging arteries and triggering a violent autoimmune response. Victims succumbed within minutes, overwhelmed by fevers, blindness, and ultimately death.
Jimzon had secured this monstrous tool years ago—an unexpected prize during a raid—and had guarded it zealously ever since. It was never meant to be used lightly, and now, as the moment of vengeance arrived, he unleashed it without hesitation.
Four synchronized explosions rang out, sharp and efficient, their impacts reverberating through the compound. Small bursts of green mist erupted from the buildings, hissing as they spread. Within seconds, the noxious gas began seeping through cracks in windows and door frames, insidiously infiltrating the structures.
To his right, the door of the closest building slid open with a sharp whoosh. Silhouettes of Nhaths stumbled out, their movements erratic and desperate. They clawed at their throats, their wet, rasping coughs punctuated by panicked wheezes. Their alien forms swayed, some collapsing to their knees as the gas took its toll.
Jimzon didn’t hesitate. He raised his plasma rifle, the weapon humming to life as he squeezed the trigger. The air filled with the sharp shrill of plasma rounds, glowing streaks cutting through the dim light. He unleashed a merciless barrage, his weapon set to full-auto.
The Nhaths fell where they stood, their bodies crumpling into a ragged pile at the doorway. Jimzon’s expression was unreadable, his focus absolute as he swept the barrel of his rifle over the fallen. The sharp sizzle of plasma dissipating into the air was the only sound that remained, punctuated by the faint hiss of the spreading gas.
Jimzon rounded the corner, moving with predatory precision. Ahead, more Nhaths stumbled into the roadpath, their elongated forms doubled over in fits of violent coughing. Their rifles hung limply in their hands, the creatures struggling to focus through the haze of disorientation and agony. Some tried to aim toward the front gate, their trembling limbs betraying their intent, but their footing faltered, and they staggered like drunkards into the open.
Without hesitation, Jimzon charged. His plasma bayonet ignited with a sharp hiss, the blade crackling as it plunged deep into the chest of the nearest Nhath. The alien let out a choked gurgle, its clawed hands twitching as the plasma burned through its body. Jimzon’s raw strength nearly lifted the creature off the ground before he yanked the blade free, the lifeless form slumping forward.
In one fluid motion, he threw his rifle on its sling, letting it fall against his back. Gripping the dying Nhath by its scaly neck, he hauled its body upright, using it as a body shield. The creature’s own weapon was still clutched in its clawed hand, and with calculated efficiency, Jimzon twisted its wrist, wrenching the rifle free.
The Nhath’s comrades hesitated, their confused eyes widening as Jimzon turned the stolen weapon on them. Plasma bolts burst from the barrel, streaking through the air with a harsh crackle. The Nhaths barely had time to react before they were cut down in a hail of their own firepower.
Once the group lay still, Jimzon discarded the body shield, letting it crumple to the ground like a broken marionette. He paused for a moment, his chest rising and falling with heavy breaths, the rifle still warm in his grip.
	Despite how skilled a soldier Jimzon was, he had never taken pleasure in violence. To him, it was a necessity, a means to an end. But tonight was different. His carnal bloodlust was fueling him, pushing him beyond his usual limits. Each strike, each enemy he vanquished, sent a surge of adrenaline through his veins. The taste of vengeance was on his lips, and it was intoxicating.
	Every blow he delivered was accompanied by a primal roar, a manifestation of his pent-up anger and grief. He didn't just defeat his foes; he annihilated them with a savage intensity that bordered on madness. He wasn't just hungry for violence, he was starving for it.
Panting, sweat slicking his brow, Jimzon’s eyes flicked over his shoulder, his vision locking onto his men as they methodically cut down the remaining Nhaths. His voice crackled over the radio, a frenzy in his tone
“No! Mine! Mine—let me kill them!” he growled, the words rasping through clenched teeth.
His squad exchanged glances, a fleeting moment of hesitation before they lowered their weapons, ceasing their fire.
Jimzon didn’t wait. He dropped the empty magazine from his mother's Royal pistol, fingers shaking slightly, before slamming in a new one marked with red paint. His hands were steady as he aimed. His first shot exploded at the feet of the nearest Nhath, the impact triggering a fire charge that launched the alien a foot off the ground. The second shot blew the legs off another, sending its screaming body crumpling.
Without a moment’s hesitation, Jimzon fired again, the shot exploding the first Nhath’s head in a burst of blood and bone, a grotesque firework in the dark.
A low, almost delirious chuckle bubbled up from his chest, a release of tension, of pent-up madness. His eyes tracked the last building, where a few remaining Nhaths stumbled out, hands raised in futile surrender.
He paused, his pistol aimed at them for a long moment, before he holstered it with deliberate calm. With a predatory gleam in his eye, he approached from behind, plunging his plasma knife into one’s throat, leaving it embedded as it gurgled its last. The other didn’t fare any better; he seized the Nhath by its skull, smashing its face into the wall with a sickening thud, until nothing remained in his hand but the jagged tip of its spine.
Castle Team had never witnessed this side of Jimzon before, and to be honest, they were horrified by what they saw. Yet, not a word was spoken. The only sounds were the subtle shifts of armor and the occasional darting glance through their visors, avoiding direct contact with the terrifying change in their leader. Berji's eyes flicked to the last building yet to be cleared. The iron doors were still locked tight, but the mounted turrets and cameras that once stood vigilant now hung limp, their mechanisms fried by the EMP blast.
"Hey guys, this building was already gassed," Berji called out, voice low but tense. "But it’s the only one with turrets. If they’ve got those, they might have respirators inside. Stay frosty."
The squad's eyes remained fixed on the door, weapons trained. All except Jimzon, who was crouched beside a lifeless body. Pavlock, hesitant but resolute, approached and placed a hand on Jimzon’s shoulder, his fingers lightly tapping against the heavy armor.
Jimzon’s body tensed, and he let out an animalistic snarl, eyes flashing with a raw fury as he turned his gaze on Pavlock. But then, without a word, he returned to the task at hand. The body at his feet was a Nhath, its legs shredded, its remains propped awkwardly against the rubble. Jimzon held its head, submerging it in a small pool of molten magma that hissed as it burned as if he were drowning it.
Pavlock’s voice was steady, though a hint of concern lingered in his tone. “Hey, Jimmy, just one more building to go.”
Jimzon snapped back to reality in an instant. He stood, his movements fluid and precise, then casually fired a single shot into the back of the Nhath's head, silencing the still-twitching corpse. Without hesitation, he turned and pressed forward, his eyes locking onto the last building in their path.
He stormed up the steps, his boots echoing with each forceful step. Yake, lagging slightly behind, called out, his voice cutting through the tension. “What’s the plan here?”
Jimzon's voice cut through the tense silence as he barked, "Stay behind me!" He slapped a detonator onto the door and hooked a smoke grenade to its base. Without a moment's hesitation, he jogged back to the bottom of the stairs, switching to his thermals. He detonated the charge with a loud, deafening bang, sending a burst of smoke and debris into the air, clouding the building's interior. Jimzon, never missing a beat, charged in immediately, the smoke swirling around him as he sprinted inside.
The moment he entered, his movements were fluid, instinctual. He shot twice, his pistol roaring in the chaos, each shot a clean headshot. The illuminated silhouettes crumpled to the floor without so much as a blink from him. The house was clear—except for one last room, tucked away at the back. It was different from everything else. An ornate door, intricately carved with gold designs, stood before him. This must be the leader’s office, the final obstacle. He reloaded his pistol, switching to normal plasma rounds, and sprinted toward the door. With a quick shot to its hinges, he dove through, the world slowing as he breached the threshold.
Time stretched as he moved through the door. His eyes tracked the Nhath’s pistol-arm, its slow-motion aim locking onto him through the air. Jimzon could almost feel the heat of the plasma bullet as it whizzed by, grazing his face before it tore through his shoulder. The pain was sharp, but he pushed through, breaking through a small table as he landed awkwardly. He quickly regained his footing and shot the Nhath’s hand, the plasma blast tearing through its palm and blowing its fingers clean off, sending the rifle clattering to the floor. The Nhath staggered back, a growl of frustration escaping its throat.
Jimzon stood, unfazed by the pain, eyes narrowing at the Nhath. The creature didn’t look scared—if anything, it looked furious. It paced behind its desk, blood dripping from its hand. Without missing a beat, Jimzon flipped the desk over, scattering papers and trinkets in all directions. The Nhath, still clutching its wounded hand, glared at him, its anger palpable.
Jimzon studied the Nhath before him, noting its weak stance. A knee brace was wrapped tightly around its left leg, a vulnerability that didn’t escape his trained eye. With a calculated step forward, Jimzon struck, delivering a vicious kick to the Nhath’s side. There was a sickening snap, the sound of bone breaking under the force. The Nhath crumpled to the ground, and Jimzon, with the calculated precision of a seasoned killer, aimed his mother’s pistol at its head.
He panted, his red blood trickling down from his shoulder, but he smiled—this moment was his, and he intended to savor it. "Tato Fein bäyn," he sneered, his words cold. Any last words?
The Nhath struggled to sit up, its breath labored, yet there was a glint of acceptance in its eyes—perhaps even a sliver of honor in facing its end. "I'll save them for my Gods," it rasped, its voice barely above a whisper.
"You speak Galactic Standard?" Jimzon asked, his eyebrow lifting in surprise.
"Better than you," the Nhath retorted, the bitterness evident in its voice.
Jimzon leaned in slightly, asserting his dominance, his voice laced with a cruel edge. "How does it feel knowing you are about to die?"
The Nhath, despite the pain and the imminent end, met his gaze with eerie calm. "Familiar," it said, "for I've been on the other end of a barrel before, right where you are."
Jimzon’s lips curled into a sneer. "I’m nothing like you."
The Nhath’s smirk, though faint, didn’t falter. "We both fight for money. To survive."
Jimzon’s eyes darkened. "I fight for justice. For freedom."
The Nhath’s expression shifted slightly, a knowing glint in its eyes. "A Guardsman, I see. I know your kind well. Is that what this is? Justice? Freedom?"
Jimzon’s grip tightened on the pistol. "It’s freedom from you. The world will know your violence no longer."
The Nhath chuckled bitterly, despite the blood leaking from its body. "Yet the Guards' violence seems to be everywhere. So does its money. I knew you brought no protection to this system."
Jimzon holstered his pistol, his fingers itching to strike, but as he grabbed the Nhath by the collar, something caught his attention. A metal chip embedded in the creature's neck. His curiosity flared, a strange sense of familiarity nagging at him. The chip seemed too deliberate, too out of place.
"Is this a translator in your neck?" Jimzon asked, his voice cool, though his mind was beginning to turn. The weight of something important pressed down on him.
"Do not bother wasting my time. Just shoot me," the Nhath growled, its voice rough, but the translator embedded in its neck glowed faintly, a soft pulse of light. As the light flickered, a symbol appeared—an insignia. The unmistakable emblem of the Guard.
Jimzon’s eyes narrowed as he processed the image. "A Guard translator? Who did you kill for this?"
The Nhath sneered, defiant to the end. "I paid for it, with my own blood and comrades."
Jimzon’s jaw tightened. "Answer me."
The Nhath, seeing the desperation in Jimzon’s eyes, seemed to recognize the moment—there was nothing left to lose. "Job gone bad. A routine one to steal some Corefuel. Got shot in the neck, patched up, and bought this with the credits I earned."
Jimzon’s eyes widened as the words Corefuel left the Nhath’s mouth. A rush of recognition hit him instantly—he knew this creature now. It wasn’t just another enemy.
In the academy, Jimzon had studied it extensively. Corefuel was rare, a resource coveted across the galaxy, and he knew its origins well. Waketin had once been a capital planet, in the Before War. But that was all in the past. Waketin had been obliterated by some unthinkable weapon—some bomb, or something like it that had wiped the planet from existence. All that remained were the shattered fragments, asteroids of dense mantle and core, drifting in space. They were the perfect resource for starships, their remains transformed into Corefuel.
His father, Korjin, had made his fortune from the Corefuel, pioneering the technology to synthesize it. He had mined the rich remnants of Waketin, his engineering team settling the fragmented remains of the planet with nothing but a dream: to escape the endless violence of the galaxy. Waketin, or what was left of it, was now called the Wakeful Islands by the locals, and it had become the only place Corefuel could be mined
He could feel his grip on reality slipping, his world spinning as the weight of the words settled on him. Barely holding his sanity together, he forced out the words, his voice tight and strained. "Who shot you?" The question came out in a breath, barely more than a whisper, as if the very act of speaking it might shatter him completely.
The Nhath sneered, almost with a twisted sense of pride as it spoke. "Some bitch, hit me right  in the neck. I ought to thank her. I never would've gotten a translator if it weren't to cover up the scar. Speaking galactic standard has been great for business."
The words hit Jimzon like a hammer. His mind flashed, memory colliding with rage. Mom… he thought, and in an instant, the floodgates opened. His mother’s face, the image of her final act, burned into his mind. She had shot a Nhath in the throat, protecting him—Jimzy, the frightened child hiding beneath the floorboards.
The red haze descended, fury clouding his vision. Without thought, he lunged, slamming into the Nhath with all his weight, pinning it to the ground. His right fist came down in a savage, bone-crunching hook, knocking out the creature’s tooth. He didn’t stop. The left hook followed, sending more teeth flying, the crack of bone a sickening echo. His gauntlets, sharp and unyielding, connected with the Nhath’s face again and again.
Each punch landed with brutal precision, the alien’s skin bruising, swelling, and breaking under the relentless assault. Blood—thick, purple, and slick—splattered across Jimzon’s face, his arms, his chest, caking him in it as he continued to pound the creature’s skull. His gauntlets ground against bone, shattering it with each brutal strike. The Nhath’s face began to deform, the flesh giving way under the unrelenting force, its features mangled and unrecognizable.
Jimzon let out a visceral, primal scream—a raw, animalistic sound of pure fury. It echoed through the room, reverberating in his chest, as if he could tear the universe apart with the force of his anger. He felt the Nhath’s blood slick on his skin, staining him, the alien’s body growing limp beneath him. Still, he didn’t stop. He didn’t care. His punches kept coming, the sound of metal striking flesh and bone a dull, rhythmic thud. He wasn’t thinking. He wasn’t even aware of the world around him anymore.
His breath came in ragged, broken gasps, but he kept hitting. The Nhath’s head was a pulpy mess, a mass of twisted tissue and bone. It no longer resembled anything living. And yet, even after the life had been beaten from the creature’s body, Jimzon’s rage wasn’t spent. His fists continued to strike, now at the cold metal floor, the motion mechanical and repetitive, as if he were caught in some endless cycle. The blows were hollow, distant, as if he were fighting an enemy that no longer existed.
It wasn’t until his body gave way, exhaustion finally overtaking him, that he stopped. He collapsed back onto his heels, gasping for air, his chest heaving with the weight of his fury. His team stood silently behind him, watching the destruction unfold, but not one moved to stop him. They didn’t know how to.
Finally, when the last of his breath left him, Jimzon let out a guttural wail—a scream that wasn’t just of rage, but of something deeper, something darker. 
It was the scream of Jimzy. A scream he had held since he was the boy who had been forced to hide under the floorboards, that fateful night when everything had been ripped from him
Vengeance was his, but it was not glorious. 

*add last nhath has respirator


Chapter 10:
The automatic doors whooshed closed behind Berji as he stepped into the Mothership’s rec room—a vast, pulsing oasis of relaxation and camaraderie. The space was alive with a symphony of sound: cheerful chatter, bursts of laughter, and the steady rhythm of Moonballs dribbling across polished surfaces mingled with the soft scuffle of sneakers on waxed floors that gleamed like liquid silver. Towering overhead, a cavernous ceiling lent an open, airy feel, while walls of warm, budget-friendly wood added a touch of nostalgic comfort to the futuristic setting.
The room itself unfolded like a grand, multi-zone auditorium. On one side, the arena of physical prowess boasted dedicated zones for Moonball and weightlifting—each area resonating with the clang of weights, the thud of exercise balls, and the determined grunts of competitors pushing their limits. In contrast, the opposite wing offered a sanctuary for leisure: clusters of sleek, modern tables, state-of-the-art vending machines dispensing curious, interstellar snacks, and multiple screens broadcasting holo-movies that danced with vibrant colors. Tucked into a corner was a creatively reimagined “Zero-G Cue” table—an inventive twist on traditional billiards adapted for low-gravity play—drawing in a small group of enthusiasts eager to test their skills. 
“Berji! You’re late—scared I’m gonna kick your ass in cards again?” Yake called out, his voice cutting through the ambient buzz with playful authority as he spotted Berji. Yake was comfortably ensconced at a well-worn table alongside Pav. The table itself was a feast for the eyes: clusters of luminescent starfruit—exotic, otherworldly orbs that shimmered with the hues of distant nebulae—were artfully arranged next to a neat pile of iridescent poker chips, forming a semicircular fortress of friendly competition. Yake lounged confidently at the center, his eyes sparkling with mischief and challenge, while Pav sat slightly off to the side, with just a few chips in front of him, equally ready for the next round of banter or a heated card game.
“Nah, man, just couldn’t sleep much last night,” Berji replied as he settled into the booth.
“Is that code for another lady friend?” Pav chuckled, raising an eyebrow.
“Haha, not this time.” Berji’s gaze wandered over the bustling rec room before he asked, “Where’s Jax?”
“Just missed him—pilot school’s running asteroid drills or something today,” Yake answered with a shrug.
“Gotcha,” Berji muttered.
After a brief pause, Yake observed, “You’re awfully quiet tonight.”
“Yeah, um—do you guys know when room inspections are?” Berji asked, his tone edged with concern.
“Well, our PTO ends in two night cycles—so, two days, I guess,” Pav offered.
“Yeah… that’s what I thought,” Berji said, his voice dipping into a note of quiet disappointment.
Pav leaned in conspiratorially. “Ooooooh, did you loot something on that Nhath op you shouldn’t have?”
Berji’s eyebrows shot up in annoyance at Pav’s loud insinuation. For a moment, he said nothing.
“Oooh, I gotcha,” Pav whispered eagerly. “What do you have?”
With a sly smile, Berji replied, “Lemme just show you guys at my dorm.”
“Whoa, hang on, pal—you owe me a game, remember?” Yake teased, a playful glint in his eye.
With a stubborn huff, Berji dug into his pocket, flicked a few credits onto the table, and gathered a handful of chips. He drew two cards and placed them face-up in a practiced gesture. “I fold. C’mon, let’s get going,” he declared. It was clear he didn’t have time for a lengthy debate—or a full card game—tonight.
Berji’s room pulsed with shifting neon hues, a chaotic blend of color and clutter that somehow felt inviting. Strands of LED lights snaked along the walls, their glow flickering between blues, purples, and greens. Luminescent logos and symbols—some stolen, some scavenged—hovered in faint holographic projections, casting shifting shadows over the room. A ceiling-mounted projector spun a slow-moving aurora borealis, streaking bands of violet and emerald across the walls like a dream frozen mid-motion.
The lighting was dim but deliberate, every step triggering subtle shifts in color across the floor. Shelves jammed with trophies of past missions lined the walls—defunct pistols with cracked barrels, scavenged gears, golden chains, battle-worn foreign helmets, and a few dented ashtrays still dusted with redleaf embers.
The room smelled of synthetic flowers, a poor attempt at masking the earthy musk of redleaf smoke, which still clung stubbornly to the furniture. Cluttered, but lived-in. Messy, but intentional. Pav flopped down into a massive beanbag near the corner, sinking into it with a satisfied sigh. Yake leaned against the wall, arms crossed, scanning the room with a half-smirk.
“So… where is it?” Pav asked, absentmindedly spinning a poker chip between his fingers. His tone was casual, but there was a glint of expectation in his eyes.
Berji didn’t answer right away. Instead, his gaze flickered toward the door, checking it, his shoulders stiffening ever so slightly. The easy energy from before drained out of him, replaced by something colder, something heavier.
Yake picked up on it immediately. “I hope you’re about to tell me you got some comet caps, right?” he asked, a smirk tugging at his lips.
Berji let out a slow exhale. “No…” He rubbed his face, his fingers pressing hard into his temples. “It’s not drugs, not some cool expensive shit, not anything fun, or anything I even want to have.” He hesitated, then exhaled sharply. “Actually, it sucks. It’s pretty fucking bad, and I didn’t sleep last night because I don’t know what the fuck to do.”
Pav and Yake exchanged glances, their relaxed postures shifting ever so slightly.
Pav raised an eyebrow. “What, did you kill someone or something?”
Berji snapped his head toward him, eyes narrowing. “No, obviously not,” he muttered, voice low, like he didn’t appreciate the joke. He leaned forward, resting his elbows on his knees, fingers laced together. The slow-spinning lights in the room washed his face in flickering shades of red, painting restless shadows across his features. Across from him, Pav and Yake sat under the cool wash of blue light, their faces cast in the calm hues. 
His foot tapped the floor once, an unconscious habit, a physical sign of the pressure pressing down on him.
Yake and Pav waited.
Berji finally spoke. “You guys remember those processors I pulled from the Nardow raid, right?”
“Yeah…” they both answered, eyes narrowing.
He hesitated for half a second, then pushed forward. “I spent a few days turning them into a skeleton key,” he said, watching their reactions. Their brows furrowed slightly out of confusion, but they didn’t speak. “It can hack things." He stated bluntly. "Took a while to get it working, but I wanted to test it out.”
He exhaled, rubbing his hands together. “So after Jimzon… freaked out or whatever the other day, while we were waiting for Jax's pickup, I figured—why not see what the Nhaths have? So I plugged into their leader’s terminal.”
The room felt noticeably quieter.
Berji glanced between them. “I don’t know what I expected.” His voice dropped lower. “But it wasn’t this.”
Slowly, almost reluctantly, he reached into his pocket and pulled out a small black chip. A faint green light pulsed at its edge, slow and steady, almost like a heartbeat. The room’s neon glow caught the reflection in his fingers, making it seem like the device was breathing.
He plugged it into his terminal.
A sharp, electronic chirp echoed through the room as a holographic projector flickered to life. The wall morphed into a screen, lines of scrolling data and fragmented files snapping into focus, stark white-blue against the darkened room.
Berji stood, walking toward the display. The light from the projection stretched his shadow long across the wall, distorted and looming. He swiped his hand across the display, scrolling through files, his expression unreadable.
“You see these files here?” His voice was steady but tense. “Most of them are redacted, stricken, or censored—but if you really dig…” He waved his hand, opening a new window labeled INVOICES.
The screen flickered as lines of text snapped into focus.
Pav squinted. “I thought Nhaths didn’t use terminals? After the war, that EMP that shut everything down aren't they all paper?"
Berji shook his head. “I’ll get to that.”
He pointed at the list. “Most of this is normal shit—food deliveries, bike parts, fuel shipments—but look here.” He tapped a line of text, highlighting the transaction. 1 million credits. The sender: GBD Fund.
“GBD?” Yake asked, skepticism clear in his tone.
“Guard Board of Directors Fund,” Berji said. His voice was flat. “The private payments we don’t get to see as soldiers. At first, I thought I was reading it wrong. Maybe it was ransom money—a hostage situation, something like that.” He exhaled. “But then I kept looking.”
He scrolled further, revealing dozens of identical transactions.
“There’s piles of these invoices. And it’s not just a one-time thing. This is how they made most of their money.”
Yake’s brow furrowed. “What are you trying to say?”
Berji hesitated, then spoke.
“Nothing yet. I can’t quite make sense of it.” He zoomed out, pulling up a timeline. “But I cross-referenced the dates of these payments, and every single time there was a Nhath raid somewhere in the galaxy that week.”
The room was silent, but the air got heavy.
Berji continued, his voice quieter now. “All those raids? They were on neutral planets, colonies, settlements—places that weren’t part of the Guard. But they get attacked, and afterward? They usually join the Guard’s Alliance for protection.” His throat felt tight, but his voice stayed firm. “The Guard has been funding criminals across the galaxy. Then swooping in to save the day.”
He closed the screen, unplugged the drive, and attached it around his necklace.
There was a long silence.
Pav seemed a bit flustered. “I don’t know, man. That’s a pretty big accusation. You sure you didn’t smoke too much redleaf and start reading into things that aren’t there?”
Berji’s face weakened. “You don’t see it? It’s clear as day.” His voice wavered—just slightly, like he was doubting himself. He turned to Yake. “Tell him.”
Yake exhaled sharply, shaking his head. “You’re really gonna trust information from murderous space pirates over the organization that fed you, clothed you, educated you—” He picked up a fistful of gold chains from the table, letting them clatter back down. “—and let you take all this loot?”
Berji rubbed his temples, frustration creeping in. “Look, guys. I was in denial all night too. But I researched, top to bottom, hoping for it not to fit together.” He leaned forward, voice lower now. “You know I’m not a risk taker. But this? This could get me kicked out. Or worse. But we need to find out.”
“Yeah, or worse.” Yake’s expression darkened. “Just throw it away.”
“No.” Berji shook his head. More firm this time. “I can’t. You don’t see how important this is? There’s corruption in the Board of Directors. We can’t just let that slide.”
Yake scoffed. “You think that file is enough to make change? The Directors will either laugh or send you to a firing squad.”
Berji’s gaze hardened. “Not just me. You guys know too.”
Yake froze for a second, then his frustration boiled over, realizing what he was getting roped into. “Bro, are you fucking serious? Why would you tell us this? I don’t want to get wrapped up in your bullshit.”
Pav raised both hands. “Guys, relax. Let’s just figure something out. Room checks aren’t for two days. We have time to think.”
Berji inhaled, his whole body tense, like he was holding something in. “Look, I thought it over, and I think I know what to do. It’s just… risky.”
Pav grinned, trying to lighten the mood. “See, Yake? There’s a simple answer. It’s gonna be fine. Berji, what’s your game plan?”
Berji sat stiff as a statue, finally speaking. “We get Jimzon to hack Director Liles’ terminal. Tonight.”
Pav’s eyes widened. “What?”
Yake’s head snapped toward him. “Are you high or something? You want to get us all court-martialed? You want to bring us all down with you? What the fuck is your problem?”
Berji sighed. “Look, this information isn’t world-changing on its own, but I know my skeleton key works. It’s undetectable. It’s a magnet—all you have to do is place it anywhere on the terminal, and after a minute, it’ll have copied everything. Those pre-war processors were so advanced that the Guard hasn’t even figured out how to defend against them. We can smash the drive after, but we need to know what the fuck is going on.”
Yake’s face flushed red, but he forced himself to stay composed.
Berji pressed on. “Jimzon has his meeting with Liles tonight for the promotion. None of us could get anywhere near his office. But Jimzon? Liles treats him like a little brother. The guy rescued him as a kid—he won’t suspect a thing. He won’t even notice.”
Yake exhaled sharply, his voice edged with frustration. “So you want our friend, who just went on a PTSD-fueled murder spree, to commit treason by stealing top-secret Guard documents—all because you have a hunch?”
Berji’s shoulders slumped slightly, his expression unreadable. “You have a better idea?”
Pav chimed in, shrugging. “I’m not against the idea… Like you said, if Liles never finds out, it won’t hurt him, right? That’s pretty much how the Guard operates anyway.” He paused, glancing at Yake. “But do we even think Jimzon’s gonna get that promotion? I love the dude, obviously, but…” He hesitated, lowering his voice. “I don’t wanna sound like an asshole, but he’s got a few screws loose right now. I honestly don’t know if he’s ready.”
Yake gave a short, bitter laugh. “I agree. On that mission, I saw him waterboard a Nhath’s face into magma.” His tone darkened. “It wasn’t… necessary.” He ran a hand through his hair. “I mean, yeah, I shot those Crags too, but he enjoyed it. I don’t know if this meeting is gonna go well.”
"Lava." Pav chirped in.
"What?" Yake asked losing his focus.
"Nothing" Pav smiled to himself.
Berji sighed, his eyes narrowing, frustration creeping into his voice. “Honestly, I don’t think there’s any other option. I can’t destroy this, and we can’t let the Guard know we have it. Even if the meeting doesn’t go well, it’ll still happen. We just need him in the office for one full minute. That’s all. And yeah, if my family was murdered in front of me, I’d probably be pretty fucking pissed off too.”
Yake’s eyes narrowed, his voice low. “If this goes wrong…” He let the words hang for a moment before shaking his head. “You leave me out of it.”
Berji glanced at him, a quick nod. “Fine. Whatever, yeah.”
“No, I’m serious.” Yake’s tone turned more intense, his fists clenching. “I’ve fought next to you on every battle, but I will put my life on the line before my career. If I die, my family—my brothers—will get paid. But if this shit blows, I fail them.”
Berji met his gaze, his own jaw tightening. “Yeah, I get it. It won’t fail.”
Pav, quiet until now, finally spoke up, his voice steady. “When did you say the meeting is?”
“Tonight.” Berji replied, his expression grim, the weight of the plan settling in.

Chapter 11
	Jimzon’s eyes flickered open, disturbed by an overly eager knock on his door. His mind fought to stay anchored in the remnants of sleep, but the sharp, rhythmic raps on the door were persistent.
"I’ll be right there, relax," he mumbled, his voice thick with morning grogginess, laced with a familiar irritation. He sighed, the exhaustion still clinging to him like a second skin, and sluggishly swung his legs off the bed. His feet met the cold metal floor with a dull thud as he walked to the door, pressing the button to open it.
Standing in the hallway was Berji, his grin wide and slightly forced. The intensity in his eyes barely masked the nerves beneath his casual exterior.
"Hey, brotha. Sorry I missed the card game. Needed some sleep," Jimzon muttered, blinking sleep from his heavy eyelids, the bags under his eyes deepening with each passing day. He rubbed his face, feeling the rawness of a few restless nights catching up to him.
"No problem, man. Don't worry about it. I just figured I’d check in on you before the big day," Berji said, trying to inject some excitement into his tone, but the underlying tension was clear.
“Big day? Shit. Is that Liles meeting today?” Jimzon asked, his voice groggy, still not fully processing the weight of the day.
“Yeah, man. A few hours from now actually. Just wanted to let you know—Castle Team’s rooting for you,” Berji said, though the forced cheer in his voice betrayed his anxiety. His eyes flickered nervously to Jimzon, searching for any sign of hesitation or reluctance.
Jimzon rubbed his eyes, fighting back the fatigue that made every movement feel like a slow crawl. “Fuck. I’m not gonna take it. I’ll message him and call it off.” His tone was final, the exhaustion in his voice evident.
Berji stepped forward, trying to hide the urgency in his stance. "No, no, no. You earned it, Jimzon. You kick ass on and off the battlefield, man. Just... do it for us, okay? And if not for us, do it for the sick personal transport ship they’ll throw your way."
Jimzon squinted at him, a half-smile tugging at his lips, though it didn’t reach his eyes. "You just wanna joyride it, huh?"
Berji tried to keep his composure but couldn’t help chuckling. "You got me, bro. But seriously, we’re all excited for you." His voice softened as he looked at Jimzon, his expression sincere beneath the joke.
Jimzon’s gaze turned distant for a moment, his mind wandering to the conversation he had with the rest of the team just a few days ago. The weight of those words still lingered in his mind. “A few days ago, we were all talking about what we’re gonna do after enlistment’s over. If everyone’s moving away... maybe I should too. Start fresh somewhere. Somewhere far from all this. Maybe I shouldn't sign on for a few more years”
Berji’s expression shifted—his usual cocky grin softened to something more genuine, almost protective. He stepped forward, placing a firm hand on Jimzon’s shoulder, as if grounding him in the moment. “Don’t worry about that right now, bro. We’re in our prime now. Together. And with you leading us, I don’t think we’ve even peaked yet. You keep pushing forward, get a couple years of stacking cash, and when you leave, you’ll be in an even better spot. Castle Team’s not just a unit—it’s who we are, man. Always.” He rolled up his sleeve, revealing a tattoo etched deep into his forearm—a black dragon coiled around the words “Castle Team,” the emblem fierce and proud. "Just because our enlistment will be over, doesnt mean Castle Team is."
Jimzon’s gaze softened as he stared at the tattoo. The weight of their shared history, the bond they’d forged over years of blood, sweat, and sacrifice, was undeniable. He let out a sigh, his internal conflict swirling in his mind like a storm. “Guess a ship wouldn’t be too bad,” he said quietly, a reluctant smile tugging at the corners of his mouth
"More than just the ship, dude," Berji said, leaning against the doorframe with a sly grin, his voice light. "You’ll get to choose our ops, too. Think of it as a chance to explore those places you've always wanted to see."
His eyes relaxed, shaking off skepticism in his expression. "Maybe you're right. Could be nice, I guess."
Berji's grin widened, the excitement bubbling just beneath the surface. "Atta boy. The meeting’s in, uh... three hours, so, maybe let’s start with a shave?"
Jimzon reluctantly nodded. "Yeah, yeah, alright," he muttered, dragging a hand across his face as if waking up fully from his grogginess. He started to walk over to the bathroom sink, the faint hum of the electric razor coming to life as he began applying lime green shaving cream to his stubbled face.
Berji hesitated for a moment before speaking again, shifting his weight from one foot to the other. "Also, uh…" He scratched his neck, his voice a little more hesitant this time. "I think I need your help with a small favor."
Jimzon glanced at him over his shoulder, still rubbing the shaving cream into his face, his brow furrowed in confusion. "Yeah, what’s up?"
Berji fidgeted, glancing at the ground and then back at Jimzon, his eyes darting nervously. He leaned against the wall, clearly struggling with how to phrase his request. "It’s a small thing," he said, his tone sheepish, though the words didn’t match his body language.
"Like a wingman thing?" Jimzon teased, raising an eyebrow as he continued his shave.
"Uh, no, not exactly," Berji admitted, shifting uncomfortably. "Well, kinda, actually."
Jimzon paused, lowering the clippers slightly, his face now a mix of curiosity and concern. "What did you do this time?"
Berji scratched his head, his face flushing a little as he avoided Jimzon's gaze. "Last night, I—uh, I went to the Pub. Ended up drinking with some rookie riflemen. I know we drink, but these guys… they just love to get trashed. So, uh, so did I." His words trailed off, and Jimzon could sense the hesitation in his voice.
Jimzon’s electric razor buzzed louder as he continued to listen, still focused on his reflection in the mirror. "And?" he prompted, a slight edge to his tone, sensing there was more.
Berji bit his lip, clearly uncomfortable. "And... I got too drunk. I, uh... ended up drunk texting this lady I met. Who turned out to be Lile’s wife."
Jimzon’s clippers abruptly stopped, the buzzing cutting off sharply. He stared at his reflection for a moment, processing the absurdity of what Berji had just said. His eyes flickered to Berji, then back to the mirror. "Are you fucking retarded? Is this a joke?" he asked, his voice deadpan but laced with disbelief.
Berji's face flushed deeper, but not out of embarrassment for the imaginary drunken incident. It was the guilt of lying that really stung. "Not a joke, unfortunately. But…" He trailed off, almost hoping Jimzon wouldn’t push.
Jimzon’s jaw tightened, a mixture of frustration and genuine concern creeping into his tone. "You do realize he can just demote you to the academy, right? Like, even when I’m shitfaced, I’m not a fucking idiot like that," he snapped, shaking his head in disbelief.
Berji let out a long sigh, clearly uncomfortable with the entire situation but knowing there was no way out now. "But…" he started again, his voice quiet, almost pleading. "I know a way that won’t happen."
"I am not wingmanning you two," Jimzon said, wiping the shaving cream from his face with an exaggerated sigh. "I'm not going to make Lile’s wife fucking divorce him."
Berji shook his head quickly, the desperation clear in his eyes. "No, it’s way simpler than that. Since it’s the weekend, she won’t see those messages until tomorrow. So... I know a way to unsend them."
Jimzon raised an eyebrow, skepticism dripping from his tone. "Well, do it right?"
Berji let out a sharp breath, his nerves getting the better of him. "That’s where I need your help. I’ve got this device, it just needs to hook up to Lile’s computer. He and his wife share accounts, so I’ve already programmed it to delete the messages."
Jimzon chuckled at the absurdity of it all. "You want me to hack Lile’s computer during Captain meeting? I don’t know about that man," he said, returning to the task of trimming his hair, clearly conflicted.
"Please, man." Berji’s voice dropped into a pleading tone.
Jimzon paused, his clippers buzzing in the silence between them. "Look, I can try to talk to Lile about it, so you don’t get demoted. But maybe there's a lesson here. Stop flirting with every woman you see—it’s actually a pain sometimes."
Berji’s face fell at the reprimand. "C’mon, man. I don’t wanna be an asshole, but I just helped you out with a pretty big favor." His tone was frustrated, but his voice softened with regret.
Jimzon sighed, remembering the Nhath mission. That was a big ask, and Berji had been there for him. "Alright.Yeah. Sorry for snapping at you, yeah. You've been a great friend. You've seen me fuck up, so… yeah, I’ll do it. It’s not traceable, right?"
"Of course, believe me," Berji said, his voice firm but sincere. "This thing is foolproof. I don’t want it traced back to me either."
Jimzon wiped his face with a towel and took one last glance at his clean-shaven reflection in the mirror. He turned back to Berji with a resigned look. "Alright. I hope you learned your lesson though. I’ll get it done."
Berji’s face lit up as he reached out his hand, a wide grin spreading across his face. "Thank you, brother. This is huge."
Jimzon gave a small smile, dapping him up. "Big day for both of us, I guess."
"You said it," Berji replied, his grin unshakable. 
Jimzon had no idea that, in agreeing to help, he had just unknowingly become the central figure in a treacherous conspiracy—one that would unleash the most devastating plot the Guard would ever face.

Chapter 12:

(context: jimzon is meeting director liles for promotion, but his recent ex gf kelsa is there, but hes starting to have a clear head abt things)

	Jimzon stood outside the heavy oak door of Liles' office, rolling his shoulders as if shaking off unseen weights. He reached into his pocket and pulled out the small device Berji had given him. Just a simple task—place it on Liles' terminal, wait for the light to turn red, then slip it back into his pocket. Easy.
His fingers curled around it for a moment longer before he tucked it away. He straightened his uniform, brushing off near invisible specks of lint, more out of habit than necessity. The slender azure coat fit stiffly over his shoulders, gold buttons catching the light, too polished, too precise. His white gloves felt like they belonged to someone else's hands. A neat arrangement of medals sat over his heart—a splash of color against an otherwise cold canvas.
He adjusted his hat and exhaled. No real reason to be nervous. And yet, his pulse beat against his temples, steady but insistent.
He never felt like himself in this uniform. Too formal. Too rigid. Too impersonal. Even when lower ranks saluted him in the halls, their respect felt like a barrier rather than an honor. This outfit didn’t make him a leader. It made him untouchable.
With one final breath, he pushed open the door.
Fortunately, none of these feelings showed on his face. He moved with measured confidence, striding toward the secretary’s desk.
“Hey…” His voice faltered. “Kelsa?”
She looked up, her expression neutral—polite enough to pass as friendly to anyone else, but he knew her warmth ran deeper than that.
“Jimzon. Hope you’re well. I take it you have your meeting with Liles now?”
“Yeah. I’m a few minutes early, but…” His eyes flicked to the desk. “So, you’re a secretary now?”
“Not quite.” She gave a polite smile. “Still a pilot. Just filling in for Miss Liles while she’s recovering.”
Jimzon lowered his voice. “Oh. She got a hangover?”
Kelsa frowned slightly. “Nope…" she raised an eyebrow "she’s been out for a few days. Ulcers. Too much warp driving at her age. Figured I’d do her a favor.”
Jimzon felt a pang of guilt. “Ah. Yeah. Well, uh… tell her I wish her the best.”
“I’m sure she’d appreciate that,” Kelsa said, tilting her head. “Though maybe she’d rather hear it from you.”
He hesitated. “Yeah, uh… I’ll get back to her.”
“She still talks about you.”
His chest tightened, guilt pressing down. His eyes flickered away for a moment. “I still think about her a lot.”
“Just tell her. She took care of you those first few years.”
“Yeah… she was real kind.” Jimzon exhaled, rubbing the back of his neck. “I just don’t like thinking back to those academy days. It’s not that I meant to ignore her—I just got so focused on moving forward, on leaving the past behind, that I didn’t realize I was shutting out the good people along with it.”
Kelsa studied him for a moment before nodding, her approval cautious but sincere. “Good luck on your promotion, Jim.”
“Thank you, Sergeant.”
Jimzon took a seat, a strange feeling settling over him. Kelsa wishing him luck was nice. Bumping into his ex wasn’t exactly on his to-do list, and neither was being reminded of the woman who took him in all those years ago. But maybe Kelsa had a point. Maybe reconnecting wouldn’t be so bad.
	Liles was the one who pulled him out from the floorboards that day. The memories were old, blurred at the edges, but he could still recall celebrating his eighth birthday with Liles and his then-girlfriend—now wife. A small cake, a cheap dinner, laughter that felt borrowed but warm. It wasn’t that he forgot. It just wasn’t something he carried with him.
They had been kind—more than kind—but they were still just kids themselves, barely out of college, doing what they could for a boy with nowhere else to go. And he had been grateful. But the closer he got, the more he worried the ghosts of his parents might get jealous. So he buried himself in his studies, convinced himself he didn’t need anyone. Time passed, and the distance widened. At some point, the guilt of pulling away became its own reason to stay away. Holidays turned into obligations. Conversations felt rehearsed, polite, distant. They were always kind, but kindness just made it worse.
Maybe that was on him. Maybe it wouldn’t be so bad to fix things.
He had introduced Kelsa to Miss Liles once. It was good they kept in touch, he supposed. Maybe it meant the door had never fully closed.
But it was strange—Berji swore he saw her while he was out drinking. If she’d been sick, that didn’t add up. Then again, knowing that man’s beer goggles, nothing could surprise him anymore.
Director Liles' mahogany door swung open with a soft creak. "Jimzon! Just the man I wanted to see!" Liles' voice was warm, filled with a genuine enthusiasm that immediately softened the sharp edges of the Mothership’s sterile atmosphere. He slapped Jimzon on the back—firm, but not too much—and motioned to the chair across from his desk. "Take a seat, buddy."
Jimzon lowered himself into the chair, his body sinking into the worn leather, feeling an odd comfort in the rustic charm of the room. The shelves of leather-bound books, the faded maps lining the walls, and the soft glow of antique globes gave the space an almost earthy feel—so unlike the cold, clinical corridors of the Mothership. A lingering trace of cigar smoke clung to the air, and for a fleeting moment, Jimzon could almost imagine himself back on solid ground. "Thank you, sir," he said, his voice steady but with a subtle edge of relief.
Liles waved off the formality with a chuckle. "Didn't mean to keep you waiting. You know how much of a pain paperwork can be."
Jimzon smiled, nodding slightly. "Of course, sir."
Liles' tone shifted, becoming a little more apologetic as he leaned forward, folding his hands on the desk. "Ah, I should’ve given you a heads-up about Kelsa. My apologies, Jimzy. Hope it wasn’t too awkward for you."
Jimzon straightened up, his voice measured. "Not at all, sir. Actually, it’s reassuring that we can keep things professional." He paused, then added carefully, "She mentioned your wife... my condolences about her ulcers."
Liles gave a soft laugh, a touch of melancholy in it. "Happens to the best of us." His expression lightened. "Between you and me, buddy, it probably wasn’t just the warp drive. Could’ve been the astrovin, too." He winked, as if sharing an inside joke. "You know how much we both enjoy our wine."
Jimzon’s lips twitched into a small chuckle, though he wasn’t sure if it was out of sympathy or because he truly understood the toll alcohol could take. He’d had his own run-ins with the stuff, and it always seemed to sneak up on you when you least expected it.
"Anywho," Liles said, glancing at his terminal before checking the clock. "Let’s get this started."
Jimzon straightened up, alert but calm.
"Jimzon, I’m not sure how formal you want this meeting to be, but let’s just say well… you’ve earned it. Loyalty, courage, a strong work ethic, adaptability—everything you need to be a top-tier soldier, and then some. A few years ago, I’d worried that promoting you might look like nepotism. But now, it’s clear—you’ve truly found your place here. We even talked to your team. You guys are a solid unit. High marks across the board, consistent results, and a flawless casualty record on every mission. You’re a rare breed, buddy. I think it’s time you earned that Captain’s rank."
Jimzon paused for a moment, taking it in. "That means a lot, sir."
Liles smiled, his tone shifting to something lighter. "Well, consider it done. I’ll get the paperwork filed by the end of the day. Since we’re ahead of schedule, let’s move on to the fun part."
Jimzon leaned forward, curiosity piqued. "Wow, already?"
"Efficiency," Liles said with a grin, "efficiency, efficiency, efficiency. That’s my motto. First order of business: upgraded sleeping quarters. Now, would you prefer top-floor suites, or something closer to the Captain’s Club?"
Jimzon hesitated for a moment, considering. "Actually, sir, I was thinking about keeping my current dorm."
Liles raised an eyebrow, clearly surprised. "Jimzy, you’ve made it. This is what it’s all about. If you’re worried about moving your stuff, we can always send some Privates to take care of that for you."
That struck Jimzon as funny. He could fit all the contents of his room in a backpack. He couldn’t imagine the headache it would be to move Berji’s room
Shit. Shit. Shit. He was literally talking about Mrs. Liles. How had he forgotten? The skeleton drive, or whatever it was called. Things were moving too fast, and he wasn’t prepared. He needed to keep Liles distracted. Luckily, he knew the man well. All he had to do was keep him talking.
"I just like how close I am to my team," Jimzon said, leaning back slightly in his chair. "I wouldn’t want them thinking I’m above them. But a change of scenery might be nice. Your office has always had a cool vibe."
Liles gave a knowing smile. "Thanks, Jimzy. Even back when I was younger, they said I was old-fashioned. But it’s nice to hear you think so."
"Yeah, those maps you’ve got up. I love the stories they tell." He said, trying to buy some time.
"You recognize them?" Liles asked, intrigued.
"A little bit," Jimzon replied, nodding toward the map behind Liles. "But what’s that one in the center? Where’s it from?"
Liles’ eyes brightened. "Ah, you’ve got a good eye." He turned his chair slightly, facing the map, before looking back at Jimzon. "That one’s a real gem."
Jimzon casually slid the skeleton key out of his pocket, discreetly pushing it under his thigh. He had to get it out of sight. "Yeah, it’s a battle, right?"
"Bingo," Liles replied, his eyes focused on the map again. Jimzon carefully nudged the key with his shoe, aiming to slide it onto the console beneath his feet. The magnet might just do the trick.
"The Battle of Rudcuff," Liles said, his tone softening. "It was a hard day, but we earned that victory."
Jimzon struggled to attach the skeleton key to the console without drawing attention. His fingers fumbled slightly, but he kept his expression neutral, hoping Liles wouldn’t notice. "That was one of the first battles of the Guard, right?"
Liles chuckled softly, shaking his head. "For someone who’s as great a soldier as you, history was never your strong suit." He leaned back in his chair, a fond glint in his eye as he continued. "But yeah it was within the first few years. Our first and second battalions—at the time, they were the whole damn army—all volunteers. We were sent to take a colony from some Targaths. Nasty creatures. They’d burrow deep into the ground, slowly building this magnetic web under the crust, completely undetectable. As soon as we landed, they somehow activated it. The shockwaves tore through our fleet as they began to fall. Crashed half of the Outer Rim ships right into the surface." Liles paused, his voice softening, remembering the grim scene. "It rained hell on those soldiers. Their armor, their weapons—they just got pulled straight down to the floor. They couldn’t move. They couldn’t fight back. Or maybe they did, no one really knows. There just wasn't anybody left when my unit came as reinforcements."
Jimzon, still focused on the terminal, slid the skeleton key into place with a quiet thud. His heartbeat quickened, but he forced a small, nonchalant cough, hoping to cover the noise.
Liles didn’t seem to notice. He continued with a distant look in his eyes, clearly lost in the memory. "Those men were real heroes. But thankfully, we’ll never return to those days. The galaxy's a lot safer now, thanks to people like you."
Jimzon nodded slowly, his voice more reflective than usual. "That’s... intense. I don’t know how they managed it back then.
"I wonder too some nights," Liles mused, leaning back in his chair, his fingers tapping absently on the desk. "Sometimes I wonder if we're too soft these days."
Jimzon chuckled, his tone light. "Us? Nah. Them? Absolutely."
