Liles laughed in return, the sound warm but tinged with a touch of bitterness. "Glad to hear that. Maybe I’m just getting old, complaining about the new grunts. Who knows?" He leaned forward again, shaking his head as if clearing the thought. "Anyways, enough rambling.
Your personal cruiser—it's docked at Bay 23, ready for your orders. What color are you thinking?"
Jimzon had been mulling it over for a while, and now the choice felt right. "Purple."
Liles raised an eyebrow, clearly surprised. "Purple, huh? Interesting. I expected azure, something a little less flashy. But hey, guess anyone would want to flaunt their new ride."
Jimzon's eyes are momentarily distant. "Purple’s the one. It feels right."
Liles nodded, clearly accepting it. "Fair enough. You have a name for it?"
"The Powitt."
Liles tilted his head, intrigued. "Sounds exotic. Is that a royal name?"
Jimzon's gaze briefly flickered to the floor as memories of his late brother surfaced. He swallowed before responding, voice steady but a touch somber. "Not quite, sir. It's Modanick."
Liles leaned back in his chair, the name seeming to settle in his mind. "Badass." He began typing something into his terminal, then froze, a frown crossing his face. "Wait a minute... this thing’s acting up. Damn terminal’s frozen."
Jimzon nervously glanced at the terminal. The skeleton key still hadn’t lit up. Why was it taking so long?
Liles, oblivious to Jimzon’s tension, scribbled something on a notepad. "Well, anyways," he said, not looking up, "I can just write up the form for now. Purple, The Powitt... alright, looks like we're all set for today," Before pausing with a much more serious tone. "But there’s something I wanted to discuss with you."
"Of course, sir." Jimzon’s voice was steady, but there was an edge of uncertainty behind the words.
Liles paused, his expression softening. "You seem a lot more clear-headed since the Nhath op. I have to admit, I was a bit hesitant, but I’m glad to see you’re doing better."
Jimzon nodded, his voice sincere. "Thank you, sir."
Liles extended his hand across the desk, a gesture that felt like a formal yet warm acknowledgment. "Any other forms we need will be sent your way, but as soon as you leave this door, you’re officially Captain Jimzon Avaris. Nice ring to it, doesn’t it?"
Jimzon’s gaze flicked toward the skeleton key once more. His breath hitched as the light finally flashed red. With a small shift in posture, he stood up, deliberately dropping his hat to the floor, and with purpose, he shook Liles' hand firmly.
	"You're not far from being a Director, Jimzon. Keep up the great work. Let me get that hat for you."
Jimzon quickly crouched down, picking up his hat and tossing the drive into it before thrusting it back on his head. "No need. Don’t want your old man knees to give out."
Liles chuckled, clearly unaware of the heist Jimzon had just pulled off. "Very funny."
"See you soon, Liles," Jimzon said, eager to head back to his room.
Just as he turned to leave, Liles called out. "Hey, Jimzy, what’s that?"
Jimzon froze, his hand on the door handle, his heart skipping a beat. "What is what, sir?"
He turned to see Liles pointing directly at him.
Shit. Shit. Shit. How did he know?
Liles squinted, his gaze fixed on Jimzon’s chest. "Those medals… you’ve got the bottom row in reverse order. Don’t let any MP like Sergeant Cotts see that. Be careful out there. You represent the Guard even more now."
Jimzon’s breath caught in his throat. "Yes, sir. Of course, sir."
With that, he quickly stepped out the door, relief washing over him the second it closed behind him.

Chapter 13:

Jimzon stared at it. The Powitt.

It was the ship he had always dreamed about. Deep purple hull, trimmed with clean yellow highlights. Just big enough to carry five men and some cargo, yet sleek. Compact, balanced. Elegant without showing off. It had a back gunner, dual turbines, and armor laced with quantum threads that shimmered faintly under the hangar lights. Grav-rudder stabilizers meant it could pirouette through tight debris fields like a knife through smoke.
He’d dreamed about ships like this since before he even knew words for them. Now it was real. Sitting there, quiet and perfect.
	He had found his home at the Guard. He had actually made something out of his life even when he had every excuse not to. The best part is, he had earned every bit of it. No handouts, no shortcuts, just hard work. 
He took a breath, then pulled out his pager.
"Castle Team, if you can make it—I’ve got a surprise in Bay 23.”
The message sent, he looked up again. It glimmered like gold, illuminated by the hangar forcefield behind it. For the first time in a long time, it felt like something was truly his.
Berji was the first one down the elevator. He stepped out with a mix of nerves and excitement, eyes locking on the ship before walking up to Jimzon. “That was fast. Like... real fast.”
Jimzon gave a small grin. “Turns out Liles knew me better than I thought. He ordered it weeks ago. Just needed the name painted on.” He glanced back at the ship, then at Berji. “You’ll get yours soon enough. Green and bronze, right?”
“Yes, sir,” Berji said with a grin.
Jimzon raised an eyebrow, smiling faintly. “Thanks, man—but don’t call me that. Still feels… weird.” He reached into his jacket and pulled out a slim skeleton key. “Anyway. Before I forget.”
He tossed it toward Berji, who—caught off guard—fumbled it before finally catching it against his chest.
“You got it? Everything?” Berji asked, eyes narrowing slightly.
“Waited for it to turn red, just like you said,” Jimzon replied. He teasingly wagged his finger. “You owe me one.”
Berji stared at the key, trying not to look as relieved as he felt. “Yeah, man… wow. That’s a weight off.”
Castle Team had no idea the weight that key held. What was on that drive could topple empires.
The elevator doors hissed again, and Yake and Pavlock stepped out, talking over each other.
“Yoooo, this is yours? No way,” Pavlock said, wide-eyed.
Yake followed, eyeing the hull. “Purple?” He gave it a light slap. “You sure a man like you can handle this kind of ride?”
Jimzon grinned. “Can’t believe it’s all mine. Mostly because it’s not. It’s ours. Next PTO we get, we’re taking it somewhere quiet.”
“Somewhere warm,” Pavlock added, “but maybe not a place that fries my skin off while I’m tanning. The Nhaths live in some real shit holes.”
Jimzon chuckled—genuinely. It was the first time in a while that word didn’t sting. “Just sun and silence. That’s all I want.”
“Congrats on finally getting your pilot’s license, brotha,” Pav said.
Jimzon laughed. “Technically not yet. That’s why I’ve got Jax—and this guy.” He pointed a thumb at Berji, who stood nearby, looking a little pale.
“You shipsick already?” Jimzon asked. “We haven’t even stepped into the Powitt.”
“Something like that,” Berji muttered, eyes still on the key.
“We celebrating with beers?” Pav asked, already half-expecting the answer.
“I was thinking we joyride to the belts of J.LOT for dinner,” Jimzon said.
“Normally I’d call shotgun,” Yake added, “but unfortunately, no takeoffs until 0600.”
Pav threw up his hands. “Beers it is!” he cheered.

He told himself not to get used to this. Good things didn’t last. But this… this felt like it might.

Radar says two—no, three inbound!" Pavlock shouted, the red lights of the console pulsing across his face. Sweat glistened on his brow as he squinted at the incoming blips. "Who's on the turret?!" he bellowed over the thunder of the engine.

Wake up! Jimzy, please!" A voice—urgent, trembling—pierced the void. Female. A soft hand found his face, fingers dusty and trembling. Not striking—begging.

Chapter 17
A bead of blood traced its way down Jimzon’s forehead, glistening briefly before dripping onto the scorched floor of the smoky cabin. Another followed, carving a crimson path through the dried blood already caked across his face. His body, still secured by the restraints of his chair, hung limp and unnatural, his chin slumped against his chest.
The cabin was steeped in chaos, an eerie, mechanical dirge filling the space. An ominous red light pulsed slowly, bathing the room in intermittent flashes of warning. The flickering of dimmed panel lights offered brief glimpses of the destruction—exposed wires sparked sporadically, sending tendrils of acrid smoke curling through the air. Broken controls jutted out at odd angles, their usefulness now obliterated.
Suddenly, Jimzon’s chest heaved violently, a fit of coughing breaking the room’s oppressive silence. His body jerked upright as he gasped for air, his breaths ragged and shallow. Shaking hands clawed at the harness, fumbling to release himself. With a final tug, the straps gave way, and he collapsed to the floor in a graceless heap. His palms pressed against the ground as he tried to steady himself, his head pounding like a war drum, the dizziness threatening to drag him back into unconsciousness.
Forcing his eyes open, he looked ahead and froze. Where the wall of his ship once stood was now a jagged, gaping void. Beyond it, the world outside gleamed in stark contrast to the devastation within.
A forest stretched before him, both alien and magnificent. Towering trees, their trunks so broad they seemed like the foundations of some ancient, forgotten city, loomed in every direction. Their bark shimmered faintly, as though infused with a metallic luster, and their canopy wove an intricate tapestry of green so thick that only narrow shafts of sunlight filtered through.
Where the light managed to break through, it cast radiant beams that danced across the ground, illuminating the strange flora below.
Soft, emerald grass rolled out like a carpet beneath the trees, interrupted only by clusters of alien vegetation. Flowers with translucent petals glowed faintly, their colors shifting with each tilt of the light. Azure mushrooms, some as large as chairs, dotted the landscape, their caps glistening with dewdrops or perhaps some other secretion. Vines coiled lazily around the wreckage of his ship, as though nature had already begun to claim it.
The air was thick with an earthy aroma, tinged with a faint sweetness that Jimzon could taste with every labored breath. He struggled to his feet, each movement agonizing and unsteady, and staggered toward the jagged edge of his ship. His boots crunched against debris, shards of glass and twisted metal strewn like forgotten relics.
His first step outside was tentative, his legs wobbling beneath him. The planet’s ground was surprisingly soft, cushioning his weight as though it had been prepared for him. He inhaled deeply, the air cooler and richer than anything he had known aboard his ship.
Jimzon stood there, silhouetted against the wreckage of the Powitt, his gaze sweeping over this uncharted world. It was a strange blend of beauty and mystery, a paradise stitched together with hints of danger. As his senses gradually returned, so too did the questions.
	Jimzon’s eyes widened in alarm as realization dawned on him. His head whipped back toward the battered shell of his ship. The Powitt, once sleek and radiant with its glimmering purple paint job, now sat in ruin, its once-pristine exterior crumpled and filthy. Dirt streaked along its hull in chaotic smears, marking the violent path it had carved through the forest upon impact. Behind it stretched a long, jagged trench in the earth, nearly a mile of churned soil and shattered vegetation.
Without hesitation, he turned and darted back into the ship. He raised an arm to shield his face from the thick, acrid smoke that still hung in the air like a shroud. The smell of burnt metal and scorched wiring stung his nostrils as he stumbled forward, his boots crunching against debris scattered across the cabin floor.
Each step toward the Gunner's seat felt like a battle. The thick haze robbed him of sight and choked his lungs. He tried to yell, but the words turned to ragged coughs, his voice breaking under the strain. Staggering, he forced himself onward, his throat burning as he cleared it. Finally, through the oppressive fog, he found his voice.
“Pav… Pav!” he shouted hoarsely, his desperation cracking his words. “Pav!”
His voice wavered, teetering on the edge of panic. Fear coiled in his chest, threatening to overwhelm him. Then he saw him. Slumped motionless in the Gunner’s seat was Pavlock.
Jimzon’s heart dropped at the sight. Pav, who was always brimming with life and energy, seemed unnervingly still. “No… no, no, no,” Jimzon murmured, rushing to his friend’s side. His hands trembled as he grabbed Pav by the shoulders, shaking him gently. “Pav! Wake up!” he pleaded, his voice cracking with every syllable.
When the shaking yielded no response, Jimzon grew more frantic. He began to lightly slap Pav’s face, his tone shifting to a desperate whisper. “Pav… Pav… Pav, come on.” The words tumbled from his lips like a prayer.
Finally, he reached for the buckle of Pav’s harness, fumbling with the release mechanism. With a soft click, the straps gave way, and Pav’s body slumped forward, limp and heavy. Grunting under the weight, Jimzon hoisted Pav onto his shoulder, staggering back through the smoke-filled cabin. Each step felt like an eternity, the suffocating air threatening to drain what little strength he had left.
As he stumbled outside into the open air, the sunlight cut through the haze like a lifeline. Jimzon’s legs buckled, and he collapsed onto the soft grass, lowering Pav to the ground beside him. His chest heaved as he struggled to catch his breath.
With shaking hands, Jimzon pressed two fingers against Pav’s jugular. For a moment, terror gripped him, and he froze, fearing the worst. Then, a faint, steady pulse met his touch. Relief flooded his body like a tidal wave, and he quickly moved to check Pav’s breathing. He held the back of his hand beneath Pav’s nostrils, exhaling sharply when he felt the subtle warmth of each breath.
“Thank God,” he whispered, his voice barely audible.
The adrenaline that had carried him this far began to wane, and the weight of everything crashed down on him. Overwhelmed, Jimzon sat back on his heels, covering his face with his hands. He squeezed his eyes shut tightly, trying to block out the world, if only for a moment. The crash, the smoke, the fear—it was all too much.
But they weren’t safe yet. Castle Team was still scattered, still incomplete. He clenched his fists, letting out a guttural grunt of frustration as he pushed himself back to his feet.
“Yake… Berji…” he muttered, steeling himself.
With a final glance at Pav, who lay breathing but unconscious on the grass, Jimzon turned and made his way back into the wreckage of the Powitt. His team needed him, and he wasn’t about to leave them behind.
	Pavlock grunted and began to roll on his side. "Fuck am I hungover.. or something" he said all dazed and confused running his hands across the blades of grass. blinking slowly. His posture was if he tried to catch his balance, despite laying on the ground.
	Jimzon looked at his poor friend that he dragged into this mess with. "Hey man we'll get you some water, just relax for right all right? I'll be right back. 
“Fuck,” he muttered "am I hungover... or something?” His slurred words thick with confusion as he rubbed his temples, trying to piece together the chaos in his head. His posture wavered, as if his body were fighting to find balance, even though he was lying flat on the ground.
Jimzon crouched next to his friend, his face a mixture of relief and concern. He placed a steadying hand on Pavlock’s shoulder. “Hey, man, just take it easy,” he said, his tone gentle but firm. “We’ll get you some water. Just relax for now, all right? Stay right here. I’ll be right back.” Pavlock gave a faint, dazed nod, his focus drifting as Jimzon stood and turned back toward the wreckage.
Jimzon’s heart pounded as he stormed into the Powitt once more, the acrid smoke stinging his eyes and throat. His boots crunched over shards of metal and shattered glass as he navigated the wrecked cabin. Every step felt like a race against time.
“Yake?” he called out, his voice hoarse but urgent. “Yake!” He moved through the dim, smoky space, his eyes scanning frantically. Yake had been strapped in just a few feet away during the crash. He should still be here.
Jimzon’s breath caught as he reached Yake’s seat. The harness had been unbuckled, the straps hanging limply over the sides of the chair. His heart sank as he noticed the trail of blood leading away from the seat. A series of smeared, bloody footprints and handprints marked the floor and the walls, disappearing into the haze ahead.
He knelt beside the chair, his hand brushing over the abandoned harness. “Shit…” he whispered under his breath, his mind racing. The footprints led toward the ship’s exit, but the trail abruptly vanished the moment it left the wreckage.
Jimzon swallowed hard, his chest tightening with worry. Wherever Yake was, he must have been alive—alive enough to walk. He must be okay - right?
He pushed back to his feet and hurried outside, his boots crunching over debris. The sunlight hit him like a slap to the face as he emerged from the smoky confines of the ship. Pavlock was sitting up now, his hands braced against the ground as he tried to steady himself. His sweat-soaked face tilted upward, and his gaze met Jimzon’s as he approached.
“Hey, Pav,” Jimzon said, kneeling next to him again. “Have you seen the cockpit? Do you see where it ended up?”
Pavlock blinked a few times, his vision slowly sharpening. He wiped his forehead with the back of his hand, smearing a mix of sweat and grime. “Yeah… uh…” he started, his voice still groggy. He wiped his eyes again, trying to clear his head.
“It’s behind the wreck,” he said finally, pointing a shaky finger to Jimzon’s left. “Over there. That tree.”
Jimzon turned to look where Pav was pointing. His stomach dropped when he saw it. Another chunk of the Powitt’s twisted remains lay wedged against the base of a massive tree. The cockpit, unmistakable with its reinforced windows, was upside down, its frame bent and crumpled as if it had been hurled like a toy.
Jimzon felt a pang of dread in his gut, but he didn’t hesitate. “Stay here,” he told Pavlock firmly, rising to his feet. “Don’t move. I’ll check it out.”
Pavlock didn’t respond, his gaze distant as he tried to process the chaos around him.
Jimzon steeled himself and began to make his way toward the cockpit, his heart hammering in his chest with each step. The closer he got, the more the scene unfolded in grim detail. The once-pristine, reinforced glass of the cockpit—designed to withstand the vacuum of space and the onslaught of enemy fire—was now shattered. Razor-sharp shards glinted in the sunlight, scattered across the ground like deadly confetti.
He paused in front of the wreckage, taking in the twisted frame of the cockpit wedged firmly against the tree. Smoke billowed from gaps in the mangled hull, and faint crackles of electricity punctuated the eerie quiet. With a sharp inhale, Jimzon crouched, braced himself, and jumped. His hands gripped the jagged edge of the cockpit, and he hoisted himself inside with a grunt.
The smell of burnt metal and scorched wiring hit him like a wave, stinging his eyes and filling his lungs. He coughed, shielding his mouth with his arm as he pushed forward. The interior was a disorienting mess; with the cockpit upside down, every surface seemed out of place.
And then he saw him.
Berji was still strapped into his chair, but the inverted orientation meant he was slumped awkwardly against the ceiling. His arms hung limply above his head, swaying slightly with the subtle tremors of the wreckage. Smoke obscured most of his figure, but Jimzon could just make out his silhouette, slack and motionless.
“Berj!” Jimzon yelled, his voice cutting through the suffocating haze. His heart raced as he waited for a response.
A muffled groan emerged from Berji’s lips—a small, guttural sound, but enough to send a wave of relief washing over Jimzon. He was alive.
“Hold on, brother,” Jimzon muttered as he reached for his belt. With a quick flick of his wrist, he activated his plasma blade. The brilliant, searing light illuminated the smoky cabin, casting sharp shadows on the mangled walls. Jimzon worked quickly, slicing through the harness straps that held Berji in place.
The moment the last strap gave way, Berji’s body fell. Jimzon lunged forward, catching him before he hit the jagged remains of the console. “I gotcha, brother,” Jimzon whispered, his voice steady despite the panic clawing at his chest. “It’s alright. I’ve got you.”
Berji’s weight was dead in his arms, his face turned downward and barely visible through the thick smoke. Jimzon could feel the heat of the smoldering metal around them and knew he didn’t have much time. He adjusted his grip, hoisting Berji over his shoulder as he made his way back through the wreckage.
The bright light of the outside world hit him like a lifeline as he staggered out of the cockpit. His boots found the soft grass again, and he carefully lowered Berji to the ground beside Pavlock, who was now sitting upright, watching with concern.
But as Jimzon laid Berji on his back, his breath caught in his throat.
As Jimzon laid Berji down on the grass, he finally saw the full extent of the damage—and it was horrifying.
Berji’s face was… a mess. A deep gash split through the middle of it, cutting down to the bone. His friend's nose was completely torn off his face, leaving nothing but a dark, hollow hole in the middle of his face as if he were looking at a skeleton. Blood streamed from the wound, soaking what was left of his uniform and pooling on the ground beneath him.
His lips too were ripped off, exposing his teeth in a grotesque, permanent snarl. The skin and muscle on his right cheek were gone, leaving raw flesh and bone exposed. His right eye was missing, just gone, a mangled socket that leaked blood and fluid down his ruined face. The left eye was swollen shut, surrounded by bruised, purple skin that throbbed with every shallow breath he took.
Shards of glass and pieces of metal were stuck in his skin, digging into his flesh like tiny daggers. His face and neck were covered in dirt and soot, clinging to the blood and making it look even worse. The smell of burnt flesh and sweat hung in the air, heavy and sickening.
Jimzon stared in disbelief. This wasn’t just a wound—it was carnage. His friend, someone who had always been full of life, was lying there, barely holding on.
Berji’s good eye cracked open, just barely. His gaze found Jimzon, and the look in his eye hit Jimzon like a punch to the gut. It wasn’t just pain—there was fear. Desperation. He was scared.
Berji let out a low, guttural groan, his breath wheezing out in short, labored gasps. Every exhale sounded wet and rattling, like something was broken inside him.
“Berj…” Jimzon said, his voice shaking. He reached out and put a hand on Berji’s shoulder, his fingers trembling. He didn't know what to do.
Berji’s hand shot up, grabbing Jimzon’s arm with more strength than Jimzon expected. His grip was tight, his fingers digging into Jimzon’s sleeve as if holding on for dear life. His body shook with every shallow breath, his chest barely rising and falling.
Jimzon leaned closer, his heart pounding. “I’m here, man. It's okay,” he said, trying to keep his voice steady.
Berji didn’t say anything—he couldn’t. He just stared at Jimzon with that one bloodshot eye, his grip never loosening. It was like he was begging him not to leave, not to let go.
Jimzon swallowed hard, his mind racing. His friend was alive, but barely. The damage was too much, and Jimzon had no idea how to fix it. All he could do was sit there, holding Berji’s hand, and try to push down the overwhelming fear that he might lose him any second.
Jimzon cradled Berji’s battered body, his mind racing as he tried to push away the dread creeping into his thoughts. He couldn’t let himself believe it was hopeless—not yet.
Behind him, Pavlock stumbled closer, his footsteps crunching softly on the grass. When he caught sight of Berji’s face, he froze in place, his eyes wide with shock. His mouth opened, but no words came out at first, just a sharp intake of breath. Finally, his voice broke the silence, shaky and uneven.
“My gods…” Pavlock stammered, his voice barely above a whisper. He stood there, rooted to the spot, his face pale as the reality of the situation sank in. His gaze locked with Berji’s, and for a moment, neither of them moved—just two men on opposite ends of incomprehensible suffering.
“Gods…” Pavlock said again, his voice trailing off into a horrified mutter. He shook his head as if trying to snap himself out of a nightmare. “Yake isn’t… he isn’t like this too, is he?”
Jimzon’s jaw tightened, and he forced himself to focus. “No,” he said, though his voice wavered. “Yake’s… he’s disappeared. I don’t know. I don’t know where the fuck he went. There’s no trail, no sign of him—nothing.He’s just gone.”
Pavlock blinked, his mind trying to process what Jimzon was saying, but his focus kept snapping back to Berji.
“Here, help me,” Jimzon said, his tone urgent now. “We’ve gotta get him out of here. Find help. Medicine. Something. Anything. He won’t last much longer like this.”
Pavlock hesitated for just a moment longer before nodding, snapping out of his daze. “Yeah. Yeah, you’re right,” he said, his voice steadier now, though still trembling with the weight of what he was seeing.
He knelt beside Jimzon, carefully sliding his hands under Berji’s broken body. Together, they managed to lift him, his weight pressing heavily on their shoulders. Pavlock gritted his teeth, steadying himself as Berji’s blood seeped onto his hands and arms, sticky and warm.
Berji let out a faint groan, his head lolling to one side. Every shallow breath he took sounded agonizing, each one a small victory against the odds.
“Hang in there, brother,” Jimzon muttered, half to Berji and half to himself.
With Berji slumped between them, his legs dragging behind and leaving faint streaks of blood on the grass, they began their slow, grueling journey deep into the forest.

Chapter 18

Castle Team, or at least Jimzon and Pav, had been wandering for what felt like days. So much weighed on their minds, yet barely a word passed between them. They were still carrying Berji’s body, stopping every few miles on their trek to check if he was still breathing. His groans were quieter now, faint and fading, but the bleeding hadn’t stopped.
Sometimes it felt like they had passed the same cluster of trees or the same patch of bulbous mushrooms, walking in endless circles. But the smeared trail of Berji’s blood along the foliage confirmed they were moving forward, just slowly. Painfully.
“Let’s stop here. I think I see some berries,” Jimzon said, his voice hollow.
They gently lowered Berji and propped him against a jagged rock. Pav crouched beside him, deliberately avoiding eye contact with the half-conscious man. He swatted away insects buzzing near Berji’s face, swatting away a few off his exposed cheek. Jimzon returned moments later, hands trembling, holding a small bunch of teal berries cupped in his palms.
“Can we eat these?” Pav asked, after Jimzon dropped a few into his hands.
“Maybe,” Jimzon replied. “I saw some small varmint eating this type earlier on the walk. I don’t think we have much of a choice.”
He stared at the berries. The fuzz on their skin clung to his tongue as he popped one in. When he bit down, it tasted like freshly waxed wood—bitter, unnatural, and wrong.
“How do they taste?” Pav asked.
“Beggars can’t be choosers,” Jimzon said flatly. “Could be worse.”
Pav hesitated, then dropped one into his mouth. He spit it out immediately. Jimzon almost cracked a smile. He knew Pav wouldn’t eat anything outside his rotation of the same three meals.
“I’ll wait till we find something else,” Pav muttered.
“I don’t think we can afford waiting,” Jimzon said. His voice was low, the edges frayed. “We shouldn’t even be alive.”
Pav struggled for words. “But we are.”
Jimzon looked at Berji, struggling to breathe, as he watched him cough up blood-laced drool. Something twisted inside him.
“Are we?” he muttered. “Are we actually alive? Or are we just barely surviving?”
He ate another berry, grimacing, then let the rest fall from his hand and scatter on the ground.
“We gotta talk about Berj.”
“What about him? There’s life everywhere on this planet—we can find someone to help,” Pav said.
Jimzon gritted his teeth. He hated what he was about to say. Maybe it was the hunger, the first real hunger in a long time, making him think this way.
“Exactly. There’s life everywhere. And we’re wanted men. We’ve left a perfect blood trail from the crash site to right here. Whether it’s bounty hunters or some wild animal, we’ve already pushed our luck as is.”
Pav looked at him with furrowed eyes. “What are you saying?”
“You know what I’m saying.”
He glanced at Berji, where flies were landing on his face again.
“We could go a lot farther if we weren’t slowed down.”
“Are you seriously saying we abandon our dying friend?” Pav’s voice rose. “I’m not leaving him.”
“None of us wants to do this. But what else can we do? We tried. We really did. He’s not getting any better. We can come back for him.”
“We have to be here to protect him.”
“With what armor? What weapons? We don’t even know the layout of this jungle. He said from the start he’d rather die than get caught. Maybe... maybe this is what he’d want.”
“He is still alive,” Pav said slowly.
Jimzon looked down at Berji. Then at the rock near his foot.
“He’s suffering.”
“But he’s choosing to hang in there."
“He shouldn’t have to suffer like this.” Jimzon said as he bent down and picked up the rock.
	SLAM.
Pav’s fist connected with Jimzon’s face. Jimzon stumbled back and hit the ground, dazed. He sat there, silent.
“I don’t know where your head is at right now, Jimz,” Pav said, voice shaking with anger. “Get it together, or just fucking leave—because I’m not letting anything happen to him.”
Jimzon’s eyes welled up. He sniffled, then stood slowly, swallowing the shame of what he’d nearly done.
“You’re right,” he muttered. “Let’s keep going. I’m sorry.”
Pav’s stance softened. “I know shit’s hard right now. It’s hard for me too—especially for him. But we need each other more than ever. I can’t lose you to delirium. Let’s just go as long as it takes.”
Jimzon nodded and moved to Berji’s side, gently lifting his arm over his shoulder.
“I’m sorry, Berj,” he whispered. “I hope you didn’t hear that.”
	Berji didn’t answer. The two pressed on into the jungle, each step heavier than the last. Then came the rustling—subtle at first, but growing, swelling. Not the soft scurry of a small animal. This was deeper. Broader. All around them. Branches swayed without wind. Leaves trembled without touch.
They were no longer alone.
	
	Chapter 19		
The rustling grew louder—closer—but there wasn't even the whisper of a breeze. The trees stood still, watching. Then came faint whines, low grumbles, like animals muttering in their sleep.
Pav bent down and grabbed a branch, knuckles white. Jimzon shifted his weight, spreading his stance, one eye scanning the brush.
“Back to back,” Pav said quietly. They pressed together, shoulder to shoulder, turning slow in a circle as the noise crept in from all sides.
Then—snap.
A small creature burst from the underbrush with a wet-sounding hop and landed in front of them. Pav raised his stick, tensing for a strike, but it just sniffed his boot.
It was no threat.
The thing looked like an amphibian, about the size of a loaf of bread, with velvet-smooth skin that shimmered pink and teal in the dappled sunlight. Its big round eyes blinked slowly.
Another hopped out. Then another. Then a few more.
“I think they’re harmless,” Jimzon said, lowering his fists.
“Yeah... I think you’re right.” Pav nudged one with his boot, gently. The creature responded by hopping onto his foot, sitting there for a beat, then bouncing off again to continue sniffing his laces.
Pav tilted his head. “Which one’s the fattest, you think?”
“Huh?” Jimzon blinked.
“They hop so fast, we’d only have time to eat one before the rest scattered.”
Jimzon narrowed his eyes as his stomach rumbled, watching the swarm. The creatures came in slight color variations—some more blue, others almost lavender—but most looked nearly identical.
Except one.
That one had a collar.
“Wait,” Jimzon said, grabbing Pav’s arm before he could move. “These are pets.”
He crouched carefully as the collared one approached. It sniffed his fingers, then licked them—its tongue was cool and sticky.
Jimzon turned the small silver tag over. Engraved in neat block letters, it read:

Mister Flimmity Dimmity
If lost, return to Calsco Fritt

Jimzon let go of the collar and gently scooped the creature into his arms. It let out a low, satisfied purr, like a living plush toy.
“It got a name?” Pav asked, watching skeptically.
Jimzon gave a half-laugh, somewhere between amused and tired. “Mister… Flimminy Dimminy.”
He turned the creature to face Pav, who raised an eyebrow as it chirped softly and blinked at him with oversized, trusting eyes.
“Well, if he’s a Mister, that’s quite the title. We can just eat one of the others.”
Mr. Flimminy curled up tighter in Jimzon’s arms, eyes fluttering closed, its breath shallow and warm.
“No need,” Jimzon said, petting it gently. “These things are way too nice—and curious—to survive on their own. Its owner’s gotta be close. And if the name’s this goofy, I’m not too worried about whoever the owner is.”
“True,” Pav said, scanning the treeline. “But if we’re gonna start running into people, we need an alibi. Fake names.”
Jimzon grinned. “You remember my alias when we went drinking in Zonar?”
“Smelvin Klombo?”
“Damn right. He’s back. And you”
Pav squinted into the sun, thinking. “Shit... I don’t know. James... Predator.”
“James Predator?” Jimzon repeated, raising an eyebrow.
“No, no—lemme try again. Dometré... Polaris.”
“Now that sounds almost believable. Isn’t Polaris a royal surname?”
“Something like that. Pretty common in Shayron, though.”
“Alright. Smelvin and Dometré. We crashed because... asteroids.”
“Yeah, good. And we didn’t have any telecoms ‘cause we’re farmers?”
“Sure. You handle that part—I don’t know shit about farming.”
Mr. Flimminy hiccuped softly in his sleep, then settled again with a sigh.
Pav nodded toward the hill then back to Berji. “Alright. Next priority: food and medicine. He won’t last long without both.”
"We'll get Berji whatever he needs" 
"I think you mean… Grug.
"Yeah.. our friend Grug."
"Hey wait.. listen" Pav said.
The jungle had gone quiet—except for the occasional chirp of birds. Or were they bugs? Jimzon couldn't tell, and he wasn’t sure he wanted to. Every few moments, the calls were interrupted by Berji's rough, wheezing cough echoing faintly from behind.
“There it was again,” Pav whispered.
Jimzon turned to him, puzzled.
“Someone’s singing,” Pav said, eyes narrowing into the brush.
Jimzon strained to listen. At first, just the rustle of leaves—but then he heard it too. A voice, faint and tuneless, drifting through the trees. As it drew closer, the words became clear:
“Flimmity
Dimmity
Where are you?
…
Flimmity
Dimmity
I miss you...”
The voice was deep and gruff, like someone who’d gargled gravel—but the lyrics were oddly gentle, almost like a lullaby.
In Jimzon’s arms, Flimmity stirred. Its eyes blinked open, and it let out a tiny, sleepy chirp, almost in reply to the song.
Jimzon and Pav exchanged a glance, then gave each other a small nod.
“Over here!” Jimzon called out. “We found your... Flimmity!”
The singing stopped.
A moment later, a voice shouted back: “Hold on! I’m coming!”
The jungle erupted with crashing footsteps—loud, rhythmic, and fast. Something huge was barreling toward them.
"Bahahaha, there you are, Flimmy!” the voice bellowed.
Flimmity leapt from Jimzon’s hands, chirping excitedly as it bounded toward the sound.
From the undergrowth burst a massive figure—nearly seven feet tall, with a mane of thick curly hair and a grin that split his face. He was tan, muscular, and scarred—but the kind of scars that added charm, not menace. A neatly trimmed black beard framed his mouth, and tribal tattoo markings curled along one cheek.
He wore a heavy fur hide slung across his shoulders, a cross-draw of revolvers across his chest, and a bandolier packed with ammunition. His boots were caked with mud, his knee pads scuffed and worn from use.
The man scooped Flimmity up with one massive arm and cradled it like a child, chuckling.
“There’s my little slimeball,” he cooed, entirely unbothered by the size mismatch. “What are you folks doing off the trail, anyway?” the man asked, still looking down at his pet. “Taking an extra-long break? Ahahaha. Don’t worry, I won’t get you boys in trouble.”
Pav straightened up and said a little too forcefully, “No, uh—we’re farmers.”
“Farmers?” The man raised a brow. “Didn’t know there was a farm around here.”
Jimzon stepped in quickly. “No, no—sorry, there’s been some confusion. We’re lost. And our friend—he really needs help.” He motioned toward Berji, who was slumped against a tree, barely conscious.
The man recoiled, his face twisting in alarm. “Holy fuck! Oh my god. Oh my god.” He stepped closer and squinted at Berji. “Who kicked his ass?”
Pav blurted, “No one—uh, gravity.”
The man didn’t even flinch at the answer. “You the ones who crashed that ship a few clicks back?”
Jimzon and Pav stayed silent, trying to read the man’s tone. He didn’t seem hostile—just curious.
He continued, unfazed. “What are you lot doing out here? We sent a rescue party, didn’t find anyone on board. Hell, we didn’t find anything. Thought it was abandoned.”
 “Yeah, we just need help,” Jimzon said quickly. “Fast. Food, medicine—whatever. We can work.”
“Bahaha! Well, if you’re offering, we could sure use it.” He unclipped a radio from his belt and spoke into it. “Dispatch, this is Calsco Fritt. Found three locals in need of medevac. Forwarding my position now.”
The radio crackled, and a woman’s voice came through: “Is it the Halgo family again?”
“No, no. It’s—hey, kids, what’re your names?”
“That’s Smelvin,” Pav said, pointing to Jimzon.
“And that’s Dometré Polaris,” Jimzon added. “And that’s Grugg… Treeleaf.” He flushed a little under the weight of the lie
“Negative, Dispatch. Three young men, one critical. Bring some wafers on the helo,” Calsco said, then turned back to them. “Is Grugg gonna be okay?”
“Just about,” Calsco replied to himself with a nod. “He won’t be pretty or nothin’, but yeah—he’ll walk, he’ll talk. That sort. Say, you’re Polaris?”
Pav tried to look calm and gave a gentle nod. “That’s right.”
Calsco raised a brow, impressed. “Funny you ended up here so soon. We’ve been waiting for a liaison from the family but didn’t hear back—thought y’all ghosted us.”
“No sir,” Pav said flatly.
“They’ll be glad to hear that,” Calsco chuckled. “We’ll get the council ready by tomorrow. Pickup should be ten minutes out, so sit tight. Welcome to the Trailblazers, boys.”
Jimzon shot a sharp look at Pav that clearly said Why the fuck did you say you were a royal liaison?
Pav gave him a sheepish shrug and mouthed, I panicked.
Things were looking up. Help was on the way. Berji might actually make it.
But something still tugged at Jimzon’s thoughts.
Why had Calsco called them locals over the radio… if he knew they were from the crashed ship?

Chapter 20
Jimzon and Pavlock leaned back in their chairs, their plates scattered with crumbs, a rare sense of peace settling over them.
They were inside a bungalow raised two stories high among ancient trees. The wood smelled faintly of pine resin and fresh sawdust. Soft sunlight filtered through the leaves above, casting shadows that moved gently across the floor.
Inside the small space, a thick woven rug covered the floor beneath a simple table holding the remnants of their meal. Nearby, a kitchen nook contained handmade pots and utensils. A modest living area with woven chairs sat next to two beds made with rough-spun blankets.
Green ferns and wildflowers in pots climbed the walls in wooden holders, their leaves bright against the warm amber glow of the wood.
Outside, the village stretched below into the forest. Cabins made from salvaged timber and branches lined paths bordered with smooth stones and wild herbs. At the center stood a tree so massive its upper branches nearly vanished into the clouds. Its bark was thick and weathered, roots twisting deep into the earth.
Strings of tiny lanterns hung between branches and poles, glowing softly with firefly light. The air carried the scent of pine needles, damp earth, and smoke from a nearby campfire. Quiet voices and laughter drifted through the woods, filling the space with warmth and life.
Jimzon looked out against the windowsill, watching the ferns sway in the breeze. “Is this the planet you punched in when we jumped? ’Cause I gotta say—you picked pretty damn well.”
Pav gave a lopsided grin. “I was aiming for that spring break planet we hit during the academy.”
Jimzon blinked. “You mean Lerroni? It's not that hard to say.”
“Yeah.” Pav shrugged. “Couldn’t roll my R’s"
Jimzon chuckled, then glanced around. “So... where are we?”
Pav’s grin faded a little. “Not sure. But no Guard signals, no patrol routes, no satellites overhead. I think we can lay low. Wait for it to blow over.”
“Blow over?” Jimzon turned from the window, his voice tight. “They tried to shoot us out of the sky, Pav. We killed a Guard pilot. This... this isn’t a rough patch. This is life now.”
Pav didn’t respond. The silence stretched.
Jimzon exhaled slowly, pulling the drive from his pocket. It felt heavier now. “The drive Berji gave me... this is what they want. If we were just deserters, they’d slap a bounty on us, defame us, let mercs handle it. But this? They need it back.”
Pav looked over at him, serious now. “So what do we do? Destroy it? Sell it?”
Jimzon stared at the device like it might answer for him. “No. This... this could bury the Guard. They’d pay anything to make it disappear. Maybe we leak it?”
Pav didn’t answer right away. He leaned forward, elbows on his knees. “Look... messed-up shit happens all over the galaxy. But the Guard’s not all bad. We’ve seen what it can do when it actually works. If we leak that drive, it’s open season on Guard settlements. People will tear it all down without thinking twice. And when they do? There’s no backup plan. Just chaos. Another dark age—same kind the Guard clawed out of a few decades ago.”
The room was quiet except for the wind in the leaves outside.
Jimzon glanced out the window again, jaw tight. “Yeah,” he said softly. “I know.”
“So… we just let the Guard leadership keep doing whatever they want?” Jimzon asked, staring out the window like he expected to see warships on the horizon.
Pav exhaled slowly. “I don’t know. Maybe. If we stay quiet, maybe they’ll think we died in the crash. If they ever find the ship wreckage that is”
Jimzon turned, holding the drive between his fingers like it was radioactive. “So that’s it? We just live out our days as Smelvin, Grugg, and Dom until we die? What about your parents?”
Pav’s jaw tensed. “I’ll see them again. But yeah—right now, I’m Dometré. And tomorrow night, Calsco says I’ve gotta meet the council.”
“You don’t seem nervous.”
“It’s a bunch of Crags, man. These aliens are easy as hell to trick. If the Royal settlements haven’t reached out to them, it means no one important cares about this place and its not worth their time. We fake our way through, maybe walk out with a ship or some credits.”
Jimzon frowned. “Crags? Look, I get it, I hate them too, but people can smell bullshit. Maybe just tell them there was a misunderstanding.”
Pav laughed, but there wasn’t much humor in it. “You can smell bullshit? Remind me again how many years you spent playing loyal Guard?”
Jimzon raised a hand in surrender. “Alright, fair. But seriously—have you thought about your pitch?”
“Yeah,” Pav said, leaning back. “I’ll tell them the Royal Senate’s holding a vote on some amendments, everything’s tied up in the political process. Bureaucracy. Maybe toss in that I’ve got friends in the capital, and if they help us out, I can put in a good word.”
Jimzon gave a slow nod. “That could… work. Add some flair, we could get a ship. But where do we go after that?”
Pav scratched his temple. “Once Berji wakes up, we figure it out. Until then, we don’t overthink.”
Jimzon hesitated. “Well… There’s one thing I don’t get. Earlier Calsco said, ‘Welcome to the Trailblazers.’”
“Probably his speeder club,” Pav shrugged. “Civs love that kind of naming shit.”
“Maybe. But remember when he asked if we were the ones who crashed the ship? Yet, over the radio, he called us locals.”
Pav blinked. “I didn’t catch that.”
“I like him,” Jimzon admitted. “But something’s off. I don’t trust him yet.”
“He gave us food. A roof. Beds. He doesn’t even know who we are.”
Jimzon’s voice dropped. “We shouldn’t be in the business of trusting anyone right now.”
Pav’s eyes sharpened. “Well, you better. He said he’d check in about Berji after we ate… and I think that’s him walking up now.”
Jimzon sighed and brushed the crumbs from his shirt. “Alright. Just play it cool.”
Three knocks hit the door, loud but not angry, just deliberate. Pav opened it.
“Ah! My friends,” Calsco beamed, his deep voice filling the room. “Enjoying the tenderloin and rockroot, I see?” He laughed, despite the sentence having no punchline. Without waiting for a response, he dropped into the chair across from them.
“I know it’s not princely cuisine, so forgive me, my lord.” He gave a theatrical bow to Pav. “Dometre, are you and your assistant settling in well?”
Pav swallowed his nerves. “I suppose so, yes. The air out here is quite peaceful.”
Calsco grinned. “Yes, yes. Nothing but the best for Mister Polaris.”
He turned to Jimzon. “And you, Smelvin, are you from the settlements as well?”
“Yes, sir. Modanik. Born and raised.”
“Ah, beautiful, very nice.” Calsco nodded with exaggerated approval. “I read about the hurricanes out there, should one be worried to visit?”
Jimzon kept his tone steady. “Not where I live, sir. We just close the windows.”
“Ah, yes, Sensible. Be safe, of course yes. It’s the rural folks who catch the big ones, no?”
Jimzon gave a tight smile. “Yes, always a pain, those storms.”
Calsco raised an eyebrow. “Always? I also read there hasn’t been one in… two centuries.”
Jimzon froze for half a second, jaw tightening, but Calsco didn’t let him flinch.
“You’re not really from Modanik, are you?” he said, voice lower and slower now. “And you too 'Mister Polaris'…”
The room chilled. Pav glanced at Jimzon. The look they shared said it all. Could they take him? Should they run?
Jimzon broke the silence. “Alright, sir. You got us. We’re just lost farmers.”
Calsco reached into his coat and placed a metal disk on the table. It lit up with a soft hum, projecting a hologram of Jimzon’s face in cool blue. A line of text hovered above:
WANTED DEAD OR ALIVE – HIGH TREASON SARGEANT PAVLOK JOJEN – GENERAL JIMZON AVARIS
Calsco leaned back, eyes glinting with some intensity.
“A very, very dangerous farmer.”

Chapter 21
Pav and Jimzon stared at Calsco, his smug grin lit faintly by the flicker of the hologram. The blue light danced in his eyes, but there was no heat in them. No cruelty, no gloating, just that blank, unbothered calm. Their so-called savior had been lying from the start.
The remainder of Castle Team wore the truth on their faces. Tight jaws. Flickering eyes. Temples pulsing with dread. They’d been caught.
Jimzon glanced down at his plate, then to the dull dinner knife beside it. He wondered if it could cut deep enough—through Calsco’s throat, into his windpipe. Probably not. Still, his fingers hovered closer.
Across the table, Pav was already watching him. They held the stare. Years together had made them fluent in the language of glances and micro-movements. Pav’s eyes slid toward Calsco, a question barely formed.
Should we? Now?
Jimzon’s fingers twitched—subtle, but clear.
I’ve got a knife.
Pav’s chin dipped half an inch. A nod. But then—stillness.
Wait.
Jimzon followed his lead. Calsco outweighed them both. The man wore his daggers like decoration on his chestplate, but he hadn’t flinched once. If they moved, it wouldn’t be clean.
Jimzon swallowed and cleared his throat, forcing composure. He wanted to lie—something smooth, clever—but there was nothing left to bluff with. The hologram floating in the air, even distorted, caught every scar on his face. Every line. Every shadow. Unmistakable.
He turned slightly, angling himself toward Calsco, just enough to keep his hand closer to the knife.
“So,” he said, voice flat. “You turning us in?”
Calsco’s lips curled into a smile. “I thought about it. Truly. But no,” he said, his voice booming with theatrical calm. “I am businessman. And I don’t do business with the Guard.”
The tension in the room dropped half a degree—but only just. Pav gave a small, uncertain shake of his head.
“So… now what?” he asked.
Calsco ignored his question.
“You boys need work on your lies,” he said instead, waving a hand like a bored professor. “You—” he pointed to Jimzon, “—the farmer with plasma scars? And you,” he turned to Pav, “the prince with a beard? Everyone knows the royal family’s clean-shaven.” He let out a belly-deep laugh and casually took Pav’s glass, sipping from it without permission.
“What even brings a prince and a dirt farmer together, eh? Very funny. Very theatrical. Honestly… you have no idea how lucky you are to find me. I could be swimming in Guardcoin right now— I could buy a ship with a brothel in the cargo hold.”
Jimzon’s jaw tightened. “So why didn’t you?”
Calsco leaned back in his chair, eyes gleaming. “Because I see long-term. I’m more than a former chieftain. I’ve fought for money, sure. But I have… how you say… principles. Spirit.” He tapped his chest. “And that spirit? It doesn’t kneel to the Guard.”
Jimzon and Pav said nothing. 
Calsco reached over and tapped the hologram off. The room dimmed as the blue light died.
“Relax, boys. Pavel, Jimzo, Grugg—whatever your names are. I don’t care.” He shrugged. “But out there?” He pointed toward the window. “Out there, people will.”
He leaned forward, his voice low but steady.
“You’re lucky it was me who found you. I fed you, gave you clothes, a bed. Your friend’s still breathing because of my medbay. And you’re lucky his face is gone. Because if it wasn’t, some nosy herb-peddler down the trail would’ve recognized him and had all three of you strung up for Guardcoin before dawn.”
Calsco grinned, but there was no humor in it.
“And what do I get in return? No ‘Thank you, Mister Calsco, how can I ever repay you?’ Your reaction is to reach for your knife?” He chuckled. “I tease, of course. You couldn’t kill a warrior like myself with that blade.”
Jimzon leaned back slowly, jaw tight.
Calsco kept going, tone shifting.
“You think you’re clever, the both of you. You’re not. You’re two half-starved fugitives pretending to be diplomats and dirt-farmers. You are alive because of blind, stupid luck. And luck?” He held up a thick finger. “It runs out. I know this”
Jimzon exhaled through his nose, jaw working behind clenched teeth. He conceded, choosing not to argue. “Well… thank you, Mister Calsco,” he said, voice stiff and forced.
Calsco tilted his head toward Pav.
“Thanks, Mister Calsco,” Pav muttered.
Jimzon narrowed his eyes. He knew what was coming. “So,” he said carefully. “What do you want?”
Calsco held a hand to his chest, mock-offended. “You wound me. You are my guest and you think I want something in return?”
He let the silence stretch.
“But… since you’re offering,” he said, his grin returning. “There is a favor you can do.”
Pav and Jimzon both sighed. “What is it?” Pav muttered.
Calsco steepled his fingers. “Your little meeting with the town council tomorrow, I want you to walk in as a 'Royal Ambassador'. Tell them you’re here to assist with our little project. I’ll vouch for the crash, tell them you're under my protection. Tell them you can discuss investments soon but all you need is a ship, some coin, and supplies for your ‘return trip.’”
Jimzon narrowed his eyes. “If you saw through us so easily, what makes you think they won’t?”
Calsco’s smile widened. “Ah, yes brother. The beard.” He unhooked one of the daggers from his breastplate and slid it across the table, its tip glinting in the light. “You’ll have to shave, of course.”
Pav stared at the blade, his jaw tightening.
Jimzon clocked the reaction. “And if he doesn’t?"
Calsco tapped the breast pocket where the hologram rested. The implication landed hard.
Jimzon pushed back from the table, scowling. “You’re fucking blackmailing us?" He said with a low angry tone. "You planned this. You made him pretend to be a royal to line up the meeting—and you only fed us so we'd be in your debt, now you're holding it all over our heads.”
Calsco’s grin flattened. “What can I say? I am good businessman.”
He stood, adjusting the lapels of his coat. “And frankly, what choice do you have?”
Pav didn’t answer. He already knew this was a bad idea. He just didn’t know how bad yet.
Calsco extended his hand. “Deal?”
After a long pause, Pav took it. Calsco’s grip clamped down tight, knuckles grinding.
“Excellent,” he said. “I have errands to run. One hour. You clean shave, I give you the tour.” He turned toward the door. “Trailblazer Calsco… out.” he announced.
And just like that, he was gone.
The silence stretched for just a second too long. As soon as they were sure Calsco was gone, Jimzon slammed his fist onto the table, silverware rattling and plates jumping. He stood abruptly, knocking his chair over, grabbed his dish and hurled it against the wall with a crash. Shards of ceramic rained down.
“FUCK!” he shouted. “Is every piece of shit in this universe a stupid fucking… manipulative, lying sociopath?!”
He dragged both hands down his face and let out a low, guttural groan.
Pav leaned against the table, arms crossed.
“How do you think I feel?” Pav snapped. “I have to walk into some alien council chamber and pretend to be royalty. I have to lie to their faces and pray I don’t screw it up. And yeah... I even have to shave.”
Jimzon’s voice dropped but lost none of the heat. “I love you, dog. But I don’t give a shit about your beard right now. We are fugitives. Do you get that? We're too valuable. Calsco’s not letting us go. He owns us.”
“Calm the hell down,” Pav shot back. “First off—this isn’t just a beard. I had to fight to keep it when I enlisted. Took weeks of paperwork just to get the cultural exemption. After everything my planet’s been through, it’s one of the only traditions we still have. All Marfeld men keep their beard once they finish the coming-of-age journey. You can trim it, shape it, whatever—but you don’t shave. Ever. Not unless you're disowning your entire family. Your entire bloodline.”
Jimzon shook his head, unconvinced. “Okay—yeah—that fucking sucks or whatever, but maybe you can find another cultural exemption, one that lets you live. Because this? This is survival now. My life. Berji’s life. Yours. We don’t get to cling to symbols if it gets us killed.”
He took a step closer, jabbing a finger toward Pav’s chest. “Didn’t you just say, ‘We need each other more than ever?’ So what the hell are we hesitating for? What choice do you have?
“Take a breath, man. Don’t take it out on me—I’m the only one with your back.” Pav’s voice was steady. “Yes, I’m shaving. Doesn’t mean I like it. If we get through this we can figure something out. If the lie works and we do get a ship, we steal it or something. I can fly. It’ll be easier to disappear without getting shot.”
Jimzon’s fists clenched. “I fucking hate this universe.”
Pav rubbed his beard, then grabbed Calsco’s knife. Without another word, he walked into the bathroom and shut the door behind him.
Jimzon paced the cramped bungalow, the stale scent of pine and sawdust doing nothing to soothe him. This place looked like some vacation spot he and Kelsa had once talked about. It was filled with those dumb, pretentious hippie plants clinging to every windowsill, their leaves curling like dying hands. Tiny pots crowded the shelves, bursting with wildflowers that seemed to choke each other out in their fight for space and sunlight.
The walls were cluttered with wooden knickknacks—carved animals frozen in awkward poses, driftwood sculptures balancing precariously—just clutter pretending to be art. And then there were the paintings. Fucking paintings.
One caught his eye—a crude, grotesque image of a man with a straw hat and rake standing next to a creature plowing a field. The man's limbs were twisted, absurdly out of proportion, like some nightmare spilled onto canvas. It looked malformed and agonized, its awkward hands gripping the tool as if it barely understood its purpose.
Jimzon’s eyes locked on the farmer’s face—huge, haunting eyes that seemed to stare straight through him, empty and aching, as if the figure was trapped in eternal suffering. The colors were wild and violent, splattered with chaotic reds and sickly greens, as if the painter was clawing their own rage and despair into the scene.
Why the fuck would anyone waste their day on this nonsense? People were starving, dying out there—real pain. And here, someone was pouring their soul into this madness, like their suffering somehow made their art more “authentic.” Another tortured soul screaming their “truth,” as if that made it special or new.
Jimzon’s breath hitched, anger flaring hot and sharp. With a brutal punch, his fist smashed through the glass frame and right through the farmer’s huge, haunted head in the painting. The canvas tore beneath his fingers as he drove his hand wrist-deep into the cracked drywall behind it, leaving a jagged hole that began to stain with blood from his knuckles.

Chapter 22
Pavlock stared at the mirror, dragging his fingers across his bare jaw. The reflection didn’t feel like his. Too smooth. Too soft. Like someone had peeled off his face and replaced it with a stranger’s. He looked younger—but not in a good way. Vulnerable. Weak. On Marfeld, they say hair remembers.
That beard had seen war, seen his brothers. It had been there through sun and storm, from deployment zones to firelit dinners. It had seen his family the night before he left for the Guard. And before that, it was proof—proof he had survived the journey.
The last time Pavlock was clean-shaven was the morning of his coming-of-age ritual. Seventeen, sent into the wild with nothing but clothes and instincts. The tradition was old, sacred. Some walked into blizzards. He’d heard stories of boys skating across frozen rivers on bark, stitching pelts together just to keep their toes.
But for him, it was summer. He remembered the heat, the endless buzz of insects, the blisters on his hands. The first thing he made was a knife—barely worthy of the word. A sharpened stick wedged into split wood. It's what made him want to be an engineer, creating from whatever was around him. He remembered he'd stuffed his pant pockets with dry kindling, treating it like it was gold.
They never told you what you’d face. That was the rule. No man who completed the trial could speak of it. All you knew were the names: the Path of Blood, the Path of Root, the Path of Sky.
He had walked all three, for weeks. And the beard had grown throughout, slow and coarse, a quiet crown. 
Now it was gone. All of it. Washed down the drain in minutes.
Pavlock looked down at the sink, then opened the bathroom door. Jimzon was slumped against the wall, legs stretched out, eyes unfocused. The table lay overturned, its cloth tangled, silverware scattered across a mess of shattered plates. One of the paintings had a hole punched through it—another tantrum.
“Hey,” Jimzon said, forcing a grin. “Didn’t know you had a jawline. Looks good."
“Yeah. Thanks,” Pav replied, dry. “Can you clean this up a little? I don’t care that you broke shit—I just don’t want to piss off Cal.”
Jimzon let out a long breath. “Yeah. You’re right. My bad.”
He pushed himself up and started using his boot to scrape the broken plate pieces into a pile. Pav sat down nearby, eyes scanning the room. He noticed the blood first—Jimzon’s hand was still bleeding, leaving dark drops on the floor. He said nothing. Just closed his eyes and tried to think.
But it was hard to think with the sound of ceramic scratching across the floor. The approaching sound of footsteps thumping closer didn’t help either.
“There is my prince. Ahahaha, very good,” Calsco said as he walked in, laughing to himself. “Ah, Jimbo. Not a fan of art, I see? My little soldier.”
He tossed two white cardboard boxes onto the table. Their fake names were scribbled across the tops in marker: Smelvin and Dometre.
Pav stepped forward and opened his box slowly. Inside were blue robes trimmed with gold thread.
“What are these?” he asked.
“Your disguise,” Calsco replied. “Royal-sewn robes. Fake, of course, but real enough.”
Jimzon took a glance into his box and pulled back the fabric. It was a mix of deep blue and purple.
“Doesn’t look like a farmer’s outfit.”
Calsco wagged his finger. “You may be a philistine when it comes to art, but you have a good eye. No farmer today. You’ll be his squire.”
Jimzon let out a weary sigh, knowing there was no point in arguing. “A squire? His squire?” He jabbed a thumb toward Pav in disbelief.
“Exactly. Think about it,” the man replied smoothly. “Why would a royal ambassador travel with a farmer? Sure, you could be his bodyguard, but that sends the wrong message. We don’t want people seeing you as a threat. No, no... a squire fits the image.”
Jimzon frowned. “Why not something a little more reasonable? A financial advisor. A medic. Even a chauffeur. Squires are treated like jesters out there.”
“You have much to learn about showmanship Jim. Optics matter. You as a chauffeur? Please. Your ship’s wreckage is spread across half a mile of farmland.” He laughed, wagging his finger with amused condescension. “You’re funnier than I thought.”
He turned toward the corridor. “Now go get changed. I’ll give you the tour once you look the part. Hustle.”
After twenty minutes, the duo emerged in full costume. Pav, against all odds, looked the part. Clean-shaven, glowing skin, and his hair sculpted into soft, deliberate waves. He wore a glimmering blue suit with a flowing robe draped over his shoulders, the fabric catching light like water. Fake gold jewelry clinked at his wrists and fingers, just flashy enough to be believable.
A slim band of gold, no thicker than a sewing needle, curved gently to rest behind the head—like a crown stripped down to a whisper. It hugged the back of the skull like an inverted halo, sleek as wire, glinting in the light. If a pair of glasses lost their lenses and turned to face the wrong way, you'd have something close. Not ornamental, yet just regal enough.
Jimzon, on the other hand, looked like the most miserable jester ever conscripted. His squire’s uniform was a dull navy blue, broken up by four narrow yellow stripes that ran down the chest like bars on a cage. His shoulders bore oversized purple epaulettes, cheap and puffed. A thin, wiry purple tie dangled from his neck, clearly designed more for mockery than style. And the worst part was the thick, gold-rimmed sunglasses that swallowed his eyes and made him look like a confused insect.
Calsco leaned back with a grin. “Not half bad, you two. I had doubt, but… maybe you pull this off. Maybe.”
Jimzon scowled. “What now?”
Calsco snapped his fingers. “Ah, Jimzo. You forget something.”
“I’m not wearing the stupid fucking hat.”
“Just try. One time. Show Pav at least, let him laugh before we go.”
Jimzon reluctantly placed the hat on his head. It was soft cloth, matching the deep blue and yellow of his uniform, with two floppy ends that dangled backward like wilted antennae.
Pav tried to contain a grin. “Looks convincing.”
Jimzon glared at him, then yanked the hat off and flung it to the floor.
Calsco watched with calm amusement. “You got fire in you, boy. I like this. But listen, hat is more than joke. It lets you speak free. People see fool, not fighter. That silly hat? It protects you more than armor.”
“I’m not fucking wearing it, Calsco. Understand that.” Jimzon threatened
Calsco leaned back slightly, unbothered. “I thought maybe you say this. So… I bought this.” He reached into his coat and handed over a small, crinkly plastic pouch.
Jimzon stared at it, jaw tightening. He didn’t need to look inside, he already knew.
Scowling, he tore the package open and pulled out a thick, ridiculous black mustache. With a defeated sigh, he slapped it onto his upper lip.
Calsco clapped his hands once. “Perfect. You are invisible now. Nobody suspect.”
Jimzon muttered, the mustache twitching with every syllable. “Whatever. Let’s just get this over with.”
Without another word, Calsco turned and strode toward the exit. He had to duck low to pass through the doorframe, his massive shoulders brushing the sides. The duo followed him out onto a wide balcony.
Calsco rested both hands on the railing, taking in the view below. The air was crisp and buzzing with distant traffic. Somewhere out there, their act was about to begin.
“I know you two were half-delirious before,” Calsco said, gesturing grandly with one hand, “so now—take good look. Trailblazer Base Delta. Much to see. Lot to take in.”
Outside, the village unfurled like a secret stitched into the forest’s ribs.
Treehouses and cabins, each uniquely built from scavenged timber, rusted sheet metal, and crooked branches, clung to the terrain like nesting birds. Some homes rose on stilts above tangled brush; others hunched low and moss-covered, half-swallowed by the undergrowth. Their rooftops were patched with bark shingles, scavenged satellite dishes, and layers of woven reeds, giving each one a mismatched, cobbled-together personality — like the forest had grown the village itself in a moment of wild generosity.
The paths winding between them were narrow and softly worn, paved with river-smoothed stones and lined with creeping thyme and spiky purple sage. Clusters of glowing mushrooms clung to roots and logs, their light faint and flickering. Someone had planted mint and feverfew along the walkways, their scent mingling with pine sap and woodsmoke, cutting clean through the damp, earthy perfume of the forest floor.
Above it all, at the heart of the settlement, rose the Elder Tree — not merely tall, but mythic.
Its trunk was wider than a house, bark furrowed like centuries of wrinkled skin, charred in places, silvered in others. Gnarled roots burst from the base like petrified serpents, some forming natural benches, others tunneling deep beneath the visible world. Its branches coiled high into the canopy, too tall to follow with the eye — they vanished into the mist like spires of some half-remembered cathedral.
Lanterns dangled from those limbs and from makeshift poles, strung together with fraying rope and copper wire. Their glass shells glowed with a warm, insectile pulse — not electricity, but bio-light: captured fireflies or alchemical moss, gently pulsing. The light refracted through beads of dew, scattering gold and amber halos across the leaves, turning the entire village into a trembling constellation.
From nearby came the crackle of a firepit, the clink of mugs, the hush of soft conversation and laughter muffled by the woods. Smoke curled lazily into the dusk, carrying with it the scent of roasted root vegetables, old leather, and the faintest trace of some unfamiliar spice — sweet, peppery, almost medicinal.
Somewhere deeper in the woods, a string instrument played — slow, reverent, like a lullaby hummed to the trees. It mixed with the buzz of insects and the whisper of wind, becoming part of the forest’s voice — not louder than it, just folded in.
Many of the homes weren’t grounded at all but clung to the trees themselves, perched high in the canopies like watchful wooden nests. Thick wooden staircases spiraled tightly around the trunks, some carved directly into the bark, others built from mismatched planks and salvaged scaffolding. Ropes, bridges, and swinging walkways connected one elevated home to another, swaying gently with the breeze, as if the village itself breathed with the forest. Lanterns dangled from the undersides of platforms, casting slow-turning shadows across the lower paths like drifting constellations.
	The whole village breathed like one body — old, hidden, fragile in some ways, but very much alive.
The pair took in the view in silence for a moment, their eyes drifting over the glowing village tucked into the trees.
Pav finally broke it. “So… what exactly are the Trailblazers? And what is this place?”
Calsco leaned on the railing with pride. “You not hear of us? Hah. Makes sense, you are ex-Guard. After the war, trade routes, shipping lanes—all gone. Whole sectors cut off. Now Guard has monopoly on whatever is left, controls what little moves.” He waved a hand over the forest below. “Here, we rebuild ports. Or build new ones. Bring wealth back to these people. Start fresh.”
Pav raised a brow. “How… suspiciously noble of you. So what’s the deal with the council here?”
“The council is natives. Locals. Old families. They are gracious hosts to our cause—though… little odd, most of them. But we are few in number, and they don’t mind us. Give us housing, land, we split profit from goods that run through this sector.”
“So you’re in it for the money?” Jimzon asked.
Calsco shrugged, half-smiling. “Ehh… money always good. But no. Everyone here starting new life. This is honest work. No stealing. No plundering. We not even paid for some years. But I travel, I help, I build something real. That’s worth more.”
Pav squinted. “So what exactly do you build?”
“Me? Nothing.” He tapped his chest, proud. “Promoted. Manager now. I supervise. Pick my own task, do my own rounds. Right now? I oversee native flooms.”
Jimzon glanced sideways. “Flooms?”
Pav echoed, brow furrowing. “Flooms?”
“You recall Flimmity?” Calsco asked, his voice almost reverent. “He is floom. Many flooms live here. Very important creatures. My pride. My joy.”
Jimzon gave him a sidelong glance. “Hard to picture a guy like you as a shepherd.”
Calsco chuckled. “I have walked many paths, son. I know where greener pastures are. Flimmity—he took quite a liking to you.”

Jimzon gave a dry half-smile. “Hard to believe we were so hungry we almost wanted to eat one.”
Calsco’s eyes widened. “Eat one? Are you crazy?”
Jimzon held up a hand. “Sorry—I didn’t mean it like that. We didn’t know what he was. We were starving.”
Calsco relaxed a little, “Yes, yes. Of course. You were hungry. You didn’t know. You are not familiar with flooms, no?”
“I guess not.”
“Hmmm... how I explain this? I am no scientist,” Calsco said, scratching his chin. “Flooms—yes, they can birth baby flooms, but they also lay eggs. Strange eggs. For there is no yolk. Only this… blue liquid. Only flooms can make it, and only on this planet.”
He looked at them, as if testing their reactions.
“They pick up life force from things. Not stealing. Not taking. Just… absorbing. Like plant does sunlight. They are smart, very aware. They take energy from world around them, and when time is right, they lay eggs on dry grass or withering gardens. A week later—grass grows back. Greener than before. Flooms are like... living fertilizer. Does that make sense?”
Pav frowned. “So... are the eggs nutrient-dense? Or is the liquid the real nutrient? Why not just evolve to pee it out? Eggs take forever to break down.”
Calsco let out a sharp breath through his nose, part amused, part insulted. “These eggs break down only if nature wants it. Planet takes what it needs, when it needs. No rush. No waste. That’s why air here so pure. Life is everywhere because life is shared. Floom is the ecosystem. All things live because of flooms and floom lives because of life.”
Jimzon raised a brow. “So the blue stuff is like a growth hormone? That's cool I guess.”
Calsco smiled, softer this time. “More than that. It is medicine. Powerful. Your friend, Berji? He rest now in sensory tank, full of it. Right there.” He pointed to a wooden lodge near the center of the village, its roof covered in moss and fiber-thread lanterns.
“Nothing but best for my boys,” he said. “Even you. I put just little in your food, after crash. Helped you heal faster.”
“You drugged us?” Jimzon snapped. “What if we were allergic?”
Calsco waved a hand dismissively. “No one allergic. It is essence of life itself. Purest form. Believe me—if I drugged you, you would know. Is not subtle. Quite the high, actually. Nothing else like it. If you are one, you would be high for a lifetime,” He chuckled, then added, “These flooms... they special. Very nervous creatures. But loving.”
“They didn’t seem nervous,” Pav said, crossing his arms. “They ran right up to us.”
“True. They have good judgment of character. Strong instinct. Flimmity resting in your arms—that is reason I did not turn you in.”
Jimzon didn’t know what to say. He looked down, unsure whether to feel guilty, grateful, or both.
Calsco smiled, already turning away. “You boys... lot of life in you. I see it. Come. We have errands. Time to meet the natives.”

Chapter 23
Castle Team followed Calsco as he led them through Trailblazer Base Delta, weaving down a packed-dirt path into the heart of the village. Jimzon’s eyes skimmed over the pottery and carved sculptures that lined the trail—he didn’t care. What caught his attention was what wasn’t there.
For something called a “base,” it lacked every basic defense. No automated turrets. No watchtowers. No perimeter fencing, no posted guards. Worse, the layout was a tactical nightmare—winding alleys with poor sightlines, elevation breaks perfect for snipers, zero hardened structures for fallback. Terrible choke points and tons of blind spots. If this place ever got hit, they wouldn’t stand a chance. It wasn’t a base, it was a trap someone forgot to spring.
The homes that dotted the path were simple domes, built from clay or carved into the hillside. As they passed, Jimzon caught glimpses of the natives watering their plants or sitting on stoops. They were small—no taller than toddlers—but cloaked in roughspun hoods that hid their faces. Their tunics came in muted, earthy colors—tans, burgundies, forest greens—and they carried satchels and baskets full of tools or herbs. They moved with a kind of frantic energy, hanging lanterns, hammering nails, but rarely seemed to accomplish much.
Frankly, Jimzon thought, they’d be terrible allies. A single rifle’s recoil would surely knock them over. He couldn't picture them holding a firing line, let alone clearing a room. Maybe they’d be useful for crawling through vents or tight tunnels—but even then, he wouldn’t trust them. Not with his back.
The only Crags he ever tolerated were the ones who kept quiet during planetary occupations—sold cheap snacks from shacks, stayed out of the way. The rest—Vitulas, Targaths, Splendoorians—they all disgusted him. They each may wear different skin, yet held same rot underneath. Prone to violence, thievery, and degeneracy. They didn’t deserve the soil nor the wealth their planets stood upon.
To Jimzon, “Crag” wasn’t a species. It was a category. Non-human.
And yet, watching these ones—these odd, hooded things—he couldn’t help but feel a strange flicker of something. Not empathy. Not respect. But maybe... a sliver of recognition. The way they hammered nails with too-small hands, the way their robes dragged like oversized costumes—it reminded him of kids playing pretend. Dressing up like grown-ups. Mimicking a world they didn’t understand. Pathetic, yes. But maybe, in a way, endearing.
Pav, on the other hand, had taken a liking to the creatures from the start. Though they kept their distance, many paused to wave as he passed, or pointed him out to their children who softly bounced with excitement at the sight of him. They must have never seen a royal before. That thought made him straighten his back, lift his chin, and offer a few graceful nods and waves in return. He was enjoying the attention. A confident smile settled comfortably on his face.
Calsco clapped his hands together as they reached the center of the village.
“Take a deep breath, boys,” he said, inhaling dramatically. “You’re breathing the cleanest air in the known systems. Now—I have a few errands before our big day, but I assume you’d like to check in on your friend Grugg no? Am I safe to presume that’s either Yakib Yalitio or Berji Kegeron, correct?”
Pav gave a reluctant nod.
“I figured as much,” Calsco said. “Pavlock—apologies, Prince Dometre Polaris—you’ll follow me. I’ll help you prepare for the meeting. As for you…” He gave Jimzon a theatrical look. “Jizzin? Smelvin? Whatever it is—go find your friend. Stay out of trouble, keep your head down, and meet us back here when the Sunset Bell chimes.”
Jimzon was certain Calsco was butchering his name intentionally now.
“Alright,” Jimzon said, giving Pav a quick nod. “Dom, I’ll see you later. Good luck.”
"Same to you, man. Let me know how Grugg’s doing. If he’s awake, tell him we’ll be out of this mess soon,” Pav said.
Jimzon nodded and gave a half-hearted wave. Pav looked oddly cheerful—too cheerful, maybe—but Jimzon didn’t have time to think about that. He had to focus. He turned and began to trot off.
The sunlight pressed against his skin, as intense as it was invasive. The squire suit clung to him with sweat; it wasn’t built for this kind of heat. But he figured he’d have to get used to it. If there was one lesson his life never let him forget, it was adapt or die. He muttered it under his breath like a mantra, trying to make it stick.
Still, something felt... off. Sure, being the most wanted man in the universe was stressful—but the strangest part was the lack of a deadline. When he had days off before, there was always a clock ticking in his head: deployment date, mission brief, drop time. Now, nothing. No countdown. Just wandering toward a medical center on an alien planet, sweating through a borrowed uniform, waiting for something to go wrong. It felt like a sword was dangling above him, held up by a single thread, inevitable to snap at any moment.
He jogged up the short steps, the oversized costume jingling faintly with every movement, pins rattling, zippers flapping against his chest. The wooden door in front of him looked rustic, but as he approached, it slid open with a smooth hiss. That caught him off guard.
Inside was a jarring contrast from the village outside. Sterile white walls. Fluorescent ceiling lights. The faint chemical scent of waxed tiled floors. It could’ve been any medbay on any warship. Clean, cold, and impersonal.
He stepped up to the front counter, where a woman was shuffling through a stack of papers.
“Excuse me,” he said.
She looked up with a cheerful smile. “You must be the Jester, right?”
“Squire,” he mumbled, “but yeah.”
“I assume you’re here to check on your friend?”
“Indeed. Thank you ma'am.”
“Of course! Um... do you happen to have your medical records with you?”
“Not on me, no.”
“Oh, that’s fine. I’ll have someone prep the decontamination chamber so you can go in then. I don’t think we need any paperwork signed... but would you still be able to cover the cost of the visit?”
“I, uh... don’t have any money on me. Just this outfit.” He gestured down at his ridiculous garb. “Guess I’m underprepared.”
She laughed. “That’s so silly—I love it when a squire visits. Want me to just charge the prince’s room?”
“Sure... how much would it be?”
