“You’re hilarious,” she giggled. “I’m sure the prince can afford it. Have a seat—I’ll call you when we’re ready.”
Jimzon didn’t respond. Just gave a flat, tired look and nodded. He sat down in the plastic waiting chair, removed his glasses, rubbed the bridge of his nose, and exhaled slowly.
A little girl sitting in the waiting room looked up from the puzzle in her lap. She blinked at him, tilted her head, then popped out of her chair and walked over with the kind of confidence only children and fools possessed. Without asking, she plopped herself in the seat across from him.
"Are you a clown?" she asked.
Jimzon glanced at his absurd outfit. The oversized tunic, ornamental boots, and the comically oversized tropical flower pinned to his chest. He could hardly blame her.
"I guess so," he replied.
"Tell me a joke," she said, swinging her legs.
"I don’t know any right now."
"Please, please, please. You have to know one!" she pleaded, leaning forward like she was about to burst from the suspense.
He scratched the back of his neck. "Uh… why did the floom cross the road?"
"I don’t know," she said with excitement.
"Me neither. Maybe he thought there was something on the other side."
She stared at him, unimpressed. "You're not a very funny clown."
Jimzon leaned back in the chair. With a deadpan look, he pressed the flower on his chest twice. It honked loudly twice.
The girl didn’t laugh. Her mouth held a perfect little frown, and she turned to look out the nearby window. A few moments passed in silence before he spoke again.
"Say, kid. What are you doing here anyway?"
"My teacher told me my daddy lost his leg," she said quietly.
That hit him in the chest harder than he expected. He blinked, then sat up straighter.
"Gods," he murmured. "Well… if he’s here, he’s in good hands. They’re taking care of my friend too."
The girl shook her head. "He isn’t here yet," she said with a pout. "My teacher said he walked on a mind."
"A mine?" he asked, gently correcting her pronunciation, trying to make sense of it. "Is your daddy a Trailblazer?"
"Yes," she said brightly. "Mommy was too."
The past tense made Jimzon’s smile falter. He looked at her a moment longer, then forced a reassuring tone.
"Everything’s gonna be okay," he said. "I had a friend walk on a mine once, and he ended up just fine," he lied. "There’s a lot of people out there who’ll take care of you and your dad. Try not to worry about the things you can’t control, alright? How are you holding up?"
The girl stared at her shoes.
"I don’t know," she murmured. "Daddy really likes his legs. I think he’s probably sad now."
Jimzon exhaled softly and nodded. "Yeah… I’d be sad too. But hey, cheer up, kid. Want to hear another joke?"
She wiped her nose with her sleeve and looked up, hesitant. "Okay."
"How do you organize a space party?"
"I don’t know. How?"
"You planet."
The corner of her mouth twitched.
"How much do rainbows weigh?"
She blinked. "How much?"
"Not much. They’re actually pretty light."
She giggled. A real one this time. Jimzon smiled, the tightness in his chest easing just a little.
A nurse stepped into the room, clipboard in hand. "Mister… Squire? We’re ready for you."
He stood up, but looked down at the girl one last time. "Hang in there, okay?"
"I will, mister," she said, grinning now. "You’re a funny clown."
Jimzon got up, feeling a little lighter, and approached the nurse.
"Before we take you to visit your friend, do you have any allergies or injuries we should know about?"
"No, ma'am."
"Alright. Follow me through this doorway. You'll be sprayed and dried all in just a few seconds, then the airlock will open and you can see Mister Grugg. Also, for our records—do you know his last name?"
"Grugg’s?"
"His name is Grugg Gruggs?"
Jimzon gave a faint smile. "Sure. Yeah."
She looked confused, but nodded. "Alright. Strange, but… head right in. A doctor will assist you on the other side.
He stepped inside and stood in the center of the cramped, closet-sized room as the door slid shut behind him. He drew a slow breath, steadying himself. This was not his first decontamination shower. The faint hum of pipes came alive, and mist began to spray down cool and harmless at first. He lifted his arms, bracing for the usual routine.
Then the fire hit.
A sharp, stabbing pain exploded in his shoulder, like molten metal searing through muscle and bone. Beneath the fabric of his suit, two plasma burns glowed softly, a hot purple light pulsing faintly against his skin. It felt as if something beneath the surface was boiling, writhing, clawing desperately to break free. His legs buckled, and he dropped to his knees, gasping. The agony spread to his thigh—sudden, white-hot torment that clawed upward, breaking his breath again. Every nerve screamed, electric jolts ripping through him, his skin flaring as if aflame from within.
He grunted, struggling for air, wanting to scream, his chest heaving violently while powerful fans blasted the mist with brutal force, pushing the burning chemical deep into his pores. The Guard’s decontamination spray was designed to be gentle, crafted to avoid allergic reactions and plasma burns that were now relics of the past for most civilians. But for him, it was torture—each second was another wave of razor-sharp fire slicing through his flesh and soul.
His throat tightened; he choked and drooled uncontrollably, swallowed by pain and panic. The world spun, colors bleeding at the edges.
When the door slid open, a doctor waited calmly. He looked down at the trembling figure in the jester’s suit, kneeling and gasping like a broken animal.
“First time, I take it?” the doctor said with a smug smirk.
“Yeah,” Jimzon choked out between coughs.
“Won’t take long to get used to. Remember, it’s a mind over matter thing. Unless you’ve got a severe parasite, scales, or plasma burns, you should be fine. Don’t worry.”
Jimzon forced himself to his feet. “Yes, of course. Bring me to Grugg.”
“Right this way.” The doctor led him down a dimly lit corridor to a small dark room with four chairs and a large glass window. “Your friend is right in there. It’s a sensory deprivation tank, so he can’t see or hear us. This,” he knocked gently on the glass, “is a one-way mirror so we can observe him.”
Jimzon approached the window. Berji floated face-up in a pool of glowing blue floom liquid that cast a soft, eerie light over his silhouette. Tubes hung from the ceiling, snaking into his throat and connecting to a respirator.
Jimzon’s stomach twisted. His friend looked like a corpse bloated, and barely breathing, but at least no longer bleeding. His face was partially obscured, but what was visible was deep, dark red, scarred and uneven. It was painful to admit, but Berji looked as grotesque as when Jimzon had pulled him from the wreckage. How could he even imagine how his friend would react when he woke up?
Is he going to survive?” Jimzon asked, his eyes locked on his friend’s still form.
“He ought to, yes. Needs two or three more days, at least.”
Jimzon said nothing.
“There are a few things you should know. His brain, thankfully, didn’t suffer any permanent damage. But if he wakes up, your friend might not be the same.”
Jimzon’s brow furrowed. “What do you mean if?”
“His body endured wounds so catastrophic that parts of his organs began to shut down. His body was dying before his mind. This floomicide we use is highly effective, but it has its limits. He’s alive, but essentially comatose. We tried to resuscitate him, but as doctors, there’s only so much we can do. We can’t cure death, only come close. You’re lucky he made it here in time.”
The doctor’s voice grew softer. “If he wakes, his face will be permanently disfigured. Mentally, it’s a huge trauma—especially with the effects of floomicide. The drugs are powerful, but they cause a loss of grip on reality during the healing process.”
Jimzon looked again at Berji, wanting to comfort him somehow but knowing there was nothing he could do but wait.
The doctor added, “Since you’re not from around here, you might not know there is… something else we can try.”
Jimzon’s voice was steady. “I’ll do it.”
“I appreciate your enthusiasm, young man,” the doctor said, his tone measured. “As I said, this is the best we can do medically. But there are shamans in the village who might be able to wake him. We don’t fully understand the methods they use, but between us, I’ve seen it work.”
“Shamans? You really believe in that stuff?”
“Sometimes I’m surprised I do myself," The doctor smirked, then pulled down his collar to reveal a large circular scar burned into his chest. “We were clearing land for a helipad when a stampede of Kraskors hit us. Those beasts are horned, massive and they charge like a moving wall a mile wide. They crush everything. Trees, rocks, especially people.”
He paused, voice low. “There was a native with us, surveying the land and he tried to warn us, but it was too late when one targeted me. I felt the horn tear through my chest, straight into my heart.”
His eyes darkened. “After that, I don’t remember much. But my mates told me the shaman ran over, murmured strange words, and somehow stopped me from bleeding out. They say he ‘paused’ my dying body. Animated my legs just long enough to walk back to the car. Then hooked me up to floomicide. Call it magic or whatever you want but it saved me.”
“Gods… I’m glad you’re here then. That’s fucked,” Jimzon muttered.
“God,” the doctor corrected, a slight smile tugging at his lips. “And I’m glad to be here too. Since then, I vowed to help as many as I can. If you want to wake your friend, you’ll need to find a shaman.”
Jimzon nodded, absorbing the weight of those words.
He stepped outside and found a bench by the dusty main road. Sitting down, he felt an unexpected calm wash over him. He closed his eyes and took several deep, steady breaths.
An old man soon shuffled over and settled onto the bench beside Jimzon. His weathered hands moved with practiced ease as he unzipped a faded, patched fanny pack resting on his worn knees. From inside, he pulled out a small handful of seeds, rough and dusty, and scattered them gently across the cracked dirt at their feet. Almost instantly, tiny fletchling birds appeared from the underbrush, fluttering down with soft chirps and quick, eager hops. Their delicate bodies bounced lightly as they pecked at the scattered seeds, feathers shimmering faintly in the warm sunlight, their lively presence breaking the silence that hung between the two men.
“Excuse me, sir, mind if I ask you something?” Jimzon said, breaking the quiet furthur.
“Go for it,” the old man replied without hesitation.
“Do you know any shamans around here?”
“Oh yeah, they’re around. There’s a feast tonight they lead. Odd fellows.”
“Thanks. I’m new to the area, Things here are… a lot different.”
“You’re telling me. These kids have it easy nowadays.”
“Easy?” Jimzon muttered, thinking of the little girl he just talked to.
“Maybe not easy. Maybe I’m just bitter. I grew up during the war.”
“That must’ve been real hard.”
“It was. Some days, it feels like nothing’s changed. I thought fighting would fix our problems, but here we are. Other times, it feels like everything’s changed, and I’m just a fish out of water. Just another old man now”
“I certainly feel you there.” Jimzon remarked.
“What’s with the getup anyhow?”
“I’m a squire. Gotta wear it if I want to get paid.”
“Hmpf,” the old man said with a knowing look. “Soldier to soldier, there are plenty better careers out there.”
“Soldier?”
“I know one when I see one. Your eyes keep scanning the trail. You can relax here, son.”
“It’s a… tough adjustment.”
“It always is. But it isn’t always.”
“I like that,” Jimzon said quietly.
“You got a wife?”
“No, sir.”
“Don’t waste your time here then. You’re still young. The time for settling comes later.”
“I’m trying. I’m just a bit lost.”
“You’re never lost if you’ve got a good head on your shoulders. This is the place to be happy,” he said, tapping his temple. “It’s not the place itself.”
“Easier said than done. I don’t know if I ever will be.”
“You’re damn right it isn’t easy. But you can have the pain of giving up or the pain of growth. Just take in what’s around you. The sun is shining, birds are chirping, life is flourishing. Today may be your worst day, but it could be someone’s best day. Things always change. You can change. You’re not stuck like this,” the old man said, his voice steady and calm.
“I used to think I was strong" Jimzon choked out. "On the battlefield, I found… order in the chaos. I liked it. I was good at it. But… now I see I’m not the man I thought I was. My entire life was just me lying to myself, and now I don’t fit anywhere. I fucked my life up, and it’s my fault.”
“Hey, kid. You can’t hate yourself into being better. I wish someone told me that at your age. I know you have questions, but you also have the answers. You just need to think more openly. Sometimes the only way out is in. Stop looking for the bad and the ugly. Yeah, it’s there, but that’s not all that’s there.”
Jimzon began nodding slowly. "How'd you get so smart?"
The old man chuckled softly. "I used to be a soldier for hire, then I aged out. Had a lot of time to think. To process. One day, I was out hiking and saw a beetle drowned in its own sap. For the first time, I understood."
"Understood what?" Jimzon asked, curious.
"Everything," the old man replied quietly. "I donated what was left of my fortune and joined here."
Jimzon was confused, but somehow satisfied with the answer.
"What’s your name?"
"Den. It’s been a pleasure, kid."
"I'm Smelvin. I'll see you around."
"Take care of yourself."
"You too sir" Jimzon echoed.
That conversation lingered in Jimzon’s mind long after he left the bench. There was a lot to think about.
The sky was shifting into a calm yellow, signaling the approach of sunset. Jimzon adjusted his fake mustache, slick with sweat, and almost forgot he was still wearing his costume.
A soft rumble vibrated through the ground. He turned toward the road to see a convoy of hover trucks approaching, painted white and neon orange, moving cautiously and deliberately. They passed close by, their engines humming quietly.
The trucks carried men dressed in similarly covered outfits, their uniforms marked with the Trailblazers’ shovel logo. Strapped across the flatbeds were massive, dead alien beasts—the Kraskors the doctor had mentioned. These creatures were enormous, their heavy bodies weighing down the trucks’ rears. Their tough, weathered skin was marked by deep folds and creases, mottled with dust and cracked dried mud. A giant tusk protruded from one face, frozen in a permanent snarl, teeth yellowed and clenched even in death.
Five or six hover cars followed, filled with people who seemed energized and excited by the procession.
As Jimzon reached the town square beneath the great Elder Tree, his thoughts drifted to the little girl he had met earlier. He couldn’t help but wonder if that young girls father was going to be alright.
He leaned against a smooth stone pillar, a small local monument, waiting for time to pass. His eyes landed on the inscriptions carved into the surface written in the Maspin alphabet, a class he had been forced to take at the academy. He squinted, trying to decipher the unfamiliar symbols. All he could make out was a fragment: "Cycle, Sleep, a tree, of One… May we rest fully awake."
In truth, the words were gibberish to him, though he sensed a hidden meaning buried beneath the words. He scanned the pillar and the settlement further, searching for anything else he could recognize. Mixed in among the unfamiliar script, he spotted some Galactic Standard, undoubtedly placed by humans, and a handful of simple words in Maspin he knew: "Store," "Food," "School," "Library." He found himself quietly impressed. This place was not as alien as he had once feared.
From behind him, drifting through the massive Elder Tree, came the sound of singing. The language was still Maspin, deep and passionate, the words flowing smoothly and elegantly from one to the next despite his inability to understand.
Curious, Jimzon turned to the towering tree. Its wide entrance was nearly two stories tall, open with no doors, only a welcoming sign that read something like “Welcome All.”
Drawn by the music, he stepped inside the hollow trunk. The acoustics were stunning; every note wrapped around him, pulling him deeper with their magnetic reverb. The space was dimly lit but perfectly comfortable, neither cold nor warm. Along the walls, colorful abstract mosaics climbed upward into the shadows, telling stories in vibrant shards of glass and stone.
Above the center hung a grand chandelier, illuminating a gathering of humans and aliens alike. The atmosphere was calm but charged with a quiet power, an ancient peace woven through the room’s very air.
Strangely enough, in the center of the room, there was an abyss. A perfect, yawning pit that seemed to descend endlessly into darkness. It was circled by smooth stone, forming a low barrier to prevent anyone from accidentally falling in. Yet atop that stone, standing with effortless balance, was an alien figure singing into the void.
Jimzon couldn’t tell if the figure was a priest or performer—his clothing looked ordinary, not ceremonial. A small hood covered most of his face, shadowed and unreadable. But the song he sang, even muffled by the language barrier, carried something ancient and heavy in it. Jimzon strained to understand, picking out fragments of the lyrics in a broken translation:
"...my days are done, my life was fun, time to be, a part of one
The tree grows, for what we know, for what we know.
I tended the garden, until it bore fruit
I amended my wrongs, I spoke a song
Somehow, someday, I shall see the light of day
My children are safe and have some of their own
I built and given all I had ever known
Presently, I'm absent. I extinguish my flame and return the same
For what we know, for what we know..."
His voice trembled slightly near the end of the verse. Then he paused. Slowly, he lowered his hood.
Jimzon’s breath caught. There was no head—at least, no physical one. Instead, a gentle swirl of gray smoke billowed from the hood’s opening. In place of eyes, two floating spheres of water hovered in the haze, constantly shifting and rippling, like tears caught in zero gravity.
The figure looked around the room with serenity. No fear. No hesitation.
He removed the necklace from his neck and set it gently at his feet on the stone rim. Then, he lifted his face—if it could be called that—toward the sky and continued singing, his smoky form stretching and wavering with the melody like a candle’s flame in the wind.
"Even graves grow flowers, I may sink like a stone
For what we know, for what we know,
But you'll never be alone."
With that final line, he opened his arms in a gesture of calm surrender and stepped backward, falling soundlessly into the abyss.
The room held its breath. No gasps, no cries—just stillness. Then, with a gentle hiss, a stream of glowing blue steam shot up from the abyss, curling upward before vanishing into the trunk of the Elder Tree. It was absorbed without resistance, like the tree had been waiting.
An alien figure stepped forward with quiet reverence. They picked up the necklace, turned it in their hands, and carefully removed a small blue gem embedded in its center. Without a word, they approached the mosaic-lined wall and placed the gem among thousands of others—each one glimmering faintly in the dim light, like stars frozen in bark.
Jimzon blinked. Something in him loosened, and a single tear slipped down his cheek, uninvited and unexplained. He hadn’t even realized he’d been holding it in.
"What the fuck?" he muttered under his breath.
Next to him, a human Trailblazer stood silently, arms crossed. Jimzon turned to him, bewildered.
"Did… did he just die?"
"Yep," the man said, without flinching.

Chapter 24
	“Alright,” Jimzon said, giving Pav a quick nod. “Dom, I’ll see you later. Good luck.”
"Same to you, man. Let me know how Grugg’s doing. If he’s awake, tell him we’ll be out of this mess soon,” Pav said.
Jimzon nodded and gave a half-hearted wave before turning his back and sulking away.
Pav furrowed his brow, frustration simmering just beneath the surface. He could feel the shift in Jimzon’s demeanor. Cold, clipped, quietly hostile. It wasn’t just stress anymore. It was something deeper, meaner. They were supposed to be in this together, but Jimzon had been unraveling since they landed. First the outburst about mercy-killing Berji. Then trashing a bungalow that didn’t even belong to them, smashing furniture like a tantruming child. He’d been taking his fury out on Pav in subtle, constant ways. Snapping. Dismissing. Glaring.
Pav understood the pressure. He felt it too. They’d lost everything—their ranks, their pay, their future. They weren’t soldiers anymore. Just fugitives with no command structure, no allies, no path forward. But Jimzon was supposed to be the captain. The leader who stayed calm when the world caught fire. Instead, he was flailing, unpredictable, volatile.
And Pav was tired of pretending not to notice.
He tried to bury the thought as soon as it formed, but it clung stubbornly. If Berji had been the one awake instead… maybe things would feel more stable. Maybe he’d have someone to rely on. Someone who didn’t treat the people around him like sandbags to punch through. He hated himself for thinking it. But he couldn’t deny it either.
“Time for business,” Calsco said, glancing at Pav. “How much do you know about Royal customs?”
“Uh… not much, I guess,” Pav shrugged.
Calsco raised an eyebrow. “Okay, well—how about the history of the royals?”
“History? Eh… not really.” Pav scratched his head. “I thought they were just the guys with fancy hats and parades”
Calsco blinked. “Nothing? The largest empire in the galaxy and you know nothing?”
Pav smirked. “They’re not that large, right? Don’t they only rule like, what, ten percent of the galaxy?”
“Not that large? And how much of the galaxy do you own, boy?”
Pav straightened his prince disguise and gave a mock regal tone: “Ten percent, Your Highness.”
Calsco chuckled. “Good answer. I’ll teach you on our walk to the council.”
They set off along a dirt path that soon gave way to soft limestone underfoot.
“Where to begin... Well what do you know about the royals?” Calsco asked.
Pav thought for a second. “They uh… wear blue. I’ve done some ops with royal special forces—they always had intricate armor and better rations.”
“Ah, okay. So, nothing,” Calsco said with a grin. “What do they teach you in the academy, anyway?”
“Hey, man, I’m an engineer. History isn’t exactly my thing.”
“Well, it is today. The Royal System and its colonies survived the Great War mostly by avoiding it. Cowards? Maybe. But clever cowards. There was still collateral, of course. There always is. They’ve been brilliant, sophisticated… and also soft. Weak. Drenched in ceremony and self-importance.”
“So you want me to act smug and pretentious?”
“Yes. And no. See, everyone with a drop of Royal blood—cousins, nephews, disgraced uncles—they all get carved a seat in government. Lifelong safety nets. Old money. They never worry about finances or consequences. So what do they chase instead?”
“What?”
“Status. Coolness. Rebellion—but the kind you can wear like a jacket and take off before dinner. It's not apart of them. Many of them go around slumming in poor-boy fashion, talking like they’ve survived hardship, when the closest they’ve come is skipping brunch. It’s all a performance. A soft rebellion.”
“Why fake being poor?”
"To them, struggle’s exotic. It’s fashion. Makes them feel dangerous. Gritty. But it’s all for show. They want the look of hardship without the weight of it—so they can say ‘I made myself’ with a full stomach and a safety net. But real poverty? That’s mold in your lungs, a hole in your gut, and no one coming to help. There’s nothing cool about it. It doesn’t build character. It breaks people. And they wouldn’t survive a day"
“Got it. So basically, I’m a guy pretending to be rich, who’s pretending to be poor.”
“Something like that.”
“Look, man, I’m no actor.”
“You don’t have a choice. From here on out, every day’s a performance. You’re a wanted man. You already look the part. That’s what counts.”
“The outfit?”
“Presentation is everything. Politics is theater—gestures, posturing. People don’t always care if it’s a lie. They care if it’s a believable hill to stand on, one that is picturesque, something that feeds their ego. Even if a council member suspects, they’ll keep up the act if they think they can brag to their friends about dealing with royalty, claiming they have acquaintances in high places—whether it’s true or not.”
“I don’t know, man. You seem to know a lot about this, but those guys just seem like hippies. Are you sure they’re like other politicians?”
“Haha, yes. I’m sure. Customs might shift from planet to planet, but a politician’s mind? That never changes. Senates, councils, it does not matter. They all claim to protect the people, to be like chainmail armor—each link strong and united. But in reality? They’re like piranhas, tearing at the weakest link just to climb higher towards the crown.”
“Like I said, man, I build bridges and bombs. Lose the metaphors.”
“People drawn to politics want power. And to get more of it, they’ll betray everyone around them—even their own values—if it means they come out on top.”
“You’re sounding a lot like The Autumnals.”
“Don’t mention their name. They started that war, and now we’re the ones stuck cleaning up the mess.”
A heavy silence fell between them. Pav realized he’d hit a nerve—these weren’t just political rants. Something deeper had been cut.
“Say, Prince,” Calsco said with a knowing look, “you seem to know so little about politics. How did your friends manage to convince you to go AWOL?”
Pav hesitated, searching for words. He wasn’t sure if he wanted to open up — or if he fully understood it himself.
“I guess... I did my time as a soldier. Thought it was time to retire. Started seeing through a lot of lies, realizing I wasn’t really helping the universe the way I thought I was. They promised to cover my education and let me travel the galaxy. It’s hard to be an engineer when half our continent got blown back to the stone age. I didn't know what I signed up for”
Calsco nodded slowly. “I see. Whatever you saw must have been real bad to cause seven defections.”
“Seven?” Pav blinked, confused. “I think you miscounted.”
“No,” Calsco said, checking his holowatch and reciting smoothly, “Captain Jimzon Avaris, Pavlock, Berji, Yakib, Cillus, Quinn, Kelsa.”
Pav paused, the news hitting harder than he expected. He knew his problem with Jimzon wasn’t going to get any easier now. “Kelsa?”
“Kelsadria Vot Feentor-Jayzin. You are unfamiliar?”
“Quite the opposite." Pav sighed  "Fuck. Just… don’t tell Jimzon. They used to date. We need him focused, not distracted. And the other two?”
“Quinn Domnall, an accountant and Prince Cillus Vot Shayron-Skoln.”
“Prince? How does a prince defect from The Guard if he's a royal?”
"Since the Guards and the Royals are allies, sometimes units or individuals assist each other. Medics, Doctors, etc. It says here Cillus survived the Moon of Sonaff Massacre last month. Must be a hell of a warrior going against those raiders."
“Shit. Okay, uh… I’m losing track of my thoughts here.”
“No matter. The palace is just up ahead. I’ll handle the talking. We’ll be sent to a waiting room.”
	Tucked between a cradle of forested hills, the palace rose in serene defiance of nature. Its structure was carved from smooth, unblemished sandstone—pale and radiant, as if the rock itself had never known time. Tall columns flanked its front like silent sentries, their surfaces catching the sun in streaks of warm brown and cool bluish-gray, polished to an almost glassy sheen. The massive dome above shimmered faintly, casting soft, symmetrical shadows across the stone steps below.
A hush seemed to blanket the area—no birds, no insects, only the whisper of wind sliding through distant trees.
Then, the great oak doors creaked open, not by mechanism, but by hand—if “hand” was the right word. Two red-robed figures emerged, their forms shifting subtly in the light. They were made of smoke, not cloaked in it—wisps constantly folding and reforming into the shape of humanoids. Where their faces should’ve been were only vague contours, and where eyes belonged floated perfectly round droplets of water, suspended in place like dew refusing to fall.
An envoy stepped forward from the shadowed interior. He bowed low, the folds of his formal garment catching the light like silk. When he rose, his voice came cool and measured.
"The Council of Riflo humbly welcomes Prince Dometre Polaris into its presence," the envoy intoned, bowing once more. "A chamber has been prepared in anticipation of your arrival."
"Much obliged, sir," Calsco replied with a graceful nod. "We are honored to be your guests."
Pav stepped forward, letting just enough arrogance drip from his tone to test the waters.
"Your palace is... cozy and beautiful," he said, eyes scanning the minimalist grandeur. "Yet I see no bannermen, no trumpets, no grand decor. I survived a ship crash to stand here—and I had expected at least a whisper of gratitude for my struggle."
Calsco gave him a sharp look, the kind that warned of unseen consequences. Pav ignored it.
The gatekeeper’s watery eyes shimmered, his smoky form rippling subtly.
"My excellency," he said, bowing deeper. "We meant no disrespect. In truth, your arrival outpaced our preparation. I will personally see to the chamber’s refinement. Whatever will ease your burden and reflect our gratitude, I shall provide."
Pav's smirk curled with satisfaction.
"You are a good soul, gatekeeper. I shall make it worth your while."
	The Gatekeeper bowed low, mist trailing faintly from his form. “It is my highest honor.”
He turned and led them into the palace.
Inside, the air was cool and reverent, like a temple crossed with a museum. Sculptures—identical in style to those the natives carved by hand—now stood in gleaming gold, catching the light in divine brilliance. Towering paintings loomed twenty feet high, lining the walls like silent watchers. Some depicted lush jungles and flowing rivers, others captured moments of native life, and a few descended into the strange and abstract—whorls of color, emotion, and meaning that pulsed with something primal.
At the end of the grand hall, the Gatekeeper stopped before a heavy burgundy door inlaid with gold studs that shimmered like stars. He pushed it open with a graceful motion, revealing the chamber beyond.
The chamber was spacious but intimate, with ceilings just low enough to press the air down into a calm hush. Thick fur carpets softened each footstep, muffling the sounds of the outside world. Elegant, curving couches in shades of deep blue and gold lined the room, inviting and regal. A sunken hearth flickered quietly with an indoor fire, its flames dancing beneath a carved stone mantle. In the far corner, a small pond shimmered, alive with darting alien fish that shimmered like moving gemstones in the gentle light.
Daiser gestured to the room. “Knock on the door should you need anything. A servant will be waiting at all hours. I leave you to your privacy.” He bowed and closed the door with reverent care.
Pav immediately flopped onto one of the couches, exhaling as he threw his feet up on a polished stone coffee table.
Calsco didn’t sit. He remained standing, arms crossed. “You’re lucky your arrogance worked on Daiser,” he said, voice tight with restrained anger. “I told you to be cocky, not reckless. Stick to the plan.”
“He’s just a doorman,” Pav replied, casual. “I was testing the waters. Figured that’s how a prince would talk.”
Calsco’s glare sharpened. “They’re more subtle than that. Maybe they’ll chalk up your manners to being concussed in the crash. But don’t push it. You can play with them—but not with me. And he’s not just a gatekeeper. His name is Daiser. He’s… like you. Someone surviving the pressure. Don’t add more weight to his shoulders.”
Pav felt the sting of that, but he masked it behind a smirk. Calsco's constant control was grating, the way he always pulled the strings, always had the final say. Pav was tired of it.
“You told me to act,” he said, reclining further. “This is acting.”
Without rising, he reached out and knocked casually on the wall. A moment later, the door opened with a soft hiss. An alien attendant in flowing robes stepped in, bowing in silence.
“Servant,” Pav said lazily, “bring us some appetizers. And send that gatekeeper boy in with them.”
The attendant gave a short nod and backed out.
Calsco’s voice cut like a blade. “Who do you think you are?”
“Prince Dometré Polaris,” Pav said with a grin.
Calsco’s expression soured. “You’re aware of the situation you find yourselves in, no? You are not in control. I hold every card—if I chose to turn you in, there’s no one who’d stop me.”
“To the council?” Pav leaned back, spreading his arms. “The same council you want me to lie to? The one guarding the quaint little village where you buy knockoff robes to pass me off as royalty? Please. We’re in this together whether you like it or not.”
For a moment, Calsco looked ready to snap. But then… something shifted. The disdain in his eyes softened, replaced by a flicker of reluctant admiration.
“I have taught you well,” he said, almost to himself.
Then he laughed. First a breath, then a low chuckle, and finally a full-bodied, genuine laugh that echoed off the low ceiling.
“Wherever this cockiness came from, it fits you like a tailored suit. Maybe we could keep working together after this mission.”

Pav smiled and poured himself a cup of astrovin, the liquid catching the firelight like molten crystal. “Maybe.”
Calsco leaned forward. “I’ll cut to the chase—before Daiser gets here. Just play along with the negotiations and their terms. Be reasonable. Ask for enough credits to cover fuel, food, and a ship loan. You’ll nominate me to pilot and ‘return’ it later.”
“Seems easy enough.”
“One last thing. Beryllium rifles. Whatever you can get.”
“You want guns?”
“I need guns.”
Pav raised an eyebrow. “Isn’t this a diplomatic vessel? Do they have to be Beryllium?”
"Their cargo doesn't need to be marked as weapons. And yes, they have to be Beryllium.”
“Why Beryllium?”
Calsco grinned. “I have my reasons. Best anti-piracy tool in the galaxy is still a classic Berry hole puncher.”
Pav smirked and took a sip of his wine. “Fine.”
“I’m glad you agree. Pass me that atrovin.”
Pav handed him the bottle and a goblet. Calsco ignored the glass, popped the cork, and drank straight from the neck. Three heavy gulps. He wiped his mouth with the back of his hand and let out a thunderous burp.
A sharp knock echoed at the door. Moments later, Daiser—the Maspin—entered, his smoky form gliding smoothly across the floor. He carried a low, ornate tray balanced expertly in his hands, piled with delicate hors d’oeuvres.
“I bring you the finest fermented Kraskor,” he announced, voice soft but clear, “and pickled inside-barked mycelium—staple delicacies of our cuisine.” He gestured modestly to the food as he set the tray gently on the table. “And here, a customary bottle of—ah, I see you’ve already accompanied yourselves with our local wine choice.”
Pav motioned toward an empty chair. “Have a seat. I’d like to chat.”
Daiser hesitated, tilting his head—a subtle gesture unfamiliar to him—and then lowered himself carefully into the chair.
Pav cleared his throat. “Daiser, I want to apologize for snapping at you earlier. My temper’s been on edge since the crash.”
A faint shimmer passed through Daiser’s smoky visage. “It’s quite alright, Your Majesty. I could never imagine flying, let alone crashing.”
Pav cocked an eyebrow. “You’ve never flown?”
“No, sir. I am proud to serve as caretaker of our council’s temple.”
Calsco nudged Daiser with a smirk. “The prince is a friend—no need for the charade.”
Daiser’s smoky form flickered slightly as he glanced at Pav, then back at Calsco. His watery eyes shimmered with a mix of surprise and skepticism. “Truly?”
Calsco grinned, giving a firm nod.
Daiser continued "Honestly? This job blows. Not ‘cause of you guys—no offense, Your Highness—but just... I’m gonna be stuck in this dust-box for the next seven years unless my uncle dies or I win the lottery. And we don’t even have a lottery.”
“Are you, uh… a slave?” Pav asked, sitting up a little straighter.
Daiser’s smoky form flickered as he replied, “We don’t use that word here. Maspin is a ‘slave-free territory.’ We call it indentured servitude—where I work off an imaginary debt until I can pay it off and be free, which is actually fucking hard without a paycheck.”
He shrugged, a faint ember glowing within his smoky head. “It’s whatever. I know your family has servants anyways.”
Pav opened his mouth to respond but couldn’t find the words. Daiser glanced over at Calsco and made a subtle motion. Calsco unzipped his chest pocket and passed him a cigar.
Daiser snapped his wispy fingers, and a few sparks danced, just enough to light the cigar. He inhaled deeply, the glowing embers swirling like fireflies in his smoky head, before exhaling a slow, curling plume.
“You two know each other, I take it?” Pav asked, watching their exchange.
Daiser glanced at Calsco before answering, his smoky form flickering slightly. “Yeah, we go back a few years. Guy took care of my flooms when I got sentenced to work here with my uncle.”
“Is your uncle a council member?”
Daiser drew a deep, slow puff from his cigar, the glowing ember casting flickering light across his smoky face. He exhaled a thick, curling plume. “All due respect, you’ve got a fuckton of questions, Your Majesty. But yeah, he’s the one who’s a huge prick. After your meeting, I got a long weekend for the feast tonight, so I’m just gonna kick it here with y’all till then if that’s cool.”
“I’m happy to let you spend time here, if you do something for me,” Pav said, locking eyes with Daiser. “Tell me about your uncle. I want a ship to get home, some spare coin for the trip, and rifles. Tell me exactly what I need to say to make that happen.”
Daiser let out a low, amused chuckle. “Rifles? For who? For you? Have you ever even fired a plasma rifle before?”
“Not plasma,” Pav replied evenly. “Beryllium.”
Daiser’s smoky form jerked sharply, as if taken aback. “Beryllium? Wait—wait, I can’t tell if you’re joking. You want toxic rifles? Normal rounds aren’t good enough? You don’t just wanna put holes in people—you wanna give them cancer too?”
Pav fought to keep his face neutral, eyes steady despite the jab.
Daiser’s smoky form shifted, the faint glow of his watery eyes flickering in the dim light. “Bad news—the council doesn’t even have any to spare. But yeah… maybe I know a guy who could make that happen. Beryllium rifles are banned here, and in your system too, so I’m gonna need something to make it worth my guys’ while.”
Pav leaned forward, voice low and steady. “Do you enjoy this planet, Daiser?”
Daiser snorted, the faint swirl of smoke around his head pulsing with dry humor. “What, you kidding? Hokey-poking around trees and being chained up as a political servant? Yeah, man, I love it.”
Pav’s eyes locked onto his, serious now. “Make this happen, and I can grant you freedom.”
The smoke around Daiser’s head thickened as he leaned in, voice dropping. “Don’t joke about that.”
“Freedom,” Pav repeated quietly. “Just put in a good word. If I have my way, you’ll have yours.”
Daiser nodded his head. "That guy I mentioned, its me. I am - was a chemist. I can get you those arms. Alright, your majesty. I'll get right on that. Word of advice for my uncle, he's a hardass, loves his rules. Its strange like, his love of rules transends his own rules, like, if you love your own authority he'll respect that. This street talk I use, he hates, so dont waiver".
"That seems reasonable. I appreciate that Daiser"
"Of course your excelllence, I would not bestow anything but the most advanced vital knowledge upon the steward of the Royal Colonies." He said in his fake accent. "I'm gonna get a head start on crafting those. Just find me when you need me" he said, hopping out. He gave Calsco a street handshake before exiting.
Daiser gave a slow nod. “That guy I mentioned? That’s me. I am - was a chemist. I can get you those arms.” He fixed Pav with a sharp look. “Alright, your majesty, I’ll get right on it. One word of advice about my uncle—he’s a hardass, loves his rules. Strange thing is, his love of rules transcends even his own rules. If you respect your own authority like it’s his, he’ll respect that back. But this street talk I use? He hates it. So don’t waiver.”
“That sounds reasonable. I appreciate the heads-up, Daiser.”
“Of course, your excellence. I wouldn’t bestow anything less than the most advanced and vital knowledge upon the steward of the Royal Colonies.” His smoky voice dripped with playful sarcasm in a clipped fake accent. “I’m gonna get a head start on crafting those. Just find me when you need me.”
With that, he gave Calsco a firm street handshake—smoke curling like fingers around their clasp—then slipped out the door with a confident stride.
Pav chewed thoughtfully on the tough, gamey Kraskor meat, then looked over at Calsco with a smirk. "Well? How’d I do?"
Calsco gave a slow, deliberate nod of approval. "I’m... impressed. You’re holding up your end of the bargain better than I expected. It wasn’t easy keeping quiet back there. Usually, I wouldn't lie freeing a man from his shackles. It sounds less suave, more advantageous."
Pav shrugged. "Well, I wasn’t lying."
Calsco raised an eyebrow, a hint of amusement flickering across his face. "Your generosity clearly knows no bounds. Especially when it comes to your wallet. You don’t have the credits to pay for any of this."
Pav’s gaze hardened. "When I leave, I’m not coming back. Neither is he. He can come with us."
Calsco shook his head. “You insist on digging a deeper hole for yourself. Calm your ambition. He’s a friend, sure, but too trusting. He’s better off here, long term.”
Pav shrugged. “Maybe. But it’s his choice to make. Without him, you won’t get your rifles, and you’ll run out of product fast.”
Calsco raised an eyebrow. “Product?”
Pav leaned back. “Your floom operation. You care for a creature that produces drugs, thanks to your chemist buddy. Seems he’s the only contact you actually have here.”
Calsco rubbed his chin. “Maybe... bringing him isn’t the worst idea. It’s not just for recreation; we’re taking what we can to help those who need it.”
Pav smirked. “Then he’ll owe me.”
Calsco gave a slow grin. “Free a man from chains only for him to owe you? A true liberator. Think he’ll be thrilled when he finds out you’re not really a prince? Dream big, but handle these lies one day at a time.”
“Perhaps you’re right. When’s this council meeting supposed to happen?”
“Soon enough,” Calsco said, eyes scanning the room. “They’ve been holding sessions with the townsfolk all day—today’s one of the biggest days on the Maspin calendar. It’s tied to their seasonal feast, some ancient religious tradition they take very seriously. The whole town’s buzzing with it.”
He paused, then added, “Our part starts when the Bell rings. Until then, we wait.”
Pav exhaled slowly, his gaze distant. “I hope Jimzon and Berji are holding up alright.”
The two men settled back, feet propped up comfortably as they savored the delicate hors d’oeuvres laid before them. For a few stolen hours, amid the soft murmurs of the feast preparations and the warm glow of the room, they lived like kings—briefly escaping the weight of their burdens—until the deep, resonant toll of the bell echoed through the halls, calling them back to reality.

	Chapter 25
The bell rang out like a noble declaration. Clear, low, and resonant. Its tone carried through the vaulted chamber, soft yet commanding, and drifted beyond the stone walls into the jungle canopy beyond. The air itself seemed to pause and listen.
Calsco stood first, brushing crumbs from his thighs with the back of his hand. “Showtime,” he muttered with a sly smirk, mostly to himself.
Pav rose a beat later, slower, careful not to show hesitation. He straightened his collar with deliberate precision and began twisting one of the many rings on his fingers. His thumb grazed the edge of a gemstone—an anchor to calm himself. He let out a silent breath through his nose, chest tightening beneath the ceremonial garb.
The chamber's doors creaked open. A small Maspin acolyte in a deep blue robe stepped just inside the threshold. Bowing slightly, he looked up at Pav with luminous eyes and said, with a voice like water over glass:
“The Council is ready for you, Your Majesty.”
As the acolyte led them through the archway, Pav stepped onto a corridor cloaked in royal blue. A thick carpet stretched down the hall, its deep hue almost identical to the shade of his ceremonial suit. The resemblance made his stomach tighten. Draped from the stone walls were hand-stitched banners, rough around the edges, their dyes uneven. Yet each bore the royal insignia with earnest pride. The seams wavered, the lines weren’t quite straight, but someone had labored over them. Someone had believed in what they represented.
Maspin musicians were spaced evenly down the corridor like quiet statues. They stood along the carpet, each cradling a small lute-like instrument, fingers plucking with delicate precision. The melody they played drifted gently through the air, soft and reverent, as if it were wind slipping through trees. A song not meant to overwhelm but to stir a sense of quiet awe.
Pav smiled instinctively. For a fleeting moment, wonder sparked behind his eyes. They had done all this for him.
But the feeling soured just as quickly.
It wasn’t for him. Not truly.
He was not the prince they thought he was.
The smile stayed, carefully maintained, but something behind his eyes waned. A quiet ache. His expression held its shape, but his gaze turned inward, softened by the weight of pretending. The shame did not rise loudly. It moved like a slow tide, steady and certain.
Still, he walked forward with measured grace, his back straight and his chin high, through a tribute built for someone else.
Calsco placed a hand on the prince’s shoulder. “Homestretch,” he said quietly. Then, with a soft gesture, he leaned toward a servant and murmured a command for them to fetch the squire from the town square.
Together, they walked down the hallway until they arrived before a grand golden door. The surface was engraved with an alien figure, it's limbs elongated and posture serene. It looked like a recreation of an ancient carving, polished and honored anew. The alien hovered just above the ground, arms outstretched, handing a small burning sun to the kneeling natives below. Pav stared into its eyes. For a moment, he thought they stared back.
The doors creaked open. The blue carpet, regal and earnest, ended there. Beyond it lay a room unlike any he’d stepped into before.
They stood at the base of a towering chamber. It wasn’t wide, nor especially long, but it stretched upward like a minaret. Torch sconces flickered along the walls, though they were unnecessary. The structure itself was alive.
The walls inside were grown from twisted root and sinew, as if the architecture had been coaxed into shape rather than built. Their surface pulsed faintly with soft bioluminescence. Moss clung to the walls in patches, glowing and shifting color like a slow tide. Occasionally, weightless spores drifted down like dust, caught in invisible currents that swept them away before they could settle.
The Council sat waiting.
Each member rested at a tall podium, perhaps ten feet high, forming a loose circle around the central floor where Pav and Calsco now stood. Their gazes were unreadable from below. No formal greeting came. Just silence and height and presence.
Pav stepped forward. As he did, the gravity changed. It wasn’t sudden, but subtle, like wading into warm water. His body felt lighter, looser, and a few of his hairs began to float until every follicle was floating in calm waves upon his head. It gave him an ethereal look, almost smoky. Almost Maspin.
He didn't know if that was intentional. But he knew someone had thought about it.
The ambience of the music faded into silence as the great doors sealed shut behind them with a resonant click. The chamber felt suddenly vast. Quiet.
The council stood in a semicircle beneath the roots of the chamber ceiling, their presence impossibly still. Each wore a robe of a different hue—rich crimsons, deep sapphires, pale silvers, a yellow so vibrant it almost pulsed. Primitive symbols were stitched across their chests, but it was the marks that drew the eye: each bore a glowing necklace, the gem softly pulsing with color.
Their hoods were drawn back, revealing not faces, but clouds. Swirling orbs of smoke hovered where heads should be—shifting, murmuring, occasionally flashing with subtle color like heat lightning within a storm. There were no mouths, yet the room felt watched. Listened to.
In front of Pav stood a low ceremonial table. A bowl of violet metal sat at its center, filled with bundled kindling. Beside it, a lit torch crackled quietly. And resting next to the flame—almost humming with quiet tension—was a necklace strung with a transparent gem that caught the firelight like trapped rain.
“Go ahead,” Calsco whispered, voice low and reverent, nudging Pav forward.
Pav stepped closer. He hesitated—but only for a moment—then took the necklace and placed it around his neck. The gem settled against his chest, strangely cold.
He reached for the torch and lowered it to the bowl. The kindling burst into flame instantly—not violently, but as if it had been waiting for him. The fire licked upward, then simmered into a steady, dancing glow.
All around the room, other flames answered. Small potted fires lining the edges of the chamber ignited one after another, each one casting a distinct hue that matched a council member’s robe. A quiet ripple of light moved through the room like a breath exhaled from the stone itself.
The air was filled with scent—not smoke, but something sweet and alive, like flowering fruit or sun-warmed leaves. It was impossible to place. The room seemed to bend slightly, not in shape but in feeling. Like something had turned its gaze toward Pav. 
It caught Pav’s eye that not all the council members were Maspin.
Among the robed figures stood a lone human—his presence immediately striking. He wore a sharp, outer-rim style suit, gray with precise orange trim that glinted faintly in the firelight. The colors marked him unmistakably as a representative of the Trailblazers. While his outfit was subdued compared to the ceremonial grandeur around him, he carried himself with easy authority, as if completely at home among these alien mystics. His gaze settled on Pav with quiet amusement, as if he could see through the polished front.
He was the only one who smiled, and it wasn't warm.
Pav kept his expression still, but his fingers instinctively found one of his rings, turning it slowly as he adjusted his posture. A bead of sweat cooled quickly at the back of his neck.
Then the man spoke—his voice smooth, formal, amplified not by volume but by presence.
“The Council of Riflo welcomes Prince Dometre Polaris, negotiator for the House of Kafkee. We are honored to be in your presence. May these negotiations benefit both of our peoples... Flamebearer of House Kafkee.”
As he finished, all of the council members echoed in perfect synchrony—a layered chorus from mouths that did not exist: “May your shadow burn long.”
The words resonated unnaturally long in the chamber. A faint vibration passed in the air. Pav nodded, lowering his head with careful grace.
“The pleasure is all mine, Councilman,” he said, his voice calm—almost convincing himself.
“You have arrived here on a sacred night, Prince,” said one of the Maspin, its smoke-face undisturbed as the voice emerged from somewhere deeper, older. “Tonight marks the close of the Season of Long Nights. A time of rebirth. Of retribution. Of rekindling the fire we all share.”
The words seemed to echo in the chamber despite the speaker’s stillness. As the last syllable left the air, a low hum began beneath Pav’s feet — not mechanical, but alive. The stone floor vibrated gently, like it was resonating with the mood of the room itself. The Council was pleased. That much was clear.
“May these negotiations bring renewal to both our peoples,” the Maspin continued. “As is our custom, you shall not use our names. You may address us only as the Council.”
The hum settled, slowly quieting, until there was only the warmth of the colored flames and the sound of Pav’s own breathing. The silence wasn’t empty. It watched.
"The Council has done a stupendous job making me feel welcome," Pav said with a composed nod. "I am both impressed and honored by the care you've shown in preparing this chamber. Even the smallest acts of grace reveal a people's spirit."
His gaze drifted across the room, taking in the bioluminescent vines, the woven banners, the deliberate placement of light and shadow.
"Too often," he continued, "I find myself in rooms that gleam too brightly—polished, postured, insincere. But here... there is honesty. Hope, even. I would like to help. Let us speak of terms."
As his words settled into the chamber, one of the Council—the one in deep green robes—began scribbling furiously with a feathered quill, the scratching loud and deliberate, as if to mark each word. Another, cloaked in blue, calmly stroked the thick fur of a sleeping Floom nestled on the desk before him, which gave a soft chirrup with every gentle motion.
From the far left, a voice rasped forth like gravel beneath water. The speaker was pale-smoked, with sunken eyes that shimmered like shallow pools.
"I have seen your face before," the old Maspin said slowly. "Not in life. In vision. You were kneeling. A servant."
A pause. Then, from the other side, the Councilor in red leaned forward. His mask gleamed faintly in the chamber’s colored glow.
"Live long enough," he said, "and you will see the same face a thousand times. His father stood where he now stands. Polaris... yes." The name hung in the air. "Your father’s eyes did not flicker when he lied. Do yours?"
He reached into his robe and held up a pendant carved from dull red stone. As he closed his hand around it, it began to glow with a soft heatless light. A second later, Pav’s own necklace that hung to the collar of his ceremonial tunic—responded. It pulsed faintly, a warm orange, like fire waiting to catch.
A contemplative hum vibrated through the floor, subtle but present. One councilor tilted their head and said, almost to themselves, "It is a rare sign... for the family."
Another answered, softer still. "Sacred... he may be."
The human-looking councilman rose slowly, his gavel tapping not with force, but with authority. His voice was measured, yet edged with a long-held bitterness.
“Your Majesty… as honored as I am by your arrival, I must confess—resentment clings to my heart like ash. Your clan promised us aid. Promised protection. Yet for years, our lands have trembled—volcanic eruptions, quakes, storms splitting the sky. We've pleaded. We've begged. To not even an automated reply. Only silence. Why were we abandoned? And what, I ask, has changed now?”
A heavy hum bloomed beneath the chamber, vibrating up through the roots and sinew of the floor. Anger. The council’s emotion, palpable and rising from the earth itself.
Pav kept still. He resisted the urge to shift, to wipe the sweat from his brow. Instead, he straightened his back and summoned his performance.
Act royal. Act wealthy, but also act poor. Command authority. Just stay in character, he reminded himself.
“I love my family. My cousins, my aunts, the children—every branch of the tree. We are raised to make time for one another, to uphold our bonds, no matter how far apart the stars pull us. But like soldiers have rules of engagement, so too do I have constraints. It was not neglect… not entirely. This sector fell under the dominion of my godmother, my aunt. She was keeper of this portion of the treasury. It was her hand that drew the veil. Her refusal. Her silence. I petitioned. I pleaded. And yet only her death… only that final turning of the cycle allowed my voice to reach you.”
He let the words settle.
“You spoke of rebirth tonight. That is no coincidence. In my house, old orders are crumbling. New minds are rising like dawn. Let this meeting be our first ember. A new fire, shared between us.”
	“I’m sorry for your loss, Your Majesty,” the councilman said, rising with solemnity. “We understand your journey here has come at no small cost—both the death of your aunt and the dangers of the crash. Yet your presence brings more than grief. It brings quite the optimsim. Even the young ones whisper of your arrival. It would be a shame to disappoint them. We would be honored if you would lead the prayer song in her memory during tonight’s feast.”
Pav offered a gracious nod, masking his unease. “I respect the invitation—and your openness. But I tend to refrain from singing. I would hate to disturb the peace of the evening with my tone.”
“You do not sing?” the councilman echoed, brows raising as though at an unfamiliar word.
“Only when I’m alone, I suppose.”
A faint murmur rolled through the chamber like a breeze grazing ancient stone.
“Perhaps,” the councilman said slowly, “our distance has been greater than we imagined. We were last told the Royals sang at every rite. Every loss. Every dawn. Strange…”
Pav’s pause lingered a moment too long. Just as he inhaled, Calsco stepped forward with diplomatic ease.
“Partially correct, Councilor. He has been granted an exemption—by decree. For the sake of others. His tone has been known to... shift the mood rather unpredictably. It was agreed upon by the Elder Harmonists themselves. A rare exception.”
A ripple of soft hums followed—disbelief, curiosity, faint amusement.
One of the older councilors, face obscured by layers of braided moss, leaned forward.
“Do all princes allow others to complete their truths? To speak for them?” he asked, the words drifting like smoke.
Pav tilted his head, his smile courtly, unreadable.
The green councilman brought his gavel down with a resonant crack.
“Thou seem to break tradition with ease. Such attitudes bring great shame to these halls.”
Calsco leaned in sharply, his voice low and cutting, just for Pav’s ear.
“One more lie I can’t patch, and you’ll be standing here alone, Your Grace. Get to it.”
Pav drew a slow breath, straightening with renewed poise.
“The only tradition I break is that of absence—for I am here, now. Shall we discuss terms?”
A different councilor shifted in his root-entwined seat, head tilting at a curious angle. His voice was soft but scathing.
“Your tongue wears the sand of other stars, boy. Not Polaris rain.”
A low, discordant hum pulsed beneath the floor. Pav could feel the skepticism.
The green councilman’s voice slithered with mock courtesy.
“We have allowed you privacy since your… injuries. But now, tell us—what titles do you bear? Which royals do you command? Surely your family didn’t send you here to fetch crumbs and do low work.”
Pav met his gaze, steady and unblinking.
“I have earned more titles than you can imagine. My family gave me only a chance—nothing more. Every title I carry is a mark of what I’ve endured, not what I’ve been given. I oversee Cillus vot Shayron-Skoln.”
A deep-toned gavel slammed. It was the Trailblazer councilman.
“Some job you’ve done. How can I be optimistic when he was shot—then went AWOL? Your family’s been searching for him for days.”
Pav didn't flinch.
“I oversee him because of this. Because of all his behavior. He needs structure. He needs command. He needs me.”
A hush settled. He took one breath before continuing:
“I now formally request we no longer speak of family gossip. To speak ill of another House under the banners of Shayron should be beneath this chamber.”
The hum below wavered into silence.
The elder councilor let out a low, rasping chuckle “Your uncle used to lie with the same fire in his eyes. He always danced before he broke.”
Pav stepped forward, voice low but unyielding.
 “You don’t have to kneel. Just stop pretending you have options. You need me.”
The elder leaned forward, eyes like embers beneath heavy lids.
 “Need you? We found you half-dead—starving, broken, bleeding out in the jungle. You may wear a crown, but you bleed like anyone else.”
Pav’s reply came sharp and measured, carried by the hum of the chamber, which shifted subtly—as though bracing.
 “This chamber is sacred. That doesnt't mean its safe. Choose your words as if they’ll echo forever.”
 He let the silence stretch, just long enough.
 “I’m not your guest. I’m your future. Refuse me, if you must. But make peace with what follows. You summoned me here. Ask your people if they can afford your pride.”
The hum deepened—not in sound, but in weight. It was no longer ambiance; it was understanding being made.
The central councilman rose with deliberate weight, his voice ringing with authority and exhaustion.
"Enough."
His eyes swept the chamber.
"This council forgets itself. You disgrace your seats with gossip and provocation. We will speak only of terms. Nothing more. Now, will you honor our specific requests Polaris?"
Pav held his ground, tone cool and resolute.
“I am well-versed in the laws of my nation. Therefore, before binding myself to any contract, you must state your terms in full—for the record.”
The center councilman did not blink. His gaze locked with Pav’s for a breath too long, as if weighing the soul behind the performance. Then, he spoke:
“We request an immediate payment of ten million royal credits—for rebuilding after your House’s long neglect. We request Royal soldiers, to protect our shipping lanes from the piracy your borders failed to contain.” You will help restore what your name once promised, and then abandoned.”
He paused, voice dropping like stone in water. “And finally—we request a Gift of Memory.”
The chamber fell still, the hum deepening like a held breath.
“If you cannot carry this burden,” the councilman said, his tone edged with finality,
“then take your torch and walk back into the jungle.”
“A Gift of Memory?” Pav repeated, skeptical.
“Indeed,” the councilman replied. “You must share a memory with us. It is more than symbolism. It is how our people feel what you feel. Understanding is earned.”
“What kind of memory are you asking for? I can tell you anything.”
“No.” The councilman’s tone hardened, almost gentle in its certainty. “Do not tell. Share a tear.”
For the first time, Pav felt the mask slip.
“And… how exactly do you want me to do that?”
“At the festival tonight,” the councilman said, “we will send one of ours to collect it. Fill the tear with whatever moves you—love or pain, joy or grief. It must be true.”
Pav blinked, trying to translate the concept across cultures.
“I can do that. I’ll honor the tradition.” He took a breath, shifting back into diplomacy.
“How many soldiers are you requesting?”
“One unit.”
Pav straightened. He had expected posturing, demands he could chip away at. This was surprisingly… modest.
“One unit?” he said. “Done.”
He nodded, preparing to spar again.
“And the ten million—how was that number calculated?”
Three council members struck their gavels, but the center one struck his the longest—the sound deep, shaking the earth beneath them. “How did we calculate?” he snapped, his eyes wet and reflecting invisible flames. “How did we calculate? That’s what we’ve paid in taxes—just in one solar rotation. That number is more than fair. If we were greeds like you, we’d have added the cost of the cemeteries, for the bodies we’ve buried. Or for the homes swallowed by volcanic ash, for the fields burned to nothing, for the monasteries drowned in floodwaters. How much do you think that costs? How much is our suffering worth to you, prince? How much do you value our lives? You sit there clean and soft and wonder how we reached a number while we wonder how we’re still alive. Why do you assume we work with the Trailblazers? Because you don’t care! They have died for us—bled in the mud beside us—while your family stays fat and distant, safe on a throne of excuses. Our people are dying, and dying fast. They are dying because you—”
The doors slammed open with a jarring clang, snapping the room out of its rising fury. The council members turned in unison, as if yanked by the same invisible string. In the doorway stood Jimzon—red-faced, drenched in sweat, his ceremonial suit clinking faintly with every breathless movement. He looked like he had sprinted across the continent, and maybe he had. Still catching his breath, he stumbled forward, the metallic charms and medals on his uniform jingling like wind chimes in a storm.
“I’m… sorry,” he managed between gasps, raising a single apologetic finger as if that might soften the intrusion. “Sorry I’m late, Your Majesty.” His eyes did a quick pan across the room, finally landing on the central councilor whose fists were still clenched on the table. “Sorry I’m late,” Jimzon repeated, slower this time, as though it would help. “Just continue. Pretend I’m not here.”
He shuffled beside Pav and bent over, hands on his knees, wheezing like a man who had just outrun death itself. The chamber, which only moments ago had been rattling with the thunder of rage and loss, was now suspended in silence—an almost comical quiet, interrupted only by the high, whistling inhale of Jimzon trying to keep from throwing up.
Then came a dry, sputtering cough. Loud. Unapologetic. It echoed up the curved walls and came back down twice as awkward.
“…Sorry,” he muttered again, barely audible.
No one knew whether to speak or stare. A long beat passed. Somewhere in the rafters, a moth flitted lazily through a shaft of light, entirely unconcerned.
The central councilman broke the silence with a voice much calmer than before, as if the storm had passed and now only business remained. “No matter. Do you accept our terms?”
Pav turned slightly, giving Jimzon the barest glance—half surprised, half resigned. “I accept your terms,” he said smoothly. “And I would like to formally apologize for my squire here. He's spirited, if nothing else.” He gave the faintest smile before continuing. “Given that my ship has unfortunately crashed, I will require transport home in order to report our agreement. Perhaps a modest allotment of credits as well, for the journey and repairs.”
The councilman nodded once, as if that final detail was expected. “These terms are acceptable. Can we expect payment within ninety nights of your departure?”
Jimzon, still catching his breath and wiping a streak of sweat from his brow, looked over at Calsco. The older man gave him a subtle nod—just enough to say it was safe to proceed. Pav stepped forward without missing a beat, his voice calm and sure. “We have a deal.”
“Council of Riflo,” the center councilman announced, his voice steady and ceremonial. “We may begin our voting now. At the end of each negotiation section, the council hums softly together. The pitch of the echo determines consensus. If the hum ends in dissonance, the proposal is rejected.”
A silence fell over the chamber as the council closed their eyes and began to hum—a low, unified sound that pulsed through the root-sinew walls and bioluminescent air. Slowly, their voices aligned in perfect resonance. One by one, the glowing gems embedded in their chests began to shimmer—first in their individual hues, then merging gradually into a single, radiant white. The decision was unanimous.
The center councilman opened his eyes and nodded. “Prince Polaris, you are an honorary Maspin. As a Maspin, you are welcome here anytime—as family. May we enjoy you at the feast, Brother Polaris.”
The entire council raised their gavels and brought them down in unison with a deep, ceremonial thud.
“Meeting adjourned,” the center voice declared.
While the other council members stood, an elder remained seated, his gaze fixed on Pav. Slowly, he raised a hand to the glowing stone at the center of his necklace. A faint shimmer passed through the chamber as Pav’s own pendant responded, pulsing with the same light.      
Then, without warning, a voice echoed—not aloud, but inside Pav’s mind. “You wear another’s face. Your voice is still your own. Remember your vow.” The elder let go of the gem, the glow fading. 
Pav stiffened. No one had ever been inside his head before. 

Chapter 26
The three of them stepped into the bungalow, and the moment the door clicked shut behind them, they all let out a collective sigh—part exhaustion, part disbelief, part giddy relief. For a beat, no one spoke. Then their shoulders loosened. The tension evaporated. They were safe.
“I can’t believe I fucking did it,” Pav said, grinning like a man who’d just cheated death—and gotten paid for it. “What a rush. I mean—I told command to go fuck themselves. And now we’re getting a ship? Calsco, my man, we might actually have to run this con again.”
Calsco burst out laughing, rubbing his temples. “You’ve impressed me, kid. Consider your debt wiped.”
“No way.”
“I mean it. With a few adjustments, we could run this Prince act long-term. For a first timer? You crushed it. Even when they were losing their damn minds, nobody suspected a farse.”
Jimzon smiled, but there was a hint of something under it—maybe envy, maybe just the sting of being a footnote. “If there’s a next time,” he said, “I’m not playing the damn squire.”
Calsco turned to him, eyebrows raised. “Boy, you saved us just as much as he did. They wanted his head at one point. You? You were born to be a jester.”
Jimzon frowned. “How exactly did I save you?”
“You were my backup plan,” Calsco said, completely serious. “Told you to wait by the tree because I knew you’d be late. If talks were going well, your entrance would get a laugh. If they were going bad, you’d break the tension. And we got lucky.”
Jimzon crossed his arms, unimpressed. “We shouldn’t have to rely on luck.”
Pav’s smile faded as a thought caught up with him. “Were you able to see Berji? Is he…” His voice drifted off.
Jimzon nodded, the levity gone. “He’s alive. But if we want to wake him up, I’ve gotta find a shaman. Tonight.”
Pav exhaled. “And I’ve got to give a Gift of Memory.”
Jimzon leaned back against the wall, arms folded. “Guess the job’s not done yet.”
Pav looked between them. “You mean like… for tonight?”
Jimzon shook his head, eyes distant. “I mean everything.”
Calsco was rifling through the mailslot by the wooden door, flipping open envelopes with casual efficiency. “Jizzbo,” he called, squinting at one slip, “you told the hospital Polaris here would cover a hundred thousand credits?”
He let out a bark of laughter, crumpled the paper, and tossed it behind him without looking. It landed clean in the trash.
“Yeah, well—Calsco,” Jimzon added, his tone shifting, “mind if we have a minute? I wanna unpack what happened at the council.”
Calsco checked his holowatch, already moving toward the door. “I’m late already. You two talk it out. I see you both later”
With that, his broad frame ducked through the doorway and disappeared down the path.
Jimzon peeled off the jester shirt and let it crumple to the floor, standing now in just a black tank top. His expression shifted—no more jokes, no grin, just the sharp edge of reality settling in. “Okay,” he said quietly. “We need to talk.”
He held up his fingers, counting them off one by one.“Berji. Yake. Where the hell  to hide. How we’re gonna get credits, whether or not we stick with Calsco… And the skeleton key.
He patted his pocket, firm and deliberate. The key was still there. Still their biggest risk—and maybe their only shot.
“As for Berji,” Pav said, folding his arms, “We will wake him up and I know he’ll have something to say. Something useful.”
Jimzon nodded slowly. “Yeah… you’re right.”
He hesitated, then shifted gears. “Well, now we’ve gotta talk about Yake.”
The air went heavier.
“I know we’ve been avoiding it,” Jimzon continued, “but there’s still no sign of him.”
“There was a sign,” Pav said flatly. “He disappeared with the med kit, the only rifle on board, and the rations. He left us.”
Jimzon frowned. “You don’t think he got lost or something?”
“Lost?” Pav’s voice hardened. “It doesn’t matter. The three of us were bleeding out and starving. He took what we needed and vanished. Simple as that. As far as I’m concerned—he’s dead to me.”
Jimzon looked away, jaw clenched. Then, carefully, “Do you remember what you told me when I was unsure about Berji?”
“That we stick together,” Pav said. “Yeah. I meant it. Still do.”
He paused.
“But he left us, Jimzon. We didn’t leave him.”
Jimzon’s voice was low now, almost pleading. “I don’t wanna pull rank, I know you and Yake weren’t tight, but… we need each other more than ever. I’m still captain. It’s my job to hold this crew together.”
Pav turned, calm but ice-cold. “Captain? You’re not captain anymore. That title burned with the ship. You don’t really think you’re still in charge… do you? That part of our lives is over”
“I know I’m not technically in charge anymore,” Jimzon said, his voice low but tense. “But it meant something. You guys nominated me.”
Pav didn’t flinch. “I’m not blaming you for going AWOL. But you’re not thinking straight. You threatened to kill Berji. You smashed up this place. And our last op? That wasn’t clean, either. I watched you hold a man’s face down in lava. Burned him alive.”
Jimzon’s expression went cold. “That was different.”
“Maybe it was,” Pav said, staring straight through him. “But you’re off the handle. Even Calsco picked up on it—and he barely knows you. That’s why he chose me to play the prince.”
Jimzon stiffened. “He told you that?”
“He didn’t have to. What other reason is there?”
There was a pause. Pav exhaled, tone shifting, quieter now—but no softer.
“Look. I learned a lot today. Stuff I almost didn’t want to tell you. Because I know you’ll flip out.”
Jimzon’s eyes narrowed. “You’re keeping secrets from me?”
“Yeah, fine, look…” Pav took a deep breath, tempted to stall. “Kelsa’s AWOL too. Along with two others. Strangers.”
Jimzon didn’t respond right away. He just stared at Pav’s oddly shaved jaw, like trying to find a crack in his armor.
“Let me guess. Calsco told you that?” His voice was flat, guarded. “The guy manipulates people for a living.”
“He did,” Pav said evenly. “And the bounties he read were quite clear. It’s her. How else would he even know who she was?”
Jimzon clenched his jaw, eyes squeezing shut. “Fuck.” He shook his head, voice brittle. “It’s fine. Whatever. I don’t even care," he lied.
The lie hung in the air like smoke. His fists balled at his sides, knuckles going white.
“This is so fucking stupid. What else do we even have to talk about?”
Pav didn’t bite. “Where we go next. I’ve got a few ideas. Calsco probably does too.”
Jimzon looked up sharply. “We’re really bringing him?”
“Yes,” Pav said without hesitation. “For now, we need him.”
Jimzon’s eyes narrowed. “And what happens when he turns on us?”
Pav met his gaze, steady. “He’s had plenty of chances already. He didn’t take them. That has to account for something”
“That’s not a guarantee.” Jimzon spat.
“No,” Pav said, “but it’s something. He’s been teaching me politics, Jimzon. How to listen. How to speak. And how not to get played.”
Jimzon hesitated. His jaw worked for a moment before he finally said, “Fine.” He took a slow breath, steadying himself. “If Berji wakes up, we take the ship. We drop Calsco wherever he wants to go. And if he stays and it's the four of us… we vote. Or, god forbid, we take him out if he tries to drag us into some kind of bullshit.”
Pav gave a small nod, but didn’t look away.
“One more thing,” he said. “I’m bringing a native.”
Jimzon blinked. “What?”
“He’s a slave here, technically. But he knows chemistry, black market channels… he’s resourceful. He’s Calsco’s associate, yeah but he’s the one who threaded our costumes. The detail work was his.”
Jimzon’s stare sharpened. “What else are you not telling me?”
Pav paused. The beryllium rifles. The quiet drug smuggling. He weighed it, then shook his head.
“Nothing. You can meet him. Decide for yourself.”
Jimzon let that sit for a beat. “Where to, then? Specifically.”
“We could head to Tongishaw for a while,” Pav said. “Pick up work, stash enough credits, then disappear to Shayron. Somewhere outside the Guard’s reach.”
Jimzon scoffed. “Dude. They’re allies. Shayron would extradite us in a heartbeat.”
“Not if we keep our heads down. Same as anywhere. Tongishaw’s Royal territory—no active bounties, no Guard patrols. Hell, we might even be able to claim asylum.”
“Asylum?” Jimzon’s laugh was hollow. “That bullshit might’ve worked in the council chamber, but out here? We’ve got no leverage. Those council members weren’t convinced—they just wanted credits. It was just for show”
Pav’s voice dropped. “No leverage? We’ve got the skeleton key.”
Jimzon fell quiet. His fingers tapped against his leg, tense. “We don’t even know what’s on it. If that intel ever went public, rebellions or even full scale war could break out. Systems could fall. We don't need more bloodshed."
“Then let’s just take a look,” Pav said, grabbing the touchpad from the kitchen counter. “Just search the word Royal. Let’s see what pops up.”
Jimzon stared at the device like it might bite him. He took a long breath, then picked it up and slotted the drive into the port. The tablet lit up with a soft blue glow.
“Okay…” he muttered, fingers moving across the screen.
It flashed white. Then, one by one, filenames began to load—slow at first, then cascading down the display. Dozens. Hundreds.
Jimzon scrolled, faster now, eyes narrowing. One folder snagged his attention: ROYAL_REGICIDE_DAY_00.
He turned the screen to Pav. “Regicide means… killing a king, right?”
Pav nodded, his voice low. “Yeah. Click it.”
Jimzon tapped the folder.
It began to load.

Queen Traceen has been absent for 4 years. No credible sightings. Presumed dead or compromised. Public narrative remains ambiguous.

Orchestrating public opinion shifts via controlled media agents.

 Colonel Vesk Iradin – Strategic Covert Ops Division

“Gods…” Pav whispered. “Does that say false flag?”
Jimzon nodded, barely breathing. “Yeah. This alone could start a war.”
“No,” Pav said. His voice was distant now, hollow with realization. “This is war. It’s just been waiting for the right moment.”
Jimzon scrolled, eyes locked on the screen. “They’ve been building this for years…”
Pav leaned closer, tension tightening in his frame. “Does it say when it starts? When it goes active?”
Jimzon didn’t answer right away. He found the newest entry—one near the bottom, tagged in stark lettering:
ROYAL_REGICIDE_DAY_47
He tapped it.
The screen flickered.
