The false flag operation is scheduled to commence on Cycle 4427.98.1 at 0600 hours, coinciding with the 98th Anniversary of Queen Traceen’s Ascension — a symbolic national holiday marked by mass public participation, royal ceremonies, and peak cultural reverence across the Shayron territories.
This date was strategically selected to maximize operational effectiveness through the following conditions:
High Civilian Sentiment: The anniversary is a foundational cultural event, drawing dense crowds across all major districts. A disruption on this date ensures maximum psychological trauma, public confusion, and political leverage.


Monarchical Symbolism: Orchestrating the collapse of state authority on the very day honoring the Queen's legendary rule reframes her absence as final and divine approval for regime change. Operational Cover: The increased presence of military honor guards, ceremonial units, and state media provides ideal conditions for covert infiltration, pre-positioning of assets, and initiation of staged attacks with minimal immediate suspicion.

Civilian-dense transit stations


The eastern wing of the High Castle, including ceremonial halls, archive vaults, and Queen Traceen’s ancestral chamber

“Twenty one thousand…” Pav’s voice trailed off.
“We have to stop this,” Jimzon said, voice tight.
Pav gave a single, wordless nod. Solid. Certain.
Jimzon scanned the screen. “We’ve got about a month. Maybe less.”

Jimzon’s jaw tensed. “You think the Board of Directors knows we know?”
“I have no idea,” Pav said. “Even if we showed the Royal senate, they could say we faked it. That we want war to cover our asses for going AWOL. So, prison. Or a clean execution. Best case.”
“And if they believe us…” Jimzon’s voice dropped. “Then it’s another intergalactic war. Millions of soldiers, dead for absolutely nothing.”
Pav stared at the screen, jaw locked. “We can’t sit on this. If we do, thousands of civilians die. Painfully”
Jimzon’s voice was firm now. “Then this is our top priority.”
Pav exhaled, eyes narrowing. “Looks like we’re going to Shayron. If things go right, we leave tomorrow or day after.”
“You think Calsco and your alien friend’ll help?”
“Calsco would. Daiser… he’s not a fan of authority. I think he’d be in.”
Jimzon gave a tired smile. “An anti-authoritarian slave protecting a monarchy. Nothing’s simple anymore is it?”
Pav gave a small shrug. “Guess not.”
Jimzon glanced toward the door. “Can we get arms? Just in case?”
“I know a guy,” Pav said, a smirk twitching at the corner of his mouth.
Jimzon huffed a dry laugh. “World’s falling apart, but we’ve got each other.” He looked over. “Sorry about my temper.”
Pav nodded. “Don’t worry. This comes first. I’m sure we’ll each be happy somewhere one day.”
Jimzon glanced down, remembering the old man’s words. “Happiness isn’t a place,” he said, tapping his temple. “It’s here. And right now, I’m just glad I’ve got you on this op.”
Pav looked at him, trying to wrap his head around the phrase. “So you’re happy?”
“No,” Jimzon said, cracking a tired grin. “But I’m happy you’ve got my back. That’s all I need. I know we can get this done.”
From outside, bells chimed—soft, melodic—signaling the start of the festival.
Pav looked toward the door, then back at him.
“Thank you, Captain.”

Chapter 27
Jimzon tugged at the collar of his black suit, cut in the angular, sharp-shouldered style of the outer rim. The fabric clung stiffly to his frame, unfamiliar, too formal. Beneath it, a deep blue shirt caught faint light from the bungalow's woven lanterns, giving his collar a soft sheen.
He rolled his shoulders, testing the fit. "Still not used to this thing," he muttered.
Pav stood beside him at the base of the stairs leading up to their tree bungalow, adjusting the imitation royal epaulettes stitched to his jacket. He fussed with one briefly, brushing lint from the gold threading.
Jimzon checked the path ahead, then glanced at a vine-wrapped clock nailed into a nearby trunk. "Alright. We rendezvous here in two hours."
Pav nodded, eyes distant.
Jimzon turned to him. "You know what memory you're picking?"
"I'll figure it out," Pav said with a half-shrug. "I'll make up a good one."
Jimzon raised his eyebrows. "Figure it out now," he said. Not harsh, but firm. "They said it can be joy or pain, or whatever.  As long as it's real. Don't fake it."
Pav hesitated. "But what if our cover gets blown?"
"They're not gonna interrogate you, man. They don't ask for context. Just… feel something true. That's all."
A breath. Pav nodded slowly. "Alright. You're right."
"You got one in mind?"
"I think so." Pav looked off to the side, jaw tight, eyes softening like he'd just brushed against a bruise.
Jimzon didn't press. "I know whatever it is, it's intense," he said, voice gentler now. "You don't have to tell me. But if you want to talk tonight… I'm here. I've vented your ear off the past few weeks. Least I can do is return the favor."
"Thanks, man." Pav's voice was quiet. His eyes dropped to the ground, unfocused.
	"You really think the shaman will wake up Berji? He's been out for… a while. Doesn't even feel like he's still in there."
Jimzon didn't hesitate.
"It'll work because it has to."
For a moment, neither spoke. The jungle buzzed around them with distant birdsong, the soft rustle of wind brushing through canopy leaves.
Then a burst of laughter cracked through the quiet.
A group of children darted down the dirt road ahead, barefoot and shouting, weaving between tree roots as they ran toward the square. The glow of the feast fires flickered just beyond the hill, their smoke rising like ribbons into the night.
Pav watched them go.
 "Guess it's time then."
Jimzon gave a small nod.
"I'll see you soon," Pav said, straightening his coat and stepping away.
The feast stretched across several winding streets, packed end to end with long communal tables built to seat hundreds. Lanterns swayed gently from woven ropes overhead, mingling with soft string lights and torchfire that flickered against the deepening twilight. The air pulsed with warmth, laughter, clinking goblets, the distant pop of firewood.
Roasted Kraskor meat, glistening and spiced, sat beside skewers of jewel-toned fruits and vegetables. The scent alone was enough to draw people closer—smoky, sweet, and earthy all at once.
As Jimzon weaved through the crowd, his eyes landed on a small Maspin band nestled in the crook of an old tree plaza. They played soft ocarinas, a harp that looked grown from root rather than crafted, and a steel drum carved from violet amethyst. The music was cheerful, but ambient—full of gentle joy, not celebration. It drifted like a lullaby across the flickering firelight.
The Maspin musicians kept their hoods drawn but moved with the rhythm—subtle sways of the hips, small nods of the head. Even cloaked, there was soul in the way they played.
Jimzon looked closer.
None of the instruments were bought. None even looked constructed. The harp's spine had been grown—its frame a natural curve of living wood. Its strings were thin blue vines, unevenly spaced, yet tuned so perfectly they rang like glass touched by wind.
How the hell did they invent this before electricity? he wondered.
He finally settled near a quieter end of one table, scooping a plate of food before glancing to his right. A small group of Maspins sat together, their robes brushed with streaks of soft pigment, possibly ceremonial.
"Hey," he asked, keeping his tone casual, "do you guys know where any shamans would be?"
"Whoa, you're the squire, right?" a child's voice squeaked. Jimzon turned. A cluster of small Maspin had gathered near him. Hooded like the others, their ages impossible to guess by size or face.
"Yes, but do you—"
"What's Shayron like? Do they have flying carriages?" another chirped, practically bouncing.
"I guess, yeah, but where—"
"Do you know any army guys?" another voice chimed in, overlapping.
"Yeah, please, can—"
"My mommy says the new king is handsome!"
Jimzon stood. "Guys," he said, lowering his voice just enough to cut through the chaos.
The children fell still, as if a gentle current passed through them. One of them took a bite of Kraskor meat with a fork, but as the morsel reached its mouth, it vanished—evaporated into the faint shimmer of its form.
How do they even use forks if they're made of smoke? Jimzon wondered.
"I need to find a shaman," he said firmly, making eye contact with the nearest pair of glowing eyes. "I'll answer every question you have… after you help me find one."
A pause.
Then, a giggle—soft and strange, like wind through a hollow tree.
"You don't find the shaman," one child said with an amused tilt of the head. "The shaman finds you."

—----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Pav wandered through the crowd, hands clasped loosely behind his back, trying to look princely without feeling it. Maspins turned as he passed, their clouded faces tilting toward him. A few nudged each other, whispering. Some offered small waves. Children darted past him and he returned a few high-fives with a practiced smile, but the attention wore thin fast.
He wished he could keep his head down. He didn't deserve this. Not really.
Spotting a group of humans seated around a long table near the edge of the square, he angled toward them. Trailblazers. Their uniforms were unmistakable—gray jumpsuits marked with bright orange trim, dusty from travel or labor. As he approached, a few stood and bowed their heads. Others didn't bother to rise. One didn't even look up from his plate.
"Mister Prince, your… magicky-majesty—uh, thanks for coming," one of them said awkwardly, scratching his temple.
Pav held up a hand. "No problem. No need for the bowing guys,seriously. Mind if I join you?"
"Of course, of course," another man said quickly, scooting cups and plates aside to make room.
Pav sat, offering a polite nod as he glanced around the table. The men ranged from early twenties to maybe forty, all of them worn thin by work. Faces muddy, hands calloused, an assortment of scruffy facial hair. 
"So," Pav said, trying to spark something, "you guys… blaze trails?"
A few of them chuckled, but it was thin and hollow, more polite than genuine. The rest kept eating in silence, eyes down, utensils scraping quietly against ceramic.
Pav leaned back slightly, smile dimming just a touch.
"Yeah. Hard work, but great food."
"Dumb question, but like, what's the day-to-day?"
"No dumb questions, Prince. I don't know if it interests you, but it depends." He wiped his mouth, then offered a hand. "I'm Barris, by the way."
He leaned back in his seat, fork in hand. "I'm in logistics. Soil readings, elevation scans, rainfall patterns. Mostly making sure we're not building on swamp or stone."
He lifted his fork, still skewered with a chunk of grilled meat, and gestured across the table. "Vando here's security. Does the Kraskor sweeps. This one," he said, giving the meat a little wiggle, "is courtesy of him."
Vando gave a small grunt of acknowledgment but didn't look up.
"And Michael's our engineering guy," Barris added. "Keeps the generators from killing us."
Michael nodded once, chewing.
Pav smiled. "That's actually really cool. Michael, how'd you get into engineering here?"
"Studied at a Guard university on Saigo," Michael said. "Started in drone tech. My job was airflow optimization—like, keeping the vents clean on aerials. Pay was fine. Work sucked. Quit and came here. Less credits, better people."
"That's sick. I used to… read… about Guard semi-drones," Pav said, leaning forward slightly, the firelight catching on the brass trim of his coat. "Did you work on the Shinsplint or the Torkal?"
"Torkal," Michael replied, wiping grease from his thumb with a napkin. "But those days are behind me."
"How'd you guys fix the battery's ionic calibrators?"
Michael blinked, a little surprised that a so-called prince would know the term. "Good question. We didn't. Switched to seismic-magnetic batteries. Expensive, yeah—but they're solid. Real long-term stuff."
Pav nodded slowly. The clatter of cups and distant laughter layered faintly behind them. "Makes sense. But how'd you make the space?"
"Real computing tech's creeping back into Guard hardware," Michael said, lowering his voice like it was half rumor, half admission. "EMP-proof, hopefully. No pilots anymore, either. All unmanned now."
"Whoa." Pav leaned back, eyes lifting as if watching a drone arc across some distant sky. "I always thought those pilots had it rough, but damn already? I figured unmanned deep-space tech was still a few generations out."
"We did too," Michael said, stabbing a chunk of fruit with his fork. "But maybe we just got lucky. They found a whole settlement untouched by The Fry. Reworked the system into a new AI."
"Real AIs are coming back?" Pav asked, voice dipping slightly.
Michael gave a half-smile, more tired than amused. "Yeah. I mean, we all saw it coming, right? I was kinda enjoying the quiet without them."
"Ah man. Me too," Pav said with a dry chuckle, shaking his head. "Should've guessed we'd never learn."
"Yup." Michael's reply came with the weight of someone who'd already made peace with the disappointment. "Not gonna be part of that fiasco. Thought about it for a few days, back when they first reached out. But I'm a builder, not a destroyer. I don't even know what I was doing in the Guard to begin with."
Pav nodded, the flicker of torchlight dancing along the rim of his plate. Smoke from the nearby fire pits curled gently into the air, warm and spiced with meat and ash. He picked up a roasted vegetable, turning it over between his fingers as he spoke.
"I feel you, man."

—----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------


Jimzon sat alone on a moss-covered stump, half-sunk into the soft jungle floor. Around him, Maspins and humans shared laughter, their voices rising and falling in off-key harmonies—half-forgotten melodies from faraway worlds. Someone sang too loud. Someone else clapped off-beat. It should've made him smile, but it didn't.
He stared at the wooden cup in his hands. The drink shimmered faintly in the starlight—amber, pungent, untouched. After a pause, he tipped it to the earth, watching the liquid vanish into the soil with a soft hiss. 
He glanced his head upwards. The night sky seems to bleed, with its tendrils of light leaving glimmering scars across the starry night. Then he leaned back, elbows resting on his knees, and covered his eyes with the heels of his palms. His breath escaped in a tired exhale. 
This was all too much.
A soft voice interrupted the hush. "Are you lost?"
Jimzon opened his eyes slowly. In front of him stood a Maspin, his smoke-like body glowing faintly, soft and pale like fog before dawn. White tendrils coiled gently off his shoulders.
"Kind of," Jimzon answered.
"I can tell, Jimzon."
He blinked. The name hit like a slap. He stood up fast, eyes narrowing. The Maspin leaned on a twisted staff of gnarled wood, unfazed by the sudden tension.
"You've got me mistaken for someone else," Jimzon said, his voice sharpened, defensive.
"How strange," the Maspin said calmly, tilting his head. "I was told to meet a Jimzon Avaris here, at this exact time. But I suppose I was misinformed." He turned, beginning to walk away with a slow, rhythmic pace.
"Wait," Jimzon called after him. "You're the shaman, right?"
"Shaman, oracle, witch doctor… You can call me whichever title feels most comfortable. I don't care much for names."
"You said you were looking for Jimzon."
The Maspin didn't stop walking. "Yes, but sadly, I'm needed elsewhere now."
Jimzon hesitated. The words came out before he could stop them. "I'm… him."
The shaman paused. Smoke curled upward from his back like incense. He didn't turn around right away.
"Then walk with me," the Maspin said, his voice like warm smoke curling in the night air.
	 They took a few steps beneath the whispering trees before the shaman glanced sideways.
	 "I must inquisite—if you knew that I knew who you were, why the lie?"
	 Jimzon kept his eyes on the ground, ashamed. "I'm just... I'm supposed to be laying low. Please, I don't care if you turn me in. I just—" His voice cracked. "I just need help saving my friend."
	The shaman nodded slowly, the faint glow of bioluminescent moss catching in his cloak. "How noble," he said gently. "Have a seat, Jim. Galactic laws twist with the wind, but the morals I live by do not. You are safe in my presence."
	Jimzon let out a breath he hadn't realized he was holding. "Thank you, Doctor. Can I call you Doctor?"
	The Maspin smiled, soft and ancient. "How about… Friend?"
	Jimzon blinked, then nodded. "My friend, can you wake up my comrade? He's been in a coma for some time now. The Guard doctor said he might never—"
	"I am aware of the situation, Jim. I can save him."
	Jimzon's knees finally gave out. He sank onto the mossy rock beside him, his body hollowed by exhaustion. "Oh thank God," he whispered, barely able to believe the relief flooding through him.
"In order to do so, I just need to get to know you first for anything to take effect."
"So this really is magic?"
"Not quite. To an outsider, yes, it seems to be, but it is something true, and explainable. It just tends to raise more questions than answers." He said as he sat down in front of Jimzon, spreading a blanket in front of them.
"Tell me, Jimzon, when you look at the sky, what do you see?"
"Stars? Planets?"
"Amusing, but how does it make you feel? Stargazing?"
He looked back at the sky. "Small."
"Interesting. Yes, the infinite splendor of the cosmos can make us feel quite insignificant."
"I meant like… small like I'm a kid again. Like I'm small. Everything changes but the size of the sky never seems to."
"What did the sky mean to you as a child?"
"A lot. Adventure I guess. And I've seen plenty of planets, and they're not usually pretty. Neither are most people."
"What changed?"
"Everything. I thought there was a plan for my life, and suddenly it was ripped away. So I tried to carve my own path, wounded up with even less than I imagined." Jimzon said. Friend was listening but he remained silent. "What do you see?"
"I see the universe witnessing itself," the shaman said softly.
Jimzon blinked. "Hm?"
"There's much to see when you look up," the shaman continued, voice calm, unfurling like the warm smoke that makes up his body. "And just as much when you look down. But life—conscious life—is the only part of the universe capable of witnessing its own beauty. We are not separate from it, you and I. We are the cosmos, after eons of silence, finally able to look back on itself… and speak."
Jimzon followed his gaze skyward. The stars pulsed faintly between drifting clouds, veined in silver light. "Yeah," he murmured. "That is pretty cool I guess"
"You said the worlds you've known have been unpleasant," the shaman said, his tone never pushing, just guiding. "Why do you think that is?"
Jimzon exhaled through his nose, gaze drifting. "I'm a soldier. Guess that means I only ever see the worst. But man, theres a lot of it. The things I fought for weren't pretty underneath either.  It's like… the more I saw, the less I knew. Now I'm not sure I know anything at all."
The shaman nodded, listening.
"Well then," he said gently, "what did you fight for?"
Jimzon took a long moment before answering. "Freedom, maybe. Stability. Peace… I know now it sounds silly now but maybe I just fought to just to fight. Prove myself."
The shaman's expression didn't change, but something behind his clouded features seemed to settle.
"Those things still exist," he said. "You can still fight for them."
"I don't think I know how to anymore."
"There's not only one way to fight," the shaman replied. "I've walked many worlds. Seen horrors, yes… but beauty too. And every planet, no matter how broken, has its own way of showing life's goodness. Some hide it deep. Others wear it openly. But it's always there. If you're willing to look."
Jimzon let the silence stretch. A single firefly drifted near the edge of his vision, its faint pulse in rhythm with his breath. He didn't reply—at least not yet—but he didn't look away either.
"Like what—different music?" Jimzon asked.
"Yes," the shaman said, "but not just music. Everything. It's art. Architecture. Their holidays, their stories. The way they love one another. It might all look different, but underneath, it's the same impulse. The same ache to express, to belong. You can learn more about a world from its cities than any history book."
Jimzon tilted his head. "You mean like… their laws?"
"Sometimes," the shaman replied, smiling faintly. "But often, you can tell by what they choose to build. The tallest structures are what they worship most. For some, it's monuments to their kings. For others, cathedrals for their gods. And then, there are planets of glass and steel—endless skyscrapers, built for profit. Songs however, can only last so long, so these monuments are either the songs or the screams of their people"
Jimzon fell quiet, his mind ticking. "I guess the biggest places I've ever lived were the Academy and the Mothership. Both huge. All built for war." He hesitated. "I mean…I guess that's obvious, what else would they be for?"
The shaman raised an eyebrow. "What does The Guard spend its wealth building? Hospitals? Schools? Libraries?"
Jimzon opened his mouth, then paused. "Hmmm," he muttered, the sound flat, a little ashamed. He looked down at his hands.
"I met someone yesterday," he said after a moment. "Old man. Just sitting on a bench. He told me he once saw a bee drown in honey… and understood." He glanced back at the sky. "Do you know what that means?
"What do you think it means?" the Maspin mirrored Jimzon's question..
"Besides 'learn how to swim'?" Jimzon said with a faint smirk. "I guess… balance? Don't get too tired making honey?"
"An insightful take," the Maspin said, nodding. "I think you're right. To me, it speaks to the danger of excess—the way our own desires can quietly destroy us. Whether it's wealth, power, revenge… " he gazed at Jimzon. "... even the things we dedicate our lives to. Sometimes we're consumed by what we chase, even if it's something we've created ourselves."
He paused, his gaze resting on Jimzon. "It's strange, isn't it? Some die of thirst… and others drown."
"I wouldn't say it's funny," Jimzon replied. "But yeah."
Another small, peaceful silence crept up between them. The jungle buzzed gently around the edges.
Jimzon somberly spoke again. "I almost drowned once."
The stars shifted in his gaze as he leaned back, eyes tracing constellations. "It was my first year at the Academy. After my parents died. I tried to act tough, so when we had fitness… I didn't tell my instructor I couldn't swim. Saw the others doing it, so… figuredI could too."
The Maspin watched him with patient, listening eyes.
"So I sank like a rock," Jimzon continued. "Jumped in, flailed around, lungs burning. Tried to cough, tried to scream—but the water just poured in. I felt heavy. Slower. Everything was spinning, then gone. But they fished me out, eventually."
"It must've been scary," the Maspin said gently.
"It was," Jimzon admitted, "but… it wasn't. I wasn't really living for anything back then."
He paused. "When they resuscitated me, I can't tell if I actually saw my mother's hand… or if I just wanted to remember it that way." He sniffled once, quietly.
Jimzon let out a long breath. "The strangest part? The ocean… it tasted just like my tears. My grief—and those waves—they shared a taste."
The Maspin leaned back, visibly moved. "Do you feel like that bee?"
"I think I almost was. Many times."
"Yet you are still here."
Jimzon was quiet for a beat, then spoke again. "I have another question. I saw a Maspin… throw himself into a pit… and commit suicide, I think. But no one screamed. No one cried. No one tried to stop him. I just can't…"
The Maspin nodded solemnly. "Culture here is quite… particular."
He tilted his head thoughtfully. "Tell me—what's the largest structure you've seen on this planet?"
"Umm… the Elder Tree, I suppose."
"That's where this happened?"
"Yeah."
"As I'm sure you've noticed, our species isn't like most other physical life. We don't live or die the same way. Aging does not affect us."
Jimzon leaned in, his eyes narrowing slightly.
The Maspin continued. "Beneath the crust of this planet, there is an ocean. It's where our life began. The ocean itself is alive, in a sense. The roots of the trees connect to it, and the flowers connect to the roots. By extension, this entire planet is one life."
He lifted a smoky hand to his face. "Our eyes—my eyes…"
With a fluid gesture, he plucked out a shimmering sphere of water from his vaporous form. It hovered and spun like a tiny planet in his palm.
"…are fragments of that ocean. That man you saw didn't die. He returned home. Everything he witnessed in his life—he gave that knowledge back, so another could be born. We live our lives, then return with all we've known."
He gently placed the watery eye back into his face.
"Wow… so your life will be memorialized?"
"In a sense," the Maspin said, "but it's not my life. I'm just an extension of the planet's life."
"That's both really cool… and also kind of confusing."
The Maspin smiled faintly. "Life holds many mysteries."
"Indeed."
I'd like to help you and your team, Jimzon," the Maspin said softly. "You all deserve another chance. Just as you can be redeemed… so can others. That is what justice truly is. An eye for an eye leaves the whole world blind."
Jimzon looked back, his eyes beginning to water.
The Maspin continued. "There is a spell we can perform. It sustains life—but it can only be made if you vow never to kill again."
Jimzon squinted slightly, certain. "Sure. Anything."
"You accept these terms?"
"Of course. Let's do it."
"This is an oath, Jimzon. And no oath can be broken without consequence. I cannot stress that enough."
"Whatever it takes to bring Berji back."
The Maspin gave a small nod, his expression serene. "I'm very pleased to hear that. For the spell to take effect, you must eat this."
He extended a blue Floom egg toward Jimzon.
Jimzon took it carefully, examining it. "You want me to eat it?"
"Yes," the Maspin replied. "I recommend doing it in one go. It… has a bit of a kick."
Jimzon managed a nervous half-smile. He found comfort in the Maspin's calm presence, even as something heavy stirred inside him. "So Berji will wake up the second I swallow? How does this work?"
"You will soon understand."
Jimzon leaned back, took a breath, then a bite.

—----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------


The clicks of Pav's frantic wrench were constantly interrupted by gunfire both distant, some terrifyingly close. The beach itself screamed with it. Heat shimmered off the engine he was hunched over, his trembling hands slick with blood and sweat. He slammed his fists into the access panel, prying apart the warped metal. Wires tore loose in his hands. His hyperventilation fogged up his mask with every breath.
"PAVLOCK!" a voice screamed through the radio. Lieutenant Ranzi—pinned behind the shattered trunk of a palm-like tree, shouting over the high-pitched whine of pulse rounds.
"GET THAT BIRD IN THE AIR OR WE'RE FUCKING DEAD!"
Pav twisted the wrench faster, blinking hard to clear the sweat stinging his eyes. He ripped off his helmet and wiped his face on his sleeve before grabbing the welding torch.
The ignition relay was fused. Cooling system offline. Stabilizers fried. But he could bypass it. If he rerouted the secondary power through the left intake and fed it dirty, the ship might cough to life. Might.
Another explosion rocked the ground. Pav ducked instinctively as shrapnel rang off the hull. The quake from the blast knocked his tools from his hands. Behind him, Corporal Darrin screamed then went quiet.
He heard several sharp snaps then the area he'd just been standing in lit up with bullet holes.
"Two minutes!" Pav barked, not sure if anyone could hear him. "I just need two goddamn minutes!"
But he knew as well as they did they didn't have two minutes.
Static fizzled in his earpiece before cutting out completely. Comms were jammed.
The enemy bunker loomed at the top of the ridge, pouring out suppressive fire. Commander Ranzi and the last of the squad were pinned down boxed in with no line of retreat.
Only air support could break them out.
Lieutenant Berji rose to his feet, unfazed by the chaos, unleashing a torrent of LMG fire toward the ridge.
"FUCKING CRAGS!" he roared, voice cutting through the thunder of gunfire.
A moment later, it was like an invisible car struck him—he dropped hard beside Pav, blood spraying from his shoulder. He didn't scream. Just a low grunt of pain as he clutched the wound with his good hand, already moving.
Without hesitation, he drew his pistol and dragged himself behind a sandpile, still aiming up the ridge.
Pav saw Berji fall—then jammed the final wire into place, kicked the console shut, and prayed.
The engine roared to life.
A miracle.
Fans spun. Lights flickered green. Systems online.
"We're up! She's flying!" he shouted into the wind, waving frantically toward the ridge.
Commander Ranzi fired a last volley, then sprinted alongside a private toward the dropship, diving into the rear hull.
The pilot was a glassy-eyed kid, barely more than a cadet—threw Pav a thumbs-up, took the seat, and hit the throttle.
The ship lifted, uneven but steady. The engines flared as it banked hard toward the bunker—heading straight into the fire.
Pav let out a breath. It was working. They were saved.
Then… A scream of metal.
 	A cough with a puff of black smoke.
	 The left engine burst into flame.
Pav watched, paralyzed, as the ship veered left a burning arc toward the ridge…and slammed nose-first into the bunker with the force of a fucking meteor.
The gunfire ceased, as some ashen pieces of shrapnel harmlessly rained out of the sky.
Silence followed. Not the peace kind. The stunned kind. The kind that makes you question if time stopped or just you.
Pav just stood there. Staring at the smoke rising from where the bunker used to be. Where the ship used to be. 
The ridge was a victory.
Pav had no fucking clue if he'd just saved them—or doomed everyone inside.
…
A tear fell onto the tray in front of Pav.
Then another.
But no more.
"You doing alright?" Dan asked gently.
"Yeah," Pav wavered.
They both knew he wasn't.
"We honor your memory—whatever it was, it is sacred to us. You are free to go, Your Majesty."
"I think I'll stay for a minute," Pav said quietly.

Chapter 28

The sunlight kissed Jimzon’s cheek, a warmth that started gently but soon gnawed its way into his muscles. His bare back, scarred and weathered, tensed beneath the growing heat. Slowly, he opened his eyes, lifting his head against the weight of fatigue. At first, all he could see was a blinding white — an ocean of light that burned into his retinas. Gradually, the orange glow of the desert began to seep into focus, revealing the world around him.
As he stirred, grains of sand slid off his face—rolling down the curve of his nose and jawline, while stubborn specks clung to his chin and tangled themselves in his hair. Blinking hard, he rubbed his eyes with coarse fingers, wiping away the lingering grit. When he finally looked around, he was met by a horizon that stretched endlessly in every direction: a sea of orange sand, broken only by the occasional jagged silhouette of rocks thrusting up from the earth.
He inhaled deeply, the air dry and coarse on his tongue, and as he exhaled, his head tilted back, almost involuntarily, to face the sky
The night sky shimmered in shades he had never seen before. Waves of rich velvet rippled across the heavens, flowing like the waves of an ocean, while streaks of cobalt blue ebbed and flowed through the violet expanse. Golden stars dotted the vast canvas, not merely twinkling, but pulsing—alive, almost breathing. Occasionally, bursts of color flared like celestial embers, igniting briefly before dissolving back into the endless sea of night. It was as if the stars themselves were caught in some silent cosmic dance, flickering with purpose yet at peace.
Despite the harshness of the desert, Jimzon’s body felt curiously light, unburdened. Perhaps it was the lack of his armor, perhaps it was something more. His face bore the lines of weariness, but his expression was tranquil, as though he had left behind the weight of thought. Looking to his left, then his right, he saw nothing but infinite emptiness. Without hesitation, he began to walk, his bare feet pressing into the sand, leaving faint imprints behind him—fleeting marks soon to be erased by the whispering winds of the desert.
The muffled crunch of his footsteps were the only sound he could hear, each step steady yet seemingly untethered, as if he wasn’t bound by the same gravity that held him down in other worlds. He bore only a pair of rugged brown pants, patched in darker hues where they had once torn, held up by a thick, frayed string knotted tightly around his waist.
His back, however, told a different story. It was a canvas of pain, etched with countless scars, each one a testament to battles endured. Some were long jagged burn marks, the flesh long since healed but forever marked. Others were small, circular exit wounds, many stitched shut, but still bore marks of the former seams. Deep cuts had bubbled and criss crossed his shoulders and lower back from painful lashings. Claw marks raked diagonally across his ribs, while finer lines hinted at wounds once carefully sewn shut. Each scar alone seemed insignificant, but together, they formed a tapestry of pain.
Yet for all the scars that marred his back, his arms were untouched by such marks. His shoulders, though tanned, were broad and toned, his biceps bearing faint stretch marks from growth and strain. Veins webbed his hands, prominent beneath skin that seemed both weathered and dry. The sun had worn him down, his body dehydrated, his skin tough but never flaking. His face, though aged slightly by the relentless heat, remained strangely calm.
As he wandered, there was no urgency in his steps. He didn’t think about where he was going, nor did he seem to care. He moved forward because that was all there was to do.
Jimzon felt grounded. Each breath came slowly, each step deliberate, his feet sinking into the sand with a soft crunch as the grains shifted beneath him. He paused, mid-stride, lifting his foot slightly as if something unseen had touched him. Kneeling down on one knee, he plunged his hands deep into the warm, yielding sand. Fingers spread wide, he scooped up a handful, cupping it carefully in his palms. The fine grains slipped between his fingers, trickling away like a leaking faucet, yet something about this sand felt different.
As he watched closely, some grains jittered and sparked faintly, like static crackling in the air. They trembled, leapt a few centimeters in random directions, and then stilled as though resting. He held the sand until most of it had slipped away, leaving only a few erratic grains that shivered one last time before harmlessly popping out of his hands, vanishing into the air. Jimzon’s hands fell loosely to his sides, his gaze drifting across the desert, where he could now see other scattered grains occasionally popping into nothingness like tiny stars winking out in the void.
Intrigued, he knelt again, thrusting his hands into the ground. Despite standing on solid earth, the sand gave way easily, parting like water. He reached deeper—past his wrists, then his forearms, until even his elbows were submerged, the sensation oddly frictionless. He paused, brow furrowing in quiet fascination. Slowly, he pulled his arms back, his hands still cupped. As he brought them to the surface, he revealed a small, bright lime-green seed resting in his palms.
The seed gleamed faintly, though its surface bore streaks of brown, as if it had begun to wither. A faint tremor flickered in his right eye, and his expression darkened with concern. He cradled the seed close to his chest, his heartbeat steady but tense. Without wasting another moment, he resumed walking, each step purposeful but strained. Every few hundred steps, he glanced down at the seed, as if it might vanish or crumble into dust if left unwatched.
Eventually, the ground began to slope upward, a low hill forming in the distance. Jimzon trudged onward, but his strength waned. Upon reaching the crest of the hill, his knees gave out, and he collapsed beside a small boulder. He tried to rise, but his legs refused to cooperate, trembling weakly beneath him. His eyes, half-lidded and dry, locked onto the seed in his hand. Desperation flickered across his face.
With what little strength he had left, he clawed at the sand, digging a shallow hole just an inch deep. Carefully, he placed the seed at the center and swept the warm grains over it, his fingers trembling as he patted the mound down. He knew it was hopeless. The barren desert offered no life, no water—but he couldn’t stop himself. He couldn’t let it end here. His breath ragged, he bent over the spot he had planted, his lips cracked and flaking from the heat. With great effort, he gathered what little moisture he could from his parched mouth, spitting weakly onto the patch of sand. The tiny droplets barely soaked in, but he did it again, and again, three times in total.
When he had no more strength to give, he leaned back, collapsing onto the scorched earth. His vision began to blur, narrowing into a dark tunnel fringed with shimmering heat waves. The ground trembled faintly beneath him. Then, he heard it—the sound of something stirring, creaking beneath the earth, as though ancient wood were splintering and shifting.
Weakly, Jimzon raised his head. Through his dimming vision, he saw it: a small stem breaking through the surface of the sand. It rose steadily, eerily reminiscent of a hand clawing its way out of a grave. Yet it was beautiful in its defiance. The stem thickened, its bark rough and ridged, shooting skyward with unnatural speed. It grew taller and taller, its form blurring as it surged upward before slowing, pausing, as if to take a breath.
Then, like a canopy unfolding in slow motion, it unfurled dozens of branches—each one jagged and sharp, reaching outward as though grasping for the heavens. The tree stood tall and resolute, noble despite the desolation around it. Its bark was weathered but strong, each arm stretching with purpose. At the end of every branch, where a three-fingered hand might have closed into a fist, something remarkable happened. The bark curled back, and from the tips sprouted delicate pink flowers, their petals soft and translucent in the twilight glow. The flowers bloomed briefly before transforming—each one giving birth to a bright red fruit that swelled rapidly on its stem.
The fruits grew heavy, their weight too great for the slender branches to bear. One by one, they fell, landing softly on the sand. A single fruit rolled to a stop beside Jimzon’s outstretched hand, its vivid red skin gleaming like a droplet of blood against the endless orange desert.	
Jimzon stared in awe, his eyes watering. He picked up the fruit, and took a bite. The sweetness spread across his tongue like a burst of sunlight. It was as if every sweet thing he'd ever known—ripe strawberries, honeyed dew, the essence of the sun itself—had been distilled into a single, perfect drop. The taste was pure, intense, and utterly mesmerizing
Jimzon crawled toward the base of the tree and leaned against its sturdy trunk, taking slow, deliberate bites. The bark behind him was rough but reassuring, and he closed his eyes for a fleeting second, letting the fruit do its work. His weary muscles loosened, and a faint pulse of energy began to stir within him, fragile but real. Yet, even as he enjoyed this moment of solace, something on the horizon caught his attention.
Black clouds gathered in the distance—not the dull gray of an ordinary storm, but an unnervingly deep black, as if ink had spilled across the sky. They rolled forward with unnatural speed, devouring the horizon. Beneath them came a low, growling thunder, but it didn’t roar like normal thunder—it shrieked, high-pitched and grating, like nails scraping across a chalkboard, echoing unnervingly through the barren wasteland.
Jimzon tensed, instinctively pressing himself tighter against the tree. A sudden chill swept over him as the black clouds crept closer, blotting out the sun and casting a dense, heavy shadow across the land. The once blazing heat was gone in an instant, replaced by biting cold. Goosebumps rose across his scarred back, crawling up his spine and to the nape of his neck like an army of ants. His breath came out in short, visible puffs, and his fingers began to tremble despite the surge of warmth he had felt moments earlier.
A green flash shattered the darkness.
In the blink of an eye, a bolt of green lightning struck the ground with an ear-splitting crack, the light so bright it seemed to tear through his vision. Jimzon’s ears rang painfully, and for a few agonizing moments, he was both blinded and deafened. He barely had time to gather his senses before another bolt struck, then another—each one accompanied by a deafening burst that vibrated through his chest.
The sky lit up in sporadic flashes of vivid green, and each bolt left a burning scar on the earth, the scorched ground igniting in strange emerald flames that flickered unnaturally, refusing to be extinguished. Jimzon sat frozen, his body tense and unwilling to move. He felt paralyzed, as though trapped between exhaustion and terror. The fruit had restored his energy, but now his limbs felt useless, weighed down by the sheer force of the storm. His fingers twitched uncontrollably, the shudder spreading from his hands to his arms and down his spine. He tried to focus, to find some way to escape, his eyes darting frantically in search of shelter.
Then, just beyond the edge of his vision, he noticed something new—a large boulder, massive and out of place, one that was not there just moments ago. Its surface was pitted and weathered, but more importantly, it had a hollowed center—a one-room cavern carved out by time or something stranger. It was just large enough to fit him.
Behind him, the tree stood resolute, its branches stretching defiantly toward the storm. In the distance, green flames dotted the desert like a twisted reflection of the stars above. Jimzon’s mind swirled with indecision for a brief second, his body still trembling. To his right was the unwavering base of the tree, solid and ancient, but offering no true protection from the lightning that rained down around him. To his left lay the cavern, shadowed but beckoning—a faint promise of safety.
The choice was obvious. Without hesitation, he bolted toward the cavern, his feet digging into the sand as he sprinted across the cold, barren ground. Another bolt of green lightning struck just behind him, the sound reverberating through his skull like a war drum. He threw himself into the hollowed boulder, landing hard on the floor, grains of sand clinging to his clothes and filling the pockets and creases of his rugged pants.
His breathing came in ragged gasps as he lay there, shivering uncontrollably. Despite the fruit’s earlier nourishment, his body still quaked with weakness, a sensation he hadn’t felt in what seemed like a lifetime. Memories of past battles, of storms both literal and figurative, flickered through his mind. He hadn’t felt this way in years—helpless, vulnerable, shaken to his core.
Once more, a blinding flash ripped through the darkness, followed by a deafening boom that shook the very ground beneath Jimzon. The force knocked him backward, his ears ringing as he struggled to regain his balance. Before him, the tree blazed—a blackened branch crumbled from its crown, crashing to the base with a spray of glowing embers. Smoke coiled upward, a twisting column of darkness rising to meet the swirling storm above.
Jimzon’s breath quickened, each inhalation sharp and shallow, his heart pounding violently in his chest. He stared at the burning tree, the green glow of the surrounding flames flickering across his face. Despite the fire that licked at its limbs, the tree stood firm—unyielding, unbothered by the pain. It didn’t falter, it didn't bow.
Maybe he shouldn’t either.
Fueled by something deeper than instinct, Jimzon rushed toward the tree, his feet digging into the scorched sand. He gripped the rough bark, ignoring the sting of heat against his hands as he climbed upward. The flames hissed and crackled around him, their searing touch blistering the heels of his feet, but he didn’t waver. He reached into his pockets, grabbing handfuls of sand, and hurled them at the burning branches. The sand smothered the smaller fires, falling like a dull rain over the embers.
When the sand wasn’t enough, he used his hands—slapping, stomping, smothering the flames with whatever strength he could muster. The heat seared his skin, the air around him thick with ash, but he didn’t stop. He couldn’t stop. This tree wasn’t just any tree—it was his. He didn’t know why, but he knew, deep within his bones, that it was his duty to protect it.
By the time the last of the flames had been extinguished, Jimzon’s body trembled from exhaustion. He let himself fall from the tree, landing hard on the ground and rolling to soften the impact. His hands were raw, his breath ragged, but he smiled faintly as he looked up at the charred branches. The tree was scarred, damaged, but alive.
Jimzon stayed at the base of the tree throughout the night, leaning against its rough trunk. The storm raged on around him, green lightning continuing to strike the barren land, each bolt followed by a crack of thunder so loud it rattled his bones. Yet he didn’t flinch. He had stopped flinching long ago. He sat still, unwavering like the tree itself, until eventually, the storm began to fade. The sky lightened, the winds calmed, and a deep, peaceful sleep overtook him.
When Jimzon opened his eyes again, dawn had broken. The air was crisp, and sunlight poured over the horizon in soft, golden waves. He blinked, momentarily disoriented, but what he saw filled him with quiet wonder.
The tree stood taller now, its branches stretched wide in every direction, each one heavy with delicate pink blossoms. The petals swirled gently in the breeze, drifting lazily across the desert like soft snowflakes. He watched as the blossoms scattered their seeds across the land, carried far by the wind.
The tree had grown—not just taller, but older, wiser. Its trunk had thickened, its bark gnarled and furrowed like the face of an ancient being. Where Jimzon had once rested against the rough bark, there was now a smooth, sculpted hollow—shaped perfectly to cradle him, as though the tree had molded itself in gratitude, offering him a place to rest.
He sat up slowly, feeling the warmth of the sun against his skin. Crossing his legs, he rested his hands on his knees, breathing deeply and evenly. The air tasted clean, filled with the faint scent of blossoms. He became still, as still as the tree itself, his chest rising and falling in a steady rhythm.
Time seemed to blur.
His clean-shaven face began to change—the first rough patches of stubble emerging on his chin and cheeks, dark but flecked with gray. The stubble thickened into a beard, coarse and wild, as days seemed to pass in mere moments. His hair grew longer, falling in uneven waves past his shoulders, until it cascaded down his back in silver strands.
Wrinkles etched themselves into his skin—lines of age, of wisdom, of countless moments lived in silence. His beard stretched further, past his crossed legs, flowing like a river of silver caught in the morning breeze. His once strong, sturdy frame thinned, his muscles wasting away as though time itself had pressed fast forward. Yet he remained still, unbothered by the passage of years, his mind serene, his breath calm.
The desert around him remained unchanged, but Jimzon transformed—aging rapidly, as though the very essence of life was being drawn out of him. He continued to sit, unmoving, until his head began to bow forward, his body no longer able to support its own weight.
With a final, deep exhale, he leaned forward, and as he did, something remarkable happened. His body didn’t fall with a thud—it dissolved.
First, his fingers crumbled into fine grains of sand, carried gently away by the breeze. Then his arms, his legs, his chest—all of him turned to dust, caught in the wind like the seeds of the tree. His silver hair unraveled into countless strands of shimmering sand, drifting across the barren landscape.
At last, nothing remained but the faint outline of where he had once sat, a memory etched into the earth, soon to be swept away by the endless desert winds into the desert he arose from.

Chapter 29

Jimzon collapsed forward. Gravity swallowed him and spat him onto his back.
His breath caught. The stars above locked into sharp clarity, no longer spinning. The reaches of this night are painted with vibrant auras, their glowing green and blue waves adding otherworldly vibrancy to the dark expanse. Everything felt too vivid, too fast.
“What the fuck?” he gasped. He wiped his mouth which still carried the syrupy aftertaste of the floom egg.
He sat up in a jerk. His hands pressed into soft ground. No dust. No desert. His skin looked smoother. His limbs lighter. He was young again.
“What—”
He rolled onto his palms, pushing into a crouch. Confusion still thick in his chest. Then he saw the cloak. The boots.
The Friend stood before him.
“Your friend has awoken,” the Friend said.
Jimzon squinted hard. His eyes were wild. “How long… what…  you…?” The words tumbled out, half-formed, breath hitching.
The Friend didn’t answer. He simply turned toward the horizon. “Visit me again before you depart. I am needed elsewhere. But it has been my pleasure.”
Jimzon sat frozen, still gripping the earth, as if it might vanish beneath him. His fingers touched his own face, tracing it like a stranger’s. Still his. Still real.
His heartbeat slammed in his ears. His body couldn’t keep still. His brain offered no words, only a repeated 'what?' thought cycling over and over. Was he just drugged? Or was it something more?
Berji.
Berji.
BERJI.
His feet moved on their own. He launched into a sprint before his thoughts could catch up. He ran full tilt, tearing through the strange forest. Trailblazers flinched as he blew past them. Maspins stood still and watched him blur by. Trees and mushrooms whipped past like scenery on rails.
He didn’t slow. He didn’t speak. He just ran.
In seconds, the medbay doors came into view and opened with a whoosh.
He rushed in.
The secretary behind the front desk stood up quickly. “Sir… sir, wait—”
The doctor from earlier stepped out from a side hallway and gently stopped her with one hand. His other hand went to his coat pocket.
Then he looked at Jimzon with a soft smile, like he’d been expecting him.
“I take it the shaman found you,” the doctor said, voice calm, a gentle smile forming beneath tired eyes.
Jimzon, still catching his breath, gave a quick nod.
“Good,” the doctor replied, turning slightly to glance at a nearby monitor. “He’s just starting to come around. The nurses are easing him out of the chamber now. Give him a minute to adjust and I’ll let you in.”
Jimzon stepped closer. His voice barely came out. “He’s really awake?”
“He really is.” The doctor turned back to him and reached into a side drawer. From it, he pulled a small box, wrapped in textured cardboard and tied neatly with a length of white string.
“The shaman came by earlier,” the doctor said, holding it out. “Before the feast. Told me this was for your friend. Wanted me to say it was from you, oddly enough. No idea what’s inside.”
Jimzon accepted the box slowly, his fingers brushing over the string. It was light. Delicate. He fought the urge to open it right there.
“Wait,” he said. “He came by before the feast? I hadn’t even met him yet.”
The doctor gave a short, amused exhale. “You know how it is with them. I’m fairly sure it’s the same one who saved me. Funny how things line up.”
Jimzon nodded again, this time more distantly. His eyes shifted to the wall clock. He cradled the box to his chest as if it might vanish if he let it go.
“You haven’t been offworld recently, have you?” the doctor asked.
Jimzon shook his head. “No, sir.”
“Good.” The doctor stepped over to a console beside the hallway door and keyed in a long passcode. With a soft click, a sealed door slid open.
“You can skip the decontamination chamber,” he said. “Room four-sixteen.”
A flicker of light sparked in Jimzon’s eyes. He took a step forward, but the doctor held out a hand, stopping him just for a moment.
“One last thing,” he said, his voice lowering a little. “Take it slow with him. He might not seem like himself right away. Could be confused. Unsettled. But he’s still in there. Just… go easy.”
Jimzon met his gaze and nodded, more serious this time. Then he turned and made his way toward the door, the package clutched tight in his hands.
Jimzon nodded to the doctor and moved with purpose down the hallway. But as his hand reached for the door, something stopped him.
He froze. A sudden weight pressed against his chest. It wasn't fear exactly, but something deeper.
He swallowed and forced himself to breathe. Then, with effort, he knocked softly. Not too loud. Just enough.
His hand found the handle and turned. The door creaked open into near darkness. Only the red glow of exit signs painted the room in a low hum. Shadows clung to the corners like fog.
“Berji?” Jimzon called out. His voice cracked, barely above a whisper. “It’s me. Jim. We’re okay now.”
There was movement in the dark. A figure sat in the far corner, turned away.
“Jimzon?” The voice was familiar but hollow. Strained. “Is it actually you?”
Berji turned slightly, but the light didn’t reach his face.
“Yeah,” Jimzon said, stepping inside. “I ran here as fast as I could. Pav’s coming soon too.”
“Wait,” Berji said, sudden and sharp. “My face… I don’t want you to see it. Leave the lights off.”
Jimzon stopped in place, heart aching. “It’s okay, man. I’m not here to judge. I just want to be here. That’s all.”
Berji stayed quiet for a moment. Then, softer, he said, “Just wait… I need a minute. Everything’s still… too much.”
Jimzon nodded, even though Berji couldn’t see it. “Alright. Sure. No rush. I uh… I have something for you.”
“A gift?”
“A surprise, I think.” Jimzon walked slowly to a small table by the wall and set the box down. 
Berji let out a dry, exhausted breath. “I’m not really one for surprises anymore… but I suppose it couldn’t hurt.”
“You can open it now if you'd like,” Jimzon said, sitting on the edge of a chair. “I’ll keep my head down.”
Berji stood. His movements were slow but steady, like someone relearning how to carry their own weight. He stepped to the table, pulled the string, and let the wrapping fall in a quiet whisper.
Something shifted in the silence.
Jimzon didn’t dare look. He just listened. A small sniffle. The rustle of cloth. The sound of breath catching.
“Do you like it?” Jimzon asked, voice low. He risked a glance upward.
“I love it,” Berji said.
He was standing tall now. The dim red light caught his face for the first time.
A mask covered it. Intimately hand-carved, shaped with reverence and intent. Riveted wood, smoothed and stained. It wasn’t smiling. It wasn’t frowning. It simply watched, with the quiet stillness of a mountain. It carried a presence. A calm wisdom. An ancient weight.
Jimzon stared, heart thudding.
The mask looked like it had always belonged to him. A perfect fit.
“I’m happy you—” Jimzon began, voice catching.
Before he could finish, Berji stepped forward and wrapped his arms around him.
Jimzon blinked, surprised, but hugged him back without hesitation. The moment stretched. Quiet. Still. Then, he felt Berji stiffen.
“This is real?” Berji asked, voice distant, almost dreamlike.
Jimzon nodded against his shoulder. “Yeah, man. You’re safe.”
Berji pulled back slightly, his hands still on Jimzon’s arms. “Say something only the real Jim would know.”
Jimzon blinked. “Uh, like what?”
“Something I wouldn’t know. Something I could never guess but somehow makes sense now.”
Jimzon scratched the back of his neck, thinking fast.
“Okay. Uh… back in the academy, before we officially met each other, I copied your answers on the math exam. It’s why I always have you do the ordinance calculations in battle instead of Pav, even though he’s the engineer. I didn't want to tell you since I knew you studied hard, but I ended up becoming captain"
Berji didn’t say anything at first. Then, softly, like a breath releasing tension that had built for years. He said, “Oh thank god… thank god.”
He sat back down slowly, almost collapsing into the chair. The mask on his face gave away nothing, and that made it worse. Jimzon couldn’t read him. Couldn’t see the hurt or relief or confusion. Just the carved wood staring straight ahead.
“You okay, man?” Jimzon asked, voice gentler now. “What’s on your mind?”
Berji sat with his elbows on his knees, hands clasped. His voice was low, almost hoarse. “I thought about this moment. For so long. Waking up. Actually waking up. That floom stuff… it felt like I was gone for years...”
Jimzon moved closer and sat beside him. He placed a hand on Berji’s back and let the silence settle for a few seconds, steady and grounding. No questions. No rush.
	Berji’s voice was quiet at first, like he was still testing the sound of it in the air.
“I… I started counting the days. Then the weeks. Trying to track time, even though time didn’t mean anything in there. Then the dreams started. And I had to keep reminding myself they weren’t real.”
He rubbed his hands together slowly, as if trying to warm himself.
“I started pulling apart every day I could remember, going backwards. Just to stay sane. How I tied my boots the morning we crashed... What I ate the day before. Every detail. Over.. and over.”
His breath caught for a moment.
“But the days kept going. And I kept sinking. I thought of every single memory I’d ever had. Every one. And I was still asleep.”
He leaned forward, elbows on knees, mask pointed at the floor.
“I dreamed about waking up, Jim. Over and over. Sometimes it felt real. I’d open my eyes, feel everything again, just to get yanked back under. Over and over.”
Jimzon’s chest ached just hearing it. He rested a hand gently on Berji’s back, grounding him.
“That’s awful, brother. But it’s over now. Pav and I made some good friends. You’re in good hands. You’re safe.”
Berji nodded slowly, but it didn’t feel like agreement. His voice returned, heavier than before.
“I’ve had so much time to think, man. So. Much. Time.”
Jimzon nodded. “I know. I’m sorry it happened like this. You didn’t deserve that.”
Berji shrugged faintly, almost like the words bounced off him.
“It doesn’t matter what I deserved. It happened. It always would have. It was inevitable. There’s nothing we could’ve done to stop it.”
Jimzon tilted his head. “You still feeling yourself at all?”
Berji was silent for a beat, then said, almost whispering through the mask, “You won't find the same person twice. Not even in the same person.”
“What do you mean?” Jimzon asked, concerned.
“I’ll… explain later.”
Jimzon exhaled through his nose, jaw tight. “Alright. Let’s get you to the bungalow. You need rest.”
Berji stood and placed a hand on the wall, his fingers dragging slightly on the surface.
“Where are we?”
“There’s a lot to catch you up on,” Jimzon said, already leading him toward the door. “I’ll explain everything. But for now, if anyone asks, your name is Grugg.”
Berji blinked under the mask. “Grugg?” He sounded bewildered.
…
Pav leaned against the stairs outside the bungalow, arms crossed, scanning the road with growing impatience. A few trailblazers passed by, chatting softly as they carried leftover platters and stray decorations back to their rooms. The celebration was clearly winding down.
Where the hell was Jimzon? He was supposed to be here half an hour ago.
With a groan, Pav sat on the steps, head resting on his hand, boredom and worry gnawing at him.
Then he spotted movement at the far end of the street. A familiar figure approached—fast.
“Ji—” Pav caught himself. “Ahem. Smelvin! That you?”
He stood as Jimzon came into view, walking beside another man in a trailblazer uniform. The stranger’s face was hidden behind a wooden mask.
Jimzon raised a hand. “Prince! I have Grugg!”
Pav’s eyes widened. He broke into a jog toward them, slowing only when he reached the masked figure.
“Berji?” he asked, hope rushing into his voice. He pulled him into a hug.
Berji returned it with measured strength. “Pavlock… it’s been a while. You’re a prince now and your beard is gone?”
Pav let out a short laugh. “Guess the people like their liars clean-shaven”
He stepped back, eyes scanning Berji up and down. “God, it’s good to see you alive, man. Let’s get you inside.”
The three of them entered the bungalow.
The candle lights were dimmed, the air warm with the scent of leftover food and something sweet from the kitchen. They sat, talked, and slowly eased into the night. 
…
Berji sat back, trying to process everything, his masked face tilted slightly as if hoping it might help things make more sense.
“So let me get this straight,” he said slowly. “We crash-landed on a planet with psychic chickens. Then Yake abandoned us. After that, you two got blackmailed by a warlord into smuggling weapons, using fake identities: one of which is a foreign ambassador, so you could convince a magical council of singing, psychic smokestacks into giving us a ship. And somewhere in all that, you found a drug-dealing shaman to snap me out of a coma, so we can stop a false flag attack orchestrated by the biggest military in the galaxy?”
He paused.
“And now we’re freeing a slave to pull it off?”
Pav and Jimzon exchanged a glance. Both gave reluctant little nods.
Berji exhaled through the mask. The sound was low and measured, almost thoughtful. “Honestly? I don’t know how the hell you managed any of that. But we’re here. So somehow, you did.”
He leaned forward, elbows on his knees.
“I always had a feeling I’d wake up in the middle of a mess,” he said. “But this… this is something else. There’s a lot of moving parts here.”
Pav spoke up, his tone quieter now. “We just wanted you back. That was all we cared about. After everything you’ve been through… if you're not ready, if you don't want to jump into this mission, you don’t have to. No pressure. Really.”
Berji tilted his head. “You think we can pull this off?”
Jimzon didn’t hesitate. “We’re going to have to.”
There was a silence. Then Berji nodded slightly.
“Well,” he said. “If the problem has a solution, then it’s not much of a problem at all.”
Jimzon cracked a tired smile. “So you’re in?”
“I’m in,” Berji said. “But I should warn you… I’m not exactly the same man you remember.”
“I know the mask takes some getting used to,” Jimzon said, his voice gentle. “But you’re still our brother. No matter how you look.”
Berji turned toward him, the mask catching the low light. “I don’t mean the mask,” he said. “I mean… the Berji who left the mothership isn’t the same one sitting in front of you now.”
Pav leaned forward, brow furrowed. “What do you mean?”
Berji exhaled, and the sound filtered through the carved wood like wind through branches.
“When my mind first started waking up… I kept replaying things. I asked myself—what if I had taken a different seat on the shuttle? Would I have survived the crash the same way you did?”
Jimzon and Pav glanced at each other but said nothing. They both shifted slightly, uneasy. The silence thickened.
Berji continued. “Then I started going further back. When I got shot in the shoulder on Sakasin… what if it had landed a few inches higher and hit my head instead? What if I’d enlisted a day later and ended up on another team? What if I never met you at all?”
Jimzon’s jaw tightened. Pav’s foot tapped once, then stopped. Neither spoke. They didn’t have to. Their faces said enough—half guilt, half grief, and no answers.
Berji leaned back, folding his arms across his chest. “I relived every injury. Every close call. And the more I thought about it, the more I realized none of it could have happened any other way.”
Jimzon looked up, concern flashing in his eyes. Pav sat up straighter, listening carefully now.
“Every choice I’ve made,” Berji said, voice low but steady, “every habit I formed, every word I spoke, every unconscious twitch of muscle… it all led to that exact moment. The bullet. The crash. The coma.”
His gaze lifted slightly, though the mask hid whatever emotion stirred beneath it.
“And the guy who pulled the trigger on Sakasin? Same thing. His training, his instincts, his whole life built up to that one movement. That one shot. Every decision he had ever made put him in that exact position.”
He paused.
"Nothing was luck. It wasn’t random. It was the result of everything that had happened before. It’s like neither of us had any real control—we were always going to end up in that moment, on a direct path toward each other. The bullet landed exactly where it was always meant to. The crash too… it’s as if dominoes were laid from my very birth to that exact moment. It was inevitable.”
Jimzon peered, as if trying to see through the mask. " I’ve never really thought about it like that, but I don’t know. It seems like that moment was so intense for you that you’re trying to make sense of almost dying and how scary that is. Cause yeah, it’s fucking scary, dude, and I’m happy you’re here, but I don’t know. It seems like you’re saying that we don’t have free will"
“That’s exactly what I’m saying,” Berji replied from behind the mask. His hands rested still on the table. The food in front of him remained untouched, growing cold.
Jimzon leaned back slightly, brow furrowed. “I’m not sure I follow. I mean… can’t I just stand up right now and start screaming? Just because I want to? Isn’t that free will?”
Berji leaned in " You could, but this conversation would be the reason why you did that, because your whole life has shaped you to react that way. Everything that’s happened to you, every experience, has molded you into the person who would make that choice, or to ask me."
Jim furrowed his eyebrows "Yeah, maybe it influenced me or whatever, but it’s my choice whether I want to or not. I’m not just watching my life happen. I’m making it happen. All my choices are mine."
Berji rested his forearms on the table, his voice thoughtful beneath the mask. “You’ve heard of the butterfly effect, right? How one small, almost meaningless action can ripple out and change everything? I’ve been thinking about that. A lot. I can trace and pinpoint my entire life through it. Moment by moment.”
He paused, eyes fixed on the middle distance, lost in a memory only he could see.
“It’s all cause and effect. Everything. Our lives have a trajectory just like bullets—set in motion long before we realize we’ve been fired. Since the Big Bang, it's all one motion. It’s hard at first to shake the feeling that none of us are really steering. All we can do is try to enjoy the ride while we can.”
Pav leaned forward, elbows on the table, his voice a little sharper. “Yeah, cause and effect is real, but that doesn’t mean we’re passengers. We still get to choose how we react. I don’t know about you, man, but I believe I’ve got a soul. That the part of me that chooses. That isn’t just momentum from the past. It’s me. I’m not just some product of my parents or my past. Sure, maybe the butterfly effect sets up the situation, but I’m the one who decides what to do with it"
He glanced at Jimzon for a beat, then looked back at Berji.
“In the academy, I got really into quantum physics for a while. There’s this idea called superposition. Basically, a particle exists in all possible states until someone observes it. Then it collapses into one. What if we’re like that? What if every choice we make is a collapse into one version of reality? Maybe that’s where the freedom is. Infinite outcomes. Maybe we do have control after all.”
Berji let the thought settle for a few seconds before replying, voice calm and measured.
“See, the thing about physics, at least from what I’ve seen, is even the people who dedicate their lives to it still don’t fully understand it. And you say you believe in a soul. Okay. That implies something greater—some kind of creator, something that built us.”
He looked between them, slow and deliberate.
“And if someone made us—someone powerful enough to create all this—then they already know how it ends. Every step. Every thought. Every choice. It’s already written.”
Pav exhaled, thinking aloud more than arguing. “The hard thing about talking about God is… if he’s powerful enough to create the universe, then he’s probably powerful enough to give us free will too. Right? I guess that’s the paradox. Infinite power means infinite options.”
He paused, running a hand over his chin before continuing.
“But honestly, the whole idea is so abstract it kind of slips through your fingers the more you try to hold onto it. Maybe I shouldn’t have brought it up. I don’t know. It just feels right to me, like something bigger is out there, whether we understand it or not.”
Berji nodded slowly. “I’ll give you some credit there. I don’t know if I exactly believe in God, not in the traditional sense. But sometimes... yeah. Things feel like they go way beyond us.”
He leaned back slightly, the mask hiding whatever expression was behind it.
“There’s this theory I read years ago. Messed me up for a while. It goes like this. There are only three possibilities. Either life entirely goes extinct before we ever develop the technology to simulate a universe, or we do get the tech but decide not to use it for ethical reasons. Or option three: we actually go through with it. We build the simulation.”
He let that sink in for a second before continuing.
“Here’s the trippy part. Even if 99% of people decide to say say no, say it’s wrong, unethical, whatever… if just 1% go through with it, that’s all it takes. One group. One person. One decision. And suddenly, simulated realities are real.”
Berji tapped the table lightly once with a finger.
“And if we can do it, that means someone else already did. Reality can simulate reality. Layers on top of layers. Stack enough of those, and maybe that’s what God is. Just the top layer.”
His voice lowered.
“And maybe we’re not even real. Maybe this entire conversation—every word, every breath, every choice we think we’re making—was already planned out. By someone else. Somewhere else.”
Jimzon leaned forward, his tone more serious now. “Okay, but if we’re just puppets in some cosmic show… does that mean people aren’t responsible for the bad things they do?”
Berji nodded slowly, as if he’d wrestled with the question more times than he could count. “That’s the part that really messes with me. If everything’s just cause and effect, then yeah—maybe people don’t truly choose to be good or bad. Maybe they never had a real choice. But society can’t work like that.”
He paused, fingers tapping lightly against the table.
“Even if someone couldn’t have done anything different, their actions still have consequences. If someone hurts people, we can’t just shrug and say, ‘Well, they were destined to do that,’ and let them walk free. Whether it’s fate or free will, we still have to act. We still need justice. Accountability. Even if free will is an illusion… the world runs on the assumption that it’s real.”
Pav frowned, arms crossed. “I don’t know… that sounds like you’re leaning pretty close to nihilism. Like, if everything’s predetermined, if we’re just the output of some quantum code running in the background, then nothing actually matters, right?”
He paused, then softened a bit, his voice more reflective.
“Or maybe it depends on how you look at it. Maybe the idea that we are in a simulation, created by something bigger, means there’s more to this. Maybe it’s actually kind of beautiful. If someone—something—cared enough to create this reality, even if it’s just code or layers of perception, then that means life can be infinite. And that means we matter because we’re part of it.”
He looked toward Berji again.
“I just think the idea that nothing matters is… depressing. And maybe even kind of weak. If everything really is a simulation, then maybe that’s the point. Maybe we’re here because it matters. Maybe there is a point to all of this.”
Berji rested his hands on the table, voice calm behind the mask. “Simulation or not, there are so many unconscious choices our brains make. How we grow up, how our minds are wired, our genetics, our environments, the cultures we’re raised in—it all shapes how we act. Every bit of it. It’s like this long, invisible chain of dominoes, already tipping before we’re even aware we exist.”
He glanced down at his hands.
“We never really see where it’s going—only where it’s been. And from where I’m sitting, it feels like we’re just following a set trajectory. Just like you said Jimzon, things happen because they have to.”
Jimzon shook his head slowly. “I don’t know, man. I’ve never liked the idea of letting the past define us. Yeah, sure, it shapes us but I still feel like I’m steering the ship. It’s messy. It’s chaotic. But that’s what makes it real.”
He leaned forward, his voice more intense now.
“If everything was predetermined, you’d think it would be easier. But it’s not. I have to work and think to figure things out. That takes energy. My energy. That takes risk. I don’t know the outcomes, and I still push through. I make decisions. I take responsibility. I built my life, step by step. And if determinism is real, fine. But I still had to choose to show up. And that should count for something.”
Berji nodded slightly. “Maybe the hardest part is accepting that we’re not meant to have the answers. Not with mammal brains like ours. Maybe there is no final answer. Maybe it’s always both, free will and fate, tangled together.”
He paused, then added, softer, “But honestly? I’m just glad I woke up. Maybe I’m not a nihilist after all… because I still want to be here. I still want to save Shayron.”
Jimzon smiled faintly. “Wherever your head’s at, you’re our brother. If this was all destiny or some cosmic design to give you time to think—fine. You’re still our Berji.”
Berji looked at the two of them for a long moment, then said quietly, “I just don’t feel the same. Since I woke up… I can’t tell if I’m becoming someone new, or returning to someone I used to be.”
Jimzon stood, steady and sure. “We’re all changing. Together. And we’ve got a galaxy to save.”
Chapter 30

“Alright lads, the ship should be waiting at the helipad tonight, just after sunset,” Daiser said, wiping a streak of grease from his tunic.
“Everyone packed?”
Pav glanced around at Calsco, Jim, and Berji in the bungalow. “Pretty much. I guess we packed light when we came here anyway,” he said with a smirk.
“I’ve got some of my men hauling my cargo up to the pad now,” Calsco said, absently stroking the small floom in his arms. “The rifles are in a little box marked ‘Proxine batteries,’ so try not to toss it around.”
“Hey Cal, I gotta ask — why not just ask me for the rifles?” Daiser cut in.
“Honestly, partner, I had little faith in this one,” Calsco replied, pointing at Pav. “Last thing I needed was to be sitting on some hot weapons here.”
Pav frowned. “I get why Daiser wants to leave, but why are you so desperate to go if you’ve got a solid job here?”
“My wife,” Calsco said, the grin dropping away.
“You have a wife? Weren’t you just saying if you had your own ship you’d run a brothel?” Jimzon asked, half-teasing.
“Boys like you wouldn’t understand,” Calsco said softly. “She’s fierce, brilliant, beautiful. She knows no one else could compete with her.”
“Why leave, then?” Pav asked.
“It’s not exactly by choice,” Calsco said. “I can’t return unless I come back as a hero… or reclaim my chieftain title.”
“So this whole Trailblazer job—just a way to be a hero?” Pav pressed.
Calsco chuckled. “No. That’s what the guns are for.”
“We’re not helping you kill anyone,” Jimzon said flatly.
“I’d never ask,” Calsco replied. “This fight’s mine alone.”
Jimzon gave a small nod. “For what it’s worth, I wish you luck. Berji, you’ve been awfully quiet—what’s on your mind?”
Berji didn’t look at him. Still gazing out the window, he said, “Isn’t it strange how multiple truths can exist at once, yet still undermine each other’s validity?”
The room went still.
Daiser leaned toward Pav and murmured, “Is he always like this?”
“Yes… I suppose that is… strange,” Jimzon said, trying to be polite. “Well—uh—anything you all want to do for our last day here?”
Daiser shook his head. Calsco uncorked a bottle of astrovin. Berji kept staring out the window, lost somewhere else entirely.
“Hey, wait, Cal—you said you needed to go back as a hero, right?” Jimzon asked.
Calsco looked at him mid-sip, then wiped his mouth with the back of his hand. “What about it?”
“Pav. You wanna fill them in?”
Pav frowned. “You don’t wanna do it with me?”
“I’ve got an errand to run. I’ll be back.”
“Where?” Pav asked.
Jimzon gave a faint smile. “I’m not sure. The shaman will find me.”

…

Jimzon found himself standing inside the elder tree. The air was cool and still, smelling faintly of old sap and damp stone. He paced in slow arcs around the central abyss, each footstep sending a soft echo spiraling upward through the hollow. His eyes lingered on the mosaics along the walls—weathered fragments of color, set deep into the living wood.
He reached out, letting his fingertips trace the ridges and grooves of each stone. They were uneven, almost warm, as if still holding the memory of the hands that had set them in place. Just like in the ceremony he’d witnessed, he knew each stone represented a life once lived here.
It reminded him of the monuments outside the Guard Academy. Out front stood a great fountain, flanked by long walls plated with brass and etched with thousands of names from the Guard’s earliest battles. Rows upon rows—privates, sergeants, captains—each letter cut deep and filled with gold leaf.
He’d once tried to read them all. Even after hours, he was still only halfway down the first wall. After that long, the names blurred into one another—no longer people, just an endless tide of letters. Yet he couldn’t help wondering: which of them had been the funny ones, the hotheads, the dreamers? Who had joined for glory, and who just wanted to pay for school? Who had once imagined themselves an athlete, an opera singer, a scientist? Who had planned to be a mother or father—or already was one?
Some surnames he recognized as parents or siblings of people he’d trained beside. That was always the worst part. He knew none of them had gone peacefully. The causes of death ran the full, grim spectrum: live-fire accidents, drone strikes, slugs to the gut. Suicide bombers, snipers, ships swallowed whole in stormy seas. Shelling. Disease. Incendiaries. Bayonets. A parachute that failed to open.
It was easier, somehow, not knowing their faces. Without faces, you could almost convince yourself they’d never really been there at all.
