At the elder tree, the weight felt lighter. Most of the names here belonged to those who had lived long, full lives, leaving when they chose. The tree would keep growing, and the wall at its base would keep filling—old stones rising with the bark, making room for the next generation.
Jimzon found himself wondering if the academy had ever carved the name Tiam Rockstead into its own memorial wall.
He’d met Tiam back in early cadet school, during the Guard Scout Corps program—a summer camp for kids with no home to return to between academic years. They’d clean their uniforms and bunks until they smelled faintly of starch and metal polish, pack their bags, and march in formation across the countryside, rain or shine.
Some days they’d take shots at distant targets. The cheers when someone actually hit one were far louder than the report of the rifles.
They marched everywhere—through open fields, along cracked roads overtaken by weeds, up into mist-shrouded hills where radio towers blinked like far-off stars. When it rained, the steady drumming on their ponchos made the world feel far away and almost safe. When the heat was brutal, they passed around communal canisters, sharing them like contraband.
That’s where Jimzon met his first friend. Tiam.
At night, they had thirty minutes to write letters. Most of the kids just doodled or scrawled fake messages from fake families. But Tiam wrote a letter every night, sealing it carefully, though no one ever came to collect mail.
Sometimes they told stories by flashlight. Tiam once swore he’d seen a drone crash in the woods behind the old rail tracks. Jimzon had told him he knew he was lying.
“Maybe,” Tiam had said, grinning in the dark. “But it’s a good lie, right?”
And it was.
They didn’t have real beds—just frame cots and thin mats—but it didn’t matter. The bugs outside hummed like lullabies, and the air carried the mixed scent of burned grass and soap. Every night they were tired enough to forget whatever was supposed to come next.
Jimzon remembered the time Tiam stole extra snacks from the commissary—only a handful of protein wafers, but back then it felt like a feast. He didn’t keep them for himself, either. Behind the latrine wall, he passed them out: one to Jimzon, one to Dren, even one to Salla, who usually refused anything that broke regulation. They ate in silence, stifling laughter, terrified someone would hear the crinkle of the wrappers. The wafers were dry, almost flavorless, but that night they tasted like contraband gold. Tiam had loved stealing snacks—not for the food, but for the thrill, and he got really good at it. “Pressure makes diamonds,” he’d always say.
There was the time he swapped the drill sergeant’s whistle for one he’d carved from a bar of soap. The first time the sergeant blew it, the pitiful wheeze it made broke the entire platoon. They all paid for it with extra drills, running until their legs shook, but Tiam never confessed. He just smiled—smug, secretive, and proud—every lap of the way.
Tiam reminded Jimzon of Powitt… or at least of the brother he imagined Powitt might have been. He felt like a younger brother, but also an equal—someone he could lean on and laugh with. As the years passed, they became inseparable.
When they turned sixteen, they were deployed to a small garrison to stand guard in an occupied city. It shouldn't have been any real danger.
The town on Resmole was small, nestled along a river between the mountains. At nighttime, local militias would emerge to take potshots from the cover of darkness, though their bullets never found their mark. The gunfire used to jolt Tiam and Jimzon awake those first few nights, but they'd grown accustomed to it. Even the distant crack of rifles had become familiar—a reminder that danger lurked somewhere else, somewhere farther away. During daylight hours, the militias retreated into their tunnels, leaving the streets eerily quiet.
On one particularly dull evening, the two young soldiers sat guard by the gate, sharing a greyleaf cigarette. Jimzon had settled himself near the munitions crates, methodically scraping dried mud from his boots with a knife blade. The routine had become second nature.
Then came the sharp crack of a bullet striking a potted plant on a third-story rooftop. The ceramic shattered, sending the plant tumbling to the street below in a cascade of soil and broken stems.
Neither of them flinched.
Jimzon barely raised an eyebrow as he took another slow drag from his cigarette, watching the dirt settle in the sunset light.
"Dude must really hate gardening," Tiam said.
"Hell, I would too if I lived in a desert. Can't grow shit here." Jimzon flicked ash into the street.
"Hey Jimzy..." Tiam's voice shifted, losing its casual edge. "I think I saw something move on that ridge."
Jimzon lazily glanced toward the mountain. "Up there?"
"Yeah... just like a shadow moving or something."
"Probably just the wind shaking a cactus. If it was a crag, he's probably already scurrying back to his tunnel." Jimzon returned to scraping his boots.
"Well... I don't know about you, but I'd like to find out."
"Find out?" Jimzon looked up. "You gonna walk over there?"
"Won't have to." Tiam pointed smugly toward the clock tower looming above them.
"We're on post. Don't bother."
"We're also soldiers. You think they gave us guns just to look pretty?" Tiam was already checking his rifle strap.
"You're just gonna get written up again, man. You're a shit shot anyway." Jimzon laughed, knowing Tiam wouldn't listen.
"Captain's away for the weekend. If anyone asks where I went, just say I had to take a leak."
Jimzon peered up at the darkened mountain ridge, then back at his friend. "I don't know, man."
"C'mon. Just a peek. I've got my scope sighted for that distance anyway."
"Just a peek?"
"Just a peek."
Jimzon looked around at the empty town, weighing the boredom against the risk. "Alright, let's just be quick about it."
They slung their rifles and pushed through a wooden door leading into the clock tower. Jimzon followed a few steps behind as they climbed the narrow stairs, their boots echoing in the confined space. At the top floor, Tiam slid open the porch door and dropped to one knee, mounting his rifle on the ledge.
"Anything?"
Tiam squinted through his scope, adjusting the focus. "Yeah... it's not clear, but I think I see the Crag..."
"You see him? Let's call it in."
"No need..." Tiam said, fine-tuning his scope with practiced fingers.
"You said just a quick peek."
"I also said we carry guns for a reason." He settled deeper into his shooting position, eye pressed to the scope.
"Tiam, dude, let's call it in. Just get down from there."
"Wait... I think I—"
The crack split the night air.
Tiam's body crumpled, rifle clattering beside him. A single shot. Clean. He was gone before he hit the floor—no scream, no last words, just the awful sound of flesh meeting steel and wood.
Jimzon didn't move for what felt like hours. No follow-up shots came. No enemy appeared. No alert was raised. It wasn't an ambush or a raid. It wasn't anything at all—just one moment of perfect, senseless accuracy in the dark.
In the days that followed, Jimzon blamed himself for not yanking Tiam down from that ledge. Part of him blamed Tiam for being so reckless, for always pushing boundaries. Maybe it had been inevitable—maybe Tiam was always going to get himself killed.
But in the quiet moments, Jimzon knew the truth: neither of them had been properly trained for this kind of engagement. They were sixteen-year-old kids playing at being soldiers, and maybe it wasn't anyone's fault. Not his, not Tiam's, not even the shooter's. 
Kids shouldn't be in battlezones anyway.
Now, years later, Jimzon sighed and found himself staring down into the abyss by the elder tree. It had been a while since he'd thought about Tiam.
"Hope you're not thinking of singing your solo tonight." The shaman's friendly voice carried from behind him.
Jimzon turned around with a polite smile. "Not today." He chuckled softly. "There's a lot of work to do."
"You are still leaving tonight?"
"I am. If all goes well, I'd like to come back."
"I'd like that too, but that is not your calling."
"Yeah. I figured." His voice trailed off. "My friend who's awake—he's... not the same. He was talking about fate, and I don't know. I feel like fate's been kicking my ass."
"It may have given you beatings, but it has not won. You still continue, despite the pressure."
"Pressure makes diamonds," Jimzon said quietly.
The shaman nodded. "It does. Before we part ways, I wanted to give you a message."
"A message?" Jimzon straightened slightly. "Alright."
"The floomicide—it has bonded with you."
Jimzon's expression shifted, concern creeping in. "Bonded?"
"Yes. It flows in your bloodstream now."
"...Still? Fuck, I'm not still high right now, am I?"
"No, no. You are safe and present." The shaman's tone was reassuring. "Your mind is your own."
"So... is it a healing factor? Am I immune to pain?"
"Not that either. Perhaps a scratch will heal faster, but the juices of the floom—it's not a drug in the conventional sense."
Jimzon laughed nervously. "Are you sure? I was... quite high."
The shaman's voice grew focused, almost reverent. "That was no high. Your consciousness left your body for a moment."
"You're telling me"
"I am serious. I saw the tree you planted."
Jimzon furrowed his brows. "How did you see what I saw…?"
"It wasn't just me. My mentor saw it too."
"Mentor?" Jimzon rubbed his forehead. "Just tell me everything. This stuff always confuses me."
The shaman settled into explanation mode, his voice taking on the patient tone of a teacher. "As I was saying before, this planet shares a... a soul. The floom has been known on occasion to attach itself to individuals undergoing high stress. It may leave naturally, but sometimes it stays." He paused, studying Jimzon's face. "It seems to have taken a liking to you—it sees something in you. It is rare, but not uncommon."
"Okay..." Jimzon said slowly.
"During your vision, your consciousness vibrated quite intensely on its journey, and it woke up a few souls around it. That desert was a real place. One of many. There is a being that inhabits there—one that wishes to guide you, as it guided me."
Jimzon was quiet for a moment, processing. "That 'vision' was real intense. I don't know if I can go back, given what I have to do, or if I even want to. Maybe another time—I just have to stop an attack."
"That's why the being wants to speak with you. It wants to help you."
"How can it help if it's in another... dimension or plane of existence or whatever?"
"It can be met in the real world too. Its presence is what allowed your friend to wake up."
Jimzon sighed, the weight of everything pressing down on him. "Maybe I could use all the help I can get. Is he on Maspin?"
"Not quite, but I expect the truth will unnerve you."
"I can handle it, man, especially if it's a quick stop."
"The remnants of Wake."
Jimzon froze, staring at the shaman. "You're joking."
"I am not."
"Just have him—or it, or whatever—contact me now. Or you tell me what it says."
"It doesn't work that way. Your species is different than mine. Your body has to be close to the source."
"How am I even going to find it?"
"Just return home. The path will guide you."
"I can't do that." The words came out almost desperately, and Jimzon's hands started to shake. "I can't do that. Especially not now. I swore I'd never go back."
The shaman's voice softened. "You trusted the life of your friend and yourself in my hands. I will never ask for payment. Please know I come from wanting to help you."
Jimzon turned back to the abyss, leaning closer to its edge. "Do I have to?"
"Do you want to save Shayron?"
"Yes," Jimzon said, the word heavy with reluctance.
"Then first save yourself".

Chapter 31 

"That should be the last of it," Calsco said, dropping off a military crate as he walked up the ramp of their ship. He paused, looking over the vessel with a mixture of fondness and resignation. "She's a little beat up, but she'll have to do."
The ship rested on four slender landing struts, each no more than three feet tall, giving it the appearance of a graceful creature poised for flight. What caught the eye immediately was the central hull—no longer the gleaming metal it once was, but transformed into a perfect block of polished tree bark. The grain ran in tight, undulating lines across its boxy surface, each knot and ridge smoothed until the wood seemed to glow with an inner warmth.
The hull's design spoke to its cargo-hauling purpose, but the ship's front end curved into an unexpectedly elegant nose. A pilot window stretched across its width, thin and low like a sports car's visor—offering a panoramic view while maintaining the vessel's sleek profile. Above this organic transformation, the original tail fin and swept-back wings remained untouched, their dark alloys creating a striking contrast against the warm, honey-colored bark.
Small circular windows dotted the sides like a constellation, each porthole gleaming with soft azure light that seemed to breathe life into the wood. These drops of sky-blue radiance hinted at the vessel's inhabited nature, while the organic hull suggested something born rather than built.
Inside, the contrast was jarring. Every surface remained metal. Beige panels trimmed in the faded orange of the Trailblazers. Scuffs and rust marked the walls in places, but bright patches of paint from past crews still caught the eye, each splash of color telling a story of the ship's many owners. It was clearly a vessel that had changed hands numerous times, each crew leaving their mark, and despite the wear and weathering, an undeniable charm permeated every corner.
Crowning the entire structure was a massive vertical stabilizer—a thick, sail-like fin that dominated the ship's silhouette. This organic addition allowed for precise, almost aquatic steering, lending the craft the graceful lines of a manta ray gliding through cosmic depths rather than a mere cargo hauler grinding through space.
"Daiser, we looking good?" Calsco called out.
Daiser sat in the co-pilot seat, smokey fingers dancing across a control screen as he flicked several small switches in sequence. "Looking great. Won't need to refuel for a while." He leaned back with a satisfied nod. "Sucks we don't have hyperspace, but this ship still has some heat to it."
Pav, now wearing a light brown leather jacket with a fuzzy collar over his cargo pants, settled into the seat beside Daiser. "Anybody christen her yet?"
"They don't name their ships here," Calsco answered, "something about worldly attachments." He paused thoughtfully. "How about the Crystal Cutter?"
The team exchanged glances and shook their heads almost in unison.
"How about the Barked Beetle?" Daiser offered with a chuckle.
"Maybe" Jimzon pondered aloud. "How about the Rocksteady?"
Pav frowned. "I don't know about naming after people. I don't think we can afford any jinxes."
Calsco puffed out his chest with renewed confidence. "I got it—the Bloodfang."
"Hell nah," Daiser said immediately. "We need something low-key. Like... Masked Marauder." He paused, reconsidering. "Actually, on second thought, maybe not—"
His words were cut short as the masked Berji stepped inside the ship. The wooden mask seemed extra hollow today, casting deeper shadows across his features, but his new clothes certainly weren't hollow. He stood straight on the ramp, arms slightly extended.
"How do I look?" he asked.
It was Pav's blue royal prince disguise, only this time transformed completely. The fabric had been dyed a deep forest green with vibrant orange accents that caught the ship's interior lighting.
Jimzon groaned as he strapped himself in. "Please tell me you guys aren't running another royal con."
Calsco held up a placating hand. "No, not this time. Well, a little perhaps, but Daiser couldn't arouse any more suspicion packing up so many things from the council's temple. We had to reuse some material." He gestured toward Berji approvingly. "Your face is already hidden—we just play into the mystery. Shayron hosts all kinds of species, so a man dressed as strangely as him won't stand out nearly as much as he would anywhere else. Nobody will suspect him as a Guard deserter who survived a ship crash. He's just some exotic foreigner."
	"Whatever, man." Jimzon shrugged. "Berji, for what it's worth, it looks clean on you."
"I must pass the thanks to Daiser, for he had sewn and dyed it in the colors of my homeworld. I am the thankful one," Berji said with a slight bow.
Daiser's eyes scrunched, then brightened with mock surprise. "About time I get some thanks around here," he joked, returning to the controls.
"Is anyone going to follow us?" Jimzon asked, his tone turning serious. "Since, you know... you're a slave we're taking?"
"They couldn't keep up even if they wanted to," Calsco replied confidently, "but nah, we're good."
"And everyone here is in on the plan?"
Nods all around.
"Alright, we'll figure out the details on the way. We just have to make a pit stop first."
"A pit stop?" Pav's voice carried a note of concern. "We only have a few weeks."
"I know, I know, but this isn't leisure."
"Is it on the way?" Daiser asked, his smoky hands adjusting controls with practiced ease.
"Wakeful Islands?"
"The what fucking islands?" Daiser's hands froze mid-adjustment.
"Sorry, uh... the remnants of Wake."
Daiser let out a low whistle. "Remnants is a funny way to put it. That planet got blown to shit by the Autumnals decades ago."
"There are still a few docks we can harbor at," Jimzon insisted.
"Docks?" Daiser turned in his seat. "Dude, it's all asteroids now. It's not gonna be easy even getting there."
Pav's voice dropped to something gentler. "Jim... why do you want to go back? I thought you promised to never go back?"
Jimzon was quiet for a moment before answering. "The shaman said I had to go back and find this alien… guy. He'll help us stop it. Somehow". His skepticism was obvious.
Daiser's hands went completely still on the controls. "The shaman wants you to meet Kadam?"
"You know him?"
"I don't even know if he's real," Daiser admitted. "Shamans gatekeep a lot about him, but they still want us to paint sculptures of him and make art."
"Hey, wait," Pav interjected, "the doors of the council have an engraving of Kadam, right? He's like... handing the sun to Maspins?"
"Yeah, it's a metaphor for knowledge or power or something," Daiser nodded. "Supposedly, all the shamans learn their mysticism from him." He turned back to Jimzon with genuine curiosity. "But why you? No offense, but usually it's other hippie types they send on these kinds of missions."
"I honestly have no idea, man. Those islands are where I grew up and watched my family die. It's not something I'm keen on seeing again."
"How old are you even, man? Wake got blown up like forty years ago."
"My father and some other townsfolk used to mine fuel from the remnants," Jimzon explained quietly.
"Oh, I see what you mean now." Daiser nodded with understanding. "Yeah, that corefuel gig must've been nice work."
Calsco's ears perked up at the mention of money. "Corefuel? Mind elaborating?"
"Yeah, all seven of the Tier 1 planets have this hyper-potent fuel," Daiser continued. "It's what made Wake a superpower back in the day. My condolences, by the way, to all you guys."
Pav and Jimzon nodded solemnly, while Berji turned to stare out the window in silence.
"All?" Calsco raised an eyebrow. "Pav, aren't you from Marfeld?"
Pav scrunched his eyebrows. "Yeah, but we're all Wakari in some way."
"I'm a proud Katanzarian, boys. Not a Waker," Calsco declared with his chest puffed out.
Daiser stared at him for a moment. "Buddy... you do know all humans descend from Wake, right?"
For the first time anyone could remember, Calsco looked genuinely flustered. "We can't be sure of that. Many humans are natives to many planets."
"Dude, that's literally where your species evolved before spreading out. Yeah, it was forever ago, but that's like me losing Maspin."
"No." Calsco's voice turned defensive. "Neighboring planets evolved simultaneously. We're a near-perfect design—it was bound to happen."
"Dude, miss me with that human exceptionalism bullshit," Daiser shot back. "Your 'design' is far from perfect. Next thing I know, you'll call me a Crag." He gestured between himself and Calsco. "Look at me and look at yourself. I'm made of vapor and water, you're flesh and bone. That's what evolution does. You and Jimzon are the same."
"We... are not the same," Calsco said stiffly.
Jimzon's voice carried an edge. "What's that supposed to mean?"
"You and your friends—you are short, you worry too much. I am a man of big stature in both body, heart, mind, and soul. It is why I am so successful, and you are not. Not yet I mean to say"
"Good to know he looks down on everyone equally, I guess," Daiser muttered.
Berji's voice suddenly filled the cockpit, the acoustics of his wooden mask somehow amplifying every word. "Comrades, our sense of self is not tied to our bodies. Grace and malice can manifest in any host—our minds are merely satellites for consciousness. Understand this, and everything else will make sense."
A silence settled over the group.
Daiser broke it with a sigh. "You sound like my dad. Yeah, yeah, yeah, 'why don't we all just get along.'" He turned back to the controls. "Let's just get going. Everyone strapped in?"
The sound of seatbelts clicking filled the cockpit.
"Hey, wait a second," Jimzon called out. "You said you never left planet. When did you learn to fly?"
"I've read a book or two," Daiser replied nonchalantly. "Punching it."
He activated the thrusters, and the ship began to rise, landing gear retracting with mechanical precision.
"Hey, wait a moment, if—" Jimzon started.
"Punching it!" Daiser announced again, shoving the controls forward.
Jimzon's hand shot out for the emergency handle as the ship lurched into acceleration into the sky.

END OF ACT 2

(help me make this encounter seem spiritual yet welcoming, otherworldy yet caring



















The winds of the desert carried with them a familiar scent of petrichor and burnt sage, weaving through the sand like invisible fingers. The air felt static, charged with potential energy, as if the very atoms were holding their breath before a thunderstorm—yet the purple auroral sky stretched endlessly clear.
"Avaris…" the voice drifted on the wind, not from any direction but from everywhere at once.
Jimzon clenched his jaw. "That's me."
"Am I pronouncing that right, my friend?" The voice seemed to emanate from the sand itself, warm and patient.
"It's… close enough. Who are you?" Jimzon turned slowly, scanning the empty dunes.
"I have known no name as you understand them, but my people are called Kaspian. I understand you are struggling—internally and externally. I would like to help."
"Then why are you hiding?"
"Be not afraid. Reach beneath the sand before you. There is something waiting."
Jimzon's hand instinctively moved toward his pistol, but something in the voice—a gentleness that reminded him of his mother's lullabies—stayed his hand. He knelt and reached down, fingers sinking into the warm sand until they touched something smooth and cold. As he pulled, sand cascaded away like liquid starlight, revealing a weathered skull that seemed to pulse with its own inner light.
"Is this… your body?"
"It was. I am still tethered to it."
The skull lifted from his palms, floating as threads of purple fire began to weave around it—not consuming but defining, like aurora made solid. The flame didn't burn; it danced with the rhythm of a heartbeat, casting no shadows yet somehow illuminating everything.
"I know I'm unfamiliar with how things work here, but where I come from, skulls aren't always a good sign."
"Be not afraid." A quiet bolt of lightning descended from the purple sky, moving like honey rather than striking. It touched the floating skull with the gentleness of a blessing, and instead of thunder, the sound that followed was deep and resonant—like a ceremonial drum echoing across vast distances.
Slowly, deliberately, a translucent form began to phase into existence around the skull. The body was tall and graceful, made of shifting starlight and memories, with the flaming skull visible like a sacred heart within its chest. Where its eyes should have been, two points of warm golden light regarded Jimzon with infinite patience.
"Better?" Kaspian's voice now came from the luminous figure, though his lips—if they could be called lips—never moved.

Brynor stepped closer too, his playful expression shifting to genuine worry. "Seriously, Kels. If you're sick, you need to report it. We can't have you passing out in the cockpit."
The concern in his voice—the same voice that had asked her out twice in the past month, always with that hopeful smile she hadn't been able to bring herself to crush—made her stomach twist even tighteFr.
"I'm not sick," she said, more sharply than intended. "I just need some sleep, okay? You guys go have fun."
The three of them exchanged glances. Torres raised an eyebrow. "Since when do you turn down free drinks?"
"Since never," Hada added. "Come on, one beer. We'll toast to our extended weekend and then you can go crash."
Kelsa looked at their faces—people who'd had her back through combat missions, bad breakups, hangovers, and everything in between. People who trusted her completely. 
"Alright," she heard herself say. "One beer."

JAX CHAPTER
Jax checked his hollow watch for the third time. Three hours. Three hours since he had been escorted to this dimly lit interrogation room, and his patience was wearing thin. These Military Police guys were fine usually, that is when they weren’t busy brown-nosing every general or commander within sniffing range. Nothing wrong with going by the books, but they by the books and then some. Citations for everything: improper posture, a loose thread on a uniform, wrong adhesives used in the barracks. Once, they even dinged him for mud on a dropship after a mission upon his return.
He snorted. The Guard probably needed people like them—professional snitches who thought citing regulations was akin to saving lives. Heroes in their own heads. Jax, meanwhile, he had seen combat, had bled for his missions. These MPs? Paper-pushers in fatigues.
To most, the sterile interrogation room was intimidating. For Jax, it was a formality. They called it a “hearing” to put the fear of God—or Command—into you, but nine times out of ten, it was about issuing an infraction. Which in reality was just a warning, wrapped in Guardspeak. You could appeal, sure, but those expired faster than field rations anyway, isn't worth the time. It was all about protocol, giving them plausible deniability for following "the process." Just another bump in the internal PR machine.
If he had to guess, Pavlock was going to get a shitload of paperwork. That cartel raid with Nardow, mistaking his grenades for a flashbang was a rookie fuck up that lead to a corporal getting hurt. Honestly things could've gone worse, but the medbay patched him up within an hour anyway. Still, Pavlock’s slip-up was going to land on someone’s desk. Jax decided he’d cover for him. They'll probably try and ask about any other protocols he broke, but nothings worth mentioning if the job got done.
The metallic clunk of the door unlocking snapped him out of his thoughts. Two MPs strode in, their boots thudding against the tile like an offbeat drum. Sergeant Cotts and Sergeant Aren.
Cotts had a sharpness to her—gray hair, with a no-nonsense aura that dared you to try her patience. She had the kind of pride that didn’t bend, even when it should. She may have been tall, but was always known to be short with people. Aren, on the other hand, was forgettable. Silent, meek, the type who’d melt into the background if not for the uniform. A real pencil pusher, unremarkable in everyway.
Jax suppressed a smirk. Cotts and Aren—pilot school dropouts turned MPs. Seeing them again was almost amusing, like running into old classmates who never made it past the basics. Of course, now they held the power, or at least liked to think they did.
“Cotts!” Jax exclaimed, his tone warm and friendly, the kind of faux cheer that could grease the wheels in situations like this. “Congratulations on the promotion! I can’t believe you’re a Sergeant now.”
In truth, he couldn’t care less. But if pandering bullshit was the language these people spoke, it couldn’t hurt to give it a try. He stood, extending a hand in mock camaraderie.
Cotts approached, a friendly smile plastered across her face. But as she closed the gap, her demeanor shifted. Without warning, she delivered a sucker punch straight to his jaw. The impact sent Jax sprawling, his chair screeching across the floor before tipping over.
Dazed, he paused, the metallic tang of blood filling his mouth. Then he rolled his neck, stretching it with a slow, deliberate crack. His brows furrowed as he glared up at her. “What the fuck was that for, you bitch?” he growled, the forced civility in his voice stripped away.
The fake, dead-eyed smile was gone from Cotts’ face too, replaced by something sharper, crueler. “I’m a fucking lieutenant,” Jax snapped, wiping the blood from his nose with the back of his hand. “Let’s not forget who gives the fucking write-ups here.”
Cotts didn’t flinch. Instead, she tilted her head slightly, almost like she was savoring the moment. “Sit up,” she ordered, her voice cold, the barest hint of a sadistic grin tugging at her lips.
Jax side-eyed her as he sat up, trying to pierce through her mask and read what was going on in her head. Nothing. Just that same flat, unreadable expression. He grabbed his chair, lifted it upright, and lowered himself into it. Slowly. Deliberately. His hands rested on the table, fingers tapping in quiet frustration.
“Where are they?” Cotts asked, her tone utterly devoid of warmth, her expression carved in stone.
Jax raised an eyebrow, irritation sparking in his eyes. He looked around the room as if this were some absurd joke, his lips curling into a half-sneer. “Who? Where are who?” he shot back, his voice dripping with disbelief.
“Aren, hold his chair,” Cotts ordered, her tone clipped and commanding.
Without hesitation, Aren stepped behind Jax, planting her hands firmly on the back of the chair. The metal creaked as she forced it steady, her grip ironclad.
Before Jax could react, Cotts delivered another sucker punch, this time with calculated force. Her fist collided with his jaw, snapping his head to the side.
Jax’s face burned—not with pain, but with seething anger. His blood felt like it was simmering just below the surface, a tea kettle ready to whistle. He took a deep breath, tasting copper. “What the fuck are you guys talking about?” he growled, his voice low and venomous. “You’re the ones who dragged me in here. Just tell me what you want to know.”
Cotts and Aren stared at him, their faces carved from stone, their silence deliberate and maddening.
The room felt heavier with every passing second, the stillness pressing down like a weight on Jax’s chest. His patience snapped. “Is this about Pavlock? Or another bullshit write-up?” His voice rose, the sharp edge unmistakable. “Did you two forget how to run a hearing? The same way you forgot which thrusters to use back in flight school?”
Their eyes flickered at the insult, but neither moved. For a moment, Jax thought he saw Cotts’ jaw tighten, but she quickly masked it.
Fine, he thought. If they were going to play this game, so would he. The second this was over, he’d go straight to Liles and get these assholes canned.
Cotts and Aren moved in tandem, pulling out chairs and sitting across from him. Cotts leaned forward, resting her forearms on the cold metal table, her gaze drilling into him. “Do you know how often this mothership has enlistees go AWOL?”
Jax narrowed his eyes. “Not for decades.”
Cotts nodded slowly, her lips pressed into a thin line. She let the silence stretch, as if daring him to squirm.
“So?” Jax prompted, his voice laced with defiance. His eyes darted between them, searching for some clue, some hint of what the hell they were getting at.
The tension was suffocating, the kind that made time feel elastic. Every second stretched longer than it should.
“Just tell us where Jimzon is,” Cotts said, her voice like steel, cutting through the thick air of the room.
Jax scoffed, leaning back in his chair with a mock incredulity that didn’t quite mask his rising annoyance. “Are you guys so brain-dead you forgot to page him? If he didn’t answer, the dude’s probably passed out in his room, sleeping it off.”
Before he could finish, Aren stood up so abruptly her chair toppled backward with a loud screech against the tile. The sharp sound made Jax wince, though he quickly masked it with a smirk that didn’t reach his eyes.
Aren leaned in, her tone authoritative but oddly measured, as if trying to coax a confession. “This morning,” she began, her eyes boring into him, “seven enlistees deserted the mothership. Seven, Lieutenant. Almost all of them connect back to you.”
She paused, letting the words sink in. “Once? Maybe we could chalk it up to coincidence. But seven? Seven? We know there’s something you’re not telling us.”
Jax’s cocky demeanor faltered. His smirk faded, replaced by a slow, dawning realization. Whatever he said now, whether he knew something or not, he was screwed. His chest tightened, and his eyes darted around the room, searching for anything to anchor himself, to make sense of this sudden shift in reality.
“Jimzon?” he repeated, his voice quieter now, almost disbelieving. “Is that who left? I mean... sure, the guy was going through some shit, but I thought that was resolved or whatever.”
Cotts leaned forward now, her elbows on the table, her stare unrelenting. “Going through it?” she repeated, her tone biting. “Mind elaborating on that, Lieutenant?”
Jax let out a long sigh, running a hand through his hair. “Yeah. He broke up with his girl, Kelsa. Started, I don’t know... moping. Then he found a lead on these Crags—alien fucks who killed his family—and we helped him track them down. Wiped them out. After that, he seemed relaxed, like he’d finally blown off some steam. Took the captain promotion, got himself a slick new ride. That’s that.”
“That’s that?” Cotts echoed, her voice laced with sarcasm. “Funny story, Lieutenant, because Kelsa’s AWOL with him.”
Jax blinked, his mouth opening slightly before snapping shut again. “What?” he said, his voice cracking just enough to betray his unease. “Look, I’ll share whatever the fuck you need—just tell me what’s going on. I’m sure there’s some kind of misunderstanding.”
Cotts and Aren exchanged a glance, their expressions unreadable, before Cotts leaned back in her chair. The silence that followed felt like a vice, squeezing Jax from all sides.
“Stop me if anything’s wrong,” Cotts began, her voice icy and precise, slicing through the air like a blade. “Two pilots in your squadron—Kelsa Cray and Cillus Wole—as well as your entire battle squad—Jimzon Avaris, Yakib Yoss, and Berji Monvern—along with an accountant, coordinated the theft of an armored transport cruiser. Not just any cruiser—one carrying valuables worth the yearly salary of entire departments.”
She leaned forward, her eyes locked onto his. “And during their escape, they shot down and killed Pilot Tavish.”
Jax’s breath hitched.
Cotts didn’t stop. “You have close connections with every single one of them—through the battlefield, the academy, and your off-duty hours. So close, in fact…” Her voice dropped slightly as she gestured toward his forearm. “I can see the Castle Team insignia inked on your skin.”
Jax instinctively glanced at the tattoo, his stomach twisting into a knot. A cold bead of sweat traced a line down his temple. His chest tightened as Cotts’ words hammered into him, one after another. The insignia, once a proud symbol of brotherhood, now felt like a brand—a mark of treachery.
This doesn’t make sense, his thoughts raced. Jimzon? Kill a Guardsman? No way. He wouldn’t even loot after missions. And Kelsa? She cheated on him—why the hell would he run off with her? Berji’s contract was almost up, and Pav had that engineering internship lined up. And Yake? He couldn’t even handle the anxiety of smoking redleaf, let alone being on the run.
Why the fuck did no one tell me? I thought we were close. He looked again at his tattoo, the ink staring back at him like a mocking reminder. Real close.
Am I next? They know I’m no snitch. Right?
“Well?” Cotts leaned in, her eyes narrowing.
Jax opened his mouth, but the words wouldn’t come. His throat felt dry, his chest constricted, the walls closing in. “I… I don’t know. I—”
Cotts slammed her palm against the table, making him flinch. “Aren,” she snapped, her voice sharp enough to cut through his stammering. “Bag him.”
Aren was on her feet in an instant, yanking a black hood from her belt. She shoved it over Jax’s head, her movements quick and methodical. The world went dark as the coarse fabric scratched against his skin.
Through the muffling hood, Aren’s voice reached him, low and venomous. “We’ll make an example out of all of you.”
Cotts didn’t hesitate. Jax heard the unmistakable click of a holster, then the electric crackle of a taser springing to life.
“No—wait!” Jax shouted, his voice muffled, panicked.
Cotts ignored him. The taser plunged into his neck, sending a searing jolt through his body. His muscles locked up as an agonized scream tore from his throat, echoing in the confined room.

Jax Chapter 2
Jax’s black hood was ripped off with a soft tug, and immediately, his eyes flinched against the dim, flickering light that stung his senses. His head throbbed, and his vision swam in the low-lit haze. For a few moments, he was disoriented, caught in a fog of confusion. But there was something undeniable in the air, a nauseating stench. It hit him like a physical blow, a rancid mixture of charred, burnt flesh and something else, something sharp and acrid, like the damp, decaying smell of wet cement. Blood, thick and sticky, filled his nostrils, suffocating his already overwhelmed senses.
His hands, bound tight with coarse rope, ached from the pressure as he tried to move them. His body felt like a stiff statue, every muscle tense and uncooperative. His legs were similarly bound, and the moment he tried to step forward, he nearly lost his balance. His head spun with vertigo, his vision swimming in and out of focus. The world seemed to tilt, his neck straining as he fought to steady himself.
"Hey, buddy," a calm voice broke through the fog, low and soothing, yet with a strange edge to it. "You still alive in there?"
Jax’s blurry gaze focused slowly, fixing on the source of the voice. A man stood nearby, his face caked with layers of grime, deep grooves carved into his features as if time itself had etched its harsh mark. He looked young, but the lines on his face told a different story—his skin, rough and weathered, made him seem a decade older than he probably was.
"Buddy?" the man asked again, a hint of mockery in his voice.
Jax hung his head, allowing it to droop forward. He took a slow, labored breath, savoring the air—no, it wasn’t fresh, not by a long shot, but it was a reprieve from the suffocating black hood that had constricted his chest for so long. He let his mouth hang open, feeling drool slip from his lips as he relaxed, briefly lost in the odd comfort of the air filling his lungs.
"They were rough on you, I see," the man observed, his tone matter-of-fact.
Jax spat out a mouthful of blood, his tongue thick in his mouth. "Been worse."
The man offered a dry chuckle, shaking his head. "Oh? I think you’ll fit right in here then." He moved toward Jax, his hands deftly working the ropes that bound him. The ropes scraped against Jax’s skin, leaving angry red marks as the man untied him. Jax rubbed at his raw wrists, grimacing at the sting.
As his eyes began to focus, he took in his surroundings with slow, deliberate movements. The room was primitive—crumbling cement bricks lined the walls, their surfaces rough and jagged. Iron bars stretched across the small window, casting long, uneven shadows onto the floor. A few candles flickered weakly, their flames barely enough to push back the shadows, creating an eerie, almost dungeonesque atmosphere. There was no technology, no comforts of any kind. It was as if time itself had forgotten this place.
"Where is this?" Jax muttered hoarsely, his voice still thick with confusion.
The man gave a hollow laugh, leaning back against the cold stone wall. "Well, that's a good question. A black site, maybe. But ‘where’ doesn’t matter much when you’re this far removed from everything. You should be asking ‘what’ this place is."
Jax blinked, the fog of his mind beginning to clear. "Alright, fine. What is this place?"
The man shrugged, his eyes flickering with a hard, resigned edge. “It's a dumping ground. A place where they toss everyone they don’t feel like shooting outright—pirates, tax evaders, prisoners, anyone they want to break. People with muscle usually that they can work to death.”
The words hit Jax like a cold slap to the face. The reality of the situation began to set in, a knot forming in his stomach. Denial was fading quickly, leaving him with an unsettling, growing sense of helplessness. He fought to suppress the rising panic, but it threatened to break through.
"Who are you?" Jax finally asked, his voice quieter than before, more wary.
The man studied him for a moment before answering, a grim smirk playing at the corner of his lips. “Name’s Canreel. They got me for conspiracy to sedition—some bullshit charge a few years back.”
Jax frowned, struggling to process the words. "Conspiracy for sedition? What? That isn’t even a real thing."
Canreel’s eyes flickered with something like amusement, though it was tinged with bitterness. "Yeah, I’d say that too. Was hanging with the wrong crowd I suppose— real big mouths, said too much. They grabbed me and tossed me in here to rot."
Jax’s brow furrowed, his body stiffening as he absorbed the man's words. He rubbed his eyes, feeling the weight of the situation settling heavily on his shoulders, like a nightmare that wouldn't end. His whole body felt sluggish, drained of energy.
Canreel tilted his head slightly, sizing Jax up. "And you? What's your story? Should I be worried about being cellmates with you?"
Jax let out a long, weary breath, his gaze distant. “No. There’s been some kind of mix-up. I’m a pilot. I shouldn’t be here.”
Canreel raised an eyebrow, his mouth twisting into a knowing smirk. “Well, that’s one hell of a mix-up if it landed you in a place like this. How long is your sentence?”
Jax’s laugh was short, bitter. “Sentence? I haven’t even had a trial yet. I don’t even know what the hell is going on.”
Canreel’s expression darkened, his voice dropping a notch. “Oh, son, if you're here, you’re not getting a trial. Forget about the past. It's easier that way.”
Jax looked down at his tattoo, the one on his arm—an insignia, a mark of his service. His fingers brushed over the design, the symbol of his identity, his pride. But now it felt like a curse. Forget the past? How could he forget? That tattoo was everything he was. It was his story.
“Forget the past?” Jax muttered, his voice distant, conflicted.
Canreel’s gaze turned sharper, almost paternal. “Your tattoo... You said you were a pilot. But not a Guard Pilot, right?"
Jax’s brow furrowed. "So what if I was?"
"Son, you better hide that mark," Canreel warned, his tone serious now. “Every prisoner here would want your head for it. And the guards? They don't lift a finger unless you’re trying to escape.”
Jax’s stomach churned. “Shit...” he muttered, the weight of the words sinking in.
Canreel leaned closer, his voice dropping to a conspiratorial whisper. “As luck would have it, I have use for a pilot.”
Jax’s interest piqued despite himself. “What do you mean?”
Canreel’s eyes gleamed with a dark excitement. “About thirty years ago, The Guard's accountants figured something out—how to cut costs in a big way. Instead of paying workers minimum wage, they started filling the prisons with bodies. Pay nothing, work them to death, and funnel all that money into the war machine and keep the profits churning. This place was supposed to house a couple hundred. Now? We’ve got a few thousand. And it’s growing every day. In truth, you'd expect more but dozens die from beatings or starvation every day.”
“You said you have a use for a pilot?” Jax asked, his voice hoarse, the dry rasp of his throat scraping against his words.
Canreel’s expression darkened, an almost predatory glint in his eyes as he leaned in closer. “Tomorrow, we escape. It’s been years in the making—meticulously planned. My men know their part. Our current pilot? He's been out of the cockpit for far too long. Better if we let him stay as a gunner and let you take the reins.”
Jax’s heart hammered in his chest, a surge of adrenaline racing through his veins despite the exhaustion pulling at his every muscle. His brow furrowed. “Tell me more.”
Canreel’s lips twitched into a tight smile, though it was more a shadow than a true expression of joy. His eyes flickered toward the dimly flickering candlelight, casting soft shadows across the cell. “I will, tonight,” he said, his voice softening for a brief moment, as if savoring a secret. “Rest time’s almost over,” he added, glancing up at the old, rusted clock ticking above the narrow door, its second hand creaking with each beat, its rhythm the only sound in the room other than their breathing. “It’s almost work time.”
Jax blinked, his mind still struggling to grasp the gravity of Canreel's words. His limbs ached from the strain of being bound, and every breath felt like a battle. He had little energy left, but something inside him, something deep and primal, ignited in response to the mention of escape. His eyes darted back to the door, to the faint light flickering from the crack beneath it. For a moment, he wondered if it was the light of freedom—or the light of a deeper hell.
“Work time?” Jax asked, his voice barely above a whisper, his throat tight as if the words didn’t quite belong in his mouth. “What’s it like out there?”
At that, Canreel’s smile faded into something darker, something resigned. He didn’t answer right away, instead looking at Jax with a mixture of pity and steel in his gaze. The man exhaled deeply, his shoulders sagging for just a moment as though the weight of this place was finally sinking in on him. Then, with a quiet chuckle, he muttered, “You’ll find out soon enough.”
Before Jax could respond, the air shifted. A loud, mechanical buzz sliced through the thick, oppressive silence. The low hum of the buzzer felt like it was vibrating through Jax’s chest, the noise scraping against the inside of his skull, sending a jolt of anxiety straight through his spine. A metallic clang followed, and the door creaked open with a groan that echoed throughout the narrow room, like the sound of a coffin lid slowly being lifted.
The prison yard outside was bathed in dim, yellowish light, the dust in the air hanging thick like smoke. It felt distant, detached, like a world Jax had no part in—a place where time didn’t matter, where life and death blurred into one endless, grinding misery. He stood frozen for a moment, eyes locked on the yawning doorway. The shuffling shadows outside seemed to stretch unnaturally long, like the dark was trying to reach inside and swallow him whole.
Canreel stepped toward the threshold, his movements slow, deliberate. He glanced back over his shoulder, his eyes narrowed but his lips pulling into that same tight smile. “Guess you’ll find out,” he said, his voice low, like a secret shared only with the walls.
With a slow, heavy breath, Jax followed.

Jax Chapter 3	

The courtyard stretched into a haze of uncertainty, shrouded in a mist-like veil that played tricks on the mind. It felt both endless and oppressively claustrophobic, as though the air itself conspired to suffocate hope. But it wasn’t just mist—it was ash, drifting lazily from the smoky expanse of the sky. Each breath carried the taste of charred remnants, settling in Jax’s throat, lining his esophagus, then lungs with a permanent reminder of the world he now inhabited.
Dim orange floodlights cast a sickly glow over the prisoners as they shuffled out of their cells, their movements lethargic, resigned. Beneath their feet, the ground was a patchwork of dry, cracked cement. Crude yellow lines, chipped and faded, delineated zones with an indifference that mirrored the prison's cold functionality.
The walls loomed into the sky, a seamless gray monolith that stretched stories into the air, unmarred by cracks or seams that might offer a foothold. Escape seemed impossible, and yet Canreen had plotted one. How? Jax’s eyes drifted toward the central watchtower, a three-story structure crowned with grimy windows.
Up there, silhouettes of guards moved—faceless enforcers clad in teal-blue suits, their black masks erasing any trace of humanity. Jax’s own uniform chafed against his skin, a tight orange jumpsuit that screamed captivity. Around him, the other inmates wore the same, a sea of orange against the muted grays and smoldering sky.

The intercom crackled to life with a buzz that seemed to cut through the silence like a razor.

"ROLL CALL BEGINS IN 5 MINUTES. LOTTERY COMMENCES IN 10 MINUTES."

The words repeated, looping like a broken record, but no one seemed to care. It was just another part of the noise in this place.
Jax’s gaze swept the yard, trying to push the fear down into his gut. The air was thick with sweat, rust, and the stench of decay—decay that wasn’t just physical, but spiritual. The inmates emerged from the shadows, shuffling forward in staggered steps, some dragging their feet like they were tethered to the ground by invisible chains. Each one a different horror.
There was a thin, spindly creature at the edge of the group, its skin an angry red, as if the blood inside it had been boiled to the surface. Its body was covered in sharp, crustaceous plates that clicked together with every movement, a chilling sound, like stone scraping against stone. Another inmate—a massive behemoth, shoulders wider than two men put together—moved with an unnatural fluidity, his skin dark as charcoal and textured like cracked leather. His eyes were sunken, black pits, giving nothing away. And then there was the one made of rock. It wasn’t just the shape, or the weight—no, it was as if his entire body had turned to stone. Every movement was slow, deliberate, as if it was too much effort to even lift his arms.
Jax tried to melt into the crowd. He pulled his sleeve down, pushed his shoulders back, and forced his eyes to stay ahead. But the harder he tried to blend in, the more out of place he felt. His skin was too smooth, too soft. His face was still a reflection of someone who hadn’t been through the years of torment the others had. His clean-shaven chin, his short-trimmed mustache—hell, even his tattoo was a glaring mistake. A guard’s insignia. A target.
They crammed into the center, pushed up against one another in a twisted, uncomfortable circle. The sounds of breathing, low curses muttered between chapped lips. The air was thick with desperation, with hopelessness. The hollow looks in their eyes were the only things that stood out—empty, lifeless. They weren’t alive anymore. Just bodies going through the motions, waiting for whatever fate awaited them.
He could feel them watching him. Hundreds of eyes—some dull and hollow, others sharp and predatory—skimming over him like scavengers sizing up a fresh carcass. But one gaze felt like a dagger pressed between his shoulder blades, cold and deliberate. A Targath.
The creature wasn’t hard to spot. Even in the dim, sour light, its hulking form stood out—a grotesque mix of glistening blue gelatin and a jagged, insect-like exoskeleton. Its translucent core seemed to pulse with an inner rhythm, a nauseating throb that made Jax think of a beating heart pulled out of a chest. The single, kaleidoscopic eye wrapped around its head, face shifted and spun, reflecting fractured shards of color that almost looked beautiful—if you ignored the serrated mandibles twitching beneath it, like they were tasting the air. Its claws—massive, chitinous scythes—clinked softly as it moved, dragging its bulk forward with a slow, deliberate gait that screamed patience. Hunger.
Jax knew all about Targaths. Too much, in fact. His first deployment had been a bombing run on one of their jungle colonies, a planet so dense with life it looked like the rainforest had swallowed it whole. He’d read the dossiers, memorized the details. Targaths didn’t just kill; they dismantled. They burrowed underground, feeling for the vibrations of their prey. Once they locked on, they would erupt from the earth, claws tearing into flesh, mandibles ripping chunks from bone. Victims didn’t die quickly; they were consumed, piece by piece, their screams muffled by the weight of soil and claw.

And they loved the taste of humans.

Jax’s jaw tightened at the memory. He could still see the firestorms blooming in the jungle below, the leaves and branches igniting in a flash as the bombers laid waste to the colony. He remembered how small it had made him feel, sitting in the cockpit, watching an entire ecosystem reduced to ash. He had felt powerful then, invincible, like a god wielding the lightning bolt. Now, standing in this yard with that thing behind him, the memory soured. They hadn’t gotten all of them. Maybe they never could.
As Jax’s gaze swept the courtyard, a sobering realization hit him—humans were a rare sight here. He counted maybe a dozen, scattered like lonely islands amidst the sea of Crags and other alien species. Canreel’s warning echoed in his mind: the guards turned a blind eye to violence. It wasn’t hard to imagine why there were so few humans left. Most had probably met their end as food for the Targath or as prey to the other prisoners. He tensed instinctively, a primal need for vigilance taking hold. Letting his guard down was not an option here.
Amidst the oppressive crowd, Jax recognized a few familiar species. A Vitula with crude gang tattoos etched across its scaly arms loitered near the edge, while a Nhath privateestood apart, aloof and cold. There was even a Dead Moon raider, its skeletal frame shrouded in dark, tattered cloth that whispered against the ash-laden breeze.
But there were others—beings that defied anything Jax had ever encountered in his years of service. One stood out among them, its body vaguely humanoid but entirely alien. Its head resembled a squid, with long, sinuous tendrils that twitched and curled almost independently. Where its eyes should have been, a shimmering sphere of water hovered in the center of its face, spinning gently, its surface rippling as though responding to unseen vibrations. The creature nudged the Targath beside it with a slender limb, the movement so subtle it was almost imperceptible.
Both the Targath and the squid-like creature turned to look at Jax, their focus heavy and deliberate. A chill crawled down his spine as he locked eyes—or rather, the watery orb—with the squid. Behind him, the Targath emitted a low, wet clicking sound, its mandibles grinding together with a deliberate rhythm. It almost sounded like laughter.
The intercom buzzed again, slicing through the murmur of the crowd.

“ROLL CALL COMMENCING.”
The announcement was followed by a mechanical whine as foot-wide walls began to rise from the ground, encircling the prisoners in a crude ring. The cold, unyielding barriers climbed higher until they reached six feet, their dull gray surfaces slick with moisture from the humid air. Despite the enclosure, no one dared move. The atmosphere grew thick with tension, the kind that made the hairs on the back of Jax’s neck stand on end.
Standing taller than most, Jax’s head rose just above the wall, giving him a view of the courtyard beyond. His breath hitched as he watched the metal doors swing open.
Dozens of guards marched out in unison, their heavy boots echoing ominously across the concrete. Dressed in their signature teal-blue armor and faceless black masks, they moved with mechanical precision, forming a rigid line along the perimeter walls. Their presence, though silent, was suffocating, an unspoken reminder of the brutal authority they wielded over this place.
The mechanical voice crackled through the courtyard, its flat, unfeeling tone cutting through the tense silence. Jax froze, trying to make sense of what he’d just heard. Lottery? He glanced around, noticing a shift in the atmosphere
Beside him, a prisoner suddenly erupted with a booming cheer. “YES!”
The sound startled Jax, the raw joy in the shout so out of place it sent a chill down his spine. He turned toward the source. The man—or creature, rather—was an emaciated figure with a body that looked more like weathered wood than flesh. Twisting vines in hues of red and green coiled around his spindly limbs, shifting slightly as if alive. His eyes, hollow and sunken, glimmered with something Jax hadn’t seen much of in this place—hope.
The cheering creature muttered to itself, barely coherent. “I won? I can’t believe it. I won.” His frail hands trembled, claw-like fingers pressed against his chest as if to keep his heart from bursting with excitement.
Around them, other prisoners cast subtle glances. Most kept their faces blank, their expressions carefully guarded, but Jax caught fleeting glimpses of envy flickering in their eyes—along with something else. Sympathy.
Trying to make sense of the moment, Jax leaned in toward the cheering prisoner. “Hey, buddy… what exactly did you win?”
The vined creature didn’t seem to hear him. His gaze was fixed on some distant point, his murmurs growing softer, more reverent.
Before Jax could ask again, the prisoner to his right spoke up—a Vitula with slate-gray skin etched with angular tattoos. The alien turned his head just enough to meet Jax’s gaze, his eyes cold and knowing. “Every week, two prisoners are picked from this raffle to be killed,” the Vitula said, his voice low but heavy with finality.
Jax blinked, his chest tightening. “Huh?”
The words felt like a misfire in his brain, absurd and incomprehensible. He stared at the Vitula, then back at the still-cheering prisoner beside him.
“The Vitula turned his head just enough to meet Jax’s gaze, his expression unreadable, but his voice—quiet and laced with a faint bitterness—carried an undercurrent of longing. ‘Yeah... if only I could be so lucky.’”





One lumbered past him, its skin resembling jagged boulders fused together, its face an unreadable mask of mineral hardness. Its massive arms hung low, knuckles scraping the ground as it moved with the deliberate slowness of something that knew it had all the time in the world to crush you.

"Do you remember the story of AJAX?" Berji's calm voice muffled behind the mask.
Jimzon stared at his friend, trying to see any semblance of eyes or to understand the question. "Like… the ship?"
"Not quite, although that battleship was crested after this one story. I suppose it makes sense, you haven't heard it, being raised in a military academy and all"
"Well… how does it go then?"
"It has been some time since I heard the original, since I was a child, so forgive me if I mistake some details."
Jimzon looked out the spaceship window as his friend cleared his throat.
"Thousands and thousands of years ago, before any human had a ship capable of interstellar travel, we'd send these droids to potential planets. Rovers at first, but to keep costs down we sent repurposed house droids. Y'know the ones that were maids, or sanitation or law enforcement. Bipedal, legs, heads with almost a brain in there. They were shaped a lot like humans, so their data was pretty valuable. They scanned for things like atmospheric pressure, sunspot activity, oxygen levels, whatever. Anything to see if one was habitable. 
They'd wander these planets, uploading whatever they could, before they'd wither and break down. Except for this one, named AJAX, that must've been wandering for lifetimes.
AJAX was sent to some dry desert planet. His scalp, shoulders and back were all made of solar panels, so maybe he was lucky to have been dropped here. But those years had taken their toll. His joints were corroded, his batteries depleted, his rusted arm hung useless, but even with a twisted ankle, he just kept walking. Taking pictures of anything he deemed out of the ordinary. 
Jagged rocks, a tall pile of sand, just sending essentially useless data back home because it's what he was programmed to do.
Then one day, just as normal as the thousands of others he had, the panels on his back began to shut down one by one. For whatever reason, maybe the planets orbit, or a solar flare, the light and air was burning just enough to be deadly.
A wire on the back of his neck burst, sending sparks and alerts by his head. His mind just kept repeating "shade. shade. shade." trying to locate anywhere out of the sun. He'd left footprints across this planet, so he knew it pretty well. 
So, soon found himself under a ridge, and as his eyes adjusted the white balance to the darkness, he noticed there was another set of tracks in this same ridge.
He had no recollection of ever visiting here before, but his memory was constantly failing him, so he stepped closer, and saw the trackes suddenly stopped. 
Then, he saw a metallic hand stuck up in the sand, reaching out one that looked like his own. Surely some scrap from years ago he thought. With his one able arm, he pulled on it, ready to replace his broken one, but then, he pulled out an entire figure from beneath the sand
It looked just like him. Exactly. Except on it's arm, it was written AZE. He stared at it, before his neck sparked again.
He reached again, to rip the wires out of the back of its neck, but as he had grabbed ahold, it began to hum, and lights began to flicker to life.
The two just stared back at each other for a few minutes before AJAX broke the silence.
In a scratchy robotic voice he said "I require shrapnel wires immediately" 
"I possess none" AZE responded.
""I require shrapnel wires immediately"  He repeated, reaching again for Aze's neck.
Aze tilted his body away, as if to flinch, and pointed to a hole in his his chest. "I require a battery pack immediately," he responded.
AJAX just stands over AZE for some time. Just… looking. He looks so helpless, and… pathetic. Frankly, how dare he ask him for a battery pack? He's dusty and pathetic. AJAX feels real insulted, and for the first time, he isn't thinking about rocks or the sun, he thinks that "I am, and will continue to be".
That is, until he realizes AZE is probably thinking something similar.
"You're like me. But you aren't me."
"You as well."
AJAX begins to ponder in uncertainty. Something exists, that isn't me. An entire series of thoughts that I will never be able to witness, a plethora of experiences different than I have seen. How dare this thing know information I don't? If it knows knowledge I have yet to discover, it could be plotting to steal my battery. To end me.
One will always be stronger, smarter, or have more terabytes of landscape data, and it is going to be me.
An instant before he pounces, he pauses. He realizes, If there is an absence of aze, there will be no superior one. He will once again be alone.
AZE seems to have a different perspective. He offers one of his wires to AJAX in exchange for a bit of solar power so he can leave this place, this way they can continue together, and perhaps chart more land, and share imagery. AZE hopes his photographs are as good as AJAX.
AJAX tosses him part of his battery pack, just enough to last a few days, while AZE gently hands him a wire plucked from his own head.
The sparks stop coming from his neck, but something has changed within AJAX's core being. Rocks no longer interest him, for the first time. His existence rests in being better than AZE. AZE stands up for the first time, re-energized.
AZE begins to take a few steps, AJAX stares at his back as he walks. His battery is easily fixable, but is unable to see it. AJAX decides not to tell to inform AZE, because AJAX is naturally the superior one. It is destiny for him to be the one with a working solar pack.
AJAX realizes he knows something AZE doesn't, and stares at AZE stumble around clueless. How stupid, he thinks.
AJAX needs to stay close to this foolish being that exists only to take photos of rocks, because he will eventually need his corpse to repair his own. For he is the master, AZE will need him to recharge his battery every few days, or he will perish, so he is the fool.
He offers AZE a deal. "I will share with you my battery pack, but in exchange for my generosity, you must obey me".
AZE turns his head trying to see why AJAX has become open to sharing so much of himself. How nice of him.
The two begin to walk together. AJAX finally thinks of his first order. He informs AZE to change his name to just ZE, for the A should be reserved for the Alpha. AZE agrees not knowing what he meant by this.
AJAX continues to walk happily knowing he is the better in the eyes of ZE. ZE walks slowly behind. ZE collapses, and his awoken by a new battery charge surging within him.
ZE stands up and asks "where did I go?"
AJAX responds, "the ground"
"oh"
ZE realizes he can never leave AJAX, for his existence is bound to him. ZE wonders why he has to be the one who relies of AJAX. Why can't he still be AZE, and his company be named JAX instead?
AJAX interrupts this thought and demands ZE carry a large rock to bear him shade so he does not overheat. ZE responds "AJAX, this will slow me down, and slow us down, how can I take photos with a Boulder between my arms?"
AJAX responds, "if you wish to exist, you will obey me." AJAX ponders if there is another being out there they may meet. He would be pleased to imagine this being in awe of his magnificence and domination over ZE.
After weeks of wandering together, and several replacements, AJAX demands shade from the boulder.. AJAX sits and stares at the stars. He asks ZE "how many more of us are there?" ZE responds "I don't know"
"Of course you don't, and that is why you obey me."
ZE lifts the boulder to block the sun. "AJAX, I understand I owe you my existence, but I have a few things I would like to do, perhaps you could join me."
"ZE, your ignorance is apparent, you need me and I don't need you, so it is fate that you must follow me. Things are created to be better and worse, and you are worse."
"How am I worse? I spared you my wires, we could be AJAX and AZE once again."
"How dare you mock me, your name no longer suits you, you will know be called a bot, disrespect me once more and I shall let the ground consume you"
The bot's arm begins to shake holding the boulder. AJAX notices his weakness and wants to push the bot to its breaking point.
"You are a bot and are disposable, you were created for me to control you. Not only did fate bound us together, it ordained upon me the role of the master. Not only did fate destin you this way, but your own ignorance shows your stupidity. Your battery packs could be repaired easily by me, they are not broken, just damaged. Your miscalculations prove your ineptitude."
The bot stares at AJAX lying down mocking him. Weeks of being controlled over a lie? AJAX could have freed me all this time? He trembles, feeling something he's never felt before.
The bot let's go of the boulder and crushes AJAX. His limbs surge and tweak below the rock, but eventually becomes stagnant. The bot squats, rips out parts of his former master, and sheds his old battery pack like a shackle of a slave, and gets a new one. AZE has returned as a victor, and with valuable parts. AZE wonders if he will meet anyone again, but for now just wants to photograph the landscape. He takes his first photo in weeks of the boulder that crushed his old master. How beautiful.
For hundreds of miles, aimless footprints dot the landscape of the red planet, until the two sets meet at a cave and walk together, until only one set leaves.

"Well, the story is more than just robots. It's about how we only exist relative to others - how our identities are shaped by comparison. There's no concept of young without old, strong without weak, smart without stupid. These labels define us, but they also breed insecurity. When people get desperate to be on the winning side of those comparisons, they'll build fake hierarchies to feel superior. But anything built on lies eventually collapses."

FEEDBACK (yeah its right)
1. AJAX's psychological transition (biggest issue) The jump from "tosses him part of his battery pack" to suddenly withholding crucial information feels too abrupt. You need 2-3 more beats showing his mindset shifting - maybe he notices AZE stumbling and feels a flash of superiority, or he realizes the power dynamic this creates and gets intoxicated by it. Make us feel him discovering he likes having power over someone.
2. Deepen Jimzon's processing Right now he goes from "greedy robot gets killed" to understanding philosophical concepts too quickly. Add some resistance - maybe he pushes back on Berji's explanation, or asks a follow-up question that shows he's still wrestling with it. Let him be confused longer before the lightbulb moment.
3. Add one more layer to the robot story The story works, but needs one more psychological detail that makes it unforgettable. Maybe show AZE's internal moment when he realizes the lie - not just "trembles, feeling something he's never felt" but what that feeling actually is. Or give us AJAX's final thought as the boulder comes down.
4. Strengthen the ending "And that was a children's story?" is good but could hit harder. Maybe Jimzon connects it to something in their current situation, or asks a question that shows the story is haunting him.
Fix that psychological transition and you're easily at 8.5. Add the other layers and you're pushing 9+.
