Third Act

 "2 minutes till touchdown," Daiser's  voice announced through the speakers.
"Any last-minute questions?" Jimzon inquired, his voice deep and steady. Silence met his query. "Good," he affirmed.


“Two minutes to touchdown, lads,” Daiser called out from the cockpit, his smoky hands dancing across the dampening thrusters. “Everyone got their stories straight?”.
Rays of sunshine shined through rays of the sunrise shine through the small windows of the ship the air was thick of the smell of recycled ozone in the faint sweet scent of the small Flume resting in Calsco's lap 
“Two minutes to touchdown, lads,” Daiser called out from the cockpit, his smoky hands dancing across the dampening thrusters. “Everyone got their stories straight?”
In the cargo hold, the air was thick with the smell of recycled ozone and the faint, sweet scent of the small floom resting in Calsco’s lap.
Jimzon sat on the metal bench, staring blankly at the floor. His hands, acting entirely on a decade of muscle memory, were working the slide of the Royal Pistol he’d found in the mailbox. Clack. Clack. He popped the magazine, checked the brass, and slapped it back in. Clack. His eyes were hollow glimpsing out the window, his mind still halfway buried in the orange sand of Wake, repeating Kaspian's words over and over.
“Hey,” Pavlock snapped, leaning over and tapping Jimzon’s knee with the handle of his wrench. “Hide that fucking thing, man.”
Jimzon blinked, the fog breaking. He looked down at the gun. The white engravings gleamed under the cabin lights—a dead giveaway of Royal Guard origin.
“Right,” Jimzon muttered. He engaged the safety and shoved the pistol deep into his rucksack, burying it under a spare tunic. 
“Look alive, gentlemen,” Calsco said slapping Berji on the back, standing up and smoothing out his Trailblazer uniform with his free hand. He oozed a sickening, unearned confidence. “Let me do the talking. We are simple men doing simple labor. Grugg,” he pointed at Berji, “you don’t speak.”
Berji sat perfectly still in his deep green and orange robes, the carved wooden mask staring straight ahead. He gave a single, slow nod that carried the weight of a mountain.
“Touchdown,” Daiser announced.
The ship’s four landing struts hit the durasteel pad with a heavy, satisfying thud. The hydraulic ramp hissed, lowering slowly to reveal the bustling, humid air of the Shayron spaceport. The whine of passing speeders and the chatter of a dozen different alien dialects flooded the cargo hold.
Waiting at the bottom of the ramp were two Shayron Port Authority guards, clad in pristine ivory armor, carrying pulse-rifles lazily strapped to their chests. One of them held up a scanning ledger, looking thoroughly bored.
“State your business and manifest,” the guard droned, not even looking up.
Jimzon tensed, his hand instantly ghosting toward where his sidearm usually sat on his hip, before he remembered it was in the bag. Pavlock shifted his weight, his eyes darting toward the crate of rifles sitting just a few feet away.
Calsco didn’t miss a beat. He strolled down the ramp, holding his floom like an eccentric pet, and flashed a brilliant, disarming smile.
“Good evening, officers,” Calsco said smoothly, tapping a datapad against the guard's ledger. “Trailblazer Logistics, Section 4. Here on contract to run seismic elevation scans for the new outer-city developments. Just the five of us.”
The guard glanced at the transfer data on his screen, then peered past Calsco into the hold. His eyes lingered on Berji’s masked face.
“Who’s the freak?” the guard asked.
“Cultural attache from the Katanzarian outer rim,” Calsco lied without taking a breath. “Religious vows. Doesn’t speak, doesn’t show his face. Very sensitive stuff, you know how these foreign investors get.”
The guard grunted, losing interest. He pointed his scanner at the heavy crate strapped to the bulkhead. “What’s in the box?”
“Proxine batteries,” Calsco said, his voice dropping into a dull, corporate drone. “Heavy-duty grid stabilizers for our scanning equipment. You’re welcome to pry it open, but it takes about twenty minutes to reseal the static-foam, and I know you boys are nearing the end of your shift.”
The guard looked at the crate, then looked at the setting sun over the Shayron skyline. He tapped his scanner against his thigh.
Calsco smoothly slid a carved piece of glowing Maspin amethyst into the guard's palm beneath the datapad. "A little cultural import for the wife, officer. Hardly worth noting."
The guard’s fingers closed over the gem, vanishing it into his pocket without breaking eye contact. He glanced up at the bark-covered hull of the vessel and grunted. "This is some weird fucking ship you guys have... but your IDs check out."
“Trailblazer IDs check out,” the guard muttered. He swiped his thumb across his datapad. The light on the ramp terminal flashed from red to green. “Welcome to Shayron. Keep the religious guy out of the Royal square, they don't like masks near the palace.”
“Understood. Have a blessed evening, officers,” Calsco smiled.
The guards turned and walked toward the next landing pad.
Pavlock let out a breath he felt like he’d been holding since they left Maspin. Daiser killed the ship's engines, pocketed the keys, and strolled down the ramp, clapping Jimzon on the shoulder as he passed.
“Told you,” Daiser whispered with a grin. “Lightwork.”
Jimzon stepped off the metal ramp and onto the stone of the port, the gravity of the planet finally pulling him down to reality. They were in.

The guard tapped a command into his wrist-console and tossed Calsco a small, stamped brass chit. "You're in parking lot Alpha-29F. Clear the pad so it can descend."
Jimzon stepped completely off the metal ramp and onto the stone of the port, the gravity of the planet finally pulling him down to reality. They were in.
A loud, mechanical clank echoed behind them. Jimzon turned just in time to see the entire durasteel landing pad—ship and all—sink smoothly into a vertical shaft built directly into the cliff face. It dropped like a massive stone, then shifted laterally into the mountain, burying the ugly, loud machinery of space travel far out of sight. Peering over the railing, Jimzon saw that the cliff dropped away into a staggering, endless sea of lush emerald canopy and winding silver rivers far below.
When he turned back around to face the city, the air hit him.
It didn’t smell of promethium exhaust, recycled ozone, or ash. It smelled of jasmine and warm earth. The temperature was a flawless, balmy heat, carrying a gentle breeze that felt like a perpetual, golden afternoon. He looked up. Because of the planet's thick, rich atmosphere, the sky wasn't the deep, dark void he was used to; it was a brilliant, pale, glowing blue that made everything beneath it pop with intense saturation.
He had only ever read about places like Shayron. Every world he’d deployed to was either a fresh crater, a sterile array of military prefabs, or a slum where the locals survived in the galaxy's hand-me-downs. But this capital city unfurled before them like a living painting, completely untouched by the concept of war.
It was scaled for footsteps, not freighters. A wide, sun-dappled cobblestone avenue stretched out from the cliffs, acting like a golden road drawing them straight toward the center of the city. Flanking the street were three- and four-story buildings painted in the rustic hues of a sunset—warm terracottas, burnt ochres, and soft rose, all bordered by delicate, eggshell-white trim.
Nothing here felt stamped out by a factory. Lush, vibrant flora spilled from wrought-iron balconies that swooped in elegant, deliberate curves forged by artisans' hands. The brickwork of the streets and walls had been hand-laid; it was in those tiny, human imperfections—a slightly tilted stone, a thumbprint left in the mortar centuries ago—that the city found its absolute perfection.
Overhead, massive azure banners snapped crisply in the wind, strung between the rooftops. The locals moved with a laid-back elegance, navigating around the occasional sleek, quiet hover-scooter. They were dressed in sharp, fashionable clothes cut with neat, straight lines. Even their grooming felt curated—Jimzon noticed several men passing by sporting thick, immaculate mustaches paired with perfectly bare chins, a local style that spoke to their meticulous nature.
Just off the main plaza, nestled in the shade of a vibrant alleyway, Jimzon watched a pair of tiny, brilliant blue birds splashing and tweeting in a carved stone basin, completely unbothered by the strangers passing by.
Anchoring the entire horizon, visible from every angle like a compass needle, was the Royal Palace. It was a staggering marvel of design, with massive, sweeping domes and towering spires rising above the lush tree line. It didn't look like a fortress built to repel enemies; it looked like a crown resting gently on the earth.
Jimzon stared at the vibrant, peaceful masterpiece of a city. The phantom weight of the stolen Royal Pistol in his rucksack suddenly felt violently out of place.
"Well," Pavlock murmured, adjusting the collar of his leather jacket as he looked down the cobblestone street. "I guess we're on our own. Let's go."

Pavlock glanced down at his wrist, checking the face of a small analog watch he’d bartered for back on Maspin. "T-minus seven hours until detonation," he said, his voice tight.
"Keep your voice down," Daiser hissed, walking just a half-step ahead of him.
"I whispered!" Pavlock shot back, defensively adjusting the fuzzy collar of his jacket.
"It doesn't matter," Jimzon cut in, his tone low and focused. He kept his eyes sweeping across the rooftops and intersections. "We just need to look around and find the parade route. There’s got to be some kind of signage or a post about it somewhere."
"No problem at all," Calsco said smoothly.
They walked down the sun-drenched sidewalk, blending into a growing crowd of locals preparing for the day's celebration. The citizens of Shayron were dressed in their festival best—sharp, tailored suits and elegant dresses, now accented with vibrant, festive flair. Some had thin, azure streamers woven into their neat hair or pinned to their lapels. A group of teenagers walked by wearing exaggerated, silly festival glasses, while children chased each other across the cobblestones holding unlit sparklers. It was a picture of perfect, unbothered joy.
Without breaking his stride, Calsco casually veered toward a heavily armed Ivory Guard stationed near a carved stone fountain.
Jimzon’s jaw locked. Pavlock froze mid-step, his eyes widening in sheer panic. The entire Castle Team tensed, every muscle coiling for a fight, but Calsco maintained his composure, projecting the absolute picture of a lost, harmless tourist.
"Excuse me, officer," Calsco said cheerfully, flashing that sickeningly confident smile. "Do you happen to know the way to the parade route? My crew and I want to make sure we get a good view before the crowds settle in."
Jimzon forced himself to keep walking at a casual pace, fighting the screaming urge to reach into his rucksack for the pistol.
The guard, completely disarmed by Calsco's easy demeanor, lowered his posture and gestured politely with a gloved hand. "Certainly, sir. The procession will leave the Palace and come straight up that side street there—Sidor Avenue. Then it’ll take a hard turn down this street, Alvion. If you want the best view, head down to Park Square. All the floats slow down and stop there for a display before they head into the lower parking garage."
"Excellent. Thank you, sir. Gods bless," Calsco said, offering a gracious nod before effortlessly pivoting back to join the team.
As soon as they were out of earshot, Pavlock leaned in close. "Can you fucking let us know next time you decide to talk to a guard unprompted?" he hissed, his face pale.
Calsco brushed his sleeve off with a dismissive chuckle. "Relax. We know the route now, and we saved ourselves an hour of wandering. We don’t exactly have the time to be sneaky."
"Up there," a deep, muffled voice said.
The group stopped and looked at Berji. He hadn't raised a hand to point—a smart move so as not to draw the crowd's attention—but the hollow eyes of his carved wooden mask were tilted slightly upward.
Jimzon followed his gaze. Spanning across Alvion Street, connecting the third floors of two terracotta-painted buildings, was a narrow pedestrian skybridge. With its large arched windows, creeping ivy, and stone balustrades, it hung directly over the route the floats would have to take.
"Good eye, Grugg," Jimzon murmured, using the alias. "That’s our vantage point. Let's get up there."
As they paced down the bustling cobblestone avenue, the midday sun beat down hard on the back of Jimzon’s neck, yet a cold rash of goosebumps prickled their way down his shoulders.
He didn't say a word about the odds. He didn't have to. Instead, he pulled the brim of his cap down lower, plunging his trembling hand into his pocket. His thumb found the small, smooth stone from Wake, rubbing it in frantic, repetitive circles. He felt completely exposed. Everywhere he looked, he made eye contact with pristine, wealthy aristocrats and oblivious, pretentious royals. They looked right past him, maybe seeing a rugged laborer they’d politely thank for his service, completely unaware of the blood on his hands.
He spotted a few species he hadn't even seen on the battlefield—hulking aliens with skin like jagged granite, and others draped in iridescent, shifting scales. Yet, they all shared the same leisurely, wealthy body language as the human aristocrats. Jimzon’s combat instincts, however, were screaming. He knew some of these beings had better eyesight, sharper hearing, and faster reaction times than him. And with a mission this deep inside the capital, the Guard wouldn't just be relying on standard patrols. They had failsafes that weren't in any file. They weren't just a step ahead; they were miles ahead.
Berji’s shoulder gently bumped against his.
"Relax," Berji murmured, his voice muffled by the wood. "Just breathe. This too shall pass."
Jimzon let out a sharp, half-joking breath. "Easy for you to say. You’ve got a mask on to hide behind."
He instantly regretted the words, his stomach dropping. But Berji seemed entirely unbothered.
"You wear one yourself, Jimzon," Berji replied calmly.
Jimzon kept his eyes forward. "I know. I’m just... still not used to talking to you like this."
"I believe it was you who once told me to adapt or die," Berji said. "Granted, that was in the context of a battlefield, but I have thought about it a lot. Things change every day. That is what we are here to do. Right now, you might only see everything that is different. But if you live long enough, you realize you are just looking at the same faces on different people. The current stays the same, even as the water changes."
Ahead of them, Calsco walked with a deceptively slow, rolling gait. Yet, because he towered over everyone in the crowd by at least a foot, his long strides ate up the pavement, forcing the others to hurry just to keep up.
Beside him scurried Daiser. The vaporous Maspin was barely knee-high to the towering Katanzarian. And despite Daiser’s claims of being a distinguished 'Renaissance man,' his watery, shifting eyes proved incredibly shifty. Whenever a wealthy local passed by wearing heavy jewelry or carrying a fine silk purse, Daiser's gaze couldn't help but lock on and linger just a fraction of a second too long.
Pavlock jogged up slightly to fall into step beside Calsco.
"Hey," Pavlock said, keeping his voice low. "I know you’re in a bit over your head right now. If you need to split off and go back to the ship, I understand."
Calsco looked down at him and smirked. "Trying to get rid of me that easily?"
"No," Pavlock said, his tone earnest. "Just... I know this isn't necessarily your fight."
Calsco’s smirk faded. He looked ahead at the looming palace. "I see what you’re trying to do. I know that I have broken promises in my past. But I fight for more than just gold. I'm not going to turn you in, and I'm not going to betray you."
Pavlock tried to remain skeptical. He was used to staying a step ahead of every con artist and smuggler they met. But as he looked up, something about Calsco’s eyes made him pause. For the first time in a while, Pavlock felt he was witnessing the unbridled truth.
POP-POP-POP!
A sharp series of explosions cracked against the pavement. Jimzon and Pavlock flinched violently, shoulders dropping into a combat crouch.
A group of laughing children darted past their legs, tossing festival sparklers and firecrackers onto the cobblestones.
Jimzon let out a shaky exhale. He and Pavlock stood up straight, exchanging a reluctant, breathless smile as they reached the base of the stone stairs leading up to the skybridge.
"Hey, Pav," Jimzon said as they began to climb, the noise of the crowd echoing beneath them. "There’s a lot of things I need to thank you for, but I haven't. Whatever happens... I’m going to make sure you get out of this alive."
"Worry about yourself, brother," Pavlock said, climbing the steps two at a time. "We’re in this together. You don’t gotta worry about me. I'm right here."
"No, man," Jimzon insisted, stopping on the landing and grabbing Pavlock’s arm. "This whole situation is fucked up. I know you. You’ve got your parents. You’ve got a career. I saw how happy you were on Maspin. Honestly? For a second there, I was jealous. Ever since you told me about moving to Korno, I was sad. Because I always pictured us just kicking ass together forever. I didn't want to picture what came after. I was upset you were moving on."
Jimzon looked down at his boots, then back up. "But now that we’re facing this? I want you to go. I want you to be that engineer. I want you to be around those kinds of people and have your fucking dreams. I'm not going to hold you back anymore. I don't know what tomorrow holds, but I'm going to make sure you get out."
Pavlock’s expression softened. He reached out and patted Jimzon firmly on the back.
"Drop the guilty conscience, brother," Pavlock said quietly. "I’m going to miss you too. I feel like an asshole for even thinking of leaving. But listen to me—don’t be a hero if you don’t need to be. Do the right thing today, but don't fucking kill yourself trying to save us. I fought with you. We trained the same. I've been right by your side. I can take care of myself."
Pavlock offered a warm, wry smile. "Promise me you’ll make it out too. Hell, if you need a roommate... you’re unemployed right now. It’ll be a way out after tonight. We'll figure out hiding, or immunity, or whatever. This right here? This is the hardest part of our whole lives."
A few steps above them, Calsco leaned against the stone railing, glancing over his shoulder at Berji.
"You hear those softies right there?" Calsco scoffed quietly. "I can't imagine talking about feelings right before the vigorous heat of a battle comes."
Berji stood perfectly still, his mask angled toward the massive Katanzarian. "People say far more than what they speak," Berji said. "For what it's worth... I'm sure she would be proud."
Calsco raised a thick eyebrow. "And who is she you speak of?"
"Your wife," Berji said evenly. "You will return to her this time tomorrow."
Calsco grunted, his posture stiffening. "That is a bold claim. Do not make promises that you cannot keep."
"I promise nothing," Berji replied. "But I expect you will have the heroic return you yearn for."
Calsco stared at the carved wooden face for a long moment. Slowly, the arrogant edge in his shoulders dropped. "Well," Calsco murmured. "Wherever the hell you come from... I hope your people take you in with open arms once again, too."
The group fell silent as they finished the climb. They stepped out onto the center of the skybridge and looked down over the edge, the sprawling parade route laid out perfectly beneath them

They peered down from the stone balustrade of the skybridge, the cobblestone street below bathed in the warm, perpetual light of the Shayron afternoon.
The route was lined with Ivory Guards spaced exactly fifty meters apart. They wore long, ceremonial coats and tall, immaculate helmets, their posture rigid. Every third guard held an antique, wood-stocked rifle that Jimzon knew was purely for show, while the others stood with hands clasped behind their backs. Aside from slight variations in hair and skin pigmentation, they were a perfectly uniform, living wall separating the crowd from the cobblestone.
Jimzon sighed. Down the line, Pavlock, Calsco, and Daiser mirrored the sound.
"Well," Pavlock murmured, leaning his elbows on the stone railing. "What now?"
Jimzon didn't answer right away. He was already working, his eyes scanning the surrounding architecture with the cold, calculated precision of a sniper. He swept the third- and fourth-story windows, checking the angles, looking for the telltale glare of a scope, a shifted curtain, or an unnaturally dark shadow.
Nothing.
Every possible vantage point was choked with civilians. Families crowded onto wrought-iron balconies, laughing and passing glasses of sparkling astrovin. Old men smoked pipes of greyleaf, the thick, sweet smoke drifting over the street, while children leaned over the railings waving azure streamers and small, golden flags. Finding a shooter in that mess was impossible.
Jimzon shifted his focus back down to the street level, tracing the path the floats would take. He narrowed his eyes at the intersection directly below them.
"Look at the street," Jimzon said, his voice low and tight. "Right beneath us, the floats have to take a hard ninety-degree turn onto Alvion. If they’re going to hit this parade, this is the fatal funnel."
"The what?" Daiser asked, struggling to see over the stone railing.
"The fatal funnel," Jimzon repeated, slipping effortlessly into Guard-tactics mode. "In an L-shaped ambush, you wait until the enemy column hits the bend. The vehicles have to slow down to make the turn, which compresses their spacing. It maximizes target density. If they trigger the bombs right here, the blast radius catches the floats, the crowd on both sides, and traps everyone behind them."
He drummed his fingers against the stone. "The Guard isn't stupid. If the synchronized detonation fails, or if a bomb gets discovered early, they’re going to have a manual fail-safe right here when the highest-value target comes through the turn. A spotter, a trigger-man, maybe a shooter. Someone in this crowd, or maybe even one of these Ivory Guards, is in on it. I just don't know who."
They all fell silent, their eyes dropping back to the street, scanning the line of pristine guards.
"That guy looks rather short to be a guard," Calsco muttered, nodding toward a soldier near a flower cart.
"Everyone looks short to you," Pavlock shot back, dismissing it.
"Hey, what about him?" Daiser said, pointing a smoky finger. "He's holding his gun kind of funny."
Before Jimzon could even assess the target, the guard in question reached back and lazily scratched his ass with his free hand.
Jimzon let out a frustrated breath. "Half these guys look like recruits. I swear, half of them even have their knees locked. They’re going to pass out before the parade even gets here."
"Wait. Look," Pavlock said suddenly, pointing subtly at a guard positioned directly at the apex of the ninety-degree turn. "What about him?"
"What about him?" Calsco asked, squinting. "There’s twenty guards down there."
"And only two of them have their helmet chinstraps actually buckled," Pavlock noted.
"So?" Daiser asked, hopping slightly to try and see.
Jimzon glanced over at the guard, a sudden, sharp fire igniting in his eyes. "Yeah. Wait. Pav’s right."
"About a chin strap?" Calsco asked, sounding entirely unconvinced.
"In Guard basic training," Jimzon explained, his voice speeding up, "they show you this gruesome, unclassified video of a couple of recruits getting their jaws blown off by low-velocity shrapnel specifically because they didn’t buckle their helmets. It’s not about being uniform. They fucking scare you into doing it. Once you see that video, you never leave it unbuckled again. It's a hardwired Guard habit."
"Listen, boys, I'm all about making bold assumptions," Calsco said, crossing his massive arms. "But he's not even the only one strapped up. You said there were two."
"That's all the info we have," Pavlock argued, his eyes locked on the guard.
"Wait a second. Look at what he's doing," Jimzon said, his focus sharpening into a razor's edge. He watched the guard scanning the crowd, his head swiveling back and forth.
"You guys are really fucking bad at explaining," Daiser complained, his vaporous form barely reaching the top of the balustrade. "I can't see shit from down here."
"Do you see what he's doing right now?" Jimzon asked. "He's scanning the crowd left to right. But in the Royal Guard systems, they drill you to scan right to left. They do it when they clear rooms, they do it when they check sectors." Jimzon pointed down at the street. "Hell, they do everything kind of backwards here. They drive on the wrong side of the road. Prior to the Galactic Standard, their native written script was read right to left. That guard is scanning left to right. He's the only one doing it. He's not from Shayron."
Calsco sighed, looking down at the street. "Listen, I think it’s a good hunch. But I think you boys are paranoid. Half these guys don't look like they know what they're doing. When you really look at them, half of them are locking their knees. Even I know not to do that. These guys aren't perfect. They're probably just University conscripts or ceremonial soldiers."
"Listen, we don't really have time," Pavlock said, his voice tight with urgency. "This is the best hunch we have." Pavlock looked to Jimzon. "What do we do now?"
Berji, who had been completely silent, finally spoke, the hollow eyes of his mask fixed on the guard below. "I am with you. But we need to be sure. We need to get closer to him and see if we can find out anything else."



Jimzon looked down at Daiser, who was suddenly sporting three new golden necklaces, a handful of rings, and a jeweled broach semi-hanging out of his pocket.
Jimzon raised an eyebrow. "I have an idea," he said.

Down on the street, the Ivory Guard stood perfectly still. His chinstrap was tight, his posture rigid, but his eyes occasionally scanned the crowd with a hollow, detached focus. He was stationed directly next to one of the massive, cast-iron flower pots that lined the parade route.
Calsco strolled right up to him, gesturing wildly at the architecture while talking at an obnoxious volume. Berji walked silently at his side.
"My friend, this building right here is where, a hundred years ago, little Royal Prince Kenjen had his portrait done by the great Cenieve! Just days before his mysterious disappearance," Calsco boomed, playing the perfect, wealthy tourist. "What’s fascinating is that two apartments above it was her studio, before she went on to sculpt the very columns that mark this roadside!"
Calsco pointed a thick finger at a nearby lamp post, stepping intimately close to the guard's personal space. "And these lamp posts? Actually imported from the old Royal Capital before they packed up and moved here from the planet Maldo all those centuries ago. Granted, they've probably been repaired so much they aren't even the same metal, but still!"
As he spoke, Calsco took a deep drag from a greyleaf cigar and casually blew the thick, grey smoke directly across the guard’s face.
The guard didn't cough, but his eyes narrowed. "Sorry, you need to put that away, sir," he said, his tone flat and professional.
"Of course, of course, my deepest apologies," Calsco said, immediately stubbing it out. "It's been a while since I've been here. I'm just showing my friend the sights. He's Prince Grugg, from Katanzaria. A fellow royal! I assumed he would have similar tastes."
The guard nodded once, saying nothing.
"Anyway, Prince Grugg," Calsco continued smoothly, "I have yet to recalibrate my watch from the journey from Katanzaria. Do you have the local time? We must find our seats soon."
Berji slowly reached into his deep green robes and pulled out a heavy, golden pocket watch. "It is..." his voice muffled behind the carved wood.
Before he could finish, a short, hooded figure bolted through the crowd. Daiser snatched the gold watch right out of Berji's hand and sprinted away—only to instantly trip over his own oversized robes and tumble onto the cobblestones.
"Halt!" the guard barked.
The reaction was terrifyingly fast. In a fraction of a second, the guard's hands snapped to the firing grip of his antique rifle, his stance dropping into a perfect, lethal engagement crouch. But just as quickly, he caught himself. He stood back up, opting instead to raise a firm, open palm toward the fallen thief. "Hand it over."
Daiser scrambled backward, clutching the watch.
Berji stepped gracefully between them, holding up a hand. "Keep it," he said, his voice carrying a practiced, serene authority. He looked at the guard. "I do not want to press charges. Nor should I keep the timepiece. During this festival season, I have taken vows to practice not only abstinence of the flesh, but abstinence of my material possessions."
The guard paused, his hand still hovering near his weapon. "Are you sure, Your Highness?"
"The Prince here is quite religious," Calsco chimed in, stepping between the guard and Daiser, allowing the little Maspin to scurry away into the crowd. "Not showing his face, giving away gold... you know how it is. We will be more careful."
The guard raised an eyebrow, clearly suspicious of the bizarre interaction, but let his hand fall back to his side. "Enjoy the rest of your travels here today."
"One more thing," Calsco said, pulling a small holocamera from his pocket. He pointed directly at the massive, heavy cast-iron flower pot beside the guard. "Would you mind taking a photo for us? Right here, by these beautiful blooms?"
The guard didn't hesitate. "Of course, sir. But I must get back to my post."
Calsco and Berji leaned directly against the heavy planter. The guard stepped right up to it as well, holding the camera, completely unbothered. He even offered a polite, practiced smile as he snapped the picture.

Up on the skybridge, Jimzon pulled his eye away from the brass, coin-operated tourist telescope. He slammed his fist lightly against the stone railing.
"Fuck," Jimzon muttered. "I don't think that's our guy."
"Why not?" Pavlock asked, stepping up to the lens.
"We think the bombs are planted in those heavy flower pots, right? It's the only thing on the street big enough to hide the payload without looking out of place," Jimzon explained, pacing a tight circle. "But if that guy was the triggerman, he knows what's inside that pot. He wouldn't just casually stand right next to it. He wouldn't let Keegan lean all his weight on it for a tourist photo."
Pavlock peered through the glass, watching Calsco and Berji walk away from the smiling guard. "Yeah... what the fuck. They're just taking a photo. Even when we tested his reaction time with Daiser—yeah, he reached for his gun fast, like, scary fast—but that's not enough to prove he's a deep-cover operative. He could just be a jumpy veteran."
"It's not enough," Jimzon agreed, staring down at the crowds. "We have to meet the others back at that cafe off Garnwood and Rivern Road. Let's double-time it. Just fucking pray they picked up on something we didn't."
Right as he said it, Pavlock’s analog watch beep-beep-beeped.
Pavlock silenced it, the sound hanging heavy between them. Only a few hours left until this entire street was supposed to be flattened, turning all these laughing, celebrating people into ash and dust.

The warm, amber lighting of the cafe offered a deceptive sense of comfort. The air was rich with the scent of roasted coffee beans and steeped herbal teas, wrapping around the low hum of civilian chatter.
Jimzon and Pavlock sat crammed into a corner booth beside a heavy wooden coat rack. Jimzon had the brim of his cap pulled low, his eyes locked on the door while his index finger tapped a relentless, manic rhythm against the wooden table.
"Where the fuck are they?" Jimzon muttered, his voice barely a breath. "That stunt was so fucking stupid. Did they get pinched? What the fuck is taking them so long?"
Pavlock didn’t answer immediately. He stared down at the steaming mug in his hands, took a minuscule sip, and then shoved the cup to the center of the table with a grimace. "I don't know why I'm drinking this," he whispered to himself. "I'm fucking wide awake."
He looked up just as the cafe door swung open. "There they are."
Calsco, Daiser, and the masked Berji wove through the crowded tables and slid into the booth opposite them.
"We only have about six hours left," Jimzon said immediately, leaning across the table. "What the fuck do we do now? How are we going to find who's actually in on this?"
"It is him," Berji said. His voice, muffled behind the carved wood, was perfectly flat. "We found our man."
Calsco blinked, looking genuinely caught off guard. "Wait, how do you know? You didn't say a word about it on the walk over here."
"I hate repeating myself," Berji replied coolly. "It is him."
"How the fuck do you know?" Daiser demanded, leaning over the table.
Berji turned his featureless face toward Jimzon and Pavlock. "You two were correct about his chinstrap, and you saw how quickly he reached for his weapon. Your instincts were right. But there is more."
"What is with you guys and just yapping?" Daiser interjected, throwing his smoky hands up. "We don't have time for riddles. Just tell us!"
"His accent," Berji said.
Calsco scoffed, rolling his eyes. "The man had a Royal accent, same as anyone else in this city. Sure, maybe he was a bit softer on a couple of vowels, but—"
"No, it is more than that," Berji cut in. "He comes from Astonia."
"My engineering teacher back in the Academy was from Astonia," Pavlock argued, leaning forward. "He sounded exactly like that. The guy sounds Royal. What do you mean the 'Royal guy has a Royal accent'? Have you lost it?"
"Think about it, Pavlock," Berji said quietly. "How old was your engineering teacher?"
Pavlock hesitated. "I don't know. Sixty? Sixty-five?"
"Exactly. Nobody under the age of fifty has that accent anymore," Berji explained, leaning his elbows on the table. "Astonia always had a unique dialect that sounded perfectly Royal to outsiders. But the planet was annexed and sold to the Guard decades ago. In the mandatory Guard boarding schools and public academies, that regional accent was entirely scrubbed out."
The realization began to dawn on Pavlock's face.
Berji pressed on. "That guard down there is, what, twenty-five? Maybe thirty? He shouldn't sound like that. To a local, he sounds perfectly wealthy and Royal—it's the ideal cover. But to anyone who knows Guard history, it is a glaring error. It is a Deep Cover training flaw."
Jimzon's eyes widened. "Like Amon from our covert training cycle back in the day."
"Precisely," Berji nodded. "The Guard rarely updates their cultural infiltration datasets because their operations are usually far less subtle now. That operative was likely pulled into a black-site program when he was five or six years old, right when the Guard took over his planet. They trained him to keep the accent to blend in with the Royals here, not realizing the accent itself totally died off in his generation."
The booth fell dead silent. The jazz music playing lightly in the cafe suddenly felt miles away.
"Holy fuck," Jimzon breathed. "So that guy really is in on it." He rubbed his temples, his mind racing. "So what now?"
Berji sat perfectly still, a statue in the corner booth. "We take him out," he said calmly. "We interrogate him, or we kill him. Whatever we have to do to maintain the peace here."
Jimzon’s breath hitched in his throat.




An unfamiliar voice chimed in from just over Jimzon’s shoulder.
"Heard we're taking someone out?"
The entire Castle Team stiffened. Jimzon’s hand instantly darted into his rucksack, his fingers wrapping around the cold grip of the Royal Pistol.
They looked up. Standing beside their booth was a scrawny, slightly sunburned man wearing a loose blue tunic. He had the easy, relaxed posture of a man who thought he owned the room, despite lacking the muscle to back it up.
Flanking him was a completely different story. A towering, green-skinned alien stood silently at his shoulder. Small, fleshy tentacles draped down over a heavy breathing respirator that looked as though it had been carved from solid stone. The alien wore a sharply tailored black suit, sharply contrasted by a rugged, completely empty leather bandolier strapped across his broad chest.
Calsco immediately slipped into his charismatic facade, spreading his hands. "Yes, indeed! We are taking the Prince out on a lovely tour of this planet. A tour which we should be getting back to right about now—"
"Jim," the scrawny man interrupted, his eyes locking onto the ex-captain. "We’re all friends here. And as far as I'm concerned, I owe you one."
Jimzon didn't move an inch. His finger rested perfectly straight along the frame of the hidden pistol. "Who the fuck are you?" he growled, keeping his voice dangerously low so as not to alert the surrounding tables.
"Relax. You're in good company. I'm Quinn," the man said, offering a warm, almost entirely out-of-place smile. He gestured toward Jimzon’s bag. "And if I were you, I’d lower the weapon. We're in public. It's a bit rude."
To drive the point home, Quinn gave a subtle nudge to his silent, green-skinned partner. The alien casually grabbed the lapels of his black suit to straighten his jacket, deliberately flashing the heavy, high-grade blaster holstered at his ribs.
Pavlock squinted, his mind racing through Guard channels and news feeds. "Quinn..." His voice trailed off as the realization hit. "Wait a second. You went AWOL too. You're that guy."
Quinn's smile widened. He pulled over a stray wooden chair and seated himself at the end of their booth, completely uninvited. "I am that guy. And like I said, I owe you one. You know, it takes real balls for a Captain—well, I guess you're not a Captain anymore—but it takes balls to really say 'fuck it' to the Guard and take down a couple of ships on your way out. I had to meet you. I had to figure out what your big plan was."
Jimzon’s eyes burned with a mixture of exhaustion and lethal intent. He still wasn't warm to this guy. There was a reckless fire in Quinn's eyes that Jimzon simply didn't trust. "I don't have a plan," Jimzon said flatly. "And we really gotta be going."
"Look, I'm not trying to be an asshole," Quinn said, leaning forward and lowering his voice. "But I've gone through a hell of a lot of trouble to track you down, and I need to figure out what the fuck is going on."
Pavlock stood up, effectively blocking Quinn's line of sight to the door. "Look, we can talk later. You need to get the fuck out of here. Shit is going to go down."
Quinn’s eyes lit up with dangerous curiosity. "Brilliant. What kind of shit?"
Before Pavlock could answer, Quinn held up his hands defensively. "Look, guys, I know you weren't expecting me. I didn't exactly know a better way to introduce myself besides cornering you in a cafe. But maybe this is divine intervention that I'm here at all. I can help you." He leaned in closer, his voice dropping to a conspiratorial whisper. "Fuck the Guard, bro."
Jimzon finally let go of the pistol. He leaned back in his seat, rubbing his face with his calloused hands. "Look, man. If you thought cornering me was a good idea when I'm stressed the fuck out, you're dead wrong. But whatever made you go AWOL... I'm happy you didn't have to go out the way they wanted you to."
"I think 'divine intervention' is the wrong choice of words," Berji interjected, the hollow eyes of his mask settling on Quinn. "But Jimzon... we can use all the help we can get right now."
Beside him, Calsco held a small, flat scanner just beneath the table line. He squinted at the glowing blue hologram hovering over the screen, cross-referencing a set of recent bounty postings.
"The face matches," Calsco murmured, glancing up at Quinn. "He's legit. This guy went AWOL the exact same second you did, Jim. That’s not something Command could have planned as a setup. I'm with the hippie on this one."
Quinn tossed a few heavy, silver credits onto the wooden table. "First round's on me."
Jimzon stared at the coins, then back at Quinn. He was incredibly hesitant, but the clock ticking in his head left him no luxury for a prolonged interrogation.
"We're not staying for drinks," Jimzon said, his voice hard as iron. He leaned forward, closing the distance between them. "I'll fill you in on a couple of things as we move. There are bombs planted on the parade route outside. It's a false-flag attack to spark an intergalactic war, and we have to diffuse it. They go off in six hours."
Quinn's confident smile completely vanished. His eyebrows shot up, genuinely shocked. He had expected to join a burgeoning anti-Guard rebellion, not step directly into the blast radius of a mass-casualty terrorist plot.
"So," Jimzon continued, his eyes deadlocked with Quinn's. "If you are going to roll with us, you keep a low profile. You do exactly what we say. And when I tell you to leave... you leave. You understand?"
Quinn swallowed hard, quickly regaining his composure. He let out a breathless, adrenaline-laced chuckle.
"Yeah. Alright," Quinn said, nodding slowly. "Lead the way, Captain. What's the play here?"

next chapter

Quinn strode out into the sun-drenched plaza, adjusting the collar of his loose blue tunic.  He didn’t walk with the hyper-vigilant, sweeping gaze of a soldier. His chin was tilted up, his steps deliberate and loud on the cobblestones. He walked exactly like a man who owned the pavement.
"Keep your head down and look pissed off," Quinn muttered over his shoulder without turning around.
Behind him, Pavlock grunted, hoisting a massive, battered durasteel case onto his shoulder. Sweat beaded on Pavlock’s forehead, his jaw locked tight under the sheer weight of the crate. He didn't have to act; he looked exactly like an underpaid, overworked grip.
Quinn marched directly up to the perimeter line, stopping mere inches from the massive cast-iron flower pot. He pointed a sharp finger right at the rim.
"Put it down right here. No, uhhh closer to the edge," Quinn barked, waving his hands in a tight, frantic circle. "I need the high-angle establishing shot before the procession clears the bend. If A-cam doesn’t catch the Prince’s good side, the network is going to have my head!"
Pavlock dropped the heavy durasteel case with a deafening CLANG right against the base of the planter.
The Astonian guard snapped into motion, his pristine ivory armor flashing in the sun as he stepped squarely between Quinn and the flower pot. His hand hovered just an inch from his holstered rifle.
"Excuse me," the guard said, his voice a flat, deadened monotone. "Media zones are across the plaza. Move this gear immediately."
Quinn stopped. He looked the guard up and down, a slow, condescending smirk pulling at the corner of his mouth.
"Move the gear?" Quinn scoffed loudly, his voice echoing off the nearby terracotta walls. He reached into his tunic, yanked out a thick, laminated press lanyard, and shoved it practically into the guard's visor. "Listen, pal. I am running a six-system simulcast. Do you know how much a Coalescent galactic broadcast slot costs?  I doubt you’ve ever even seen that much money—hell, you signed away your life for this shitty job just to stand on a sidewalk.If I don't get this angle, my network loses four million credits in ad revenue.Do you want to explain to the Royal Ministry why their parade looks like it was shot on a security feed?"
"By order of Palace Security, this perimeter is closed." The guard didn't blink. "Move it, or I will have you detained."
"Yeah, yeah, Palace Security, I read the brief," Quinn talked over him, the pitch of his voice rising as he spoke at a frantic, dizzying pace. He wildly gestured with his right hand, which held a steaming, half-empty cup of dark caf. " Look, I know your whole job today is just standing around looking pretty for the tourists, but adults are working right now. We’re authorized for mobile coverage—"
As Quinn swept his hand in a wide, erratic arc toward the street, the cup tilted. A thick splash of boiling hot caf flew through the air, slapping wetly against the rim of the flower pot. Dark liquid pooled and began to seep directly down into the soil.
The guard flinched. It was a microscopic break in his rigid posture. His eyes darted straight down to the soaking dirt, tracking the liquid.
"Hey, watch it!" the guard snapped. His hand shot out, grabbing Quinn's wrist in a sudden, vice-like grip.
Quinn didn't back down. He leaned his entire body weight forward and violently yanked his arm out of the guard's hold.
"Do not touch the talent!" Quinn shouted, stepping right into the guard’s personal space. He jabbed a finger toward the dark mouth of the alleyway just a few yards behind them. "Look, I don't want to be here any more than you want me here. If you want to play crossing guard? Fine.But my floor director is currently in that alleyway right there, splicing three hundred yards of fiber-optic feed directly into your city's sub-grid. You want us to move? You go in there and tell him to rip out the cables. We're planting the rig right over this pot."
Quinn crossed his arms, tapping his foot impatiently against the cobblestones.
The guard’s jaw visibly tightened. He looked down at the spilled liquid seeping into the dirt. He looked at the heavy durasteel case. Then, he looked at the deep shadows of the alleyway.
The guard took a slow step backward. He raised his left hand, tapping the comms piece resting in his ear.
"Command, Sector 7," the guard muttered, his Astonian accent bleeding through the rigid Royal tone. "Dealing with an unauthorized press crew. Stepping off the line into the alley to clear their director. Two mics."

Quinn stood perfectly still on the sunlit street. He watched the darkness swallow the white armor. Slowly, the frantic, manic energy vanished completely from his posture. His shoulders dropped. The arrogant smirk flatlined into a cold, hard stare and a sigh that held both hope and desperation

The air in the alley was ten degrees cooler, choked by the shadows of the towering, closely packed buildings. Thick, creeping ivy and hanging flora spilled down the terracotta brickwork, plunging the narrow space into a claustrophobic gloom. The smell of overloaded trash bins mixed with the damp earth. About forty feet in, the alley dog-legged into a hard, blind L-shape.
Near the midpoint, the Astonian guard spotted his targets. Two silhouettes stood with their backs to him. A tall, broad-shouldered alien in a dark suit, heaved what looked like a heavy spool of thick black wire. Beside him, a human in a cap stood reviewing a large, unfurled blueprint.
"I don't care who told you you're authorized to film here," the guard barked, his voice carrying a lethal edge as it echoed off the damp brick. "You need to find a different block. You are not permitted here, and I will have you arrested promptly."
Jimzon didn't even flinch. He turned halfway, keeping the brim of his cap pulled low to obscure his face in the shadows. Looking right past the guard’s shoulder, he channeled Quinn’s abrasive energy perfectly.
"Paul!" Jimzon yelled into the empty space behind the guard. "I told you to deal with the perimeter guards! Come over here and get this guy out of my cable zone!"
Dismissing the threat entirely, Jimzon aggressively snapped his attention back to the map in his hands.
The guard closed the distance, his forced politeness evaporating. "Sir, I don't give a fuck what this 'Paul' guy said. You need to leave. Now."
Jimzon sighed heavily and turned to face him. "Listen, buddy, this is all just a big misunderstanding—"
"Let me see your credentials," the guard demanded, stepping fully into Jimzon's personal space.
But as the guard stepped closer, the dim light finally caught the "equipment." The guard's tactical gaze locked onto Karagash's spool. It wasn't fiber-optic cable. It was a green rubber garden hose, crudely ripped from a wall spigot, clumps of wet dirt still clinging to the brass nozzle. The guard's eyes darted down to Jimzon's hands. The "blueprint" was a checkered cafe tablecloth, covered in frantic, meaningless crayon squares and intersecting lines.
It was a trap.
The guard’s eyes went wide. He dropped his stance, his hand sweeping down in a blur to draw his sidearm.
He never even touched the grip.
From the thick curtain of hanging foliage directly above the trash bins, Calsco dropped like a stone. The massive Katanzarian landed soundlessly behind the guard, his thick, muscular arms wrapping instantly around the man's neck in a brutal blood choke. The sheer, overwhelming downward force of Calsco’s weight folded the guard backward, driving the man's knees hard into the cobblestones.
Panic flared in the guard's eyes. Thrashing against the chokehold, his hand blindly clawed down toward his holster again.
Karagash stepped forward with terrifying speed. The alien's massive, suited hand clamped over the guard's wrist like an iron vise, pinning the weapon in place. Karagash leaned down, making dead, emotionless eye contact with the guard as the oxygen supply to his brain was severed.
The guard thrashed violently for exactly four seconds. Then, his eyes rolled back, and his body went entirely limp against Calsco's chest.
"Clean," Jimzon whispered, tossing the crayon-stained tablecloth onto a nearby trash bin. "Grab him."
Without a word, Calsco and Karagash hoisted the unconscious operative by his arms and legs. Moving with military precision, they carried the body swiftly around the L-shaped bend, dragging the Guard completely out of sight from the sunlit street.


Within moments, the unconscious guard was tightly rolled like a cigar in a thick, woven rug they had ripped straight off a low-hanging clothesline.
Daiser was already at the far end of the L-bend, a blue-hot blowtorch materializing in his smoky hands. Sparks showered over the cobblestones, reflecting brightly in his watery, orb-like eyes as he melted through the rusted iron hinges of a heavy sewer grate.
"We have exactly sixty seconds until Command pings his radio for a visual check-in," Pavlock hissed. He was pressed flat against the brick wall near the alley mouth, peeking back out toward the sunlit street. "Does that grate line up with the grid, or do we gotta find a new one?"
"I see a ladder down there," Daiser called back, kicking the superheated iron bar out of the way. The grate fell open with a heavy metallic clang, revealing a dark, humid shaft. "We're set to hit the cisterns."
Calsco and Karagash stood huddled over the rug-bound operative. Jimzon swooped in, dropping to one knee. He reached into the folds of the carpet, violently yanking the tactical earpiece and the lapel mic from the guard's vest, pressing the earpiece into his own ear.
Quinn came casually strolling around the corner, wiping a stray drop of coffee off his tunic. "How was that for a show?" he asked, a subtle, highly satisfied smile playing on his lips. He looked at the massive frames of Calsco and Karagash, then down at the open grate. "Can you two even fit down there? Look, as smart as my plan was, we got really lucky. Have you all even thought about how to carry him down a ladder?"
Calsco raised a thick eyebrow. He simply let go of his side of the rug.
The heavy, rolled-up package slid off the edge of the hole and plummeted into the darkness. A second later, a soft but incredibly firm THUD echoed up from the bottom of the shaft.
Quinn winced slightly. "Right. Well, that works, I guess. I just hope that wasn't his head that hit the floor first. We don't need him dead yet."
Before anyone could answer, the stolen earpiece in Jimzon's ear crackled with a burst of icy static.
"Sector 7, Actual," a sharp, authoritative voice buzzed. "Sit-rep on the civilian interference. Do you require a QRF or a patrol element to clear the perimeter? Over."
Jimzon's eyes darted to Karagash. He pointed at the spool of garden hose. "Bring that down with you," he whispered urgently.
Jimzon took a deep breath, cleared his throat, and keyed the lapel mic. He tried to force his voice into the soft, clipped vowels of an Astonian aristocrat, masking it with standard Guard brevity.
"Command, Sector 7," Jimzon said. "Negative on the element. Code Four. Situation contained. Just finishing the verbal warning now. Over."
He released the mic, the silence in the alley stretching painfully tight.
Pavlock stared at him in sheer horror. "Holy shit, dude," Pavlock mouthed silently. "That was the worst Royal accent I've ever heard." Pavlock immediately looked over at Calsco and tapped the side of his own neck—Get ready to go hot.
The radio clicked again.
"Sector 7, copy Code Four," the dispatcher replied, his tone shifting. "Be advised, Sector 6 is shifting down your line to assist. Street is green right now, so they're moving to your alley to check the perimeter. Visual in thirty seconds. Over."
Jimzon's blood ran ice cold. If Sector 6 turned into the alley right now, they were dead.
"Negative! Negative, Command!" Jimzon barked into the mic, dropping the accent entirely and leaning fully into panicked, desperate Guard slang. "Wave off Sector 6! I am... handling a Code Brown. I repeat, 10-100 emergency. Do not approach. Over."
The alley went dead silent. Quinn stared at Jimzon, completely bewildered.
For three agonizing seconds, nothing came over the comms. Then, the earpiece clicked. A muffled, poorly disguised snort of laughter came through the channel.
"A Code Brown?" the dispatcher asked, the strict military professionalism entirely gone. "Copy that, Sector 7. You're taking a tactical dump. Understood. I'll tell Sector 6 to hold your post on the street so they don't catch the smell. Hurry it up before the pre-show. Out."
Jimzon let out a massive, shaky exhale, pulling his thumb off the mic.
"Code Brown?" Quinn asked, raising an eyebrow. "Really?"

The guard’s eyes fluttered open, his pupils dilating as they fought to process the heavy gloom. He groaned, a low, wet sound that echoed off the high, vaulted ceiling. When he tried to shift, the coarse green rubber of the garden hose creaked against the salt-crusted stone of the pillar, the brass nozzle digging into the small of his back. He hung his head, letting his chin rest against his chest for a moment before awkwardly wiping the drool from his mouth onto the ivory fabric of his shoulder.
The chamber was a vertical graveyard of history. Above, the smooth durasteel foundations of the modern city hummed with the faint, rhythmic vibration of passing speeders, but down here, the layers of the old world were laid bare. The walls were a mess of eras; modern terracotta gave way to the ornate masonry of a middle age, and deeper still, at the very base of the pillars, rotted ceramic pots lay toppled and cracked. Some were the size of a man, their bellies spilled open like broken husks, half-shrouded by patches of salt-stained mosaics that depicted forgotten kings and celestial wars.
Near the center of the room, a narrow, man-made canal cut through the stone floor. The water was shallow, barely two inches deep, but it moved with a persistent, icy gurgle. It flowed directly over the guard’s boots, the cold soak darkening the leather and pressing into his socks with a steady, numbing chill.
The air was thick and stagnant, punctuated only by the rhythmic, heavy plip-plop of water dripping from the ceiling into the dark pools collected in the corners of the room. Quinn, Pavlock, and Daiser stood just at the edge of the light, their shadows stretching long and distorted against the ancient, cracked ceramic and the damp, weeping brickwork of the cistern.

The guard’s eyes slowly found their focus, locking onto Quinn, who was crouching beside him with a look of feigned concern.
"Hey, finally awake," Quinn said, his voice deceptively light. "Good morning, buddy. You took quite a hit to the head. You feeling with us, or are you going to pass out again?"
The guard looked at Quinn, his gaze drifting immediately down to his bound boots. He gave a sharp, instinctive struggle against the garden hose, the rubber creaking against the stone.
"I know you’re eager to get out of those. Don’t worry, I made sure they weren't tied too tight," Quinn murmured, checking his watch. "We just need to wrap things up here. The sooner we talk, the sooner you’re out of here."
The guard remained silent, his jaw set.
Quinn’s expression shifted instantly. He stood up, the warmth vanishing from his face, replaced by the cold, clipped professionalism of a mid-level bureaucrat delivering a lethal performance review.

The guard tried to speak, but his throat was a desert. He let out a raspy, painful cough.

The mission was a failure. The Royals intercepted the primary payloads at the ninety-degree bend because your sector"—he jabbed a finger toward the guard's chest—"went dark."
The guard squinted, the fog of the concussion clashing with the words. "You’re... you’re not Guard," he croaked, the doubt plain on his face.
He shook his head, trying to clear the haze before locking into a practiced, rigid internal defense. "My name is Meram Kest. I am a Second Sergeant of the Royal Shayron Defense Force. I serve the people of Shayron and uphold the dignity of the Crown. I will not betray my post. I am bound by oath, not by fear."
He paused, his eyes darting back to Quinn’s civilian tunic. "You're not Guard."
Quinn let out a sharp, annoyed breath and paced behind the pillar, shooting a brief, questioning glance at Pavlock, Daiser, and Karagash as if asking did we get the right guy? He whirled back around.
"Not Guard? How long has this deep-cover shit been rotting your brain if you've forgotten where you came from? And don't forget to call me sir," Quinn snapped. "You want to know my history? I went to basic at Fort Actom. My MOS? I started as a 704B Accountant before I moved up. I did my AIT in Maldo, Officer school at Wedear, and I led supply routes to Fawn. Back in those days, I’d trade a Tipa sandwich for a Meal 25 Brellovine pasta kit just to have something that tasted like home. I don't need to explain myself to you, Kest, but I'm on a time crunch."
The guard’s posture softened a fraction, the specific military details hitting home. He looked at Quinn with a desperate uncertainty. "If you really are who you say you are... wouldn't you have a question to ask me? A code phrase?"
"Code phrase?" Quinn scoffed, stepping into the guard’s personal space. "Specialist, the entire encryption grid was purged the second your sector went dark. We need to know what the fuck happened! Do you think we’re still using 'Blue Harvest' while the Royals are crawling all over our frequencies? Do you want to guess what it’s like up there right now? All ships are grounded. The Guard embassies are being held at gunpoint. All of this started with you. As far as I can tell, you’re the first domino, and that’s no accident. You divulged information, didn't you?"
The guard shook his head violently, fighting the dizziness. "None of them went off? You... no." He gripped his hair as best he could. "Command said the failsafe was legit."
"That’s your excuse? A failsafe?" Quinn yelled, his voice echoing off the ancient mosaics. "What about guarding your fucking post? What about situational awareness? What about the shit we drilled into your brain for years? Tell me—how exactly do you think a failsafe covers your ass?"
"The whole point of a dead man's switch is if you kill the captured, they still go off!" Kest shouted back, his defenses crumbling into a panicked explanation. "We don't need to wait for the signal point!"
"Clearly, they did not trust you with 'need to know,'" Quinn said, his voice dropping to a low, mocking hiss. "Because whoever the fuck you thought had the failsafe doesn’t, and they clearly don't trust you."

They stopped. In a small alcove away from the main festival roar, an old man sat on a low stone wall, cradling a traditional stringed instrument carved from a single piece of dark, polished root. There was no crowd. Just the old man, his eyes closed, and a melody that felt like it was pulling the warmth right out of the cobblestones.
Cal paused. He didn't check the time. He just stood there, his massive frame casting a long shadow, listening.
Jim looked around, his pulse a frantic, jagged rhythm. "Cal? How... how are you so calm right now? We’re on a clock. The Palace, the bombs... how are you just standing here?"
Cal didn't look away from the musician. "If you have the chance to appreciate something before it’s gone, Jimzon... you have to. Most people spend their lives waiting for the end of the song, thinking about what comes next. They forget to feel the notes while they’re actually playing."
He turned his head slightly, the sunlight catching the scars on his jaw. "If the world is ending in an hour, I’d rather spend a minute of it listening to something beautiful. There's nothing else I can do about the time. But I can choose what I do with the minute."
Jim went quiet. He closed his eyes, letting the vibration of the strings drown out the noise of the city. As the melody looped, the air of Shayron seemed to fade, replaced by a memory of a long weekend on a forest world with Tiam.
The Guard had surprised them with a new holiday, a rare pocket of peace that felt unearned. He remembered climbing an old, rusted water tower, the metal hot under his palms, until they reached the very top. They had sat there among the graffiti—beautiful, cryptic symbols scratched into the tank—just laughing as Tiam made some joke that nearly sent them both sliding off the edge.
They were running late to a party, the world loud and waiting for them somewhere else, but up there, it hadn't mattered. For the first time, Jim didn't feel the need to rush. He didn't care that they were late. He just sat there, listening to bird calls he’d never heard before and watching the light hit the graffiti. It was the first time he’d ever really looked at a dual sunset—the clouds heavy and purple after a storm, saturating the world in colors that felt new. Down at the base of the tower, the flowers didn't smell like regular blossoms; they smelled like subtle, ripening fruit. It was the last time he’d felt truly still, totally disconnected from the clock.
A sharp clatter on the stones snapped his eyes open.
A little girl running past had tripped, her small wooden toy tumbling across the pavement and stopping at Cal’s boot. Cal reached down, his massive hand moving with a gentleness that didn't match his scars. He picked up the toy, wiped the dust off with his thumb, and handed it back to her.
"Keep a tight grip on that, little captain," Cal said softly. "A ship’s no good to anyone if it’s sitting in the dust."
The girl beamed and ran off. Cal stood back up, the mask of the Warlord settling back over his features, his gaze fixing on the Palace spires.
im’s earpiece crackled, a jagged burst of static before Pavlock’s voice broke through, sounding breathless. "Jim? Jim, can you hear me? Prodar isn't a person. It's a place."
Jim stopped mid-stride, his eyes immediately scanning the fortified skyline of the Palace. "A place? I spent an hour looking for a dossier on this guy."
"I mean, I guess it’s kind of a person—it’s a bunch of them," Pavlock hissed. "It’s a designated hub. The Palace Relay Operations and... something-something Reserve. I don't know the rest. There are twelve bombs hidden in the city, but eight of them are hardwired to the Prodar relay. If we can get inside and flip the master switch, we diffuse eight instantly. We’ll only have to hunt down the last four manually."
Jim felt the weight of the mission shift. "Okay. Intel on the guards? Do we know how many spies are inside?"
"No... that’s all we got. I was hoping—" Pavlock’s voice hitched. "I was hoping our contact could give us the rest of the acronym, maybe a floor plan. But he can't."
"Why not? Keep pushing him, Pav, we need to know what we're walking into."
"No can do. He’s dead."
Jim paused, the air suddenly feeling very thin. "He’s dead?"
"Yeah. It’s... it’s fucked up. Daiser and I are en route to you now, but the crowds are thick. We're taking the side streets. I'll fill you in when we link up, but I can't talk about it now. Just get to that spire."
Jim swallowed the pit in his stomach and tapped his comms. "Berji. You in position?"
"Affirmative," the hollow, filtered response came back. "I am in the Clock Tower. They permitted me entry for a meditation ritual—they didn't care about the binoculars. On my way here, I flagged the high-density areas. I told the crowds a pilgrimage was coming through and the streets had to be cleared for the blessing. If those bombs go off... maybe a few more people live because they weren't packed like mags in the blast zone."
Berji’s voice stayed clinical, but Jim could hear the scout’s eyes moving across the Palace perimeter.
"I have a visual on the patrol points," Berji continued. "There are blind spots near the West entrance. Half the cameras are unresponsive since The Wipe the Royal Guard left the shells up to look intimidating, but only a few are actually drawing power. Look for the 90-degree bend near the service lift. That’s your gap."
Cal looked at Jim, his jaw set. "Eight bombs for one room. I like those odds better than hunting twelve ghosts in a festival. Let's move before the Royal Guard figures out their cameras are dead."

"I managed to stage a kit near the perimeter," Berji’s voice crackled. "The duffel is tucked in a bin behind Atton’s Astrovin—the winery just off Tongishaw Ave. Look for the dumpster; it’s buried under a couple of green disposal bags. I’ll act as your eyes for as long as the signal holds."
"What’d you pack?" Jim asked.
"Just what we talked about. Tools to keep it quiet, tools to make it loud, and things to  help improvise if it gets messy "

20 minutes pass

Jim ripped the zipper.The metal teeth of the bag sang a low, jagged note as they parted. Beside him, Calsco hunkered down, his massive frame casting a shadow that swallowed the bag.
"Let’s see what the philosopher sent us," Cal murmured.
Jimzon reached in, his hands moving with the practiced efficiency of a man who had packed and unpacked a thousand rucksacks. He pulled out the crowbar first. It was heavy, salvaged iron, but both the pry-end and the hook were wrapped in layers of black electrical tape so thick it looked like a club. He tested the grip; it was silent, the metal-on-metal scream dampened.
Next came the lubricant bottle, a plastic container of high-viscosity ship-lube with the Trailblazer logo scuffed off, followed by the "Loud" kit. Jim laid them out in the shadows: four breaching strips—flat, adhesive plastic explosives that looked like oversized grey band-aids—and a bandolier.
Jim picked up the bandolier. It was a rugged, earthy green strap with three individual pouches. He unbuttoned one to find a smoke grenade tucked inside—a dented metal cylinder that smelled of phosphorus.
"Three of 'em," Jim whispered, sliding the bandolier over his shoulder and cinching it tight against his chest. "Good for a quick exit if things get hairy."
He pulled out a messy tangle of copper wiring and a heavy capacitor. " this is the shitty EMP grenades we used to make when we had drills is OpFor back in the day. Shitty, but it'll work"
He opened a small, heavy tin of steel ball bearings and magnesium shavings.
"Night-vision killers," Calsco noted, glancing up at the palace spires. "One spark and every guard in that hallway is looking at a white sun."
Jim didn't answer. Cal was reaching into the very bottom of the duffel, his thick fingers brushing past a solvent-soaked rag that filled the alley with the sharp, toxic tang of a garage. He pulled out a small, flat rectangle wrapped in bright, festive foil.
Cal held it up to the sliver of sunlight peeking into the alley. It was a Shayron chocolate bar, the gold lettering gleaming against a deep azure background.
"Interesting..." Cal murmured, the word a deep rumble in his chest. He turned the bar over, his thumb tracing the embossed royal crest on the wrapper. "The man sends us enough explosives to take a gate, a rag that smells like a shipyard’s floor, and... dessert."
Jimzon looked at the bar, then at the heavy, taped-up crowbar in his own hand. "Berji knows we haven't eaten since the spaceport. And he knows it might be the last thing we taste today."
Calsco let out a short, dry huff of a laugh. He didn't open it. Instead, he tucked the chocolate into his breast pocket, right over his heart, and looked Jimzon dead in the eye.

“You ever think about how it takes a few minutes for the light from our own sun to reach us? And how, when we look at distant galaxies, we’re seeing light that left them thousands, sometimes millions of years ago? Like, if someone out there had some insanely powerful telescope, they’d be looking at Earth and still seeing dinosaurs walking around.
It’s wild to think about—those dinosaurs, in a way, still exist. They’re out there, frozen in time, carried by that light. Everything they ever did, everything we’ve ever done, it’s all immortalized.
And time’s weird like that. Even here, it takes a tiny fraction of a second for our brains to process what we see. So, in a sense, we’re always living just a little in the past. But at the same time, the light of our present selves is already traveling into someone else’s future. It’s like we’re everywhere, all at once—past, present, and future. Makes you think, doesn’t it?”

the crash chapter here is notes from chat gpt to improve Key Points to Convey:
Pavlock's Consistency and Subtext
Pavlock equates the disorientation and pain to drinking because it’s a relatable, familiar sensation to him—one of the only ways he’s experienced losing control. This highlights his emotional and physical vulnerability in a moment when he’s truly been broken.
His thoughts should contrast his usual stoicism, adding a subtle sense of fear or self-doubt beneath the bravado.
Berjis and the Details of the Injuries
The description of Berjis' injuries should linger. Focus on the grotesque, tactile sensations—how the wounds feel and what they represent. This creates a jarring shift from the ideal of invincibility to the gritty reality of mortality.
Injuries are no longer shrugged off or glossed over—they’re deeply felt, physically and emotionally, and will leave lasting scars (both literal and metaphorical).
Disorientation and Shock in the Group
They’re all overwhelmed—not just from the physical trauma but from realizing their vulnerability. The narrative can reflect this through fragmented, chaotic descriptions that mirror their mental state.
Include small but telling interactions between characters that show their growing fear for each other. A grim glance, a hurried attempt to bandage a wound, or someone trying and failing to joke about their condition.
Pain is Permanent
Show how this pain shapes their interactions and decisions moving forward. If they’re cautious now, it’s not just strategy—it’s dread. Perhaps a character hesitates before engaging in another fight, recalling the pain they’ve just endured.

Rewrite 





You’d be surprised how 
quickly people obey. A shout. A punch to the gut. Most will inch themselves off the plank and dive into the ocean without needing a push.

Sure, there are fiery souls who talk big — who stand tall and scream about freedom — but take away the audience, and they shrink. Nobody stands up when there’s no one watching.

People worship the idea of freedom like it’s some euphoric drug. But like any drug, too much of it is lethal. True freedom is an overdose — no laws, no morals, no direction. Just chaos. And the truth is, people are too stupid to rule themselves. They’re selfish. Panicked. Fragile.

The world is cruel and twisted. It doesn’t care if you live or die.

We offer something better: order. A place in society. Purpose. Stability. Your needs are met. Crime is low. Employment is high. You may not be free — but you’re safe. And if safety means ignorance…

Well, isn’t that a small price to pay to stop being afraid?”**

You call me a villain because I took away your choices. But choice is a burden — a trap disguised as freedom. It breeds doubt, and doubt festers into fear. And fear… you know exactly where that path ends.

By removing your choices, I’ve made myself the monster in your eyes. But in truth, I’m the wall between your soul and the trenches. You can scream. You can curse me. But you won’t fall. I won’t let you.

You’ll never have the chance to misbehave, to damn yourself, because I’ve taken that power from you. And maybe you’re right to hate me for it.

But if damnation is the price, then let it be mine alone. I’ll bear the weight. I’ll sink into the fire if it means building a world where every child eats, every worker rests, and no one sleeps with fear gnawing at their spine.

Tell me — what kind of villain fights to give people peace? In this life and after?
