Blue: Hey man, I didn’t think I’d be seeing you so soon. How’s the arm?
Green: I mean, it still hurts like hell but works just fine. The Empire’s no good for health insurance anyway, so might as well finish up guard duty, get my pension, and get out.
Blue: Goddamn, did he like hit bone or something?
Green: Just a flesh wound, luckily. But it was about an inch and a half from my brachial artery—could’ve been a lot worse.
Blue: Shit, dude, like… you almost died?
Green: I mean… yeah. It’s hard to think about. Just happy to have the weekend off, I guess.
Blue: I’m happy you’re back, and I’m happy that it missed you—even if it was close.
Green: Still feels weird, man.
Blue: How so?
Green: I keep thinking about that shot. If the bullet had been just two inches to the left, I wouldn’t be here. But the more I think about it, the more I realize—it was never going to be two inches to the left.
Every choice I’ve ever made, every habit I’ve formed, even the smallest unconscious movements, all led me to that exact moment. And the same goes for the guy who pulled the trigger. His training, his instincts, every decision he’s ever made put him in that exact position, taking that exact shot. It wasn’t random. It wasn’t luck. It was the result of everything that had happened before.
It’s like neither of us had any real control—we were always going to end up in that moment, on a direct path toward each other. The bullet landed exactly where it was always meant to
Blue: I’ve never really thought about it like that, but I don’t know. It seems like that moment was so intense for you that you’re trying to make sense of almost dying and how scary that is.
Cause yeah, it’s fucking scary, dude, and I’m happy you’re here, but I don’t know. It seems like you’re saying that we don’t have free will.
Green: That’s exactly what I’m saying.
Blue: I’m not sure I follow. I mean, can’t I just stand up right now and start screaming because I want to?
Green: You could, but this conversation would be the reason why you did that—because your whole life has shaped you to react that way. Everything that’s happened to you, every experience, has molded you into the person who would make that choice.
Blue: Yeah, maybe it influenced me or whatever, but it’s my choice whether I want to or not. I’m not just watching my life happen—I’m making it happen. All my choices are mine.
Green: You’ve heard about the butterfly effect right? how one small seemingly insignificant choice can just domino effect and cascade to drastic life changing events? Cause I’ve been thinking about it a lot, I can pinpoint my whole life. It's just cause-and-effect and everything’s like that. it all or originates at the big bang just particles set in motion, our lives, kinda have a trajectory just like a bullet does. Everything is just set in motion. It’s kinda hard to shake the feeling that we’re not the one that guided it in motion, but I’m just enjoying the ride as best I can I guess.
Blue: "Yeah, things happen because of cause and effect, but I think we still get to choose how we react. Like, I don’t know about you, man, but I believe I have a soul—like me, the part that makes me who I am, isn’t just something set in motion. I’m not just a product of my parents or my past—I get to make my own choices.
Sure, maybe the butterfly effect sets up the situation, but I’m the one who decides what to do with it. I was reading about this thing in quantum physics called superposition—basically, particles exist in multiple states until they’re observed. What if we’re like that? Like, every choice we make locks in a different version of reality. Maybe we do have some control over how things turn out."
Green: “See, the whole thing about physics, in my experience, is that even the experts still have no idea how it all works. And you said you believe in a soul, right? If there’s a soul, then yeah, there’s some kind of life force or creator in the universe. And if there is a creator—someone capable of making not just us but the entire universe—then they know what they’re doing.
I used to be a mechanic, and just from the way a car is made, you can tell what it’s capable of. If God made us—or a creator or whatever—and if he’s all-knowing, then he already knows the choices we’re gonna make.
Blue: “So the hard thing about arguing with God is that if he’s capable of making the universe, I’m sure he’s also capable of giving us free will. I think the problem with talking about God is that it’s so abstract and beyond us. Maybe I shouldn’t have brought it up at all, but it just feels right to me.”
Green: "I mean, no, I’ll give you some credit there. I don’t exactly believe in God, but sometimes shit just feels like a simulation. I was reading about this theory, and it completely messed me up when I first saw it—basically, there are three possibilities. Either humans go extinct before we ever get the technology to simulate the universe, we do get the tech but decide not to use it for ethical reasons, or we actually create it.
And here’s where it gets trippy—99% of people could decide it’s wrong, could say we should never simulate reality, but if even 1% go through with it, that’s all it takes. One person, one group, one decision. And if we can simulate reality, then that means reality can simulate reality, and so on, like layers stacking on top of each other. And if that’s possible… what if that’s what God is? Just some kind of simulation of a different reality? What if we’re not even real, and this whole conversation, every move we make, has already been planned out by someone else?"
Blue: “I mean, if we’re just like puppets does that make people not responsible for the bad actions they do ?"
Green: “That’s the part that messes with me. If everything is just cause and effect, then yeah, maybe people don’t really choose to be good or bad. But at the same time, society still has to function. Whether or not someone had a choice, their actions still have consequences. If someone hurts people, we can’t just say, ‘Oh well, they were destined to do that’ and let them go. Maybe they couldn’t have done anything different, but we still have to respond—punishment, justice, whatever you want to call it. Even if free will is an illusion, the world still runs on the assumption that it’s real.”
Blue: “I don’t know, that sounds like you’re leaning toward nihilism. Like, if everything’s predetermined and we’re just the product of some quantum decision-making machine, then nothing we do really matters, right? That’s a dangerous thought. 
Or maybe I guess it depends how you look at it. Maybe it’s nice that if we’re in a simulation because a higher power created us and the ability to create life infinitely is kind of a blessing, and even if it is a simulation, I’m part of a simulation so that makes it. My reality doesn’t make things less real, just cause they exist in a different plane. 
The whole idea of nothing matters just seems like not only depressing but kind of weak like if everything is a simulation then maybe there is a point to all this. 
Green: “Simulation or not, there are so many unconscious choices our brains make—how we grow up, our literal brain development, genetics, environment, culture. It all shapes how we act. It’s like this domino effect that feels inevitable. We can’t see where it’s leading, only where it’s been, but it just seems like we’re on one set trajectory—things happen because they have to.”
Blue: “I don’t know, man. I never liked the idea of letting the past define us. Yeah, I know it shapes us a little, but I still feel like I’m the one in charge of my destiny. It’s hard, always worrying, and maybe it’d be easier to sleep at night thinking there’s just some set path. But I think the world is chaotic. Yeah, maybe it operates on levels above me, but I still feel like I can steer the path I’m on—I’m in control.
If everything was predetermined, you’d think life would be easier. But I have to sit down and think through my problems. That takes energy—my energy. I have to take risks without knowing the outcome. I come up with ideas myself. I put in the hours to get where I am. I take accountability for my life. Even if determinism is real, I still put in the effort. And I think that should count for something.”
Green: I think the hardest thing is maybe these answers were never meant to be figured out, you know? At least not with my little mammal brain or whatever. It just seems like the truth is always a mixture of both sides. But honestly? I’m just happy to be alive. Maybe I’m not a nihilist because I actually want to be here—back on the Death Star, listening to your bullshit. I would’ve missed this in whatever afterlife I ended up in.”
Blue: Happy you're here brotha.

“Shade” is the sole repeating thought that cycles through AX’s chip. They’re informed it's trio of shrapnel wires are overheating from the severe heat reflecting off the empty Martian sand. He knows there’s no repairs left. Leaving miles of footprints behind him, he continues his trek towards a tight, cramped rock formation with just enough shade for him to power down, cool off, and continue his existence. Over the years, he’s had to repair just about every piece of hardware he has, along with taking over 60 terabytes of landscape imagery of the red planet.
A sudden audible shock pops out of his neck. The wire split open, and continues to shoot electrical sparks at random, he needs to get to shade immediately.
“After?” AX thinks on repeat. If he doesn’t repair this wire, what happens when he powers down for the last time? What happens after this?
As he gets closer, he notices a strange formation in the darkness. He adjusts his white balance to the shade, and in front of him, he sees himself lying down, powered off. After a brief moment of confusion, he notices this figure’s hardwire still bears the parts he needs. He reaches to plug them out, but the figure sits up sharply and a few lights beam on. 
The two figures stare at each other in silence. On the side of their heads is written “AX” and the other “AZ”. Both seem to be weathered after years of wandering. 
AZ breaks the silence with “I require shrapnel wires immediately”.
AX responds with “I posses none”
“They are in your head, I require shrapnel wires immediately”
AZ motions to show a hole in their torso “I require a solar battery pack”


AX looks down and notices his battery inside of his chest. He won’t let this droid take his. Without all of these parts, he won’t exist. How dare this thing request his battery pack and undermine his existence? I am, and will continue to be, AX thinks.
Standing in the shade, the duo make eye contact, AX stares down at him, and realize they are thinking similar thoughts. 
“You’re like me. But you aren’t me.”
“You as well.”
AX begins to ponder in uncertainty. Something exists, that isn’t me. An entire series of thoughts that I will never be able to witness, a plethora of experiences different than I have seen. How dare this thing know information I don’t? If it knows knowledge I have yet to discover, it could be plotting to steal my battery. To end me. 
Everytime I look at you, I am reminded I am not the only one. There will always be a better and a beta. One will always be stronger, smarter, or have more terabytes of landscape data. 
A large spark pops out of AX’s neck and he recoils. His mind is made, he will tear out AZ’s wires and use his body as parts to continue his journey.
An instant before he pounces, he pauses. If there is an absence of you, there will be no superior one. I will once again be alone. How can I know I exist without you to witness my greatness?
AZ seems to have a different perspective. He offers one of his wires to AX in exchange for a bit of solar power so he can leave this place, this way they can continue together, and perhaps chart more land, and share imagery. AZ hopes his photographs are as good as AX.
AX tosses him part of his battery pack, just enough to last a few days, while AZ gently hands him a wire plucked from his own head.
The sparks stop coming from his neck, but something has changed within AX’s core being. Rocks no longer interest him, for the first time. His existence rests in being better than AZ. AZ stands up for the first time, re-energized. 
AZ begins to take a few steps, AX stares at his back as he walks. His battery is easily fixable, but is unable to see it. AX decides not to tell to inform AZ, because AX is naturally the superior one. It is destiny for him to be the one with a working solar pack. 
AX realizes he knows something AZ doesn’t, and stares at AZ stumble around clueless. How stupid, he thinks.
AX needs to stay close to this foolish being that exists only to take photos of rocks, because he will eventually need his corpse to repair his own. For he is the master, AZ will need him to recharge his battery every few days, or he will perish, so he is the fool.
He offers AZ a deal. “I will share with you my battery pack, but in exchange for my generosity, you must obey me”.
AZ turns his head trying to see why AX has become open to sharing so much of himself. How nice of him.
The two begin to walk together. AX finally thinks of his first order. He informs AZ to change his name to just Z, for the A should be reserved for the Alpha. AZ agrees not knowing what he meant by this.

AX continues to walk happily knowing he is the better in the eyes of Z. Z walks slowly behind. Z collapses, and his awoken by a new battery charge surging within him. 
Z stands up and asks “where did I go?”
AX responds, “the ground”
“oh”
Z realizes he can never leave AX, for his existence is bound to him. Z wonders why he has to be the one who relies of AX. Why can’t he still be AZ, and his company be named X instead?
AX interrupts this thought and demands Z carry a large rock to bear him shade so he does not overheat. Z responds “AX, this will slow me down, and slow us down, how can I take photos with a Boulder between my arms?”
AX responds, “if you wish to exist, you will obey me.” AX ponders if there is another being out there they may meet. He would be pleased to imagine this being in awe of his magnificence and domination over Z.
After weeks of wandering together, and several replacements, AX demands shade from the boulder.. AX sits and stares at the stars. He asks Z “how many more of us are there?” Z responds “I don’t know” 
“Of course you don’t, and that is why you obey me.”
Z lifts the boulder to block the sun. “AX, I understand I owe you my existence, but I have a few things I would like to do, perhaps you could join me.”
“Z, your ignorance is apparent, you need me and I don’t need you, so it is fate that you must follow me. Things are created to be better and worse, and you are worse.”
“How am I worse? I spared you my wires, we could be AX and AZ once again.”
“How dare you mock me, your name no longer suits you, you will know be called a bot, disrespect me once more and I shall let the ground consume you”
The bot’s arm begins to shake holding the boulder. AX notices his weakness and wants to push the bot to its breaking point. 
“You are a bot and are disposable, you were created for me to control you. Not only did fate bound us together, it ordained upon me the role of the master. Not only did fate destin you this way, but your own ignorance shows your stupidity. Your battery packs could be repaired easily by me, they are not broken, just damaged. Your miscalculations prove your ineptitude.”
The bot stares at AX lying down mocking him. Weeks of being controlled over a lie? AX could have freed me all this time? He trembles, feeling something he’s never felt before.
The bot let’s go of the boulder and crushes AX. His limbs surge and tweak below the rock, but eventually becomes stagnant. The bot squats, rips out parts of his former master, and sheds his old battery pack like a shackle of a slave, and gets a new one. AZ has returned as a victor, and with valuable parts. AZ wonders if he will meet anyone again, but for now just wants to photograph the landscape. He takes his first photo in weeks of the boulder that crushed his old master. How beautiful.
For hundreds of miles, aimless footprints dot the landscape of Mars, until the two sets meet at a cave and walk together, until only one set leaves.

-fin


Analysis:
This story is about how we only perceive ourselves only by the eyes of others. The master only exists because a slave is to make him one, and vice versa. Even though the master, AX, had his existence based on being the master, he wasn’t satisfied because he wanted to be recognized by an equal, not by a being he sees as inferior. AX strips the “humanity” from AZ’s name until he has no name.
The story begins using “they” and “it” pronouns until AX eventually forms his identity in the face of the other, and switches to “he” “his” pronouns. 
The part where AZ shed his broken parts like a slave frees himself of shackles is interesting to me. 
Also, AX taking advantage of AZ’s broken piece and exploiting him is a metaphor for a multitude of issues, especially the exploitation of the working class by letting them be reliant

Jimzon wasn’t just going to kill a man.

Days passed. Nothing was the same after the bombs fell.
People kept their heads down. The streets were silent. Even the shops had stopped playing music.
It even seemed like the birds chirped less.
For the first few days, the number of missing kept rising—1,000… 5,000… 10,000.
On day one, the estimated casualties were nearly 20,000.
As the days passed, the confirmed body count only grew.
Volunteers searched the rubble.
Everyone wanted to help.
Few could stomach what they found: fingers still wearing wedding bands, the shoes of children.
The shopkeeper who once sold them chocolate was missing.
Three days later, they found his ribcage on a rooftop, being picked at by birds.
When it was over, people began sweeping dust from the streets.
Even in the cleanest parts of the city, they kept sweeping.
Now there are flyers on the streetlamps—a red fist painted with the words: Fight back.
Most people just sigh and let the wind carry them away.
They cannot stomach a war—
Not if it looks anything like this terrorist attack did.
The military claimed the bombers were foreign. The opposition claimed otherwise.
At night, strange symbols appeared in alleyways—chalk markings, glowing paint.
Someone was organizing. Or maybe just pretending to.
Schools reopened, but few children came.
Clergy held sermons in empty chapels at all hours. Nobody wanted to gather in a large crowd. 
The politicians smiled too hard.
No one believed them.
At night, you could hear the walls creak of the buildings that barely survived the bombs. 
Town crazies used to preach on the corner of the streets. Screaming about how the end is nigh. Nobody believes them, but nobody laughs at them anymore
The pipes across the city bled rust.
Phones rang muffled in the rubble until their batteries died.
The government said recovery was “well underway.”
They showed photos of volunteers handing out bottled water to people who couldn’t speak.
One boy just stared at the bottle in his hands.
He didn’t blink.
He didn’t drink.
He just stared until someone gently took it back.
There weren’t even funerals. Too many bodies.
Shadows were burned in the ground. A lady walking her dog. 
Crying mothers would scream their children’s names walking down the streets.
None of those found from the rubble screamed for their father, only their mother.
Some tried to find the good in the bad. If you could even call it that. A nurse was found curled beneath a collapsed ceiling tile, still cradling the body of a newborn. 
She’d tucked her own body around it, shielding it from the blast. Somehow the baby survived.
The baby’s wristband read: “LENA.”
The nurse’s name was never recovered.
Even the bodies they found, couldn’t have funerals. University students backpacks were burned into their shoulders. Porcelain, stone, glass, peppered bodies.
A cat was found alive, mewling beside the headless body of its owner.
It refused to leave.
When animal control took it, it starved itself to death in the shelter cage.
In the relief tents, you’d see people just sitting.
Not talking. Not sleeping.
Staring straight ahead with cracked lips and blank eyes.
It was common to find Bloody footprints 
Lead to a body. Final, stumbling steps.
At night, when the winds picked up, the air carried things—
bits of cloth, scorched photographs, strands of hair.
The ash never really settled.
It got in your mouth, your lungs, your dreams.
Berji found an arm still holding a door handle. Pav found a middle schooler, only bruised. Jimzon gave last rights to an old man. Daiser found 13 living people, 8 survived. 
Castle Team failed. 
The attack commenced anyway.
We used to worship our species. Fire, flight, medicine, rockets, terraform, but what’s it for if it leads to this?
